# Decisions

What was decided and why, newest phase last. A fresh conversation should be
able to resume from this file, `BACKLOG.md` and the README.

The app was called Closet until 20 November 2026, when it became Nines ("The name: Nines", at the end);
the entries before that use the old name.

## How the work is organised

The brief asked for eight phases with a stop after each. That was changed with
the owner once it was clear the recognition work does not depend on the app.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Two tracks: recognition and drawing, and the app | What the tagger reports and what the drawing shows is judged from render sheets and tagging reports, not from the installed program, and it will keep growing with the taxonomy. Gating the app on it stalled the app. |
| Track 1 (recognition and drawing) is reviewed continuously, with two checkpoints and no stops | The checkpoints are coverage of the taxonomy's visible values and accuracy on the owner's own photos. It needs no packaged build. Original phase 0 and the drawing half of phase 2 live here. |
| Track 2 (the app) has four main stops, then smaller ones | A stop is worth it only where something can only be checked on the owner's machine, where direction could change, or where money is spent. A: the program on the PC and iPhone (phase 1). B: the real wardrobe in (phase 2 and single-photo import, review queue and cost cap from phase 3). C: today's outfit (phase 4 with ratings from phase 6). D: gaps and shopping (phase 5). |
| Multi-item photos and video sweeps move behind the outfit builder | They are capture conveniences; the outfit builder and shopping are the two features the owner said matter most. Open for the owner to reverse. |
| The app stores the tagger's full answer, its schema version and the cutout for every item | This is what keeps the tracks independent: better drawings need no data change, and a taxonomy edit only means re-tagging, which is free on the Claude plan. |
| Template anchor points are fixed; category, formality, season and weight class are settled before stop C | The mannequin view relies on the anchors, and outfit and gap logic read those four vocabularies. Finer details can keep growing after stop C. |
| The repository is public | The owner made it public so that Actions minutes are free and unlimited for tests, builds and agent sessions. Commit metadata and tracked files were checked for personal details first. Going public cannot be recalled once copies exist, so the rule that photos and keys never get committed now protects against something irreversible. |
| App work can be done by Copilot cloud agents, orchestrated from a Claude session | The owner has a large monthly allowance of Copilot AI credits and wants to spare the Claude plan's weekly budget. Agents work from issues, open pull requests against `main`, and `ci` is the gate. `AGENTS.md` holds their rules; `copilot-setup-steps.yml` prepares their environment. Taste-driven drawing work stays in the Claude session with the owner. |
| The orchestrating Claude session merges pull requests when `ci` is green and its own review is clean | The owner's choice, for speed with several agents. It still stops and asks before merging anything that changes a recorded decision, touches releases or the release request file, or looks risky. |
| The first agent task is a bake-off | The same well-specified stop B task goes to Opus 5.5, Gemini 4 Argon and GPT 6 Astra (the strong models the owner's Copilot offers). They are compared on tests passed, review findings and rework needed, and the result decides which model gets which kind of task. |
| Bake-off result: Opus 5.5 is the default model for agent tasks; GPT 6 Astra runs alongside it where a second attempt is worth comparing | All three passed every check. Opus 5.5's storage view needed no rework: the screen matched the rest of Settings, it reused the app's own folder helpers, changed nothing else and documented its choices; it was merged (#13). GPT 6 Astra wrote the most thorough tests (loading, error, retry and offline states) but the weakest screen, and hard-coded folder names. Gemini 4 Argon had the fewest tests, changed a shared formatter and an unrelated behaviour, and skipped the decisions log; it is not used for now. One task each, so this is a first reading. The owner's rule: Opus 5.5 for most things, and the same task also given to GPT 6 Astra as a separate run when it costs nothing extra. The cost that remains is review time in the orchestrating session, so double runs are for tasks where the result is judged by looking (a screen, a drawing) or where the approach is open; both are compared on tests and screenshots, and only the better one gets a full review. |
| `main` is protected: changes arrive by pull request with `Lint and tests (Linux)` passing | The repo is public and agents open pull requests against it. Tests do not start by themselves on a Copilot pull request, and a run started by hand does not satisfy the rule; merging `main` into the pull request's branch from the orchestrating session starts a run that does, and also tests the result against the latest `main`. |
| The orchestrating session only orchestrates | The owner's instruction once the garment drawings were good enough for now: it writes the issues, starts the agents, reviews their pull requests and merges; it writes no feature code. Features are built by Copilot agents in waves of tasks that do not depend on each other, each issue naming its migration number so two agents cannot pick the same one. The numbers must follow the order in which the pull requests merge, because a migration only runs when its number is higher than the closet's current one: when a pull request with a migration is ready, its number is checked against `main` and changed if another has landed first (an agent caught this when `0003` was about to merge after `0004`). There is no `0002` and there must never be one. Agents do not edit `docs/BACKLOG.md`; the orchestrating session keeps it. Drawing work is parked in the backlog until the owner asks for it. |
| Finished agent work is reviewed before it is combined: changes that guard money or secrets by the orchestrating session itself, larger features also by a separate reviewer that has not seen the task being built; faults go back to the agent as a numbered list, each to be shown by a failing test first | The first two features reviewed this way (Insights, outfit ratings) passed all their own tests and still said untrue things to the owner or lost track of an outfit when an item was deleted. A reader who did not write the code, and was not told what to expect, found them. |
| What's missing is to be weighed by the owner's life, not a fixed grid | The owner, on seeing the screen: it "should have the ability to be informed by my style and context clues (ie. weather), as well as persona/situations (vacation, work, lounge, exercise)". Tasks #88 and #89: situations with how often they happen, the climate of the saved place, and taste from what he wears and rates. Until he answers the questions the analysis stays as it was. |
| Garment shapes were reviewed in a standing gallery, not ad-hoc sheets | The owner expected many rounds of review. `tools/make_review_gallery.py` built one page with every garment, every taxonomy value, the weight ladder, trousers and shoes; it was published as a private Claude artifact where the owner marked each drawing "looks right" or "needs work" with a note. Marks were stored per drawing with the round they were made on, so a mark on an older drawing was flagged for another look after a rework. The owner called the drawings good enough after round 7, and the gallery's tool was removed on 20 November (it is in the git history). |
| The app's interface gets a design pass later | The owner wants the interface-design review run on the app, after more features exist; the current UI is known to need a lot of work. |
| Agents are started through the `start agent` workflow so the model can be chosen | Assigning an issue with the tools the orchestrating session has cannot name a model, and GitHub then picks one itself; the first agent run landed on a model the owner does not want and was cancelled. The workflow makes the same assignment through the REST API with a `model` field. It needs a fine-grained personal access token of the owner, kept as `COPILOT_AGENT_TOKEN` in an `agents` environment limited to `main`, because the built-in token may not assign Copilot. |
| Model ids for agents are GitHub's own, with dots: `claude-opus-5.5`, not the vendor's `claude-opus-5-5` | Found by trying: GitHub accepted the assignment with the vendor's id and then refused to start ("The selected model isn't available"); the dotted id started the agent. GitHub does not fall back silently when a model id is wrong. The ids for the other vendors' models are still to be found the same way. |
| CI runs on every pull request and push to `main` | It was manual-only while minutes cost money. |
| The backend tests also run on Windows (`windows-latest`, job "Backend tests (Windows)"), beside the three Linux jobs and not part of the required check yet; tests that need the real Windows (Credential Manager, the registry, the tray, renaming a running program) are marked `windows` and skipped elsewhere; `PYTHONUTF8` is off there, as in the packaged program | The owner's hub is a Windows PC and its Windows-only code had never run. No browser tests and no bench there: the browser tests exercise the page, which is the same on any computer. It should become required once it has been green for a while without flaking. |
| CI runs as three parallel jobs (backend and bench, web page, browser tests), with the backend tests spread over every core (`pytest-xdist`, `-n auto`); a small final job keeps the name "Lint and tests (Linux)" that branch protection requires | One job running everything in turn took about 9–10 minutes per push while many agents were pushing. Side by side it is the length of the slowest job. All 875 backend tests passed on two workers locally, in 2m20s instead of about 4m40s. Locally `python -m pytest -q` still runs them one at a time. |
| "Phase" in older entries below means the original numbering | Phase 1 is stop A, phases 2 and 3 are mostly stop B, phase 4 is stop C, phase 5 is stop D. |

## Before phase 0 (agreed with the owner)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Menswear only: mannequin, base shapes and shopping | Owner's answer. |
| Runs on a Windows PC; the Mac (Apple Silicon) build is made only when asked | Owner's answer; macOS build minutes cost 5x Windows. |
| Home is New York City, prices in US dollars | Owner's answer; used for weather and shopping. |
| Illustrations are parametric templates, not freeform SVG written per item | Same attributes always give the same drawing, redraws are free, and every item of a category shares anchor points the mannequin view needs. Freeform SVG stays as a fallback for unusual items. |
| Gap numbers ("completes 9 outfits") are counted by code | A rule-based outfit-validity engine enumerates outfits; the model proposes and explains, it does not do the arithmetic. The outfit builder uses the same engine. |
| Background removal calls the ONNX model directly | The `rembg` package drags in numba, scipy and scikit-image, the main packaging risk. Pre- and post-processing is about 40 lines. |
| The program gets a tray icon with Quit, a single-instance check and optional start at login | A double-clicked server had no window (it has had its own window since 16 November, see "Closet's own window"; the tray stays, because closing the window leaves Closet running). Nightly backups only run while it is running, so it also catches up on start. Launches take an OS file lock until the server is ready, so two double-clicks cannot both start against the same closet; a crash releases the lock. |
| API key is kept in the OS keychain, environment variable as fallback | An encrypted file needs a key from somewhere; a passphrase-derived key would stall background jobs until login. |
| Keep the uncut 1600px photo beside the cutout | A bad cutout can be redone; costs roughly 100 MB for a few hundred items. |
| Backup and restore move to phase 1; the love/like/meh/dislike chip arrives by phase 3 | Real data and migrations start in phase 1; phases 4 and 5 rely on ratings. |
| The bench ships inside the packaged program as a `bench` command | The owner does not install Python. |
| No Batches API for now | It is asynchronous, which clashes with "usable immediately", and tagging is cheap enough that half price saves pennies. Kept in the backlog. |
| Scheduled work (price drops, queued shopping searches, and optionally an overnight refresh of the gap list) runs as a Claude Code scheduled task on the owner's Claude plan, exchanging files with the app through Google Drive | No API credits. The cloud task cannot reach the PC over Tailscale, so the app writes `wishlist.json` into a Google Drive for desktop folder and reads back `price-checks.json`, validated against the same schema as any AI output. The owner chose Google Drive; the app needs no Google credentials because the scheduled task uses the Drive connector. Built in phase 5. |
| The overnight gap refresh is optional | Counting outfits is instant code. Only proposing candidate pieces and writing the explanations needs a model call; running that overnight on the Claude plan keeps the list current for free, otherwise it refreshes on demand through the API. |
| "Would this work?" on iPhone is paste-a-link or pick-a-photo; no Apple Shortcut | iOS does not let home-screen web apps appear in the share sheet. The owner is fine with copy and paste. |
| Development may use Claude subagents (Haiku, Sonnet, Opus) as a stand-in for API calls | Owner's go-ahead. Stand-in answers are scored by the same bench, labelled as such, and never reported as cost or timing figures. |
| Packaged build runs on demand and at each phase sign-off, Windows by default | Private repo with limited Actions minutes; nothing runs in Actions until the owner says go. Releases hold the downloads; no stored build artifacts or caches. |

## Phase 1

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Phase 1 started before phase 0 was fully closed, at the owner's request | The paid API key is deliberately the last step. Phase 1 makes no Claude calls, so it needs no key. |
| SQLite through Python's built-in driver, with numbered `.sql` migrations and `PRAGMA user_version` | No ORM or migration library to package. A copy is taken before any migration; a database from a newer program is refused; a failed migration rolls back. |
| Every write transaction starts with `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`, and every connection waits up to 15 seconds for another writer (`db.connect`) | The cutout, describing and pattern workers, the shop thread and requests each write through their own connection. A plain `BEGIN` that reads before it writes cannot wait: when another connection is writing, SQLite answers "database is locked" at once instead of waiting, which made "Find some" lose what it found (#159). `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` takes the write lock first, so it waits like any other write. No transaction is held open across a call to Claude or the network; a search that still cannot keep its finds says so on the wish. |
| Program-wide settings live in `settings.json`, not the database | Passphrase hash, sessions, backup folder and model address are not part of a closet, so a restored or demo closet does not change who can log in. |
| The demo closet is a second closet under `demo/`, switched from Settings | Trying things never touches real data, the owner does not need command-line flags, and backups ignore it. Seven demo items are real open-licence photos with pre-made cutouts; the rest are drawn from templates. |
| Passphrase stored as a scrypt hash; session cookie is HttpOnly and SameSite=Strict; five wrong guesses lock login for five minutes | Standard library only. The server listens on 127.0.0.1; Tailscale serve is the only way in from outside. (Replaced on 20 November: Cloudflare Access on a hostname of its own is a second way in, if the owner sets it up; see "The doors from outside".) |
| Phone access shows only an address that `tailscale serve status --json` says reaches Closet (a share of the whole site, path `/`, proxying to `127.0.0.1` or `localhost` on Closet's port), with `:port` when it is not 443. When port 443 is used by another app, the suggested command gives Closet the first free one of 8443, 8444, 10000, 10001… (`--https=`); never a path share. Stopping is `tailscale serve --https=<port> off`, never `tailscale serve reset` | The owner's PC already shares another app on 443, and the computer's bare Tailscale name opened that app instead of Closet. A path share like `/closet` would break the page, which uses absolute paths. `reset` stops every share on the computer, including the other app's. Any use of a port by something else counts as taken, so Closet never shares a port with another app. Closet shared at a path only counts as not shared. |
| Phone access shows a QR code of that address, made in the page with `qrcode-generator` (MIT, no dependencies of its own), only when such an address exists and the page is open on the computer itself (a loopback host name, `frontend/src/here.ts`); on the phone the group is one line on adding Closet to the Home Screen, or nothing when opened from there. It is drawn black on white with a four-module quiet zone, 200 px, whatever the theme, with the address as text below it | The iPhone's camera opens it with no typing. The address is private, so no outside QR service is used. Writing a QR encoder ourselves (Reed–Solomon, masks, versions) would be more code to get wrong than a small, long-used library. Tests read the code back with `jsqr` (a development dependency only), from the module grid and from the screen in light and dark. |
| Uploads carry a client-made key | A retry after a dropped connection returns the first item instead of creating a duplicate. |
| The cutout queue is rows in the database, processed by one worker thread | Nothing is lost on quit; interrupted jobs resume; jobs wait while the model is missing instead of failing; three attempts before an item is marked failed. |
| The uploaded full-size file is never written to disk | Only the 1600px photo, its cutout and thumbnails are stored, all WebP. |
| Backups are dated database snapshots, each with a list of the images it had, and one store of images named by their content (SHA-256, in `content/`), shared by all snapshots; 14 snapshots kept | Snapshots use SQLite's own backup so they are consistent while the app runs. Restore moves the current closet aside instead of deleting it. *Changed (#218, approved by the owner):* the images used to be one add-only copy shared by every snapshot, kept by item and file name, so restoring an older snapshot after a cutout was made again gave the newer cutout. Named by content, each snapshot keeps exactly its own images, and an unchanged image is still stored once, so a backup of an unchanged closet adds only its database. Backups in the old layout (a snapshot without a list, and one `images` folder) are still restored; that folder is removed once no kept snapshot uses it. Images no kept snapshot lists are removed, only after the new snapshot is in place. Restore checks each image against its SHA-256. One backup runs at a time, across programs (a lock file in the data folder), so one never removes images another is about to list. |
| A restore keeps a journal of its moves and is finished or undone at the next start | A power cut or forced quit while the backup is moved into place could leave the new database with the old images, or no closet at all. Before the first move, `restore-journal.json` in the data folder lists every move (written whole, flushed to the disk), each marked once done, and is removed at the end. Every start reads it before the closet is opened: when every new file is there and whole (the database's SHA-256, each image's size) the restore is finished, otherwise every move is undone; never a mix. A journal that cannot be read moves nothing, and is kept aside. The owner is told once, in plain words, on every screen until OK. Moves never land on something that exists (Windows cannot replace a folder or a file in use), and a move Windows refuses because another program has the file open is tried again for about nine seconds. |
| ffmpeg is left out of the phase 1 program | Video was to arrive in phase 3. The packaging spike (removed 20 November; in the git history) proved it could be bundled, but it added about 105 MB to a file that unpacks on every start; phase 3 was to decide between bundling and download-on-first-use like the model. Video sweeps were later left out by the owner, so the program bundles no ffmpeg. |
| CI and release workflows are manual-only for now | The owner has not yet approved spending Actions minutes. Files travel between release jobs through a draft release, because artifact storage is exhausted. |
| Outbound HTTPS uses the computer's own trusted certificates (`truststore`), with a bundled certificate file as fallback | The first Mac build could not download its model. The likely cause, not yet confirmed on the owner's Mac: a packaged Python has no certificate store on macOS, so the HTTPS connection fails and was reported as no connection. The system store also works on a work computer whose network adds its own certificate. The release build now checks an HTTPS connection from the packaged program, and a certificate failure has its own message. |
| A release is started with the `release` workflow's Run button (or the same call from the orchestrating session) | For one day, before the workflows were on `main`, a release was started by editing a request file; that trigger is removed now that the normal route works. Windows and macOS can be built separately. |
| A newer program that finds an older copy running asks whether to close it and use the new one, instead of only opening the old one | Before, a downloaded update looked like it had not installed, because double-clicking it opened the copy still running. The question is a plain window (a message box on Windows, a dialog on the Mac); a copy started at login (no tray icon) is never held up by a question, and the same version, a newer one or a copy run from source is still simply opened. The lock file holds a random token, and a copy quits when `POST /api/instance/quit` sends it: only something that can read the closet's own folder can do that, so this adds no way in from the browser (a page cannot set the header without the server allowing it, and it does not). Copies from 0.2.0 and before have no token, so they are stopped by their process id: on the Mac that is the same as Quit; on Windows the process is ended, which SQLite survives, and it is a one-off for those versions. After replacing, start at login is pointed at the new program, and the new copy waits for the old one's port so phone access keeps working. Checking whether a port is free now uses the same socket option the server uses, so a port whose last connections are still closing is not skipped. |
| Closet updates itself from the latest published GitHub release, checking once a day and on demand | The owner asked for it. Only `releases/latest` of this public repository is asked, without a token and with a plain `Closet-updater` User-Agent, so nothing about the owner is sent; drafts (a release still being built) and pre-releases never count. No new dependency: `urllib` and `hashlib`. Offline and GitHub's rate limit are noted quietly and tried again an hour later. |
| A download is checked against the SHA-256 `digest` GitHub's releases API gives for each asset, not a separate checksum file | GitHub works the digest out itself when the file is uploaded, so it needs no change to the release workflow (which agents may not edit) and cannot drift from the file. It comes over the same HTTPS connection as the release, so it guards against a broken or swapped download rather than against GitHub itself, which a checksum file in the same release would not do any better. A release without a digest is not installed. |
| "Ask me first" is the default; "Install updates by themselves" and "Never check" are the other choices | An update restarts the program, which closes the page for a moment and could interrupt the owner in the middle of adding clothes; they should see it first. Ask-me-first still downloads and checks the new version ahead, so Install is quick. "Never check" still allows Check now. |
| Every closet is backed up before an update, and the update stops if that fails | A local snapshot of the real and demo closets goes into `backups` (`pre-update-…`, the last three kept), plus the usual backup into the owner's folder when one is chosen. The database is the one thing an update could damage. |
| The program is swapped by renaming, and the old program watches the new one start, putting itself back if needed | Windows lets a running exe be renamed, not overwritten: it becomes `Closet.old.exe`. On macOS the downloaded zip is unpacked with `ditto` beside `Closet.app`, checked with `codesign --verify --deep --strict` and swapped in (old kept as `Closet.old.app`); the quarantine flag is removed only from that bundle. A copy of the old program started with a hidden `update-watch` command starts the new one with `--replace` on the same port, so the existing "newer copy replaces older" hand-over keeps the address and login; if the new one has not written its lock file and answered within 60 seconds, it is stopped, the old program is put back and started (with the copy of a closet from just before the update, if the new program had already upgraded it beyond what the old one can open; the upgraded copy is kept in `backups`), and the reason is written to a small file the program reads (the watcher never writes `settings.json`, to avoid two writers). The new program removes the old one once the watcher reports success. Programs started this way get `PYINSTALLER_RESET_ENVIRONMENT=1`, so the new one does not reuse the old one's unpacked files. |
| Saves are sent with `keepalive`, and waiting saves are sent on `pagehide` or when the page is hidden | A change made just before a reload or close used to be lost: the browser cancelled the save, and a text field only saved when it lost focus. Every save with a small body (an item edit, a wear, a rating, a trip tick, settings) is now a keep-alive request, which the browser finishes after the page is gone; it still carries the login cookie and goes to the same address. Photos are not (too large for it), nor questions the app can call off (Today's outfits, a trip plan, a figure). Browsers allow 64 KiB of such bodies on their way at once and refuse one that would go over, so the app counts its own and sends a save the usual way past 48 KiB. When the page is hidden or closed, an edit still being typed in an item's text fields is saved, and trip ticks waiting behind another are sent at once instead of one after the other. Saving on hide means switching to another app also saves what is typed, which is what the owner would expect. |
| Saves (`POST`, `PUT`, `PATCH`, `DELETE`) go straight to the network, past the service worker, where the browser supports it (static routing, `addRoutes` in `sw.js`) | A note typed just before a reload was still sometimes lost (#147; the browser test failed 10 times in 30): the save left the page, but with the service worker in its path the browser sometimes dropped it as the page went away; with the worker out of the way it always arrived. The worker never kept anything but `GET` answers, so nothing is lost by skipping it. Browsers without static routing (Safari, Firefox today) still pass saves through it as before. |

## Stop B

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The storage view adds up known folders on every request, nothing is stored | Sizes are always current and there is nothing to keep in step. It reads only the closet's images, its database (with SQLite's `-wal` and `-shm` files), and the shared `models`, `logs` and local `backups` folders, without following links, so nothing outside the data folder is touched; a backup folder the owner chose elsewhere is not counted. The total is the sum of those parts (small files such as `settings.json` are left out). The item count includes archived items, since they take space too. Sizes are shown with 1 KB = 1000 bytes, as the model download already does and as macOS does; Windows Explorer counts 1024, so its numbers come out a few percent lower. |
| The tagger lives in the backend as `closet/tagger/` (schema, taxonomy, prompt and API route, plan route, route order, and `models.json`); the bench uses that code | The app needs it and there must be one copy, so a prompt or taxonomy change cannot reach the bench and miss the app. At first each bench module of the same name replaced itself with the backend module (`sys.modules`), so `closet_bench.claude_cli` *was* `closet.tagger.claude_cli` and existing imports, tests and tools worked unchanged, including tests that swap out a function; most of those stand-ins have since been removed and the bench imports the backend's modules. `closet_bench` puts `backend/` on the import path itself. The drawing code (templates, renderer, fills) stayed in the bench at first (it has since moved, see below); the list of shoe styles the drawing has a shape for moved into the tagger's taxonomy, because the schema reads it and the tagger must not need the drawing code. The taxonomy test and the vocabulary test import the tagger directly (they are in `backend/tests/` now). |
| Whether the plan route can be used is checked with `claude auth status`; whether the API route can be used, by whether `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` is set | `claude auth status` reads the local login, asks Claude nothing and exits 0 when signed in, so Settings can check it on every visit for free. It runs with the API key removed from its environment, as a real plan call does, gives up after 5 seconds, and its output (which names the account and the config folder) is never returned or logged. The keychain is meant to hold the key (see "Before phase 0"); since the API key box in Settings, a key saved there (service "Closet", account "anthropic-api-key") counts too, and wins over the environment. Only a key's presence is looked at. |
| The `anthropic` package is in the packaged program, pinned with what it needs (part 2) | Both routes need it: the API route to call, the plan route for the strict answer schema it passes to `claude -p`. The program's self-check imports it and builds that schema, so a build that left it out fails its smoke test. |
| The drawing code (templates, renderer, fills) lives in the backend as `closet/drawing/`; the bench uses that code | The app has to draw an item from its tags, and there must be one copy, so a template change cannot reach the review gallery and miss the app. Done the same way as the tagger: each bench module replaced itself with the backend module, so existing imports, tests and tools worked unchanged. Nothing about the drawings changed: the review gallery was regenerated before and after and was byte-for-byte the same, the 36 drawing fingerprints in the bench's tests (now in `backend/tests/`) passed untouched, and regenerating the demo closet changes no file. |
| The app's program did not include OpenCV at first; the drawing code imports it only inside the functions that read a photo. *Replaced* by "OpenCV is part of the app" (Patterns drawn from the photo, below): the packaging spec now bundles `cv2` | Only the bench's "traced" style and the fills measured from a cutout (dominant colour, swatch, stripes, print) used it then, and the app drew from tags (the traced style and all those fills but the print were removed on 20 November). Moving those functions elsewhere would have split the module the bench imports; a delayed import keeps one module and costs nothing. `numpy` and Pillow, which the drawing code imports at load, are already in the program. The packaging spec excluded `cv2` then, so a build machine that happens to have it cannot bundle it. The moved code keeps its own style; the backend's linter skips four of its rules for those three files rather than have them rewritten. |
| The app draws an item through `closet.drawing.item`: from its stored tagger answer, or from the coarse fields alone; it never raises | The item screen must always open. A value the tagger's vocabulary does not know (or a field an older answer lacks) is dropped and the rest is kept; if drawing still fails, the plainest drawing of the category is returned. A bag, belt, hat, scarf, "other" or unknown category gives no drawing, as there is no shape for it; the app shows the photo. Drawn in style B on a solid fill of the item's colour (its own hex if it is a valid one, else its colour word): a pattern needs the photo, so it is not drawn here yet. The packaged program's self-check draws a shirt this way, so the smoke test proves the package was bundled. |
| "The same photo" means the same file as the owner chose it, compared by its SHA-256; the browser sends that hash with the upload | The page shrinks and re-encodes every photo before sending it, so the bytes the server receives can differ between browsers and between the phone and the computer; the chosen file's bytes do not. The server cannot check a hash of a file it never sees, which is acceptable for a single-user app behind its login. Without a hash (another client, a browser without the crypto API) the server hashes what it received. Stored in `photos.source_sha256` (migration `0005`); photos added before it get their received-bytes hash, the best that is known. Archived items count; a deleted item's photos are gone, so its photo can be added again. A photo of the same garment taken twice is a different file and is added; telling those apart is left to the review queue. A duplicate answers 200 with the existing item and `duplicate: true` rather than an error, so an import just moves on. |
| Bulk import uploads two photos at a time and keeps its list in the browser (`localStorage`), not the files | Two lets one photo upload while the next is being shrunk, without crowding the computer that also removes backgrounds. A browser cannot reopen files the owner picked or dropped after the tab closes (not on the iPhone, and not for dropped files anywhere), and copying every photo into browser storage would double the space a large import takes. So after a closed tab or a restart the list of names and upload keys survives, and the owner chooses the same photos or folder again. Each file is matched to its row by name, size and date and sent with that row's first key, so the server recognises the ones that went in without processing them again, and its duplicate check catches anything else. Files that went in are not skipped by the page itself: the server is the one that knows whether that item has been deleted since. |
| While Closet cannot be reached, imports wait instead of failing | An upload cut off by a lost connection goes back to waiting and the queue carries on when the page sees Closet again, so a restart in the middle does not leave a list of failures. |
| The packaged program is built and smoke-tested on Linux by the `package-check` workflow on every push to `main`, on pull requests that touch packaging or what the spec bundles, and by hand | Changes such as the tagger, the drawing code and the `anthropic` package each changed what the program must include, and a broken build was only found when a release was asked for. Linux is the cheapest runner and shares the spec, the pinned requirements and the smoke test with the release, so a missing module, data file or migration shows up there; Windows and macOS are still only built by the release, and the background-removal check (which downloads the model) stays there too. This narrows "packaged build runs on demand" (see "Before phase 0") for Linux only. No cache and no uploaded build: storage is still exhausted, and a run takes about 4 minutes without them. The smoke test now also checks the page's scripts are served with the right type, a demo drawing is served, every migration is bundled and both closets reach the latest. |

## Tagging each item (part 2)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The API route is only used when the owner turns on "Allow the API key" in Settings, and only with a monthly limit; it is off by default, for the plan route nothing is needed | The API key is billed per photo. A key that is set but not allowed counts as no route: items wait. The switch and the limit are kept in `settings.json` with the other program settings, not per closet. A switch turned on before limits existed counts as off until a limit is set. |
| Tagging jobs are rows in the same `jobs` table as cutouts (`kind = 'tag'`), worked by a second thread with the same rules | Nothing is lost on quit, an interrupted job resumes, three attempts and then failed, as for cutouts. A separate thread keeps a slow Claude call (up to minutes on the plan) from holding up cutouts. A job is queued when the cutout is ready, in the same transaction. |
| While no route to Claude can be used the job waits and does not use up an attempt; the worker looks again every minute, when a cutout finishes, when the settings change and when Settings checks the routes | "Not signed in" or "out of credit" is not the item's fault. A route that looked usable but turns out not to be (the plan's limit was reached mid-call) puts the job back the same way. The routes are worked out again before each item, so turning the API key off stops it at once, not after the queue. A job left half-done (a quit, a crash) is put back at the start of the next round, and failed once it has used its three attempts. |
| The tagger's whole answer is stored with the item (`tag_answer`, JSON), with the schema version, route, model and time | Better drawings later read the details from it without asking Claude again, and a taxonomy change only means tagging again. `SCHEMA_VERSION` in `closet/tagger/schema.py` says which shape an answer has; a test holds a fingerprint of the schema and fails until the version is raised. |
| The answer fills `name` (only when there is none), `category, color, color_hex, pattern, formality, season, silhouette, material`; a field the owner has set is never changed | `source` records who set each field: the owner's changes are `user`, the answer's are `ai`. A field is filled when it is empty or was filled by an earlier answer, so describing again updates suggestions and leaves the owner's own words alone; a name typed at upload counts as the owner's. `color_hex` comes from the colour's name. |
| Items added before this version are not queued by the migration; the owner can use "Describe it again" on any of them | Queuing a whole closet at once on first start would surprise the owner (and, with the API switched on, spend money). |
| The demo closet is never sent to Claude; its 49 items carry an answer made in advance by `tools/make_demo.py` from what it already knows about each one (route `demo`) | The demo shows the same screens as a real closet with no account and no cost. A photo added to the demo closet gets its cutout but is not described, and "Describe it again" is refused there. An older demo closet gets the answers the next time it is opened. |
| Only the kind of a tagging failure is logged, never Claude's reply, a photo, a key or a folder; the owner sees a plain sentence | Claude's reply can quote what it saw; the reason shown says what to do next. |
| A cutout or description that fails for good shows the reason its job recorded under "Details" on the item, and writes it once to the log at WARNING with the item's id, after keys, tokens, e-mail addresses and paths outside Closet's folder are taken out (`closet/failures.py`); each attempt still logs only the kind of failure | Asked for in issue #103: an unknown failure could only be read from the `jobs` table with a terminal. This narrows the row above: the recorded reason can quote up to 200 characters of what Claude Code printed, and it now reaches the page and the log once. It is cleaned when it is read, not when it is stored, so reasons recorded before this are cleaned too, and a path inside the closet's folder is shown from `[Closet folder]` so it does not carry the owner's user name. What looks like a secret is matched by shape (`sk-…`, `api_key=…`, `Bearer …`, any 32 or more letters mixed with digits), so an item id inside a path is hidden too. No migration: the reason is the failed job's `error`. |

### Review queue

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A field is doubtful when the tagger filled it and its confidence is below one threshold per kind of field: category 0.70, colour 0.85, pattern 0.70, formality 0.80, season 0.80, silhouette 0.70 (`UNSURE_BELOW` in `closet/review.py`, the one place they are written) | Starting values, to be tuned once the owner's own photos have been through the bench. In phase 0 every wrong answer came with a confidence of 0.8 or less, and one wrong colour came at exactly 0.8, so colour is asked about below 0.85; formality and season are judgement calls, and the tagger got formality wrong at 0.7, so 0.8; category, pattern and silhouette were always right in those runs, so they are only asked about when the tagger itself says it is guessing (below 0.7). Higher thresholds ask about more; the aim is a queue short enough to clear after an import. Material, the name and the details carry no confidence (or are not fields the owner sets) and are not reviewed. *Since 22 November every new item is in the queue once as well, for what it is, its name, its brand and its price: see "The item page in the owner's order".* |
| Confirming marks the field as the owner's (`source` `user`) and sets its confidence to 1; a new endpoint does it, changing a value uses the existing `PATCH` | The tagger never overwrites a field the owner set, so a confirmed or changed field stays out of the queue after "Describe it again" with nothing new stored; no migration was needed. Confidence 1 matches what a `PATCH` already stores. |
| The silhouette is one field in the queue | The tagger gives one confidence for the whole silhouette, and the item stores one source for it, so confirming or changing any part of it makes the whole silhouette the owner's. |
| "Skip for now" changes nothing; skipped items are shown again the next time the screen opens | Skipping is "not now", not "this is right". Within one visit the screen moves on and offers to go round again. |
| Waiting and failed items are counted beside the queue, not in it; in the main closet an item whose cutout is still being made counts as waiting, since describing follows it; archived items are left out | A field Claude has not answered yet has nothing to confirm; the screen says how many are still waiting or failed and links to them, so an empty queue is not mistaken for "all done". |
| In the demo closet the answers made in advance carry their confidences; five items have doubtful fields: Madras plaid shirt (colour 0.5, season 0.65), marl turtleneck (colour 0.55, silhouette 0.6), white leather sneakers (category 0.6, formality 0.6), tan chore jacket (category 0.65), grey wool trousers (formality 0.75) | So the Review screen can be tried without a Claude account. The values are in `UNSURE` in `tools/make_demo.py`; an older demo closet picks them up the next time it is opened, for fields still marked as the demo's. |
| The badge's count is asked for again when the screen changes, the closet changes or the connection comes back; offline the screen says reviewing is unavailable | One small request; the service worker does not keep the queue, as an old queue could offer to confirm fields already changed elsewhere. |

## The drawing in the app (part 2)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Every item's drawing is stored with it (`illustration_svg`), with the drawing version it was made with and what it could not show (`illustration_version`, `illustration_notes`, migration `0007`) | Drawing takes under a millisecond, but the grid fetches a drawing by an address that changes only when the drawing does, so it can be cached forever and works offline. `closet/illustrations.py` joins the item to `closet.drawing.item`. |
| The drawing is made again when describing finishes and when the owner changes `category, color, color_hex, pattern` or `silhouette`; any other change leaves it alone | Those are the fields the drawing shows. A name, rating or note cannot change it, so it is not redrawn and its address stays the same. |
| The owner's own fields win over the answer: where `source` says `user`, the drawing follows the item's field. A shape the owner set (sleeves, neck, fit, shoe height) also sets aside the answer's matching details (sleeve length, neckline and collar, fit, shoe style) when they differ | The answer's details are more precise than the coarse shape, so the drawing prefers them; but if the owner says "long sleeves" for a polo Claude saw with short ones, the drawing must show long sleeves. A colour word set by the owner without a hex is drawn from the word, not from Claude's hex for the old colour. |
| `DRAWING_VERSION` in `closet/drawing/__init__.py` is raised when templates change; an item whose stored version is older is redrawn the first time it is listed or opened, not all at once at start-up | Start-up stays quick however large the closet. Redrawing while listing costs about half a millisecond an item. If the database is busy the new drawing is still shown and stored next time. |
| The demo closet keeps the drawings `tools/make_demo.py` made; `items.json` records the drawing version they were made with. Until 20 November the seven photographed items had stripes and fills measured from the photos by the bench; since then they are drawn as the app draws a described item (see "Repo clean-up", at the end) | Until 20 November: redrawing them from tags would have made them plainer. When `DRAWING_VERSION` is raised, `tools/make_demo.py` is run again so the demo is made with the new templates. A demo item is redrawn from its tags only when the owner changes a field the drawing shows. |
| What the drawing could not show is listed beside it: Claude's "seen but not drawn" features and the drawing's own notes (a pattern drawn as its colour only, a value drawn approximately, details it does not know, drawn plain) | The owner sees why the drawing differs from the photo, and the list is what the drawing grows from. An answer that cannot be drawn at all gives the plainest drawing of its category with a note saying so; drawing never stops an item from opening. |
| The closet grid shows photos or drawings, chosen once for the whole grid and remembered on the device (`localStorage`); photos by default | A grid of drawings is easier to scan for what to wear, a grid of photos is what the owner knows. An item with no drawing (a bag, or one not described yet) shows its photo in either. The item page opens on the drawing when the grid shows drawings, otherwise on the cutout. *Changed (the owner, 22 November):* "can you make a setting where i can set drawings as the default images?" Settings' **Show drawings instead of photos** (`show_drawings` in `settings.json`, off by default, in Everyday: it is not about this device, so not beside Look) decides for every device what every small picture of an item shows: the grid, Today, Worn, trips, the Wardrobe's pieces and "Would this work?", and which picture the item page opens on. The grid's switch is kept only while the app is open (not on the device) and is set aside once the setting changes (`drawing.gridViewOf`): one rule, the setting is where everything starts, and nothing remembered on one device can disagree with it. The page reads the setting on its own when the closet opens (`GET /api/settings/pictures`, its own query key) and keeps the last word on the device (`closet.showDrawings`) for when Nines cannot be reached (the service worker never keeps the settings). Not through the settings' own query: read early, that wrote its parts (the model's state among them) over what the screens had read and made the settings look fresh to Settings, Add and Advanced, which then did not read them again on opening (three browser tests found it); a settings read or save writes the setting's own key too. Left as they were, the photo being the point: the item page's photos and its own Drawing / Cutout / Photo switch, a shoe's photos, Add and its rows, Fix the details, Retake and "Would this work?"'s photo of the piece considered. |

### Correcting an item, and its history

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The owner's details are a column of their own (`own_details`, JSON, migration `0011`), laid over the answer's details when the item is drawn; Claude's stored answer is never edited | Same rule as the coarse fields' `source`: "Describe it again" replaces the answer and leaves the owner's word standing. Keeping them apart also lets the history say exactly what the owner changed. A feature (V insert, toe cap ...) is kept as `"yes"` or `"no"`, so the owner can both add one Claude missed and remove one it imagined. An item Claude never described is drawn from a plain answer made from its fields, with the owner's details over it. |
| Migration numbered `0011`, not `0008` as the issue said | `main` took `0008` (wishlist), `0009` (record of calls to Claude) and `0010` (worn outfits) while this was open; numbers must follow merge order, or a closet already past the number would never get the table. |
| What can be fixed depends on the kind of garment (top, bottom, shoe), in groups (collar and neck, front, sleeves and cuffs, pockets, hem, sole and laces, shape, cloth); the values are the tagging schema's, said in plain words in `closet/details.py`. The number of buttons goes from 0 to 10 | One vocabulary for Claude and the owner. Ten buttons covers every shirt in the demo closet (seven) with room to spare. Bags, belts and items with no kind yet have no details to fix. |
| Each choice that changes the drawing is shown as a small drawing of the item with that value, made by the drawing code when that one detail is opened; choices that would look the same are shown as words | Choosing by looking is what the owner asked for. Drawing all choices of one detail takes a few milliseconds; drawing every choice of every detail at once (about 370 drawings, 3 MB) would make opening the panel slow on a phone, so one detail is asked for at a time. |
| A shape the owner sets on the coarse fields (sleeves, neck, fit, shoe height) removes their own matching details (sleeve length, neckline and collar, cut, shoe style) | The latest word wins, as it already does between the shape and Claude's details; otherwise "long sleeves" could be drawn short because of an older pick. The removal is recorded in the same history entry. |
| A sentence is turned into changes by Claude with the same routes as tagging (plan first, the API key only when switched on, a wait when neither can be used); the answer is checked against the vocabulary, and nothing is applied until the owner confirms | A guess applied silently would be worse than no change: values outside the vocabulary are listed as not understood and dropped, never kept. Claude may change details of the current kind, the coarse fields and the shape. |
| The sentence is first sent with the item's description only (its fields and details, not its name, notes or brand); the photo (the front cutout) is sent only when Claude says it cannot understand the sentence without it, and the answer says which was sent. Only the photo with its background removed is ever sent: while that is not ready, or if it failed, no photo is sent and the answer says why | Words are cheaper and send less; most corrections ("three buttons", "white collar") need no photo. The photo with its background may show a room or a person. |
| Reading a sentence goes through the same spending meter as tagging (`spend.Meter`, purpose `correct_item`): every call recorded, plan calls at no cost, every API request checked before it is made against its own careful estimate (`spend.estimate_correction`: the longest prompt this app writes, doubled, a photo if one is sent, and the longest answer allowed), and a refused key turns the key off as for tagging. When only the limit stands in the way the answer says so and offers the picker | A typed correction can make up to four billed requests; it must not go around the limit the owner set, or be missing from the record. |
| In the demo closet picking and the history work fully; a sentence is answered with a sentence that the demo closet is never sent to Claude | As for "Describe it again" there. A demo drawing is the one `tools/make_demo.py` made (until 20 November, for the photographed items, from the photo by the bench's fills); it is redrawn from its tags once a detail is picked. Each history entry keeps the drawing from before it with a fingerprint of everything the drawing is made from, so whenever an undo brings the item back to a state it had, in any order of undoing, that state's drawing comes back: the hand-made one once the item is as it was. |
| Every change to the description is kept in a `changes` table with its parts before and after, when, and who (`owner` or `claude`): Claude's answer arriving (also when it says the same again), a field changed, details picked, a correction by sentence, an undo. Parts are found by comparing the item before and after, so any route of change records what really moved | The owner can see why the drawing is as it is, and can go back. Notes, wears and the laundry are not part of the description and are not kept here; the name and material are, because Claude's answer fills them. |
| Who set a field is part of what is recorded: confirming fields in the review queue, or a `PATCH` giving the same value, makes the field the owner's and is an entry ("You confirmed the colour as navy"); Claude only growing more or less sure of a value is kept in its entry but not said | Otherwise undoing an earlier change could not put back who set the field, and the history would not explain why "Describe it again" leaves a field alone. |
| Undoing a change gives a field back to the tagger with the value Claude's current answer gives it (the rule an arriving answer follows, `tagjobs.fill`), not the value stored before the change, when an answer has come since | Example: the owner sets the colour to green; Claude describes it again as cream (green stays); undoing the owner's change gives cream, marked as the tagger's with its confidence, matching the drawing. Restoring the older navy would leave the field and the drawing disagreeing until the next description. Undoing Claude's new description itself puts back everything it changed (fields, name, material, confidence, the answer). |
| Changing the kind of garment drops the owner's details the new kind does not have, in the same entry (undo brings them back) | Otherwise a collar picked on a polo would stay hidden on trousers and could not be given back. |
| A change can be undone while it is still the latest change to everything it touched; Claude's first description cannot be undone; an undo is itself a change that can be undone. Undoing redraws, or puts back the stored drawing when nothing else changed since | Undoing an older change under a newer one would silently undo part of the newer one. With nothing left before it, "undoing" Claude's first description would leave an item that looks described but has no answer. |
| The service worker does not keep the details, choices or history; offline the controls are disabled and say why | They are only useful for changing the item, which needs Closet to be reachable. |

## Stop C

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Being in the laundry is its own state on an item (`clean` or `laundry`), not a value of `location`; `location` says where the item lives when it is clean | The two would otherwise contradict each other (an item "in the drawer" and "in the laundry" at once), and taking an item out of the wash would lose where it goes back to. Migration 0004 moves every item whose location was `laundry` into the laundry state and leaves its location unset, since where it lived before was never recorded. `laundry` is no longer offered as a location, and the server refuses it with a message pointing to "Put in the laundry". |
| *(Changed 23 November: see the row below.)* A wear is one item on one calendar day, the computer's local date; a day can be given but not one in the future | "Wore it today" is the everyday case and should not need a date; counting the same shirt twice in a day would mislead the outfit builder. Undo removes that day's wear. |
| *(23 November, from the logic review.)* A wear is still one item on one calendar day, the computer's local date, but a day may be given up to the computer's tomorrow (`wears.AHEAD_DAYS`); after that it is the future | From a phone a time zone ahead of the computer (Lisbon or Tokyo while the computer is in New York), "Record an outfit" sends the phone's today, which the computer called the future until its own midnight. The day stays the computer's otherwise: "I'm wearing this" with no date is the computer's today. |
| *(Changed 23 November: see the row below.)* Each item keeps a count of wears since it was last marked clean, and the time it was marked clean; removing a wear only lowers the count if it was recorded after that time | The count stays right when a wear logged before the wash is removed afterwards. Marking an item clean always resets it, even if it was not in the laundry: it has just been washed. A wear can be recorded while an item is in the laundry (it may have been worn again before the wash). |
| *(23 November, from the logic review.)* Each item keeps a count of wears since its last wash. A wear for a day the piece has been washed since (`wears.washed_since`) is kept in the log but not counted, and does not put the piece in the laundry: a day before the date it was marked clean, or, for a piece that goes in the laundry by itself, a day before a laundry day that has come when its wears in the week before that laundry day, this one included, reached its count (it was in the basket). A wear on the laundry day itself, or on the day it was marked clean, counts. Removing a wear lowers the count only if it was counted. The wears of earlier weeks carried over are not followed: jeans worn once a week for three weeks and filled in afterwards count as worn since the wash | Filling in last week from memory put every top worn then in the laundry at once, as if worn today, and kept it out of Today's outfits until the next laundry day; Saturday's outfit recorded on Sunday morning waited a week. The everyday case (worn today, recorded today) is unchanged. Following each piece's count week by week through the log would be exact but would rebuild state the owner may have changed by hand. |
| The demo closet's wear history is stored as "days ago" and turned into dates when the demo closet is first opened | So it always looks recent. It does not move after that: a demo closet opened a month ago shows wears a month and a half old. |
| The closet grid filters clean or laundry items on the page, not by asking the server | The list the page already holds has the laundry state, and it keeps working offline. The server's `laundry` filter is there for the outfit builder. |
| *(23 November, from the UI review, A7.)* "All clean" is undone in place, by **Undo** in the line under the toolbar that said what was in the laundry, not in the toast; the laundry stays shown, and the grid says "Your laundry is empty." Marking clean answers with what the wash changed (its time, and the count, last wash, when it went in and who put it there) and sending that back puts the piece back as it was, with any wear recorded since added to the count. A piece washed again since is refused | The toast has no button and goes after a few seconds; the line is where the owner just tapped, and the app's other Undos (a piece back from the cleaner's, a thing to remember) sit where the change was made. Putting a piece "in the laundry" again would have dated it today and as put in by hand, so the next laundry day would have left it, and undoing the wear that put it in would no longer take it out. Kept in the answer rather than in a table: no migration, and an undo is only offered while the page that made it is open |

### The outfit engine

The rules live in `backend/closet/outfits.py`; every number is a named constant at its top. These are the choices someone could have made differently.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Two passes: strict rules first, then the same rules as penalties with a warning each | A big closet should never be offered a hoodie on a business day, but a small one should still get an answer ("the sneakers are more casual than a business day calls for; nothing better is clean") instead of nothing. The relaxed pass runs only when the strict one finds no outfit at all. Dislike, archived, laundry and the layering order are never relaxed. |
| The day is dressed for at the low plus 30% of the way to the high | A layer is easier to take off than to find. Warmth is counted in points per piece (by place and weight class) against a target that falls about one point per 2°C; being too cold costs more than being too warm, except at 24°C and above where nothing can come off. |
| Bands: no mid or outer layer, no cool-season pieces and no heavy base tops at 24°C and above; an outer layer whenever the closet has one at 8°C and below; no shorts below 18°C; no sandals below 20°C or in rain | Round numbers for New York that the owner can change in one place. Between the bands the warmth points decide. |
| *(23 November, from the logic review.)* From a high of 30°C, heavy trousers and jeans (heavyweight or heavier, which jeans are unless their tags say otherwise) cost more points than a loved piece gains (`HOT_HEAVY_PENALTY`) and the outfit says "The dark jeans are heavy for a high of 31°C" | In the hot band the warmth points could not tell: one top with trousers reached the "too warm" note only with super heavy trousers and long sleeves, so heavy jeans were offered at 30 to 38°C with nothing said, and loved ones came before the shorts. The owner's band says "shorts or light trousers". |
| Weight class comes from the item's own weight class when it has one, then words in its material, then words in its name, then its category (a warm-season item one class lighter) | The item's own weight class is taken from the tagger's stored answer (see "What the engine knows about the cloth" below). "Plaid flannel shirt" and "Burgundy cable sweater" say how heavy they are in the name; the category alone would make every shirt midweight. Specific words win over generic fibres ("merino wool" is midweight). |
| Layering: each layer is at least as heavy and as bulky (oversized counts one class bulkier) as the one under it; a base top that is midweight or lighter and not oversized goes under anything | This is the owner's rule (no heavy overshirt under a thin bomber). Without the exception a standard tee could not go under a light windbreaker. |
| Places: base is a tee, shirt or polo; mid is a sweater, a hoodie, a shirt (only over a tee) or a jacket (only under a coat); outer is a jacket or coat | An open shirt over a tee and a blazer under an overcoat are both normal; a shirt over a shirt is not. Sweaters and hoodies are never the only top, which leaves out a turtleneck worn alone. |
| Formality: every piece within one step of the occasion and of each other; the outer layer may be two steps away; business never gets hoodies, sneakers, sandals or shorts, and formal also no tees, polos or jeans | A good overcoat goes over jeans and a chore coat over a suit on the way to work; the rest of the outfit has to agree. The category bans catch items tagged too generously (leather sneakers tagged smart casual). The mood moves the formality aimed for by half a step in the score, not the hard limits. |
| Colour: neutrals are black, white, grey, navy, tan, brown, cream, olive and light blue, and blue jeans; at most one other colour unless the colours are tonal (same family, or stored hues within 25°) | Light blue shirts and olive trousers behave as neutrals in menswear. "multi" counts as a colour that is never tonal with another. |
| Pattern: plaid, floral, camo, graphic and "other" are loud, stripes are quiet; at most one loud pattern and at most two patterned pieces | A Breton stripe under a plaid overshirt is a classic mix; two plaids are not. |
| Rain: sandals never; suede, canvas, linen and silk shoes and outer layers cost points; boots and an outer layer whose name or material says it sheds water score higher; otherwise the owner is told to take an umbrella | The stored fields say no more than that about rain. |
| Items in storage are used, with a small cost and a note to get them out | The demo's only overcoat is in storage; leaving it out would leave a cold day without a coat. Items in the laundry are never used: the laundry state (`laundry` other than `clean`) or the location `laundry`. |
| Worn in the last 4 days costs points, most for today and less each day, half as much for bottoms, outer layers, shoes and accessories | Jeans, coats and shoes are worn again more often than tops. It is a preference, so an item worn yesterday is still chosen when there is nothing as good. |
| A build-around item may break the day's rules (shorts at 5°C, a disliked shirt) and the outfit says so | The owner asked for it. Archived and laundry items cannot be built around. |
| Outfits offered differ in at least two of base, mid, outer, bottom and shoes; if the closet cannot give enough, one | Accessories do not count. The next outfit is chosen by score less a small cost per piece shared with one already offered, so the list is not three versions of the same outfit. |
| Only the best six candidates for each place, plus the warmest and the lightest, are combined | Every combination of a few hundred items is millions; this takes well under a second and keeps a warm coat in reach on a cold day. |
| *(Changed 23 November: see the row below.)* Accessories are added after the outfit is chosen: a scarf at 8°C and below, a hat at 4°C and below, a belt with trousers or jeans (preferring the shoes' colour); a bag only when built around | They do not change which outfit is best, and shoe-matching leather is the one rule worth applying. |
| *(23 November, from the logic review.)* Accessories are still added after the outfit is chosen, but: no belt with trousers that have a drawstring or elastic waist (joggers, sweatpants, track pants, lounge pants: `NO_BELT_KINDS`, the taxonomy's garments with an elastic waist, or those words in the name); and on a day the outfit is short of warm, the warmer of the scarf and the hat is tried first and neither is left at home because its colour competes with the rest. The card then says so ("The rust wool scarf is for the cold, whatever its colour") | A leather belt was added to joggers, which have no belt loops. At -8°C the demo's one scarf stayed at home in every outfit because a rust scarf was a second accent after a mustard beanie, tried first; a person puts the scarf on. Colour still decides on a day that is warm enough. |
| Scores start at 80 for an outfit that breaks nothing; loved pieces, one accent colour and rain-ready pieces add, distance from the formality aimed for and warmth off target subtract | So that 100 is rare and a relaxed-pass outfit is visibly lower. |
| The API answers 422 for a build-around id not in the open closet; the engine on its own answers with a `missing` sentence | A wrong id from the screen is a bug to show; the engine itself never raises for a closet's contents. `suggest` takes `worn_days_ago` from the request; `today` fills it from the wear log. |
| *(23 November.)* The engine remembers its rule-only answers by value: `_outfit_problems`, `_style` and the same rules inside `_search`, keyed by the pieces (compared and hashed by value) and the occasion, in tables that belong to the rules object in force and are emptied at `MEMO_MAX` answers each; pieces equal in value are made one object (`_one_of`) so keys compare by identity first. `_pick` keeps each outfit's most shared places instead of working them out again for every outfit chosen. No processes, no new dependency, and nothing computed differently | Checking one piece ran the whole gap analysis again with the piece in the closet: 40 kinds of day, most of whose outfits are the closet's own, which the closet's analysis had already judged, and the same pieces were judged together again on many days. Remembering by value removes that repetition without restructuring the analysis, so "Would this work?" still runs exactly the gap analysis What's missing runs and its answers cannot drift from it. A short cut that skips the days the piece cannot enter (the hot days, for a coat or a sweater) was measured and left out: those days take about 2% of the grid's time, and a sufficient condition for the other places needs a dozen side conditions. Processes were ruled out: the app is packaged with PyInstaller for Windows and macOS. The tests compare the remembered answers with the plain functions over a wide sample, and count the work, rather than compare with recorded answers, which every change of the rules would only regenerate. Measured on a developer machine under load, five stand-in finds judged on the demo closet: 22 to 28 s before, 4.6 to 6.4 s after; one check after the closet's gaps 4.6 to 4.9 s before, 0.8 to 1.1 s after; the closet's gaps 3.3 to 4.2 s before, 1.0 to 1.5 s after; the engine on the 40 kinds of day 2.8 to 6.0 s before, 0.8 to 1.9 s after. The full answers of 320 checks (64 pieces on the demo closet and on four small closets), 16 checks weighed by how the owner lives, the gap analyses and 1,720 outfit suggestions (each of the 40 kinds of day, with and without a piece built around) were recorded before the change and are identical after it. |

### The owner's outfit rules (#150)

The engine now reads its places, layering, temperature bands, rain, formality, colour, pattern and proportion rules from the `outfit` and `colors` blocks of `docs/menswear-taxonomy.json` (`backend/closet/outfit_rules.py` turns the owner's sentences into rules; restart the app after editing the file). Where the table above disagrees with the owner's file, the file wins; the rows "Bands", "Places", "Layering", "Formality", "Colour", "Pattern", "Rain" and the scarf and hat thresholds of "Accessories" are now the owner's, not hand-written constants.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A whole block missing from the file is read from the old hand-written rules (written in the file's own words in `FALLBACK_OUTFIT`); an empty block means no rule | Deleting a block by mistake should not leave the engine with no rules at all. |
| *(Changed 23 November: see the row below.)* The band is chosen by the day's high; the warmth target is still the low plus 30% of the way to the high | The file's bands are named by `high_min`. Within a band the warmth points still pick the pieces. |
| *(23 November, from the logic review.)* The band is still chosen by the day's high, except on a wide day: the high is in the "base only" band but the low is in a band that asks for a layer over the base (`Band.asks_layer`: a layer it requires, or "base + light mid or light outer"; with the owner's file, a low under 21°C and a high of 27°C or more). Such a day may carry one light layer (midweight or lighter, a mid or an outer layer, never both, a cool-season one too) and the warmth points decide; the card says it is "for the cool morning" and to take it off as it warms up, and an outfit still light for the morning says that more would be too hot later, instead of "take another layer" | Today dresses for the hours the owner is out, so a spring day leaving at 7am runs from 5 to 28°C. The hot band then forbade every layer while the same card said "This is lighter than 5°C to 28°C calls for; take another layer". The threshold is the owner's own bands, read at the low: 21°C is where their file stops asking for a layer. The rest of the hot band (no heavy base top, shorts and sandals by the temperature dressed for) stands. |
| A layer the band says "must" be worn is required only when some outfit in the closet can have it | A small closet still gets an answer, with a warning that the layer is missing. |
| *(Changed 23 November: see the row below.)* In rain, "outer must be water-resistant" restricts the outer layer to ones whose name or material sheds water only when the closet has one; otherwise any outer is offered with a warning. A rain-proof layer is required only where the day's band requires an outer layer anyway (from a 16°C high the bands leave it optional): the owner, 17 November, "a rain jacket is definitely not required on warm days". Without one those outfits say to take an umbrella | Otherwise a closet without a raincoat would get nothing on a cold rainy day. |
| *(23 November, from the logic review.)* In rain the outer layer is restricted to rain-proof ones only when one of them is warm enough for the day's band (as heavy as the lightest outer layer the band asks for: "insulated" on a freezing day, "warm" from 4°C, anything from 10°C). Otherwise every outer layer stays in: the rain-proof one still scores higher (`RAIN_FRIENDLY_BONUS`) and an outfit without it says to take an umbrella. The rest of the row above (a rain-proof layer required only where the band requires an outer layer) stands | On a freezing wet day (snow counts as rain) the thin mac was the only outer layer let in, so the parka stayed home and the fleece with it (too heavy to go under the mac), and the card said the outfit was too light. A person wears the parka and takes an umbrella; the owner's own line of Your style says "I carry an umbrella, so a rain jacket is never a must". |
| Never-break rules (the file says "never" or "only"): two patterns of the same type side by side, athletic pieces with dressier ones, and the layering order are excluded even from the relaxed pass; ratings never override them | The issue asks hard rules to stay hard. |
| "One dressier piece is fine" applies to a top piece (tee, shirt, polo, sweater, hoodie, jacket, coat) only | The file's example is a blazer; dressy trousers with a tee are not what it means. |
| Every piece, the outer layer too, sits within the file's reach of the occasion; the old two-step outer reach is gone | The file says "every piece within one level". A smart casual coat therefore no longer covers formal days. |
| Colour families are #152's `taxonomy.COLOR_FAMILIES` (kept equal to the file by a test), read through the rules: neutrals and brights from it, and strong colours that are tonal, share an earth, cool or warm family (not two brights) or are a listed pairing count as one accent; light blue is no longer neutral | One mapping of the owner's families for the whole app; "two neutrals plus at most one accent" then means one accent colour family. |
| Shoe colour rules apply to dress shoes; belt-matches-shoes picks no belt rather than a wrong colour | Sneakers and boots are casual and the file's rule is about tailoring. |
| Proportion rules only add or take points, with a warning; they never exclude | The issue says so. |
| *(23 November, from the logic review.)* A tapered leg counts as a slim leg in "slim top with slim or straight bottom" (and so still fights a roomy top) | The app's legs are tapered, straight or wide, so "slim" never matched and a slim dress shirt over tapered wool trousers or chinos always "fought a little" (33 times in the review's sweep of the demo). Tapered is a slim leg to most people. |
| *(23 November, from the logic review.)* On an athletic day a piece What's missing does not count as exercise clothes (`life.eligible(item, "exercise")`: jeans, tailored trousers, button-up shirts, boots and other kinds not made for sport) costs points (`MAKESHIFT_PENALTY`) and is said as a makeshift ("The dark jeans are not made for sport, so this is a makeshift"); such an outfit is never called "right for sport". The formality rules are unchanged | Today knew only formality, where casual sits next to athletic, so with the joggers in the wash it offered an oxford shirt and jeans, "right for sport", while the Wardrobe on the same closet said jeans are not exercise clothes. One rule for both halves of the app. |
| *(23 November, from the logic review.)* "Wear this again" asks the same list of what cannot be worn as the outfits do (`outfits._unavailable`), so a piece waiting to be mended is left out and filled like one in the wash. A piece Nines never chooses by itself (tennis or golf shoes, swim trunks, the owner's loungewear) comes back with the outfit it was worn in, as when building around it, and is listed for Claude too | The two lists had drifted: a piece waiting to be mended was kept and could never be placed, and kept tennis shoes were never among the pieces to choose from, so Today answered with no outfit. Wearing an outfit again is the owner asking for its pieces by name. |
| *(Changed 23 November: pinned on a trip, it is packed beside the days and worn in none; Trip packing, "Loungewear and sport on a trip".)* The owner's loungewear and sleepwear (the taxonomy's `loungewear` kinds: pyjamas, sleep shorts, lounge pants, lounge sets and sweatsuits, robes) is never in an outfit Today, Trips, What's missing or Would this work? makes, unless the owner builds around it or pins it on a trip; the smoking jacket is the exception | Every occasion the engine dresses for is for going out. Lounge pants were the bottom of the demo's best casual outfit and a robe could be the coat on a cold day, because the engine read only the category. When nothing else is left, the closet says the pieces are "for wearing at home". |
| A piece marked "needs repair" is left out of outfits as "waiting to be mended", like a piece in the laundry | The owner said it cannot be worn as it is. |
| Would this work? tries a scarf or a hat on the gap analysis's cold days made 6°C colder | The owner's rules add a scarf or a hat only on days with a high below 4°C, and the analysis's coldest day is 0 to 6°C, so every scarf and hat was "hard to wear". Only the check of a scarf or a hat moves; the days the closet's gaps are found on are unchanged. |
| Not read (listed in `Rules.unread`): watch, eyewear and gloves (no app category), the tuck rule (the app does not record tucking), pattern scale (not tagged) | The engine cannot express them yet. |

### Today's outfits by themselves

`POST /api/outfits/today` fills the day for the engine: the weather (`backend/closet/weather.py`), the wear log and the laundry (`backend/closet/today.py`). The engine itself is unchanged; it already read the laundry state as the wear log stores it (`clean` or `laundry`).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Where the owner is, is a place saved in the program's settings (`settings.json`), not in a closet. Until the owner chooses one it is **New York** (the owner's home), so Today has the real forecast from the first open; a place the owner chose always wins, and removing it goes back to New York.
