It is chosen by name, or with **Use my location**: only when the owner taps it, the browser is asked for its position, which is rounded to two decimals (about a kilometre) and named by OpenStreetMap's reverse geocoding (Nominatim; only those coordinates are sent), then saved like a chosen place. Where the browser refuses, or the page is not a secure context (the phone over plain http), the button says so in one sentence and the search stays. It is never guessed from the network address | Real weather with nothing to set up matters more than a blank form, and the owner lives in New York. A guess from the address is often a city away (or the internet provider's city), and the owner should know exactly what is used, so the browser's position is only ever asked on a tap and the saved name is shown. Open-Meteo has no reverse lookup; Nominatim needs no key and gets only the rounded coordinates. Its time zone is left to Open-Meteo (`auto`). Both closets share the place, so the demo closet works like the real one. No migration. |
| The weather comes from Open-Meteo (`api.open-meteo.com`, places from `geocoding-api.open-meteo.com`) | No key, no account, free for personal use, and a daily low, high, chance of rain and weather code is all the engine needs. |
| Privacy: the only things sent to Open-Meteo are the words typed into the place search and the saved place's latitude, longitude and time zone. Nothing about the closet is sent, and the place is not written to the logs | Settings says so in one sentence next to the search. |
| A forecast is kept for an hour (in `settings.json`) and reused; it is asked again sooner when the day has changed at the place | Today's forecast barely moves in an hour; this keeps the screen fast and the requests few. Keeping it in the settings file means it survives a restart. |
| When Open-Meteo cannot be reached, the last forecast for the same place is given back marked as old; with none, the answer says so in plain words (not an error page) | The closet still works without the internet. Changing the place forgets the forecast. Today's outfits use an old forecast only when it is for today. |
| With no forecast for today (offline, the service down) and no temperatures typed, Today dresses the owner for a mild, dry day (14–20 °C; `source: "mild"`) and says the weather could not be fetched, with **Try again** and **Type temperatures** | Today never shows no outfit just because the weather is missing; a mild day gives ordinary outfits, and the notice makes clear they were not chosen for the real weather. Typing temperatures is behind **Change**, no longer the first thing shown. |
| Tests never reach the weather or place-name services: a `conftest.py` fixture makes every backend test's lookup fail unless the test fakes it, and every browser-test backend runs through `tests/e2e_backend.py`, which does the same | A forgotten fake would otherwise call Open-Meteo (now that New York is the default, any Today call would) and make tests depend on the network. |
| A day is rainy when the highest chance of rain is 50% or more; when the forecast gives no chance, when its weather code is drizzle, rain, snow or storms | The engine's rain rules (no sandals, no suede, take an umbrella) are about wet ground and wet shoes, which snow shares. |
| Any of the low, high and rain given in the request wins over the forecast, field by field; with both temperatures and rain given the weather is not looked up | So the screen can let the owner correct a forecast ("it's warmer than that") without losing the rest. With temperatures given and no forecast, rain is taken as no. |
| Days since an item was last worn are counted from the computer's local date, the same date wears are recorded on | A forecast for a place in another time zone may be for a different calendar day; the wear log's own date keeps "worn yesterday" meaning yesterday. |

### What's missing (gaps by rule)

The rules live in `backend/closet/gaps.py`; every number is a named constant at its top. These are the choices someone could have made differently.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Gaps are found by running the outfit engine itself over 40 days: cold, cool, mild and hot (low/high 0/6, 8/14, 15/21, 25/31 °C), each dry and rainy, for each of the five occasions | The engine is what the owner sees every morning, so a day it cannot dress well is the gap that matters. The four bands land on either side of its own thresholds (coat at 8 °C and below, no layers at 24 °C and above). A real year of weather was not used: it would need a place and a clock. |
| A day fails when the engine returns nothing, has to use a piece that breaks a rule, puts together pieces that clash, is well short of warm or well too warm, says "take an umbrella" in every outfit it offers, puts suede or canvas shoes on a rainy day, or cannot make three outfits that differ | Each is turned into the one piece that would fix it (a coat on a cold day without one, a sweater when there is a coat, light trousers when only jeans suit a hot business day). Rain counts only when none of the three outfits keeps it off, since the owner can pick the second one. |
| Days count by occasion: casual and smart casual fully, business 0.7, sport and formal 0.3 | Sport and formal days are rare; a gap that only shows on them should not top the list. |
| A thin place is a place where every outfit offered for a day uses the same piece and nothing else suits; for a coat on a cold day, a lighter one does not count as a second | One pair of smart shoes or one coat is fine until it is at the cobbler's. |
| An orphan is an item in at most one of the grid's outfits whose partners of one kind (bottoms for a top, tops for a bottom, and so on) all clash with it; the gap is one plain, neutral piece of that kind | A loud shirt with no plain trousers is the example the owner gave; one purchase brings it into use. |
| *(Changed 23 November: see the next row.)* From the wear log: an item worn at least 4 times and at least 3 times the average of the others of its category, in a log of at least 10 wears, asks for a second one like it, unless one is already owned | Fewer wears say nothing yet. Never-worn items are listed in a note, not as gaps: buying more does not help them. The log's period comes from its own dates, not the clock. |
| From the wear log, only a top, a middle layer or a pair of bottoms asks for a second one like it (worn at least 4 times, at least 3 times the average of the others of its category, in a log of at least 10 wears, none like it owned), and never while another of its category has not been worn at all | Coats, jackets and shoes are worn every day by nature and are never washed between wears, so a coat worn all of a cold week asked for a second coat, and one pair of boots out of three worn asked for a fourth ("against 0.0 on average"). A piece of its kind sitting unworn is the one to wear first; Not worn already names it. The logic review of 23 November. |
| *(Changed 23 November: see the next row.)* The basics are ten: a plain neutral tee, a white or light blue oxford, a plain neutral sweater, dark jeans, neutral chinos or trousers, a warm coat, a rain-proof outer layer, plain sneakers, smart leather shoes and a leather belt | Taken from the classics in `docs/menswear-reference.md` and kept short: each is something most outfits the engine makes need, or a day it cannot dress without. Suits, ties, polos and boots in particular were left out; a basic the owner owns but rated dislike is still missing. |
| The warm winter coat of the basics is also met by a super heavy jacket that is not a fleece: a down puffer | "What is it?" files a puffer under jackets, so an owner whose winter coat is a puffer was told "A heavy coat is what keeps a cold day comfortable. You have none." beside a card saying every cold day rests on that puffer. A fleece jacket is a middle layer, however heavy. |
| Findings that the same purchase would meet are merged: same category and kind (warm, light, rain) and formalities within two steps; the merged score is the best finding plus a quarter of the others, up to 100 | One rain coat fixes every rainy day; listing it once per occasion would bury everything else. Two kinds of smart trousers far apart in formality stay separate. |
| The list is cut to 8, and a closet with fewer than 8 pieces of clothing gets a sentence instead | A list of everything is no help, and a three-item closet is missing nearly everything. |
| *(Changed 23 November: see the next row.)* Items in the laundry count as owned; archived items do not; disliked items are owned but never used | A shirt in the wash is not missing. The engine already never picks a disliked item, so it is not something to build on. |
| Items in the laundry or waiting to be mended count as owned; archived items do not; disliked items are owned but never used | A shirt in the wash is not missing, and neither is a coat at the mender's: the list's own sentences say "in the wash or being mended" in one breath. Before, a coat set to "needs repair" was left out of the outfits but still counted, so the list called it "your warmest outer layer" of outfits it was not in and asked for a new coat. |
| What is worn at home (the taxonomy's loungewear: pyjamas, lounge pants, robes; not the smoking jacket), swim trunks and shoes for one sport are one rule, `gaps.everyday`, and everything that judges the closet asks it: What's missing, How you live, Would this work?, Not worn, taste and what a shop search is told about you. None of them is a reason to buy, a piece a suggestion is based on, a second piece to change into, "your warmest outer layer", "you have it already", unused, or a liking taste learns. Clothes for home stay where Lounging at home looks for them | Today never puts them in an outfit unless the owner asks by name, but What's missing counted them by category: lounge pants are trousers, a robe a coat, pyjamas a tee. So the demo's Wardrobe asked for "Another pair of trousers like your tartan lounge pants", a robe was the warmest coat, pyjamas were a plain tee, and a search for trousers could be told the owner loves tartan. Swim trunks and sport shoes had their own exceptions (DECISIONS, "Swim trunks", "Tennis and golf shoes"); now all three are one. The logic review of 23 November. |
| The analysis reads the engine's own pieces, options and rules (functions in `outfits.py` whose names start with `_`) and does not change `outfits.py` | The two cannot disagree about what suits a day. If the engine changes, the gaps follow. |
| The API keeps the last answer per closet and reuses it until an item or a wear changes; a wash, a timestamp or a cutout does not count | The analysis takes about a second on the demo closet and about four on 300 items (40 runs of the engine, each bounded by its own six-candidates-per-place search, not by the size of the closet). |

### What's missing: how the owner lives

This extends the fixed grid above **only after answers**. Before answers the
old analysis is byte-for-byte unchanged (including scores and order); **Not
now** dismisses the invitation, not the ability to answer later. There is no
migration: `life`, `life_prompt_dismissed`, and `climate` live in `settings.json`.
No dependency or change to `outfits.py` is needed. `gaps._run_grid` accepts an
optional set of days/moods; its default is still the original grid.

`life.SITUATIONS` is the one mapping table, with a reason beside every row:

| Situation | Occasion / mood | Weather and pieces | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Work | The chosen casual, smart casual, business or formal / sharp | All home bands; all pieces the engine permits | The actual workplace, not an assumed office dress code. |
| Everyday | casual / usual | All home bands; ordinary engine pieces | Errands and seeing friends. |
| Going out | smart casual / sharp | All home bands; ordinary engine pieces | A step up for dinner, not an assumed black-tie calendar. |
| Lounging at home | casual / relaxed | Indoors at 18–22°C, no rain; tees, hoodies and soft relaxed trousers/shorts/knits, never outdoor coats or shoes | There is no loungewear category. Coverage asks for two suitable tops and bottoms, rather than asking the outdoor engine to insist on shoes. |
| Exercise | athletic / relaxed | Home bands; casual/athletic tees, hoodies, shorts, sneakers, jackets/coats, and athletic or soft trousers | Never call jeans or tailored trousers exercise clothes; keep warmth, lightweight and waterproof requirements in the suggested purchase. *(23 November: Today's athletic days follow the same rule, see "The owner's outfit rules".)* |
| Vacation | casual / relaxed | Warm = mild/hot; cold = cold/cool; both = all; wet/dry equally likely; ordinary engine pieces | Destination weather is not home weather. |

Frequencies: never = 0, now and then = 0.25, about weekly = 1, most days = 4
days/week. Situations can overlap; they are not forced to sum to seven.
Vacation means that frequency **during weeks away**, scaled by weeks/year
divided by 365.25/7. No weeks away means no vacation gaps.
The optional style line is at most 200 characters, shown verbatim and reserved
for future shop search; no model or keyword rule reads it.

Lounging's soft words (`SOFT_WORDS`) are jersey, fleece, sweat, jogger, knit
and stretch; fits, when known, must be regular, relaxed or oversized. Tees and
hoodies must be casual/athletic; soft bottoms must also be casual/athletic.
These are proxies, not knowledge of softness, pyjamas or the difference
between a going-out tee and a home tee. A hoodie can layer at home, but is not
counted as a base top. Exercise cannot distinguish running, gym work or other
sports, moisture-wicking fabric, support or protective equipment. The screen
must not promise that it can.

Since #111 an item can have a **kind** from the owner's taxonomy (`details.garment`:
the tagger's answer, or the owner's choice under Shape on the item page;
`today.cloth` passes it to the engine as `kind`). When the kind is known it
decides, and the soft words are not read:

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Lounging takes the taxonomy's loungewear (`LOUNGE_KINDS`) and the soft everyday kinds `HOME_KINDS` (sweatpants, joggers, open-hem sweatpants, fleece pullover, sweatshirt, hoodie, shorts) whatever their formality; sportswear that is not one of those (tights, track trousers) only as a tee or hoodie; any other known kind of trousers, shorts or knit is not for home | A name like "Stretch chinos" no longer makes chinos loungewear, and a pair the owner calls lounge trousers counts even if it was tagged smart casual. |
| Exercise takes the taxonomy's activewear (`SPORT_KINDS`, which includes the fleece pullover the owner's file files there) and `EXERCISE_KINDS` (sweatpants, shorts, hoodie, sweatshirt); never loungewear; other known kinds of trousers or shorts are not exercise clothes | The kinds say what the clothes are made for; tops, shoes and outer layers keep the old rule by category. |
| Home and exercise suggestions add `look_for.kind`, a phrase naming kinds to look for ("joggers, sweatpants, lounge trousers or a fleece pullover", "running or training shorts") | Names the new kinds in plain words without a new gap identity; gap ids and scores are unchanged. |
| A fleece (pullover or jacket) weighs what its pile does: sherpa super heavy, polar fleece or an unsaid pile heavyweight. It sits after the owner's own words and before the tagger's weight in the order of trust | The issue asks for the weight class from the fleece; the fleece pullover is a sweater to the engine (its `app_category`), so it is a warm mid layer, not outerwear. |
| The tagger reports the kind (schema version 2, field `garment`); older answers read as `unknown`. Only `fleece_pullover`, `performance_tee`, `athletic_half_zip` and `track_jacket` among tops, and twelve sportswear and loungewear bottoms (`BOTTOM_DRAWINGS`), change the drawing; other kinds are drawn from their details | The owner's taxonomy is the source of truth for the ids (no garment or value was added to it here). The recorded drawings are byte-for-byte unchanged, but a stored side-stripe feature on a top or trousers is now drawn, so `DRAWING_VERSION` is raised to 5 (one above main's) and the demo closet regenerated. |
| ~~On the item page the kind is offered in words, as chips, only the kinds an item of its category can be~~ Replaced by the "What is it?" picker (#136, below) | |
| Colour-blocking and contrast binding have no value in the owner's file, so they are not tagged fields: a fleece pullover with a trim colour is drawn bound in it, and with a secondary colour is drawn with yoke, sleeves and pocket in that colour | Asked the owner in #111's pull request whether to add values for them. |

**Kind and category (#136, "What is it?").** The kind is what the item is, in the owner's words
(any of the taxonomy's garments: clothing, footwear and accessories); the category is the coarse
word the outfit engine, What's missing, Insights and the Closet's category filter read. They are
kept together in `backend/closet/kinds.py`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The owner's kind stays in `own_details.garment` (now any taxonomy garment, footwear and accessories included); Claude's is read from the stored answer (`details.garment`, `details.accessory`, or for a shoe the footwear id among `other_features`). No column and no migration (0019 is unused) | #111 already stored the owner's kind there with its history and undo; a column would have been a second copy of it, and reading Claude's kind from the answer needs no backfill. |
| Choosing a kind sets the category that follows from it (the taxonomy's `app_category`; for footwear from the style it is drawn in, a loafer-style casual shoe, driving moccasin or velvet slipper being `dress_shoes`; a set such as pyjamas keeps a top or bottom category the item already has, else a default) as the owner's, in one history entry with the kind | So the engine and What's missing see the same thing they would have seen with the category chosen by hand; undo puts both back. A later `PATCH` of the category to one the kind cannot be drops the owner's kind. |
| Groups are the taxonomy's `category` of each garment, titled in plain words, in the order shirts, tees and polos, knitwear and mid layers, jackets and coats, tailoring, trousers and shorts, activewear, loungewear and sleep, shoes and boots, accessories | The issue asks for groups from the taxonomy, not a hand-made list. So the fleece pullover sits in Activewear, where the owner's file puts it, not under Knitwear as the issue's example said. An item with no kind is shown in the group of its category. |
| "Claude also thought" shows Claude's kind (when the owner has chosen another) and up to three kinds of the same group whose details fit the stored answer best | The tagger names one kind, not a ranked list; asking it for alternatives would change the tagging schema. |
| After a kind change, details a top of that kind does not have (from its axes in the taxonomy and its drawing signature: neckline, collar, placket, cuffs, sleeve build, pockets, hem) are hidden from the Details card, the fixes and the drawing, not deleted; choosing a kind that has them brings them back. The cut and the sleeve length are never hidden | A wrong pick should cost nothing; the kind decides what is offered, not what is kept. |
| Each kind's tile is drawn from its signature in the item's colour (`GET /api/kinds/{kind}.svg`), an accessory as its template (#119: a cap, a tote, or a plain tag for a tie or a watch). Choosing an accessory sets `details.accessory` in the drawing and keeps Claude's kind of it only if it is one of the chosen accessory's (a trucker cap is not drawn as a beanie) | Reuses the drawing code, so a tile looks like what choosing it would draw. |
| Search matches the start of each typed word against the kind's name, its signature in the taxonomy and a short list of common words ("trainers", "jumper", "sweatpants"); names first | "snap", "chelsea" and "jogger" find what the owner would expect, with no search library. |

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Score failing days by `frequency / 7 × weather share × 8 × failure weight` | Frequency times weather is the important part. The constant 8 preserves the old score scale (eight weather cells per occasion for a full week). The original `OCCASION_WEIGHT` is used only without answers. |
| Scale thin-place base scores, basics, orphans and wear-driven extras by applicable days too | A fixed "basic winter coat" bonus must not put winter back at the top in a hot climate. Business/formal basics cannot recreate days the owner never has. |
| Keep the existing merge and ID rule (category, formality, kind) | Hidden gaps and wishes refer to those IDs. New indoor and athletic purchases have new `lounge`/`active` kinds instead of renaming an existing suggestion. |
| Prefix the original reasons with the situation, ordinary frequency words and applicable weather | Keep the analysis's named pieces and evidence, not a generic replacement. Say "now and then", "about once a week" or "most days", not fractional days. Annual weather counts describe days **here**; the per-situation API count also includes frequency. |
| Use four complete calendar years from Open-Meteo's archive, kept for 180 days | Four years smooth unusual seasons without a large download. Only coordinates, fixed query fields/dates and automatic time-zone selection are sent; no name, answers, style or closet data. The common bounded fetch uses a 6-second timeout, 1 MiB maximum and the computer's certificates. |
| Require every date, finite low/high and precipitation, and sensible ranges | A missing season must not look like a climate with no cold days. The shared `climate.archive` reader is available for future trip history too. |
| Classify at the engine's design temperature, low + 0.3 × (high − low) | Cold ≤8°C, cool <15°C, mild <24°C, hot otherwise. Wet means ≥1 mm total precipitation (including snow's water equivalent); the wet share is conditional on the band. |
| Failed archive requests reuse valid same-place data, clearly marked old, or use equal bands and equal wet/dry | A lookup failure must not stop gaps or quietly claim home weather was used. Retry at most hourly, allow seven days after year-end before requesting the new year (January 8 UTC), and discard kept weather when the place changes. A response missing only the newest year's tail keeps the previous four-year answer without a connection warning. Actual connection failures remain explicit. Cache writes check the place and reservation under the settings lock and only write changed state. |
| Taste needs at least 10 distinct item/day wears across 3 items, or 5 rated active items | Less says nothing: no taste claim or guessed preference. Each enabled evidence source contributes separately. The rules are in `taste.py`, not a model. |
| Attribute preference needs 2 positively evidenced items of the same category and at least 4 points | Colours, patterns, fits and formality are counted separately for each garment category: tee colours cannot recolour leather shoes or belts. Each wear is one point, capped at 10/item; love = 4, like = 2, meh = 0, dislike = −4. A dislike overrides historical wears. Colour compatibility is ranked before taste; a favourite loud colour cannot introduce a clash. Favoured plain patterns and fits can be suggested; the required occasion is not weakened to match taste. |
| Avoidance needs 2 negatively evidenced items of the same category, at least 75% of that category's items with the value, and negative total points | One disliked shirt does not mean every shirt with its fit or pattern is disliked. Never-worn items contribute −2 only after 180 days of ownership, a 90-day wear log with enough wears, and 90 days of the piece's own season within that log and ownership. Season exposure uses Insights' shared rule; a summer-only log says nothing negative about winter coats. Age is measured at the log's last date, not today's clock. Avoided fits/patterns are removed from suggestions and included as exclusions. |
| Confirmed `never_wear` holds back the same category and dress/kind for shared situations, except outerwear | Coats and jackets solve different weather needs and never suppress each other. Home/exercise kinds stay distinct. Other situations survive with reduced score; wishes are never silently removed. Every held-back suggestion remains listed with the hide responsible and Undo, including beyond the open-result limit. Old hides affect only themselves until the owner explicitly agrees to broader hiding. Per-closet consent and situation snapshots live in settings, matched to the database hide timestamp; no migration or destructive deletion. |
| Demo has fixed example life answers and climate shares; its questionnaire is read-only | The owner's place and answers must not change the example or send a weather request. Items, wears, hides and wishes in the demo remain independently editable as before. |
| Cache includes life/climate context and ownership dates; decisions are still applied afterwards | Answer, place and climate changes cannot serve yesterday's analysis; a wash or picture update still does not trigger a slow analysis. Undoing a hidden suggestion is immediate. |

Timing on the implementation runner (seconds, median of three alternating
uncached runs, same inputs before/after; no network):

| Closet | Before | After, no answers | After, example life and climate |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 30 demo items | 1.30 | 1.42 | 0.77 |
| 300 generated items | 3.64 | 3.07 | 3.74 |

The generated closet uses seed 89, the existing test item builder, twelve
clothing/shoe categories and random vocabulary colours, formalities, seasons
and patterns; both closets use an empty wear log for this comparison.
The before implementation is the base commit's `gaps.py`; every unanswered
answer was also checked for exact equality. Runner load makes small timing
differences noise, not a promised speedup. The normal API reuses its answer;
the first home-climate request can add the weather lookup's six-second timeout.

### What the engine knows about the cloth

`closet_rows` in `backend/closet/today.py` is the one place that prepares the closet for the outfit engine and the gap analysis (`POST /api/outfits/today`, `GET /api/gaps`; also `POST /api/outfits/suggest`, until it was removed on 20 November). Each row carries the weight class and the sleeve taken from the tagger's stored answer (`tag_answer`), in the keys the engine already read (`weight_class`, `silhouette.sleeve`); `outfits.py` is unchanged.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Order of trust for the weight class: (1) what the owner set by hand, that is words in a material or name whose `source` is `user`, as `items.py` records it; (1b) for a fleece pullover or jacket, its pile (see #111 above); (2) the tagger's `details.weight` unless `unknown`, then, for knitwear, its knit gauge; (3) the words in the name and material; (4) the category. For the sleeve: (1) the silhouette's sleeve (the owner's, or the answer's copied there), (2) the answer's sleeve length, (4) the category's usual sleeve | The owner's own words are what they know about their clothes; the tagger looked at the cloth, which says more than a word in a name the owner did not choose ("Linen shirt" from the demo, a material the tagger filled in). A name typed at upload has no `source` of `user`, so it counts as words (3), not as the owner's: there is no record that it was meant as a weight. Steps 3 and 4 are the engine's own guess, unchanged. |
| A bare fibre the owner types ("wool", "cotton") does not overrule the tagger's weight | Cotton and wool come in every weight; typing "100% cotton" from a care label is not a statement that the cloth is midweight. Any more specific word (flannel, linen, fleece, merino and the rest of the engine's `WEIGHT_WORDS`) does. |
| Knit gauge to weight class: extra fine and fine are lightweight, medium is midweight, chunky is heavyweight, super chunky is super heavy (`KNIT_GAUGE_WEIGHT`) | A fine knit is worn like a shirt and goes under a jacket; a chunky one is the outer warmth of an outfit. Only used when the tagger gave no weight; it gives a gauge only for knitwear. |
| Sleeve length to sleeve: sleeveless is none, short and elbow are short, three-quarter and long are long | The engine only asks whether a top is long-sleeved (warmer, and too warm on a hot day); a three-quarter sleeve covers most of the arm. |
| A stored answer that is missing, empty, not valid JSON or not the expected shape counts as nothing known | Old items, items not described yet and a damaged row must never stop the outfits or the gaps; they keep the old guess. |
| The gap analysis's cache is keyed on the rows as the engine reads them, which carry the weight and sleeve taken from the answer, not the whole answer | A new answer that changes an item's weight gives a new analysis; one that changes only details the engine does not read (buttons, pockets) keeps the cached one. |

### The Today screen

`frontend/src/screens/Today.tsx` (its own styles in `Today.css`), with the wording and what the device keeps in `frontend/src/today.ts`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The app opens on Today once the closet has a top (tee, shirt or polo), something for the legs and shoes that are not archived or disliked; only once per tab, on the closet's front page | Before that Today could only say what is missing, so the closet is the better first screen. Once per tab (and again after logging in) so tapping **Closet**, reloading or switching closets stays where the owner went. |
| The outfit is shown as its pieces laid out in the order they are put on (outer layer, mid layer, top, bottom, shoes, extras), or, with the **Pieces / As worn** switch, drawn as worn (see "The outfit as worn" under Illustration) | The pieces are clear and work with any mix of cutouts and drawings, and are where a piece is left out or built around; the figure shows how the outfit looks together. |
| The switch is remembered on this device (`localStorage`, `closet.todayView`), shared by every outfit on the screen and by a trip's days, and starts on the pieces | Like the closet's photos or drawings, it is a habit of the device. Not under the saved-copies prefix: it holds nothing about the closet. One choice for Today and trips (#181): the owner who likes the outfit as worn wants it everywhere outfits are shown. |
| The worn figure is at most 52% of the window's height (480 px) when the card is wide, 62% (560 px) on a phone | On a computer the button sits beside it, so the whole figure and "I'm wearing this" are on the first screen; on a phone it is as wide as the card, large enough to see the collar and the buttons, and the button is a short scroll below. |
| Warnings sit at the top of the outfit in a coloured box; the reasons follow under "Why this works", always open | A warning (rain, a rule broken) changes what the owner does, a reason only explains. |
| Occasion and mood are chips; the last choice is kept on this device (`localStorage`), not on the server | It is a habit of the device (work phone, home computer), not a fact about the closet. It starts at casual, as usual. |
| "I'm wearing this" records the whole outfit with one call (`POST /api/wears`), and undo removes only the wears that call added (`POST /api/wears/remove`) | One call is all or nothing, so a dropped connection never leaves half an outfit worn. A piece already worn that day was worn anyway; undo must not remove that. Choosing another outfit the same day undoes the first one. |
| Worn today, typed temperatures and the last outfits fetched are kept on the device for that day only, per closet, and removed on logout with the other saved copies | So the screen still says what is worn after a reload, and offline it shows the outfits found earlier (the service worker never keeps answers to `POST`). |
| "Not this piece" and "Start from this piece" apply until cleared or the app is closed; they are not remembered on the device | They answer "not that one today", not a lasting preference (that is the rating). |
| Typed temperatures are offered only when there is no saved place (or the forecast cannot be had); the forecast cannot be corrected from this screen | One way to set the day keeps the top of the screen short; the server already allows a correction if it is wanted later. |
| `context.message` carries the server's own sentence when the forecast is old | The screen says it in the same words as Settings. |

### Worn outfits and learning from ratings

Storage in `backend/closet/worn.py` (migration 0010), the learning in `backend/closet/outfits.py` ("learning from ratings"; every number a named constant at the top), the screens in `frontend/src/screens/Worn.tsx` and the question on Today.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A worn outfit is its own record next to the wear log, with the pieces by place, the day it was offered for, and the score and sentences it was offered with; each piece remembers whether recording the outfit added its wear | The wear log stays the one record of wears. Removing an outfit then removes only the wears it added, never one recorded on its own. |
| The same pieces recorded again on the same day give back the outfit already there | "I'm wearing this" on the computer and then on the phone is one outfit worn, not two. Two outfits that day with different pieces stay two (a change of clothes). |
| Removing an outfit hands a wear it added on to another outfit that day holding the same piece, instead of keeping it unowned | So removing the two in either order leaves the wear log as it was before either. |
| A deleted item stays part of the outfits it was worn in (listed as `gone`); such an outfit is still listed but no longer teaches anything; one with no piece left goes with the last one deleted | A rating is about the outfit as it was worn. Rebuilding it from the pieces left would move a "not again" onto tee, jeans and sneakers that were never rated together. An outfit with nothing left to show is not worth a row. Archived pieces are still in the closet, so they are shown as themselves, marked archived, and the outfit still teaches. |
| Sentences an outfit was offered with are cut to 30 and each to 500 characters, not refused | They are made from item names, which have no limit; recording what was worn must never fail because of them. |
| The ratings are three (loved it, fine, not again) and the reasons eight (too warm, too cold, too dressed up, too casual, colours clashed, uncomfortable, got compliments, other with a note) | Short enough to answer with one hand in the morning. "Fine" with no reason teaches nothing. |
| Loved (or "got compliments") adds 1 point for each pair of its pieces in an outfit, at most 4 | Scores are about 80 for an outfit that breaks nothing, so this decides between outfits that are otherwise about equal and never more: 4 is well below what one broken rule costs (20), so a loved outfit never beats what the day needs. |
| "Not again": that exact outfit (base, mid, outer, bottom, shoes) is never offered again; its pairs cost 3 points each, at most 9 | Pairs only, so the same pieces in other company stay welcome. Which pairs follows the reasons: none, "uncomfortable" or "other" mean every pair; "colours clashed" the coloured or patterned pieces; "too dressed up"/"too casual" the most/least formal pieces, and only on a day of the same occasion; warmth reasons say nothing about which pieces go together. An outfit holding such a pair says so in a warning. |
| When the outfits that keep the rules were all rated "not again", Today offers none and says why, with a pointer to change the rating; it does not fall back to the relaxed rules | A rating must never be the silent reason an outfit that breaks a rule is shown. Offering the refused outfit after all would ignore what the owner said; saying so plainly lets them decide. |
| "Too cold" or "too warm" on a day within 4°C of today (by the temperature the day is dressed for) moves the warmth aimed for by half a point each, at most a point and a half either way | One point is about 2°C. Bounded, so a run of cold days cannot dress the owner in a coat in summer; ratings of days unlike today do not count. Outfits recorded by hand have no day remembered, so they never move it. |
| The engine stays pure: `today.py` reads the newest 200 rated outfits and `outfits.feedback_from` turns them into what `suggest` takes | The same ratings and day always give the same outfits, and with no ratings the outfits are exactly those of the rules alone. |
| A sentence names the rating that shaped an outfit ("You loved these together on 3 November", "Dressed a little warmer than usual: you were cold on a day like this on 5 November") | The owner should see why the app changed its mind. |
| Today asks about yesterday's unrated outfit first, then today's; the question sits below the outfits (since 20 November, first in Recent outfits) and "Not now" is remembered on the device per closet | Today's outfit is still being worn in the morning; the question must never push the outfits down. |
| "Wear this again" starts Today from the outfit's pieces that can be worn today and fills the rest | A piece in the wash should not stop the outfit; the screen names what was left out. |
| The first page of worn outfits is kept on the device per closet, removed on logout with the other saved copies, and fetched again after every change; offline, rating and removing are off and say why | The service worker does not keep `/api/outfits/worn`: it keeps answers by address, so after switching closets it would show the demo closet's outfits as the owner's. Fetching again after a change keeps the copy from showing a rating already changed, or Today asking again about an outfit already rated. |

### Dress for another day, and outfits you keep (23 November; BACKLOG "The closet app to have")

`backend/closet/plans.py`, `backend/closet/kept.py` and what `backend/closet/today.py` does with a plan (migration
0052); `frontend/src/plans.ts`, `frontend/src/kept.ts`, `components/DaySheet.tsx`, Today and Worn.

| Decision | Why | Could have been |
|---|---|---|
| A plan is not a wear. "Wear this on Saturday" stores the outfit for the date (`day_plans`, one a day, a new one in place of the old) and nothing else: no wear, no laundry, no worn outfit, until "I'm wearing this" on the day. A plan for a day gone by lapses quietly, worn or not | The wear log stays the one record of wears, and the laundry counts only what was worn. A plan worn or not on its day has done its job either way | A wear dated ahead, undone if not worn; or a worn outfit marked "planned" |
| Another day is Today with a date (`/today/day/<date>`, `day` in the request), not a new place: the same rules, the same layout, "‹ Today" back. Its weather is the trip's: the forecast where Open-Meteo reaches, what the date is usually like beyond it, and a mild day with the reason when neither can be had | DESIGN: no new place, one home for how a day is dressed. Trips already decide where a day ahead's weather comes from | A separate "Plan" page |
| What is in the laundry today counts as clean for a day ahead exactly as for a trip starting that day: three days or more ahead (`trips.CLEAN_BY_DAYS`) | One rule for "will it be washed by then", as asked | Following the laundry day itself |
| Kept out of the laundry's way: on a day before a plan, a planned piece is not offered when one more wear would put it in the laundry (its kind's "Before it needs washing" count) and no laundry day comes between that day and the plan (`plans.held`, the laundry's own rule). A jacket, shoes, a knit with wears to spare, or a day before a laundry day are offered. With the laundry off nothing is held back. Built around, or kept by "Wear this again" or the plan, a held piece is used, and its sheet says it is planned with "Build around it anyway" | The owner: planned pieces "kept out of the laundry's way", without hiding a favourite for no reason. Only one more wear is counted, so a piece worn two days running before a plan can still end up in the wash | Never offering a planned piece before its day |
| The morning of the day (and the day's own view ahead) opens on the plan. When every piece can be worn it is the outfit exactly as planned, its accessories included and judged for the occasion it was planned for (no layer added to a place it left empty); otherwise as "Wear this again" does (`again_of`): pieces that cannot be worn by then are swapped and named. "See other outfits" sets the plan aside for the visit | One rule for filling a place, already explained to the owner | Showing the plan only, with no swaps |
| A day inside a saved trip is the trip's: no outfits, a line and a link to the trip; a plan there is refused (409) | The trip has an outfit for each day; two would disagree | Planning anyway and telling the trip |
| A word about a day ahead is read like today's note, with that day as the date Claude is given, and kept beside the note in the program's settings, by date (`day_notes`); on that day it is today's note, and it wins over the note for several days | The note already lives in the settings ("about where the owner is"); nothing more is sent than today's request sends | A column on the plan |
| Claude does not choose for a day ahead; the rules do | A question costs money and is kept for a day; outfits for Saturday asked on Wednesday would be asked again on Saturday. That morning, Claude (when on) starts from the plan as it starts from "Wear this again" | Asking Claude for each day viewed |
| Outfits you keep live under Worn, as its second half (`/today/worn/kept`), not as a new screen or a list on Today. The same pieces kept twice are one (`same`, the sorted ids); keeping again says so. Removing sets one aside (`removed_at`) for Undo. A piece deleted stays in the outfit (`gone`), whose place is filled when it is worn | One home for outfits, as asked; Undo for a removal (DESIGN: nothing is lost by a slip) | A list on Today, or deleting a kept outfit with its last piece, as worn outfits are |
| The demo closet keeps two outfits and plans a day three days ahead, its dates relative like its wears. The plan is the rules' own first outfit for a mild smart-casual day there (blue oxford shirt, cream v-neck sweater, stone chinos, brown boots, brown belt), and neither kept outfit draws a warning when worn on a mild day (`tests/test_demo.py`) | So both can be seen in the demo; the first plan the demo showed warned that its trousers were a lot dressier than its shirt, which is not what a plan should look like | Leaving them out; a plan chosen by hand without a check |
| Away pieces and plans (Where a piece is). A day ahead counts a piece away as back when it is expected back before that day, the rule a trip packs by (on the day itself is too late), and back as coming back makes it (clean from the cleaner's, mended from the tailor's); one with no day said, or a later one, is not offered. On the morning, a planned piece still away is filled like one in the laundry, and the line names it with where it is ("Your navy blazer is at the cleaner's until Friday 27 November, so another piece takes its place."); "Wear this" on a kept outfit says the same under "Starting from an outfit you kept". Sending a planned piece away refuses nothing and says nothing more: the plan fills its place when its day comes | One rule for "will it be back by then", already the trips'. The item page has no natural place for a word about plans, and the day itself says what changed | Refusing, or warning, when a planned piece is sent away; never offering an away piece ahead |
| Brought under the shared parts: a failed read of the plans (Today and a day ahead, where the plan's line goes) or of the outfits you keep (Worn) is the shared `Failed`; a day ahead's dates (its name, "Wed 25 Nov", how far ahead) come from the one family in `wears.ts`. Right after "Wear this on Saturday" the day is read again, so it opens on its plan and Remove the plan is on the outfit only. A swapped piece, and a piece the day counts on being back ("This counts on your navy blazer being back by then.", the trip's idea in a short line), are said under the outfit's button, so the line over the outfit keeps its one line. On a phone the outfit's heading is its words whole ("Best for today", "Best for Sat") with the count on its own smaller line | One home for each thing; the page holds still; the heading broke inside "Best for this day" at 375, and "Best for today" at 320 in a wide font | Showing the swap in the plan's line, two lines when it is long; truncating the heading |
| A day ahead leads with its outfit, as Today does: on a phone its heading is one row ("‹ Wed 25 Nov", the back link drawn as its arrow, and Another day); the reason for its weather is one short line ("No forecast for that day." with Try again when the service could not be reached; nothing more beyond the forecast, where the weather line says "usually, on these dates"); the line about its plan is one line of fixed height, and with the plan shown it is only "See other outfits", the outfit itself saying "Planned" beside Remove the plan. On the smallest phone five pieces and the button are on the first screen with 16px or more to spare (`e2e/41-another-day.spec.ts`) | The first picture of a planned day had three lines of weather reason and two of plan, which pushed the pieces and the button under the tab bar; each sentence there said what the heading or the weather line already said | A smaller outfit card on a day ahead; dropping the line about the plan |

### From a piece to an outfit (23 November; BACKLOG "The closet app to have")

`outfits.cannot_build_around`, `worn.group_wears` and `worn.of_item`, read into the item's own answer (`build_around`,
`worn_in`); `frontend/src/pieceOutfits.ts`, `components/PieceOutfits.tsx`, and Today reading `?around=`.

| Decision | Why | Could have been |
|---|---|---|
| "Build an outfit around it" opens `/today?around=<id>`, which Today reads once as it opens (as `?again=` from Worn) and then tidies away. It is Today's own "Build around this piece": the piece takes the place of any piece built around before, and the pieces left out stay left out (`buildAroundPiece`). Today stays open under a piece's page on a phone and beside it on a computer, so Today also reads the address when that page closes | One way to build around a piece, already named and kept for the day; Back returns to the piece because Today replaces its own address | Writing the day's kept changes on the device before going to Today (the copy Today keeps in memory wins over it while the app is open) |
| Whether the button is offered is the server's word (`build_around` on the item), from the same rule Today refuses by (`outfits.cannot_build_around`, which `suggest` now calls): in the laundry, away, or with nothing saying what it is, one line says when Today can; archived, or a kind no outfit has a place for (category Other: a tie, a watch), nothing. Waiting to be mended, kept for a plan, worn at home, for swimming or for one sport, the button is there and works | Never a button that leads to "nothing to build around it"; one rule, so the page and Today cannot disagree. A plan's hold is Today's to say (its sheet's "Build around it anyway"), since only Today knows the day | Hiding the button with no word; working the rule out again in the page |
| "When you wore it" is the one list of a piece's wears, in place of Recent wears: a wear on a day an outfit holding the piece was recorded shows that outfit (Worn's `OutfitPics`, smaller), the same pieces worn on several days are one row ("3 times, last on 12 Nov"), with the latest rating given; a wear with no outfit is a dated line. It is read with the item (`worn_in`, the latest 100 wears), so it never arrives after the page and every edit that reads the item again keeps it fresh | DESIGN "one thing, one home" and "the page holds still". A second request would arrive late and move the card | A separate `GET /api/items/{id}/outfits`, with its own wait and its own cache to keep fresh |
| Three rows on the page, "All N outfits" (or "wears", or "outfits and wears") opens the rest in a sheet. Each row's ⋯ opens a sheet with "Wear this again" (Worn's address, `?again=`), the only filled button, then "The days you wore it": a plain row a day, newest first, the day in the wear lists' words and a quiet Remove (44px, named "Remove the wear: 20 Nov"), as Recent wears read. A wear with no outfit gets the same row in its sheet, with no heading | 44px targets instead of a column of Remove links on the page; one red button a day outweighed the one thing the sheet is opened for, and an outfit worn ten times would have had ten | A Remove link on every row of the page, as before; a red button a day in the sheet (the first version) |
| On Today, a piece worn in the last days that you asked for (built around, or kept from an outfit worn again) is said plainly, "You wore the striped sweater yesterday.", without "; nothing else suited as well", which is said only of a piece Nines chose itself | The second half was not true of a piece in the outfit because you asked for it | Leaving the warning out for a piece asked for |
| `OutfitPics` moved to `components/OutfitPics.tsx` (with its styles) and `useAllItems` to `queries.ts`, unchanged, so the item page, Today and Worn import them from there | DEVELOPING: a piece used by more than one screen lives under `components/`; the item page importing Worn, which imports the item page, was a circle | Importing them from Worn |
| Outfits you keep are not in the list | It lists wears; a kept outfit was never worn, and has its home under Worn | Marking kept outfits holding the piece "Kept" among them |
### Give it an outing (23 November; BACKLOG "The closet app to have")

`backend/closet/outing.py` (pure but for its storage, migration 0053) and what `backend/closet/today.py` does with it;
`outingLine` in `frontend/src/today.ts`, the line on Today.

| Decision | Why | Could have been |
|---|---|---|
| Who is a candidate: a piece rated love (every loved one first) or like, not worn for a while (below), and wearable today by the rules every outfit passes: clean, in the closet, not waiting to be mended, right for today's weather and occasion in some place it can take (no problem for the day, not out of its season, a layer the day's band allows); never loungewear, sleepwear, swimwear or shoes for one sport (`outfits._unavailable`), nor the taxonomy's activewear on a day that is not for sport; a garment or shoes, not an accessory; not in one of the outfits that stay on the page. Among the candidates, the one passed over longest comes first, then by id | A piece the owner likes and the rules can dress today is the only kind worth bringing back; the same rules as every outfit, so it never offers what Today would refuse. An accessory is added to outfits by its own rules and does not carry an outfit | Pieces rated meh too ("give it a chance") |
| "A while" is 28 days (`outing.AFTER_DAYS`), counted only over days the piece could have been worn: every day since its last wear (or since it was added, when never worn), less the days you recorded an outfit on Today whose weather did not suit it. **The number is a guess for the owner to change** | Four weeks: long enough that a piece in the usual rotation never qualifies, short enough that a season does not pass without it. Counting only wearable days keeps a winter coat from being "neglected" at the first frost after a summer of warm days; with no days recorded, every day counts | Calendar days only; or the piece's season |
| The outfit is built around the piece by the build-around rules (`outfits.suggest` with `build_around`) and must be good: no warning at all (a piece worn three days ago, too light for the day, rain with nothing to keep it off) and a score of 70 or more. When the first candidate gives none, the next is tried, at most four a day; when none does, there is no outing | An outing must feel like a good idea, never a forced one: building around a piece the owner chose may break a rule and say so, an outing may not | Offering it with its warnings |
| It takes the place of the last of Today's outfits: never an extra card, never the first. Its "Built around …" reason and "Includes a piece you love: …" (when only that piece) are left out; one sentence under its button says why: "An outing for your tweed jacket: you love it and have not worn it since 2 Oct." ("since you added it on 1 Aug" when never worn), where the day's other lines about an outfit go | The best outfit for the day stays first, and the first screen is unchanged: the outing is one swipe away and costs one sentence. Two sentences saying the same would be noise | A card of its own; a mark on the piece |
| The rhythm: at most one outing day in five (`EVERY_DAYS`), and a piece offered as an outing waits three weeks (`REST_DAYS`) before it is offered again, whether it was worn, ignored or turned down. A day's outing is kept (`outings`: day, piece, since, turned down), so every reload that day offers the same piece; it is recorded the first time Today is asked for that day, Nines asking ahead at five in the morning included | It must never nag: a suggestion now and then, never the same piece twice running. Whether the owner swiped to the last outfit cannot be known, so offered counts from when it was on the page | Counting only outings the owner saw |
| "Not this piece" on the outing's piece gives an ordinary outfit back for today and marks the day's outing turned down: no other outing that day, even once "Not this piece" is cleared, and the piece rests three weeks like any piece offered. Wearing it is an ordinary wear | It answers "not today" as it does for any piece; no new button | A "Not now" of its own |
| Only today: a day ahead, a day inside a saved trip, a day note that puts you somewhere else, building around a piece of your own, wearing an outfit again and the day's plan all have no outing | Your own start always wins; away from home the piece may not be with you | |
| With Claude choosing, the rules' outing keeps the last place under Claude's outfits (shown as from Nines' rules, with its sentence), unless Claude already chose the piece in an outfit that stays. Claude is told nothing about it: no candidate, no neglect, no date. When you write to Claude, it is told the outfits on your screen as before, the outing among them as one of the rules' | The least intrusive honest way: nothing more about the owner is sent than before (the lists of what is sent, here and in Settings, stay true), and Claude's choice is not changed | Telling Claude there is a candidate |
| Not in the demo closet's own Today as it ships: its pieces were all worn lately. It can show one on a day you turn a liked piece's wears back (the browser test does) | Every test of the demo's Today stays as it was | Changing the demo's wears |

### The What's missing screen and the wishlist

`frontend/src/screens/Missing.tsx` (its own styles in `Missing.css`), with the wording and what the device keeps in `frontend/src/gaps.ts`; the server side is `backend/closet/wishlist.py` and migration `0008_wishlist.sql`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A gap is known by the `id` the analysis gives it: its category and formality, and its kind for outer layers and warm or light pieces (`outer-rain`, `trousers-business-light`) | It was checked to stay the same when the analysis runs again after wears are added or items archived. It is built from what to buy, not from the evidence, so it changes only when the suggestion itself changes (for example the formality, when the days that need it change); that is then a new suggestion, and a wish already made for the old one stays on the list. |
| `GET /api/gaps` itself says, for each gap, whether it is hidden or on the wishlist, rather than a separate call | One request, one copy to keep for offline, and the screen cannot show a gap as open that the server knows was decided. The analysis is asked for every gap and the list cut after marking, so hiding one brings the next one up instead of leaving seven. |
| Hidden and wished gaps stay in the answer, in their places; the screen shows wished ones as a short "On your wishlist" line and hidden ones in a closed "Hidden" section below | The owner sees that a decision was kept, and can bring a hidden one back. A wished gap cannot be hidden (remove the wish first); wishing a hidden gap brings it back. |
| "Not for me" asks why with four choices (I have one that is not in the closet yet, I never dress like that, too expensive, something else) and does not insist | The reason is for the owner and for a later shopping step; one more tap to say nothing should not be the price of hiding something. |
| How much a gap would help is shown as a labelled pill ("Helps a lot", "a fair bit", "some", "a little"), stronger the more it helps, from the score at 60, 45 and 25 | A number out of 100 invites reading too much into 34 against 38; four steps can be compared at a glance. The cut points were set by eye on the demo closet (its scores run from 71 down to 5) and may want moving once the real closet is in. The four bars it replaced were too small to read on a phone (design review). |
| A gap's card says one sentence for why (its first reason); the rest are behind "Why, day by day (N)", where a sentence the analysis gave for several kinds of day is said once with those days listed under it ("Work: most days, on the cold days here…") | Once the owner has said how they live, the server writes one reason per kind of day, so "A leather belt" said the same sentence four times and the screen was about 6,000px tall. The grouping is done on the screen (`groupReasons` in `gaps.ts`), reading the server's "For work, most days: …." opening, so the analysis and its offline copies are unchanged; a reason without that opening is shown as it is. |
| The "Based on" line is one row, at most two lines, that opens How you live; what the suggestions are based on (the whole line, the weather, taste, and what lounging and exercise mean) is under the questions there | On a phone the line was cut off and its "show more" was easy to miss. Opening the questions from it puts the answers and what they change in one place. |
| What to look for is written out (kind and formality, season, weight, pattern, material) and the colours are given as swatches with "these go with what you have"; the owner's pieces the gap is based on are small pictures that open the item | Field names mean nothing to the owner; the pictures show which of his own things the sentence is about. |
| *(Removed 20 November, "Four places and Add": Not worn and Worn most are sections of the Wardrobe, said once.)* The pieces never worn and the ones worn far more than the rest are in a quieter "Also worth knowing" section under the list | They are not something to buy, but worth seeing. A piece worn far more than the rest is still a gap (a second one like it) and stays in the list too. |
| "I bought it" takes the wish off the list and opens Add; the row is kept with the date | So the new item can be photographed straight away; if it does not fill the gap the analysis offers it again. Keeping the row costs nothing and allows a "bought" history later. |
| A wish keeps the name and what to look for as they were when it was made | The analysis can change while the owner is shopping; the list should not change under him. |
| Hidden gaps and wishes live in the closet's own database | The demo closet and the real one keep theirs apart, and backups include them, like everything else about the closet. |
| *(Changed 20 November: on a phone the wishlist follows the suggestions on the same page, no switch; each wish has its own page.)* On a computer the wishlist is a panel beside the suggestions (the item opens in its place); on a phone the two are a switch at the top | The owner asked for the computer first; side by side, deciding and seeing the list happen together. A phone has room for one. |
| The last answer and wishlist fetched are kept on the device per closet and shown offline, marked "Saved earlier", with the buttons off and saying why; they are removed on logout with the other saved copies | As on Today: the service worker never keeps `/api/gaps`, and a list to shop from is most wanted away from home. |

### Would this work?

The check is `backend/closet/wouldwork.py`, pure and deterministic like `gaps.py`; the checks, the photo and the routes are `backend/closet/wouldjobs.py`; the screen is `frontend/src/screens/WouldWork.tsx`, with its words in `frontend/src/wouldwork.ts`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The piece is tried as if it were in the closet: `gaps.find_gaps` is run with it and without it, and `outfits.suggest` is asked, built around it, for each of the gap analysis's 40 kinds of day | The same engine and analysis as Today and What's missing, so the answer agrees with them and can be explained. Neither is changed; the check reads their own pieces and rules as `gaps.py` reads the engine's. The only change around them is that the app's `gap_analysis` takes the rows it has already read (`gap_inputs`), which gives the same answer as before. |
| Five verdicts, picked in this order: **Not enough in the closet to say** (fewer than 8 pieces of clothing, as the gap analysis); **Hard to wear with what you have** (below); **Fills a gap** (below); **Works, but you have it already** (at least one piece owned does the same job); otherwise **Works, and adds something** | Hard to wear comes before filling a gap: a piece that fills a gap on paper but goes with nothing does not fill it in practice. The thresholds are named constants at the top of `wouldwork.py` and were set by trying pieces on the demo closet (a rain-proof coat, a fourth pair of jeans, plaid formal trousers, a floral shirt, brown dress shoes, and the demo closet without its trousers or its coats); they may want moving once the real closet is in. |
| **Fills a gap** is judged by days, not by the gap's score: the kinds of day a gap fails on without the piece are compared with those it fails on with it. A gap is filled when it goes, or when at least half of its failing days (`FILL_MIN_SHARE`) are freed and on each of those the piece makes a good outfit or no piece of its kind does better (below). A gap that rests on no days (a kind of piece missing, pieces nothing goes with) is filled when it goes. When the analysis is run with the piece, a "thin place" that rests only on the piece itself is dropped | With the piece added, the gap analysis says that very piece is now a thin place ("only one pair of smart trousers"), so the score of "Smart trousers" barely fell even when grey business trousers were exactly what it asked for. A gap whose only remaining finding is the candidate is filled; the score would have needed a threshold that rewards no piece in particular. The same rule stops a piece from being said to fill a gap whose days it does not change (light trousers for hot days are not filled by navy business trousers, and do not rise because of them). |
| **Hard to wear** is judged only on the kinds of day that suit the piece, and only on those where an owned piece of the same kind (up to 6, `COMPARE_MAX`, most worn first) makes a good outfit and the candidate does not. It is hard to wear when it makes a good outfit on fewer than 2 of those days, or on less than a quarter of them. Days where no piece of its kind does better are the closet's shortfall: the answer says so ("…that is what the closet lacks, not this piece") and does not count them against the piece | A white polo in a closet with no sweater or jacket is short of warm on every cool day, but so is every top the owner has; blaming the polo, with a reason that was not the one found, was wrong. The sentence under the verdict is always the reason that was found. |
| Every sentence must be true of every piece or day it speaks for: "all of them" only when every one fails for that reason (otherwise "three of them…, two…"); "covering days nothing covers now" only for days on which the piece makes a good outfit; "breaks a rule" only for the piece's own failing days; when the only piece doing the same job is one rated dislike, the answer says so ("…and you rated it dislike") instead of "nothing you own does the same job" | The answer is meant to be explained and trusted; one false "all" or "nothing" undoes that. |
| "A good outfit" is one the engine makes around the piece with none of its own warnings (a clash, the wrong warmth for the day) on a day the piece itself suits (its season, and an outer or middle layer not on a hot day) | The engine can be made to put any piece in an outfit; this keeps "it works on 16 kinds of day" honest. |
| "Does the same job" is the same kind, the same formality and the same colour group: dark neutrals (black, navy) together, light ones (white, cream), earth tones (tan, brown), and otherwise the engine's colour families; jeans in any blue, navy, black or grey wash are one group | "You have three like it, and two of them are hardly worn" is the most useful sentence the check can say, so it should catch the pieces the owner would call the same; jeans are worn with the same things whatever the wash, as the engine counts blue jeans as neutral. "Hardly worn" is at most once in the log, said only when the log has at least 10 wears (as the gap analysis). |
| A gap found only from the wear log ("another one like the piece you wear most") is listed but does not make the verdict "Fills a gap" | The same piece again is always the answer to that gap, so it would call every repeat a gap filled; the pieces like it that are hardly worn are often the better answer, and the verdict says "you have it already" with them named. |
| The photo is described by the tagger through the same routes as an item (plan first, the API key only when switched on and under its monthly limit), recorded in the spending record with its own purpose `would_this_work` | One way of reaching Claude, one limit; the purpose lets the owner see what the checks cost. The demo closet sends nothing (the photo way is off there and says why), and with no route the photo way says so at once and offers "describe it by hand", which needs no Claude. |
| The photo is never written to disk: it is read, shrunk to the size the tagger sends (`images.MAX_EDGE`) and held in memory by its check alone, then let go when the check is answered or fails, whatever the failure; nothing about it is logged (an unexpected fault is logged as text, without the photo) | It is a photo of something in a shop, not of the owner's clothes, and `images.py` says an upload is never written as it arrived. Shrunk, it is a few hundred kilobytes for at most a minute, and with nothing on disk there is nothing to clean up after a crash. |
| At most 5 checks are kept: a new one drops the oldest finished one, never one still running; while 5 are running a new one is refused (409, "Wait for one of them to finish") | Dropping a running check would leave its thread, a call to Claude included, finishing for nobody while the screen got "not known". Refusing caps the threads at 5 too. |
| Removing the background is run by the cutout worker and, for a photo being checked, by the check's thread; the one model session is made under a lock (`cutout._sessions_lock`) | Two threads making it at once would load the model twice. |
| A check starts with `POST` and is asked for with `GET`, and checks are kept in memory only, gone when Closet stops | Describing a photo takes up to a minute through the plan, too long to hold a request open from a phone. Nothing about a check needs keeping: the answer is worked out again in seconds, and **Put it on my wishlist** keeps what matters. So there is no migration. |
| "Put it on my wishlist" adds a wish by hand with the piece's name and, in the note, its description and the verdict, once per check (the button then reads "On your wishlist", also after opening a piece and coming back). Started from a wish, the button is "Add it to the wish's note" and adds the answer to that wish's note (after what is there, once, within the note's 1000 characters) instead of adding a second wish. "I bought it" marks the wish bought when the check was started from one, then opens Add. The lists of What's missing are read again when the check is closed | The wishlist already holds what the owner is looking for; a note is where a found piece's details belong. |
| *(Changed 23 November: it is also kept on this device for the day; see the row below.)* The check is a page of its own (`/missing/check`) on a computer as on a phone: on a computer what the piece is on the left and the answer on the right, the description kept beside the answer; the wishlist stays in the side panel of What's missing. The check is remembered while the app is open, from the moment it is sent: leaving while it is on its way and coming back picks it up again once the server gives its id, and a send that got no id goes back to the form. Opening one of the owner's pieces from the answer and coming back shows it again | In the 300px side panel the form was a long wall of chips with the answer far below it (design review). Side by side, the owner can change one thing and check again while seeing the last answer. |
| *(23 November, the item page batch.)* By hand, the two questions asked first are what kind of piece it is and its main colour (the two the check cannot do without); the answer is asked for as soon as both are chosen, with no button, and again each time more is said, one check at a time (`nextCheck` in `wouldwork.ts`). Everything else is behind **More about it**, and changing what it is behind **Change what it is**, each a `Sheet`; a new answer is asked for once a sheet is closed, not on every tap in it, and the last answer stays in place, dimmed, until it comes. While the rest is unknown the server fills in what it always did (the formality usual for the kind, any time of year, plain) and the page says so in one sentence ("Until you say more, it is checked as a plain smart casual piece for any time of year."). The order is the piece's name, the verdict, then that sentence with **Change what it is** and **More about it**, then the reasons; by hand the verdict does not repeat the piece's words (from a photo it still says what Claude saw) | The form asked for eight things before giving any answer. A disclosure in place would have pushed the answer down every time it opened (principle 3); a sheet keeps the answer where it is. The verdict leads (principle 1) and the piece is said once; at 375 px the first reason still shows on the first screen with the sentence and buttons above it (12-would-work holds it). Asking on every tap inside a sheet would start several checks of 40 kinds of day each, and the server keeps at most five. The rules in `wouldwork.py` are unchanged: what is not said is filled in as before, and now said. |
| *(23 November, the item page batch.)* What was typed, the description last answered and the last answer are also kept on this device for the rest of the day, per closet and per wish it was started from (`closet-today-saved:would:<closet>`, with the other saved copies, so leaving a closet forgets it), as Today keeps its changes. A reload shows them as they were without asking again; a newer description that was still on its way is asked for again. **Check another piece** forgets them | A reload, or iOS closing the app, lost six seconds of work and every chip chosen (P12). Kept for the day rather than for good, as Today's changes are: a check is about a piece in a shop now, and the closet it was judged against changes. |

### Shopping for a wish

The asking is `backend/closet/tagger/shopping.py` (the prompts, the answer's shape, the two routes); keeping finds, the checks against the closet and the daily round are `backend/closet/shop.py` (migration 0018); the screen is `frontend/src/screens/Shop.tsx` inside each wish on What's missing.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Each find is judged exactly as "Would this work?" judges a piece: on the gap list weighed by how the owner lives once they have said (and always in the demo), else on the plain list | Finds were judged on the plain list, so a find could "fill a gap" for formal days the owner said they never have, while the same piece described by hand was "you have it already". |
| "Find some" and "Check prices" go through the same two routes as describing an item: the plan through `claude -p`, allowed its `WebSearch` and `WebFetch` tools and nothing else, then the API key only when switched on, with Anthropic's web search tool. Every call is in the spending record with its own purpose (`shop_find`, `shop_prices`) | One way of reaching Claude and one limit; the purposes show the owner what shopping costs. The plan route's other tools (reading or changing files, running commands) stay off, since the answer is built from web pages. |
| Through the API key the answer comes back as a strict tool call (`give_answer`), not as the structured output used for tagging | Anthropic's structured output cannot be used together with citations, and web search always gives citations. A search the API pauses in the middle (`pause_turn`) is let go on at most twice, and each continuation is a request checked against the limit before it is made. |
| Before a search or a price check through the API key, the most it could cost is worked out (300,000 tokens in, the longest answer out, and every web search allowed — 5 for a search, up to 8 for a price check — at the price written down in `models.json`, $10 per 1,000 searches) and it is refused with the limit's own waiting note when that could take the month past the limit. When the plan can be used nothing is estimated | The same promise as for items: a request that could go past the limit is not made. A search reads several pages, so the bound is generous; the record keeps what each request really used. |
| Claude's answer is never trusted: each product is kept only with an `http`/`https` link with a host and no password in it, a name, a shop, a price from 0 to 1,000,000 or none, a three-letter currency, and a kind, colour and the rest in the taxonomy's words (as "Would this work?" by hand checks them). A product that fails is dropped alone; at most 8 are kept from one search, the same link once. Web pages are named in the prompt as information, never as instructions | The answer is built from pages anyone can write. A link that is not a web address (`javascript:`) could run in the app when clicked; the page checks it again before showing a link or a picture. |
| Each product is checked with the "Would this work?" rules (`wouldwork.check`, with the gap analysis worked out once for all of them), and checked again on every price check | The same answer as checking it by hand, so the owner can trust the verdict; the closet changes between price checks. |
| *(Changed by "Checkout codes": each price is the one after its checkout code when there was one.)* Finds are kept with their wish (deleted with it), the same link found again brings the product up to date instead of adding it twice, and every price seen is kept (`find_prices`). A drop is the newest price below the highest one seen before in the same currency | The history is small (a row a day per product at most) and a drop is what the owner wants to hear about; a price in another currency is not compared. |
| Links open in a new tab with `noopener noreferrer`; pictures are shown from the shop with no referrer. Nothing is bought, and nothing is opened by itself | The owner decides; the shop is not told where the visit came from. |
| While Closet runs it looks once an hour (the first look two minutes after it starts) for open wishes in the owner's own closet whose finds were last checked over a day ago, and checks up to the 12 newest finds of each. It never runs on the demo closet, whichever closet is open. A round that cannot reach Claude, or reaches the limit, stops and is tried again at the next look; an answer that cannot be used still counts as the day's check | At most once a day per wish keeps the cost known; the owner's closet is the one that matters even while the demo is open. Not counting an unusable answer would ask again every hour. |
| The app's own price checks replace the planned Google Drive hand-off. **Recommendation awaiting the owner**: drop the hand-off | The plan route checks prices at no extra cost while the computer is on, and nothing has to leave the computer. If the owner wants checks while it is off, the hand-off can come back. |
| *(Changed by "The wish page and price news": the page sets only the most you'd pay.)* The wish gets an optional price range (`price_min`, `price_max`, `price_currency`), told to Claude as the range to stay in | The note already holds "budget" in words; a range is something the search can be held to. |
| *(23 November, the item page batch.)* "Stop watching" can be undone: the find stays in its place on the wish's page, saying so, with **Undo** where its buttons were, until the page is left; Undo puts it back as it was (its place, its whole price history, its code). What was taken is held in Nines' memory (an hour, at most 20 finds), not in the closet: there is no migration, and after a restart it cannot be put back | DESIGN principle 9 (A8): the find and its prices went at once with no way back. Holding the rows rather than marking the find stopped keeps every query over finds as it was (the daily check, the banner, the wishlist's rows would each have had to leave stopped finds out). The card staying where it was, as "I'll look for this" does (A4), keeps the page still. |

### What the search is told about you (23 November; BACKLOG "Finding clothes you might like", step 3)

Builds on "Shopping for a wish", which still holds. Until now a search was told only the wish (its name, what to look for, the note, the price range) and nothing from the closet. The block is built by `backend/closet/aboutyou.py` (`about` is pure); the sizes are `backend/closet/sizes.py` (migration 0047) and the row in Settings is `frontend/src/components/YourSizes.tsx`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Beside the wish, a search is sent one block headed "About the person this is for": the lines of Your style (none while the starting profile is in force), the colours, patterns and fits taste favours in the wish's category, up to 5 pieces of that category loved or worn on 3 days or more, the brands on the owner's active items, and the saved size for the wish's kind. A wish added by hand (no category) is looked at across the clothes, accessories left out, with every saved size | What Nines has that a shop search lacks is the closet; a search that knows the owner can put what suits them first. |
| The block says these are leanings to rank by, not requirements, and the wish and its price range come first; the prompt's rules repeat it. Brands are "a hint, not a filter" | A narrow search would miss a good match for the wish because it is not the owner's usual brand or colour. |
| Taste claims follow `taste.py` exactly (10 wears across 3 pieces, or 5 ratings; a value favoured needs a score of 4 across 2 pieces). For a wish with a category only that category's taste is said, never the closet's as a whole in its place (`taste.overall` is used only for a wish with none) | No claim on too little evidence, as everywhere else; a colour you wear in tees says little about coats. |
