| A piece is said in the closet's own words: its kind, colour (and pattern when not solid), fit and material, loved and the days worn. Never its photo, its name, its notes, its fit notes or How I wear it | The owner types names and notes freely; they can hold anything. The kind and colour are what a shop search needs. |
| Bounded whatever the closet's size: at most 1,500 characters of style (each line at most 300), 5 pieces, 8 brands, and 8 brands that fit differently across the sizes said. Stable order: loved pieces first, then most worn, then by id; brands by wears plus ratings (love 4, like 2), then by how many items, then alphabetical, a dislike leaving the item out | A search's cost grows with what it reads; the same closet gives the same words, so the tests can quote them. |
| Sizes are kept in the closet's database (`sizes`, one row per kind and brand), not in the program's settings | They are about the person whose closet it is, like Your style: the demo has its own and a backup takes them along. |
| Five kinds: tops, knitwear, trousers and jeans, jackets and coats, shoes, mapped from the categories through the same kinds trips count (`trips.KIND_OF`) and the shoe categories. Accessories have none | The kinds a size differs across in menswear, short enough for one sheet; a belt or a hat is rarely bought by size online. |
| A size is free short text (at most 20 characters), a brand's name at most 40, at most 10 brands per kind | Sizing differs by kind, country and shop ("M", "32x32", "10.5 US", "40R"); the only reader is Claude, which reads words. A fixed list would be wrong somewhere. |
| With no size saved for a kind, the size most common on the owner's own items of that kind is offered with "Use it", and is never sent until saved | Nines suggests and the owner decides; an item's size can be old or a gift's. |
| Sizes live in a row of Settings → Everyday with a sheet, not in the style conversation | A size is a fact you type once, not a preference that comes up in talk. |
| *(A look's sentence also names what a sale look sends: "On sale now".)* One sentence under "Find some" says what a search sends; the demo closet, which never searches, does not show it | The owner should know what leaves the computer, where they tap. |


### Insights

The findings live in `backend/closet/insights.py`; every number is a named constant at its top. The screen is `frontend/src/screens/Insights.tsx` (styles in `Insights.css`), with the wording and what the device keeps in `frontend/src/insights.ts`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| *(Superseded by "Four places and Add": Insights is part of the Wardrobe page.)* Insights is reached from a button at the top of the Closet screen, not from the main navigation; while it is open, **Closet** stays highlighted | The tab bar on a phone already holds four entries and other screens are being added; Insights is about the closet's own pieces, so it belongs with them. |
| Money is shown with `$` and never converted; prices stay in cents as typed | The price field shows `$` and the owner lives in New York. There is no currency field, and adding one would need a migration. |
| Cost per wear is price divided by times worn (all time, laundry included); a piece with a price and no wear has none and is listed with its price as "not worn yet"; the closet's own figure is what was paid for the pieces with a price over their wears, once there are 5 | Zero or infinity would mislead. The best and worst lists hold at most 3 each and never the same piece twice, so a closet with few priced pieces still shows both. |
| Never worn: not worn at all, added at least 30 days ago, a log of at least 10 wears, and at least 7 days of the piece's season since it was added or since the log began, whichever is later | A new piece has not had its chance, and with almost no log everything looks unworn. 7 days is short on purpose: the demo closet's log goes back only about two weeks and must not change, and the per-piece sentence says how long it has been ("since you began recording what you wear"). |
| Not worn for a long time: 90 days of the piece's season since its last wear, once the log itself goes back 90 days | Counting only days of its season means a winter coat last worn in March is not neglected in August; it is in December. |
| When nothing at all has been recorded in the last 90 days, Not worn for a long time lists nothing and What the closet is made of gives no share in use; both say "Nothing has been recorded as worn since 20 July 2026." instead | After a break from recording, every piece ever recorded was "not worn for 90 days of its season or more", each with Archive, and the share was "0 of them (0%)". The log stopped, not the wearing. The logic review of 23 November. |
| Not worn leaves out what Today never offers (`gaps.everyday`: swim trunks, shoes for one sport, clothes for home); the route reads each piece's kind as the item page shows it | Nines never puts them in an outfit and a trip that packs them records no wear, so they stayed under Not worn, with Archive, for good. |
| Seasons are New York's months: warm clothes April to October, cool clothes October to April (both in April and October), "all" every month | The owner's home. A southern-hemisphere closet would need these flipped; there is no setting for it. |
| Worn most is shown for the last 30 days, 90 days and all time, 5 pieces each, once a window has 5 wears; ties go to the piece worn more recently, then by name | Short windows show habits, all time shows favourites. The window is counted back from today; when the log is shorter the sentence says how far it goes back. |
| The spread in a kind of clothing (last 90 days, at least 2 pieces and 5 wears) is "a few do the work" when the fewest pieces that together have 80% of the wears are at most half the pieces; otherwise "fairly even" | A plain, explainable cut-off; a statistic like the Gini coefficient would need explaining. |
| What the closet is made of counts every piece in use by kind, colour, formality and season, and the share worn in the last 90 days once the log has 10 wears | The share means little with a few wears recorded, so it is held back until then. |
| From a never-worn piece: **Archive** archives it at once (as from the item; undone from Archived); **Keep** hides it from the not-worn lists for 90 days, remembered on this device only (`localStorage`, per closet) and kept after logout | It is a habit of the device, like the occasion on Today, and needs no migration. **Show again** lists every kept piece again. |
| The answer is worked out on each call, not kept on the server | It is a few passes over the items and the log, fast even on a large closet. |
| The last answer is kept on the device per closet (with Today's and What's missing's saved copies, removed on logout) and shown offline marked "Saved earlier", with Archive off; the service worker does not keep `/api/insights` | The service worker keeps answers by address only, so after switching closets it would show one closet's sentences and prices as the other's. |
| Names are put into sentences as the owner typed them ("your New Balance 990", "your Grey wool trousers") | Lower-casing the first letter reads better for plain names but breaks brands and places, and there is no telling them apart. |
| A sentence claims only what is true of every piece it counts: when nothing is listed because it is too early to say for some pieces, it says that rather than "everything has been worn"; the never-worn headline gives the shortest time among the pieces listed; worn most says how many more were worn as often when they do not all fit | Each sentence is read first and on its own. |
| The demo closet has prices on 13 pieces (`tools/make_demo.py`, `PRICES`); a demo closet made before this gets them added once when it is opened, if none of its items has a price | So the screen has something to show without changing anything else in the demo data. |
| Each card leads with a short label, then one figure ("$61.08 a wear", "3 pieces", "18 of 30 worn"), then one sentence. The figure is worked out on the page from the numbers the answer already carries (`insights.ts`), and the cost-per-wear sentence is rewritten there without it; the server's sentences are unchanged | The long sentences buried the figure. Keeping them on the server unchanged keeps the saved offline copies and the backend tests as they are. When there is too little to show a figure, the card has only the sentence saying why. |
| Pieces with a price and no wear are listed once, in **Not worn yet**, not again under Cost per wear | The same pieces were shown twice on one screen. The answer still carries `not_worn` for anything that wants it. |
| Cost per wear is the last card, as wide as the card beside it, with no figure leading it: its figure is inside its sentence (the server's own: "…worn them 50 times: $57.18 a wear."), and Best value and Most per wear are folded away under one line until opened. The screen now leads with Not worn yet | The owner, 16 November: as the first and widest card, with the largest number on the screen, it set a calculating tone for everything after it. It is worth having, not what to judge every piece by. This replaces the rule above that every card leads with a figure, for this card only. |

## Reaching Claude: two routes

Decided with the owner after phase 1: the app can reach Claude two ways, and
uses whichever is available.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Two routes: the owner's Claude plan through the Claude Code command-line tool (`claude -p`), and an API key | Either can run dry: the plan has usage limits, the API key has a credit balance. |
| Default order is plan first, API key second; testing and the bench default to the plan | Plan calls bill nothing. The API key is only used when the plan cannot be. |
| The owner can choose the order in Settings (`tagging_route_order` in `settings.json`: `auto`, `api-first`, `plan`, `api`), and it applies to everything that reaches Claude (describing, landmarks, typed corrections, "Would this work?") | Some owners would rather pay per photo than use their plan. The two orders that put the key first are refused until a key is set, and removing the last key puts such a choice back to `auto`, so items do not wait on a key that is gone. The key is still only used once allowed with a monthly limit, whatever the order: the order says which is tried first, not whether the key may be used. |
| A route that is out of credit, not signed in or not installed is skipped for the rest of that run; a bad answer is retried on the same route and never moved to the paid one | Falling back must not turn a prompt problem into a bill. |
| The plan route removes `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` from the subprocess environment and never uses `--bare` | Claude Code prefers an API key when it sees one, which would bill the API silently; bare mode cannot read the plan login. |
| Same prompt, same schema, same validation on both routes | The plan route passes the schema with `--json-schema` and reads `structured_output`; images are written to a temporary folder and opened with the Read tool, the only tool allowed. |
| The plan route's answer form (`tagging.SENT`, made for the API route until 16 November; see the next row) keeps every word of the main fields (category, colour, secondary colours, pattern, formality, season) in the structured-output grammar, and in the details and the silhouette only lists of up to 12 words; a longer list there is plain text with its words named in the field's description, and Closet checks the answer against the full form (`tagging.fitted`): a word outside its list becomes `unknown`, `na`, `none` or `other` (the first the field has), a list's stray words are dropped, anything else is asked again | The API compiles the form into a grammar and refused the full one once two shoe fields were added (schema version 5, 939 listed words): "The compiled grammar is too large", 400, so no item could be described with the API key (16 November, v0.5.0). The form sent lists 327. Loosening the main fields too was tried and a boat shoe came back as category "other". All five of the owner's shoes were described right with it through the plan route. |
| On the API route the whole answer form goes as a plain tool (`describe_item`, not `strict`; `tagging.answer_tool`), asked for with `tool_choice` auto, and nothing is sent as structured output; Closet checks the answer against the form itself: a main field (category, colour, secondary colours, pattern, formality, season) outside its list is said back in the same conversation (the reply as it came, then a `tool_result` error naming each field, what came and the words it allows) and asked again, an answer without the tool is asked again from the start, and what is still outside its list at the last try is made to fit (`tagging.fitted`) | A form sent as structured output (or a strict tool) is compiled by the API into a grammar, and the API refuses a grammar over a size it does not publish ("The compiled grammar is too large", 400). The 939-word form was refused in v0.5.0; #235 cut it to 327 words, which met every published limit (0 optional fields of 24, 11 nullable of 16) but could not be tried against the API, and the owner reported a description through the API key failing the same way again (16 November). A plain tool is not compiled, so the form's size no longer matters and it can keep every word of every list in view; the main fields stay honest by being asked again rather than by the grammar (loosened to plain text they once let a boat shoe through as "other"). Forcing the tool is refused by Claude Opus 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5, so it is asked for. The request with the whole form is bigger: `spend.TEXT_TOKENS` is 18,000 (was 8,000). Checked with the real SDK against a local stand-in server (`backend/tests/test_templates_and_tagger.py`); not yet against the API itself. |
| The plan route hands Claude Code its prompt on standard input, in UTF-8, and only the options on the command line (the schema written without spaces); on Windows it looks for `claude.exe` before an npm `claude.cmd` and starts it with no console window | Windows starts a program with at most 32,767 characters of command line, and describing an item needed about 37,000 (the prompt and the schema), so the plan route could never have started on the owner's Windows hub; without the prompt it is about 30,400 with a long program path, and a test checks every plan call stays at least 1,000 under. Standard input is how `claude -p` reads a prompt given no argument; it is given from a temporary file, not a pipe, because on Windows writing a prompt larger than a pipe holds ignores the timeout, so a Claude Code that never read it would hold Closet up. UTF-8 is said outright because Windows would otherwise use its own code page, which cannot hold every character of a prompt or an answer. The owner's words (a typed correction, a shop's address) are then never on a command line, where `cmd.exe` would read some characters itself if Claude Code was installed through npm. On the Mac this changes nothing the owner sees. |
| Cost shown for plan calls is labelled as an estimate at API prices | Claude Code reports `total_cost_usd` as a client-side estimate; nothing is charged per call on a plan. It includes Claude Code's own overhead, so it overstates what the API route would cost. |
| The spend cap applies to the API route only | Plan usage is limited by the plan itself. |
| Every request to Claude is recorded in the real closet's database (`claude_calls`, migration `0009`): when, what for, route, model, tokens in and out as reported, cost, and the date of the prices used | The record must survive restarts and be in backups; `settings.json` is not backed up, the closet's database is. It is the real closet's whatever closet is open, and the demo closet is never sent to Claude, so it never spends. No photo, prompt, answer, key or account is stored and nothing more is logged. Restoring an older backup forgets the requests made after it, so that month can read lower than Anthropic's bill. Plan requests are recorded with their tokens but cost 0: nothing is charged per request on a plan. |
| The monthly limit is in US dollars, the currency of the price list; the month is the calendar month in the computer's local time | That is what the owner sees on the clock; Anthropic bills in dollars. A new month starts at zero. |
| When Claude Code's sign-in is refused ("Not a valid API key for this workspace", "Invalid API key", not logged in) the item waits and says why, instead of using up its three tries | Found on the owner's first item in 0.2.0: Claude Code was using an API key Anthropic refused, Closet read the refusal as a bad answer for that photo, and after three tries said to retake the photo. Nothing was wrong with the photo, and asking again cannot help until the owner signs in again. `claude auth status` still says signed in in that case (it only checks a sign-in exists), so Settings shows the plan as ready; the waiting item's note is where the owner learns what to do. Waiting items are asked again once a minute, so they go ahead by themselves after the owner signs in again. The note uses the same mechanism as the monthly limit's, and each replaces the other. |
| The API key is pasted in Settings and kept in the computer's keychain, through the system's own tools: Apple's `security` command on the Mac (the key handed over on its standard input, never on a command line) and the Credential Manager API on Windows (through `ctypes`). No library was added for it. On other systems there is no keychain and the key can only come from `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` | Decided before phase 0 ("kept in the OS keychain, environment variable as fallback") and asked for by the owner after the plan route failed on their Mac. The `keyring` library would have brought several more packages (and a Linux one with heavy dependencies) for three calls per system. A saved key wins over `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, because it is the one the owner chose in the app. Before it is kept, the key is checked with Anthropic by listing one model, which costs nothing: a key Anthropic turns down is not kept; if Anthropic cannot be reached the key is kept and the owner is told it was not checked. The key is never sent back to the page, not even its last characters, never logged, and never put into the environment of Claude Code, which must keep using the plan. A keychain that cannot be read counts as holding no key. Every test uses a stand-in keychain. The Mac part is tested against a stand-in `security`; the Windows part saves, reads, replaces and removes a key of its own in Credential Manager in the CI's Windows job. |
| Before each API request (a retry included) this month's cost so far plus the most that request can cost is compared with the limit; if it would pass, the request is not made and the job waits without using an attempt, and the waiting items say "This month's spending limit for the API key has been reached." | Waiting is what already happens when no route can be used, so raising the limit, a new month or the plan coming back lets the queue carry on by itself. The plan route is tried first and used whatever the limit. The most a request can cost is worked out carefully: 8,000 tokens of instructions and schema (measured about 4,100) plus 1,600 per photo (the most a photo counts for once Anthropic has scaled it to about 1.15 megapixels), and the whole 4,000-token answer allowance, about $0.003 for one photo. So the month can end a little under the limit, never over it. |
| While a request to the API is on its way, the most it can cost is held against the limit, for every worker at once, until it is recorded at what it cost or has failed (`spend.Meter`) | Describing, landmarks, the pieces in a photo, typed corrections, "Would this work?" and shopping each ask Claude from a thread of their own, and each compared the month's spend with the limit alone: with a little room left, all of them could see it free and together pass the limit (found in the review after the fast wave, #161). One lock for the whole program checks and holds, so a second request that would not fit as well is not made (it waits, as at the limit) until the first has come back. A request that fails lets go of what it held, as no answer is billed. |
| Suggested limit: $5 a month | At the prices written down it covers well over a thousand photos even if the plan is never available, so a first import of a whole wardrobe fits, while a fault that sent the same request over and over would stop within a few dollars. |
| The estimate for waiting work counts one request per item ("at most, each described at the first try") | A bad answer is retried, which can cost more, but every retry passes the same check against the limit, so the limit holds. Before an import of several photos the Add screen says what they would cost at most "if your plan can't be used". |
| When Anthropic refuses the key (401 or 403) or says the account is out of credit, Closet turns "Allow the API key" off itself, keeps why in `settings.json`, says so in Settings, and the jobs wait | Asking again would fail the same way. The owner turns it back on after fixing the key or adding credit, which clears the message. |
| Prices are kept in `closet/tagger/models.json` with the date they were written down (`prices_checked`, 2026-11-03); a model not in it is priced as the dearest one listed | The figures were written down by an earlier session from Anthropic's pricing; when the spend cap was built (2026-11-13) Anthropic's pricing page could not be reached from the build machine, and two independent summaries from October 2026 gave the same figures. Prices change: the owner should check them on Anthropic's pricing page before relying on what Closet shows, and Settings says when they were written down. What Closet shows is its own working out; the Anthropic account shows what was really charged. |

Facts checked in Anthropic's documentation on 2026-11-12:
- Windows install needs no Node.js: `irm https://claude.ai/install.ps1 | iex` in PowerShell, or `winget install Anthropic.ClaudeCode`. The program lands in `%USERPROFILE%\.local\bin\claude.exe`; the app looks there as well as on PATH.
- Signing in once with `claude` persists (stored in `%USERPROFILE%\.claude\.credentials.json`); later `claude -p` calls from another program use it. `claude auth status` exits 0 when signed in.
- The support pages say `claude -p` under a plan login draws on the plan's limits. The legal page restricts third-party developers offering Claude login to their users, and says this does not prevent an end user signing in to the unmodified Claude Code program with their own plan. A personal program calling that program on the owner's own machine appears to be inside those terms, but the docs do not say so in as many words, and Anthropic reserves the right to change enforcement.
- When a limit is hit the message reads "You've hit your session limit · resets …" (or weekly). The exit code for that case is not documented; the app matches on the message.

### Outfits chosen by Claude (decided by the owner, 17 November)

The owner: "we could make this so much better by inserting real intelligence into it". Decided in the same
conversation: Claude chooses the outfits, on Opus through the API key ("opus via api"); the menswear rules become
preferences ("yes rules become preferences"), with only what cannot be worn staying fixed (in the laundry, archived,
being mended, rated dislike); today's engine stays as the fallback when Claude cannot be reached. Built in steps
(BACKLOG, "Outfits chosen by Claude"). Step 1, the day, is below.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The forecast is asked for hour by hour as well (temperature, what it feels like, chance and amount of rain, wind and gusts, humidity, UV) and read for the hours the owner is out (`closet/day.py`): from now until 22:00, from 7:00 if earlier, or the hours a day note gives. Once those hours are all gone, they are read again in full | The owner: "weather should be much more complex". The whole day's low is usually at four in the morning, when nobody is dressed for it, and a shower at ten should not put a rain jacket on at three. The window starts at "now" so the line on Today is about what is still ahead. |
| The engine is now told what those hours feel like at their coldest and warmest, and whether it rains in them, in place of the whole day's low, high and chance of rain. With no hours in the forecast (an older kept forecast, or the service gave none), the whole day's values are used as before; temperatures typed by hand always stand | Wind and humidity are in the weather service's feels-like figure, so the engine gets them without new rules of its own. This changes what the engine dresses for on windy and humid days and in the afternoon; it is the step-1 use of the richer weather, before the stylist reads all of it. |
| An hour is wet at a 50% chance of rain or more (the day's old threshold), or with 0.2 mm when no chance is given. The words: light rain under 1 mm in the wettest hour, heavy from 4 mm, "most of the time" at 80% of the hours, two separate showers said as two and more as "on and off"; breezy from 20 km/h, windy from 35, very windy from 50, strong gusts from 60; strong sun at UV 6, very strong at 8; humid when it feels 24 °C or more at 70% humidity. "Feels like" is said only when it is 2 °C or more from the thermometer at either end | Each word is only said when it changes what to wear. The numbers are the engine's own choices, in one place (`closet/day.py`), until the owner's style profile says otherwise. |
| The day in the owner's words is one sentence on Today, read by Claude (`tagger/dayread.py`) for the place, the first day and how many days, the hours outside, how much of the day is outdoors, what it is for, and the occasion when the sentence makes it clear. Claude is never asked for the weather: Closet searches the place on the same map as Settings and looks up its forecast itself | The owner: "easier to set to home and then give it prompts (I'm on vacation in x for x days, or I'm going to x today and will be outside most of the day)". A language model reads free words well and is a poor source for a forecast. |
| What Closet took from the sentence is shown back in parts built by the code from the reading (place, until when, hours outside, what for), not in Claude's own words | The owner sees exactly what Today will act on, and a wrong reading is visible at once. |
| One note at a time, kept in the program's settings (`day_note`), not in a closet. It ends by itself after its last day; a note for days ahead waits. The home place is never changed by it, and its forecast is kept apart from home's (`weather_away`) | Like the place, the note is about where the owner is, not about a closet, and it is short-lived, so it needs no migration and is not in backups. Keeping home untouched is the point: "set to home and then give it prompts". |
| A place that cannot be found keeps the note without one and says so; when Claude cannot be asked, the limit is reached or nothing about the day was found, the note before stays and the page says why | The hours and the occasion are still worth having. Nothing is lost by trying. |
| The sentence goes to Claude on a new `styling` role (Opus), through the API key first when it is switched on, else the plan; counted against the monthly limit with purpose `read_day`. The demo closet never asks | The owner chose Opus through the API for the outfit intelligence; the same role will choose the outfits. The demo stays free of any call to Claude, as everywhere else. |
| The note's occasion is used when the request names none, and the page selects it when the note is saved; the owner can still choose another | A wedding should not need two taps, and the owner's own choice still wins. |
| The sentence is typed behind **Change**, first in that panel, and a note that holds is one small line under the weather, cut short with "…" when it is long (the whole of it is behind Change) | A row of its own under the weather pushed **I'm wearing this** off the first phone screen (the browser tests for Today caught it). Behind Change an ordinary day's screen is exactly as it was, and a note costs one line of 20 px. |
| The dot between the parts of Today's weather line now follows each part instead of leading it | The line is longer now and wraps on a phone; a wrapped line no longer starts with a dot. |

#### Step 2, the stylist: what Claude is told and what is kept from its answer

Built first as code and a developer tool, not yet part of Today, so it could be judged and measured (`closet/styling.py`,
`tagger/stylist.py`, `tools/style_demo_days.py`).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| One question, text only. Claude is told, in this order: the owner's style profile, the guide, every piece that can be worn today, the day, what was worn lately and what the owner said, the rules engine's six best outfits, and what to give back | Everything a person choosing clothes would want in front of them. No photos: a line of words per piece is enough to choose with, and a call stays near 5 cents. |
| The guide is the `outfit` section of `docs/menswear-taxonomy.json`, written out as plain lines and introduced as "preferences to weigh, never rules" | The owner: "rules become preferences". The file stays the one place the owner's menswear notes live; the engine and the stylist read the same notes, one as rules and one as advice. |
| The style profile is the owner speaking and wins over the guide; the owner's notes on a piece win over both. Until the profile is kept in Settings (next), it starts as one line of the owner's: "I carry an umbrella, so a rain jacket is never a must…" | The owner: "not sure what menswear rules I care about"; "I have an umbrella so not sure rain always needs to require a rain jacket, but it could favor one, and waterproof boots". The order of who wins is said to Claude in so many words. |
| Each wearable piece is one numbered line: name, kind and the places it can take, colour and pattern, cloth and weight, how dressed-up, season, cut, the owner's rating, when last worn, storage, and the owner's notes in quotes (`wear_notes`, `notes`, `fit_notes`). Claude answers with the numbers | Numbers are short, cannot be misspelt, and one that is not in the list is caught. The 32-character ids never go to Claude. |
| Only what cannot be worn is a rule: pieces in the laundry, archived, being mended, for home, left out today or rated dislike are not in the list (the engine's own `_unavailable`), so they cannot be chosen | The owner's decision. Everything else (layering, warmth, colour, formality) is Claude's to judge. |
| What was worn lately is told by the pieces' names, newest twelve, with the day's temperatures and occasion and what the owner said (the rating, its reasons, their note) | A piece worn last week may be in the laundry and have no number. This, with the profile, is all Claude remembers between calls. |
| From the answer an outfit is kept when every number is in the list, each piece is in a place it can fill, it has a top, a bottom and shoes, no piece is in two places, it holds the piece built around (or kept from an outfit worn again), it is not one the owner rated "not again", and it is not a repeat of one before it. An accessory that is not in the list, is no accessory or is a second of its kind is dropped and the outfit stands. Every outfit left out is said in a sentence | The few things a model can get wrong that would make an outfit unwearable or break the owner's word. Kept outfits have the engine's shape (`pieces`, `reasons`, `warnings`) with `by: "claude"` and no score: Claude's reason replaces the number. |
| Claude gives `why` (one or two sentences on the outfit as a whole), `watch` (a real caution or nothing) and `read` (one line on what about the day shaped the choices) | The owner: "outfit should be considered in totality". The reasons are the explanation now that no score is. |
| Effort medium on Opus | Tried on the demo closet (38 wearable pieces) on four made-up days through the plan: every outfit of 12 was wearable. Medium took 16 to 23 seconds and would cost about 5 cents a call through the API key (4,500 tokens read, 1,400 to 2,100 written); low took 14 seconds and about 4 cents. Medium noticed more (a top worn two days ago, loafers on a wet walk home). The outfits are prepared ahead, so the seconds matter less than the eye. |
| Claude drew on what was worn without being asked twice: "close to the cable sweater and parka look you loved on the 7th", "you said the hoodie was too warm on a day like this" | Recorded because it is the answer to "learning should be very deep": the wear history in the brief already does a good part of it, before any profile is learned. |

#### The style profile and the note on a piece

Kept and edited before the stylist is part of Today, so the owner can write them first (migration 0031,
`closet/styleprofile.py`, Settings → **Your style**, the item page's **How I wear it**).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The style profile lives in the closet's own database (`style_profile`), not in the program's settings | It is about the closet's owner and their clothes, as the ratings are: it goes into backups with them, and the demo closet has a profile of its own that cannot change the owner's. The day note and the life answers are about where the owner is and how they live, and stay in the settings. |
| Every save adds a version (`text`, `source`, `created_at`); the newest is the profile in force, and a save the same as the one in force adds none | A later step lets Claude propose changes to the profile from what the owner wears and says (`source` `claude`); with the versions kept, any of them can be shown or put back. Nothing reads the older versions yet. |
| An empty text means the starting profile (`styling.DEFAULT_PROFILE`), and the starting text saved unchanged is kept as an empty one. **Back to the starting text** saves an empty text | The starting lines can be improved in an update; an owner who never wrote their own then gets the new ones. `text_for_stylist` gives None for it, so `styling.brief` uses its own default. |
| At most 4,000 characters, with the owner's line breaks kept | Room for a page of a person's habits (about 600 words, some 1,000 tokens on each call, a fraction of a cent), short enough that the profile cannot crowd out the closet in the brief. Line breaks are how people list things. |
| How I wear it (`wear_notes`) is a field of its own beside Notes and Fit notes, not part of Notes | The stylist is told which is which ("how I wear it", "my note", "fit"): Notes holds anything (where it was bought, a repair), and a note on how a piece is worn is the one that wins over the profile and the guide. It is plain text with no limit of its own, like Fit notes; the stylist reads its first 240 characters (`styling.MAX_NOTE`). |

#### Step 2, the stylist on Today: asked in the background, kept for the day

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Today always answers at once with the rules engine's outfits, and says how Claude's stand: still choosing, ready, or why not (`context.stylist`). Claude is asked on a thread of its own, and its answer is kept in the closet (`style_answers`, migration 0032; `closet/stylejobs.py`) | A call takes about 20 seconds: no request waits on it, and nothing is lost if the page is closed meanwhile. The engine is the fallback the owner asked to keep, and it is what makes "at once" possible. |
| While Claude is choosing, Today shows that it is, in place of the outfits, with **Show Closet's own picks meanwhile**. It does not show the engine's outfits and then swap them | The owner chose Claude's outfits; showing the rules' picks first and replacing them twenty seconds later would move the screen under the owner's thumb every morning. The engine's are one tap away for a morning in a hurry. |
| One answer serves the whole day for the same choices: the occasion, the mood, a piece built around or left out, an outfit worn again, the day's note and place, the style profile, and the owner's notes and ratings of pieces. Changing any of these asks once more, and going back is answered from what is kept | A question costs about 5 cents and 20 seconds, so it is asked when what Claude is told has changed in a way the owner chose, and not otherwise. |
| The weather is not part of that: an answer is asked again only when the low or the high dressed for has moved 3 °C or more, or rain has come or gone. Which pieces are clean is not part of it either: an outfit of Claude's with a piece that can no longer be worn is simply not shown, and Claude is asked again only when none of its outfits is left. A piece added or washed during the day waits for tomorrow, or for **Ask Claude again** | The hours dressed for move through the day and laundry changes with every wear, so tying the answer to them would ask Claude many times a day for little. Three degrees is about one layer. |
| Once an outfit has been worn today, Claude is not asked again by itself (drift, a failure); only **Ask Claude again** does. When none of the outfits Claude chose can be worn any more by then (the one worn went into the laundry), Today says so and shows the engine's, rather than calling the rules' outfits Claude's | The day is dressed. Wearing an outfit also puts pieces in the laundry, which must not start a new question, and must not leave Claude's line about the day over outfits it did not choose. |
| At most 20 questions a day, the owner's messages to Claude among them (12 before the conversation was built). Asking again at the limit keeps the last answer and says why nothing changed; a failure is tried again after 5 minutes; a question still unanswered after 5 minutes (the program was closed) is asked again at once | Bounds on what a day can cost (about a dollar at the most) and on a stuck state. |
| Claude's outfits come first; when fewer than asked for can be worn, the engine's follow, marked **From Closet's rules** | "See another idea" always has somewhere to go, and the owner can tell whose idea each is. |
| When Claude cannot be asked or gives nothing wearable, the engine's outfits stand with one plain sentence saying why and **Try again** | The owner: "maybe it's worth having a backup offline case". Never an empty Today. |
| Claude does not choose in the demo closet, nor when **Let Claude choose my outfits** (Settings → Your style) is off; the answer then has no `stylist` at all and Today is exactly as before | The demo never asks Claude, anywhere. The switch is the owner's way back to the rules, and the reason every earlier test of Today is unchanged. |
| Through the API key first when it is switched on, else the plan; counted against the monthly limit with purpose `style_outfits`, estimated before each call for a closet of some 250 pieces | The owner: "opus via api". The estimate is the most a call should cost, as for the other calls. |

#### Step 2, asked ahead: Claude's outfits are there before the first look

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| While the program runs, once a day from five in the morning (or when the computer is next awake after that), Closet asks Today for the owner's closet by itself, which starts Claude choosing; the answer is kept like any other, so the first open of the day has Claude's outfits at once (`closet/styleahead.py`). No switch of its own: it follows **Let Claude choose my outfits** | The owner (18 November): "start with the proactive outfit". The first open of the day was the one that always waited, 20 seconds or more. Five o'clock: a late night is still yesterday's evening, and the day's forecast is in. |
| It asks with the occasion and mood the page last asked Today with, since that is what the page sends on its next first open; anything else (a piece built around, a typed day) is the page's alone | An answer is kept for the choices it was asked with, so only the same choices find it. They are kept in the browser, per device; the server now remembers the last ones sent. With different choices on the phone and the computer, the answer asked ahead fits the device used last. |
| It asks for the hours from when the owner usually first looks at Today (the middle one of the hours of their last seven first looks), not from five in the morning | The day is read for the hours still to come. Asked at six for a morning that is freezing until nine, the answer would be for the freezing hours, and the owner looking at nine would be asked for weather three degrees off: Claude would be asked again and the wait would be back. |
| Not when Today has not been opened for more than three days, and never in the demo closet | An owner who is away, or who does not use Today, is not asked for (about 8 cents a day through the API key, the choice and its look). Three days covers a weekend. |
| Only for a day read from today's fresh forecast; with no forecast (or an old one) it tries again a quarter of an hour later, as it does when no route to Claude can be used | Outfits for a guessed mild day would be asked again as soon as the owner opened Today with the real weather. A quarter of an hour, not every two minutes, leaves a weather service that is down alone. |
| One question a day: when Claude could not be reached, it is not asked ahead again; the owner's own first open follows the usual rules (asked again after 5 minutes) | A computer left offline all morning must not use up the day's questions before the owner gets up. |
| Not built: a set time chosen by the owner, or a notice on the phone when the outfits are ready | The computer has to be awake for either, and when it is, the answer is there within a minute of five o'clock or of waking. |

#### Step 2, the look: Claude sees each outfit drawn on the figure before offering it

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| After Claude chooses, each outfit is drawn as worn on the figure (the same drawing as **As worn** on Today), and Claude is shown the pictures and asked to look at each as the owner would in a mirror, and at the set side by side: colours and patterns together on the body, where each layer ends against the next, whether the outfits differ to the eye. It keeps an outfit exactly or changes it; Today has the outfits only once that is done (`closet/stylejobs.py`, "The look"; `tagger/stylist.py`, `look`) | The owner's plan of 17 November ("it also looks at the drawn figure of each before offering it") and 18 November ("Claude looking at the drawn figure"). A list of pieces is chosen in words; the picture shows what words miss. Tried with the real Claude on the demo closet: sound outfits were kept as they were (once with a caution added that only the picture gave: "the trousers stop above the loafers, so wear dark socks"), and of two bad ones planted, two plaids over shorts with dress shoes was rebuilt and an outfit that was "one flat beige from collar to cuff" was changed. |
| The look is a second, shorter question: the day, the style profile, the closet, the outfits chosen and what the owner said today, with the pictures (360 pixels wide); not the guide, the history or the rules' shortlist. Low effort | The choice was already made with all of it; the look checks a picture. About 2,000 tokens and three small pictures: about 3 cents through the API key and 13 to 30 seconds through the plan, on top of the choice (about 5 cents, 20 seconds). |
| Only the day's plain questions are looked at (the morning's, asking again, the weather moving, other choices). An answer to a message of the owner's is not | A reply in the conversation takes 6 to 18 seconds and should stay that quick; the owner is there to say what is wrong with it. The morning's question is asked ahead, so its longer wait is not seen. |
| The look can only improve an answer. When the outfits cannot be drawn, Claude cannot be asked a second time, or nothing it gives back can be worn, the outfits first chosen stand; an outfit lost from its second answer keeps its place after the others. What it gives back passes the same check as the first answer | A second question is a second chance to fail, and must not cost the owner the first answer. |
| What Claude saw is kept with the answer (`seen`: its sentence, how many outfits it changed, how long it took; migration 0034) and is not shown on Today. A changed outfit carries its own words on why it works | The record is for judging whether the look earns its 3 cents once it has run on the owner's closet for some weeks. On the page, the outfit's own reason is what matters, not how Claude got there. |
| A question's time to count as lost (5 minutes) starts afresh when the look begins | A slow choice followed by a slow look would otherwise be taken for a question lost when the program stopped, asked again and paid for twice. |
| The waiting card says "under a minute", and "longer than usual" after 90 seconds ("about 20 seconds" and 60 before) | The two parts together took 44 and 50 seconds through the plan in two runs on the demo closet (the look 27 and 30 of them: through the plan each picture is opened as a file). |
| No switch of its own | It follows **Let Claude choose my outfits**. If the record shows it never changes anything on the owner's closet, it is taken out again, rather than left as a setting. |

#### Step 3, the conversation: the owner writes to Claude about the outfits

The owner: "having a convo might result in better outfits if i can say specifically why i like or dont like a combination"; "learning should be very deep".

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Under Claude's outfits on Today is a box, **Tell Claude what you think**. A message ("too dressy", "swap the boots", "why not the navy blazer?") is a question of its own to Claude, asked in the background like the day's first one; Claude answers in one to three sentences and gives the outfits again when something changes. When the owner only asked a question, Claude gives no outfits and the ones on the page stand | One way of asking and keeping serves both, so a message survives a closed page and is counted and limited like any other question. Tried with the real Claude on the demo closet: 6 to 18 seconds a message, and "why not the denim jacket?" was answered without touching the outfits. |
| While a message is being answered, and when it could not be answered, the outfits on the page stay as they are; the message is shown as being answered, or with one sentence saying why not and **Send again**. A failed message is not sent again by itself | The owner is looking at outfits they may be about to wear: a failure must not swap them for the rules' picks. Sending again is the owner's call, since they may have moved on. |
| Claude is told what the owner sees: the outfits on the page in order, which are its own (with what it said about each) and which are the rules', and, on a phone where one outfit shows at a time, which one was in front of the owner as they wrote | "I don't like these shoes" only means something beside an outfit. The owner can also say "the second one". |
| The day's conversation is told to Claude with every later question that day: asking again, the weather moving, a changed occasion | "Too dressy" holds for the rest of the day, whatever else changes. It is not part of what an answer is kept for (the key), so going back to earlier choices is still answered from what is kept. |
| The conversation is the day's: tomorrow starts empty, and yesterday's messages are not told to Claude | Most of what is said is about one day ("too warm for this"). What lasts is kept on purpose, below, where the owner can read and change it. |
| What lasts: when a message shows something that holds on other days (a taste, a rule, how a piece fits), Claude may offer to keep it, at most two things an answer, each one sentence in the owner's voice. **Nothing is kept unless the owner taps Save.** A thing about their style becomes a new line of the style profile (Settings → Your style, marked as written by Claude in its history); a thing about a piece becomes a new line of its **How I wear it** | The owner asked for deep learning and for the rules to be theirs. The profile and the notes are what Claude reads every day, in words the owner can read, correct and delete; a hidden memory would be neither. Asking first keeps a passing remark from becoming a rule. This replaces the earlier plan that Claude "rewrites the part of the profile it bears on": adding a line the owner has seen is safer than rewriting their words. |
| Saving such a thing does not ask Claude again, though the profile and the notes are part of what an answer is kept for: the day's answers are moved to the new words | Claude offered it in the same answer in which it already acted on it, so the outfits on the page already follow it. Changing the profile by hand in Settings still asks again. |
| While a message is being answered, no other question is started: asking again and the weather moving keep what is on the page, and other choices (another occasion) wait on the message, then ask | Claude's words back ("Done: the boots instead") must never sit beside outfits from another answer, and the later question should be told the answer the owner got. |
| A message is refused with a sentence, and stays in the box, when Claude's outfits are not on the page, while another message is being answered, when Claude cannot be reached by either route, and at the day's limit. At most 500 characters | One message at a time keeps the thread in order and a double tap from costing twice. |
| Not done: ratings of worn outfits do not yet offer lines for the profile; a message cannot yet be sent about the rules' outfits when Claude's are not on the page | The first waits for the interview (step 4), which settles what the profile should hold; the second has nothing of Claude's to talk about yet. |

#### Style through conversation (step 8 of the order of work, 20 November)

The assessment (`docs/assessment/ux-settings.md`, section 5; `homestead-borrow.md`, section 7) and DESIGN principle
6: what Claude reads about how you dress is kept as lines you can read and remove, and what the app cannot learn from
the clothes it asks in conversation, one question at a time. Migration 0037, `closet/styleprofile.py`,
`closet/stylechat.py`, `closet/stylequestions.py`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The lines of Your style are rows (`style_lines`: the line as written, where it came from, when, its place, and the empty lines before it), not worked out from the versions of the text each time they are shown | A line needs an identity to be changed, removed and put back on its own, and two lines can say the same thing; working provenance out of a diff at every read would make "Remove" guess which line was meant. The versions are still the record: every change to the lines adds a version of the text built from them, so the history is kept as before and nothing that reads `style_profile` changes. |
| What the stylist reads is still the newest version of the text, and the migration does not touch it. The rows are carried over from it once, in the migration: each line that is not empty, with the empty lines before it counted, so the text built back from the rows is the same text | "What the stylist reads does not change for an existing closet." A closet carried over reads exactly what it read before; from the first change to a line on, the text is the lines in order. |
| Where a carried-over line came from is read from the history: the oldest version of the unbroken run of versions, up to the one in force, that has the line in it (the run breaks at a version without it, the empty "back to the starting text" one included). A line Claude wrote (`source` `claude`) is one kept from the conversation on Today; one of the two starting lines is marked as a starting rule | The versions already said who wrote each change and when; this is the date a line has stood in the profile since. A line that went away and came back is dated from its return. |
| Four sources, said in a few words under each line: a starting line ("Starting rule"), the owner's ("You said this on 12 Nov"), kept from a conversation ("From a conversation yesterday"), an answer to a question ("Your answer today"); the day in the words the wears use (`whenWorn`) | The owner reads why a line is there and whether it is still true; a line Claude offered is marked as such even after it was kept. A line the owner changes becomes theirs, from that day. |
| While the starting profile is in force it has no rows: its lines are shown from the program (ids -1, -2) and changing or removing one keeps them as rows first. Adding a line keeps the umbrella line and drops "Nothing else is written here yet", as a line kept from Today always did. With no lines left the starting profile is in force again; Undo of the first line added (from a conversation or a question) brings it back exactly | The starting lines can still be improved in an update for an owner who never wrote their own. The profile is never empty: an empty text was already the way back to the starting one. |
| A removed line is kept (`removed_at`) and can be put back where it was, with its source and date (Undo under the list) | DESIGN principle 9: what removes something can be undone. |
| **Edit the text** stays, beside **See all lines**, as the way to see and edit it whole. Saving it keeps each unchanged line's source and date; a new or changed line is the owner's | Some people would rather write a paragraph; the list must not lose where a line came from because the owner fixed a typo elsewhere. It stays on the card (not under Advanced) because it is how the owner checks exactly what Claude reads. |
| The style conversation is Today's conversation without the outfits, sharing its code: the same component on the page (`components/Talk.tsx`), the same way of asking (a row per message, answered on a thread, lost after 5 minutes), the same routes (the API key first), the same failure words less what they say about outfits, and the same `keep` and `unkeep` for a line offered. Its rows are a table of their own (`style_talk`) | A message about your style is not a question about the day's outfits: kept with them it would count against Today's 20, appear in Today's conversation and be told to Claude choosing outfits. Its own table keeps the two apart while the code that asks and keeps is one. |
| What Claude is told: the profile in force, the day's conversation, the message. No closet, no guide, no day, no tools, no streaming; low effort, an answer of at most 1,200 tokens, recorded with purpose `style_talk` under the monthly limit (estimated at 10,000 tokens in) | The assessment's "Do not copy": the cheap version. A message costs well under a cent through the API key; Claude talks about how the owner dresses, which needs the profile, not the clothes. |
| Claude may offer at most two lines a message, about the owner's style only, each one sentence in the owner's voice, kept only on **Save to Your style** and taken back with Undo. Undo was added to Today's offers too (`undo: true`), taking a piece's line back out of its note when it is still there as kept | DESIGN principle 9: Claude offers and the owner decides; and a tap kept by mistake should not need a trip to Settings. |
| A line kept from the style conversation is a change to the profile like one made by hand: Today asks Claude again the next time it is opened. A line kept from Today's conversation still moves the day's answers to the new words | Today's offer comes in an answer that already acted on it; a line kept in Settings has not been seen by the outfits on Today. |
| The conversation is the day's, as on Today: told to Claude with each new message, empty again tomorrow; at most 20 messages a day; refused, with a sentence shown before the owner writes, in the demo closet, with no route to Claude and at the day's limit | The same bounds as Today, for the same reasons. The demo never asks Claude. |
| Six questions the stylist cannot infer (`closet/stylequestions.py`), each with why it matters and where its answer is kept: running warm or cold, what you wear for work, colours you never wear, how dressed-up at weekends, how things should fit, anything you never wear. Asked one at a time in the style conversation while nothing has been said that day; answered with a tap on a choice (its own sentence) or in the owner's words (the question's lead-in and their words), or skipped; each with Undo. A skip is kept, so no question is asked twice | Homestead's `questions.py`. No call to Claude: the answer is the owner's own words, so it is saved at once as a line marked as an answer, which they can see and take back. The life questions' "style in a few words" was dropped in step 6; these replace it with lines the stylist reads. |
| Not done: Today does not ask the questions yet; ratings of worn outfits do not offer lines; the How you live form is not asked this way | Left for later (BACKLOG, "Style through conversation"). |

#### Today's date on a phone

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| On a phone Today's header shows the day as "Wed 25 Nov"; a computer keeps "Wednesday 25 Nov" | Found on Wednesday 18 November: the longest day name wrapped onto a second line beside **Worn** and **Trips** and pushed **I'm wearing this** under the tab bar, on Wednesdays only. A browser test now sets the page's clock to a Wednesday, so it is checked every day of the week. |

### One Add for clothes and shoes (17 November, asked for by the owner)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Add has no switch any more: the owner chooses, takes or drops any mix of photos, and Closet asks Claude what each shows (one piece, several pieces, a shoe, and which photos are the same shoe) before anything is added (`tagger/addsort.py`, `POST /api/add/sort`). Then each photo goes the way it always has: one piece through the resumable import, several through the pieces' boxes, a shoe with all its photos at once | The owner: "clothes/shoes import could all be combined and classified to the category it is". Nothing new happens to a photo after it is sorted, so the import, the boxes and the shoe's views stay as tried and tested. |
| When every photo is one piece, they go straight in; otherwise they wait in a list the owner can change (a choice by hand is kept if the list is sorted again) and **Add these** sends them. When Claude cannot be asked, and always in the demo closet, the list says so and the owner sorts by hand | The everyday case keeps the one tap it had. A shoe or a photo of several pieces is worth a look before it is cut up or grouped. |
| Up to ten photos go in one question, each at most 512 pixels on its longest side, on the `sorting` model (purpose `sort_add`). Shoes are grouped within a question only. More than 60 photos at once go in one piece per photo without being sorted | Enough to tell a shoe from a shirt and to match a shoe's sides, at a fraction of a cent. A folder import should not wait minutes before the first photo goes up. |
| **Take photos** opens the camera on the page, which stays open between photos, for anything (where the browser allows it: a secure address such as Tailscale's https one, or the computer itself; elsewhere the phone's camera app, one photo at a time). The photos are sorted once when the camera is closed, never while it is open, and nothing is added while it is open | The shoe screen had this camera; dropping it with the switch was a mistake, made on the wrong belief that the phone reaches Closet over plain http (it uses Tailscale's https address). Sorting each photo as it is taken would be slow and was turned down by the owner ("don't prompt live"). |
| A shoe is checked against the photos its drawing needs, one list on the server (`tagger/addsort.SHOE_VIEWS_NEEDED`: the outer side, the front and the inner side, from the drawing work of 17 November). The same sorting call says which side each shoe photo shows, and the tray shows each shoe as a checklist. A shoe missing one is still added, with a sentence saying what is missing | The owner: a shoe missing a photo "will be allowed in". One list keeps the page in step when the drawing needs other photos; without the front, the drawing skips its check of white leather against suede, and without the inner side it guesses that side from the outer one. |
| A shoe photo that shows the pair is held back: **Add these** waits until it is removed, or the owner taps **It's one shoe** when Closet is wrong | The owner: the drawing cannot take the second shoe out of a photo, so "enforce one shoe for now". |

### Laundry that keeps itself (17 November, approved by the owner)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| *(Changed 23 November: a wear for a day the piece has been washed since is not counted and puts nothing in the laundry; Stop C, the row on the count of wears.)* When a wear is recorded, a piece goes in the laundry by itself once its wears since the last wash reach its count in **Before it needs washing** (tops 1, knits 3, trousers 3; outer layers, shoes and accessories never). It uses the same setting as trip packing, by the trip's kinds of clothing | The owner: "maintaining what is in the laundry seems like a huge pain, but I realize it affects what outfits can be put together". One setting for how often a thing is worn before a wash, wherever it is used. |
| Everything in the laundry that went in before the start of the last laundry day (Sunday until the owner chooses another, or none) is clean again: its count starts again and it is offered again. Checked before each request reads the closet, at most one look per laundry date | Most people do laundry on a rhythm. Washing on the day itself counts from the next one, so this morning's tee is not marked clean before it is washed. |
| A wear undone takes a piece back out of the laundry only when that very wear put it in (its time in the laundry is the wear's). A piece the owner put in by hand stays where they put it | Undo should undo. A piece already at its count from older wears (the demo's history) still comes back out. |
| A piece in the laundry with no time of going in (the demo closet's, made that way) is never washed by the laundry day; migration 0030 dates what is in the laundry already from its last change | The demo shows a closet with some laundry in it, and an upgrade must not empty the owner's laundry basket at once. |
| On by default, for both closets; one switch in **Settings → Your closet → Laundry** turns it off, and then nothing goes in or comes out by itself | The owner asked for less upkeep; off, everything is as before. |

### Prompt caching on the API key (17 November)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Describing an item marks the form (the `describe_item` tool, sent first) for Anthropic's 5-minute prompt cache. A shoe's drawing, its checks and corrections mark the photos. One-off questions (sorting a shoe's photos, landmarks, the pieces in a photo, corrections by sentence, shopping) mark nothing | A write to the cache costs 1.25x input and a read 0.1x (0.05x on Sonnet 5.5 and Opus 5.5). A bulk import describes items back to back, and a drawing's checks resend its photos, so these read back what was written. A single question would only pay the write. |
| Spending counts cached tokens at their own prices (`cache_write`, `cache_read` in `tagger/models.json`). The estimate before a cached call prices its whole prompt as a cache write, the dearest way it can go | The API's `input_tokens` leaves the cached part out, so the monthly limit would otherwise undercount. An estimate must never be below what the call can cost. |

## Illustration

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Style B: bold outer outline, inner lines about a third of its weight, garment-coloured fill | Owner's choice. The demo closet is drawn in it. Styles A and C stayed in the bench for comparison only until 20 November, when they were removed (they are in the git history). |
| Template proportions are measured from reference drawings, not drawn from memory | The first shirt had a collar made of two separate shapes, a neck opening too narrow for a neck and a placket drawn over the collar. The owner supplied a flat drawing of a shirt; an independent review measured it (normalised to shoulder width) and the top template was rebuilt from those numbers. |
| A collar is one folded band that is part of the garment's outline | Seen from the front: a U-shaped opening with the band line across it, two leaves meeting in an X, and on button-front shirts the collar stand with the top button below the meeting point. Polo and zip collars get shorter leaves and no stand. |
| Seams are a separate layer under folded parts | The placket, yoke and armhole seams lie on the body, so the collar covers their tops. Details that sit on top (pockets, sleeve hems) stay in the upper layer. |
| An armhole seam is drawn only where it marks something (`templates.seam_shows`): a shoulder that is not the usual set-in one (dropped onto the arm, raglan, saddle), or a sleeve or yoke cut in a second colour, where the seam is the edge between the two colours (the snap-front fleece, a varsity jacket's sleeves, a western shirt's coloured yoke). An ordinary set-in sleeve has none, on a tailored jacket or coat too | The owner, 16 November: the outline already shows where an ordinary sleeve meets the body, and the seam ran across busy fronts (prints, plaids, pockets) on every top. Sleeves of a pattern cut at another angle show the join by themselves, so they get no line either. DRAWING_VERSION 17: saved items are drawn again the first time they are shown; the demo closet was regenerated (21 of its 22 tops changed; the fleece, whose sleeves are a second colour, kept its seam). Tests in `test_drawing.py` hold each case. |
| Collars and shirt cuffs are the body's colour | Tinting them made a white shirt look as if it had a grey collar. Rib cuffs and hems kept a slight tint until 22 November (see "A top's ribbing, a collar in the trim colour, and plain weave on a knit"). |
| The drawing keeps each garment's own details; the template only supplies consistent proportions | The owner's correction: the first schema tagged seven coarse shape fields and guessed everything else from the category, so every polo got two buttons and a henley looked like a long-sleeved tee. That was never agreed. Now the tagger reports a `details` block (how the front opens, button count and colour, pockets, hem, cuffs, cloth surface, contrast trim, waist, shoe style, sole and lace colour, feature flags) and the drawing follows what it saw, not the category. |
| Details the drawing cannot show yet are recorded, not dropped | `other_features` holds free-text distinctive details ("embroidered chest logo"). The app shows them beside the drawing as seen but not drawn, and they are the list the option vocabulary grows from. |
| The detail vocabulary is the owner's menswear taxonomy (`docs/menswear-taxonomy.json`), word for word | The owner supplied it after seeing the first ad-hoc detail fields. The tagger reports its visible axes (fit, sleeve length, neckline, collar, placket, cuff, sleeve construction, pockets, hem, fabric structure, colour texture, pattern and its orientation); back details and closure material, which cannot be seen from the front, are not asked for. `backend/closet/tagger/taxonomy.py` holds a frozen copy of those axes, kept equal to the JSON by a test, the mapping from each value to the drawing, and a coverage table saying whether each value is drawn exactly, approximately or not yet. |
| Weight and fibre are tagged and drawn | The owner's correction: they decide how a garment sits and what it can be layered over or under (a fleece shirt against a linen one, a thick wool jacket against a thin bomber, a wool flannel against a light cotton one). The tagger reports fibre, a weight class (lightweight, midweight, heavyweight, super_heavy), knit gauge and corduroy wale. The drawing shows weight as depth (see the next row), bulk and band depth, and fuzzy cloth as a soft edge. The outfit engine will use the weight class for layering rules (what fits under what) when it is built. |
| A thick cloth is drawn with depth, not a bolder line | The owner found that a heavier outline did not read as thicker cloth. A heavy or super-heavy garment now has a band of shade inside its lower and right edges, a soft shade beside every seam (the stitching sinks into the cloth), and a shade under the collar and pocket flaps (they stand proud of the body). The shade is always a darker tone of the cloth: a lighter one on dark cloth looked like gloss. The outline is only slightly bolder than a midweight one. Tried and dropped: showing the cloth's underside below the hem and cuffs, which read as a thicker outline. Light cloth has no positive sign of its own yet (drape wrinkles are the idea). |
| Hip patch pockets are in the taxonomy | The owner asked for `hip_patch_double` and `hip_flap_double` to be added to the pockets axis after a flannel overshirt in the test photos had no matching value. |
| Detail fields use "none", "na", "same" or 0, never null | The API limits how many nullable properties one answer schema may have; the coarse fields already use most of that allowance. |
| Cloth surfaces (corduroy, rib knit, cable knit, waffle) are drawn tone-on-tone over the colour | They are regular enough to draw, and a drawn surface stays crisp at any size. Irregular cloth (marl, plaid, floral) still comes from the photo as a tile. |
| Fits are calibrated from real garment measurements, not from a reference drawing | The owner found everything drawn slim. Checked: the regular fit had a chest of 0.85 of the shoulder width, where a real regular shirt laid flat has about 1.2 (56 cm pit to pit against 46 cm shoulders). The stock illustration used for the shirt was itself slim at the chest. Its collar, placket and details stay; body and sleeve proportions come from flat measurements of real garments. |
| Sleeves start at the edge of the body, never inside its width | The owner's first note on every shirt in the review gallery: "arms should be in line with the width of the shirt". The measurement-based recalibration had drawn the real, narrower shoulder seam and laid the sleeves over the sides of a wider chest, so the arms began inside the garment. The drawn shoulder point now sits just outside the chest's width, the armhole runs down the body's edge, and no sleeve overlaps the body. Body width and length still come from real measurements; the armhole is drawn deeper than life so the sleeve keeps its width. |
| Bodies are drawn at 84% of their flat width | Drawn at the full flat measurement (a 56 cm chest), the owner found every garment too wide: laid flat, the cloth that wraps round the body lies out to the sides. Lengths stay true. The share is one constant (`BODY_W`); the owner was shown 100%, 92%, 88% and 84% side by side and confirmed 84%. The owner's chest is about 39 inches, which is the size the measurements already assume (a medium: a shirt 56 cm across the chest laid flat). |
| Anchor points are points on the wearer, not on the garment | Neck base and the shoulders of a 46 cm frame, the same for every top. How far the cloth extends beyond them is the fit. |
| Tailored jackets have their own construction | A blazer drawn as a shirt body with lapels was rejected as narrow and wrong. Jackets are built from the measurements of a real two-button jacket: structured shoulder, suppressed waist, lapel gorge and roll to the top button, button stance, cutaway quarters, welt and flap or patch pockets. Built by a Copilot agent (#12) in its own function, so no other drawing changed; a test holds a fingerprint of every other drawing and fails if one changes by accident (when a drawing is changed on purpose, its fingerprint is recorded again in the same change). Jackets use the same 84% share of their flat width as every other top, so a jacket and the shirt under it stay in proportion. |
| Jacket details read the way a tailor counts them | Fixes to the faults left in the tailored jacket (#109). A double-breasted jacket tagged with one or two buttons is read as the buttons that fasten, so it is drawn as the usual 6x1 or 6x2 (six buttons in three rows; on a 6x1 the lapels roll to the bottom row); tagged with more, that is every button on it. A single-breasted jacket tagged with three buttons is a three-roll-two: two buttons below the lapel and the top buttonhole showing on the lapel's roll, its button hidden. A jacket's buttons are drawn at least 3:1 in contrast against the cloth (the level the web accessibility guidelines set for large shapes), lightened or darkened as little as needed; on black or navy, where a darker shade would not show, the jacket's inside is drawn lighter than the cloth instead. Sleeves hang at most 19 degrees from the side with a rounded underarm, so the two outlines do not run into a dark wedge; lapels broaden with a fuller fit (0.4 cm for each cm of chest it adds). |
| An overcoat is drawn from the measured overcoat at knee length, with no breast pocket (#181) | The tagger's coarse length stops at "long", and every tailored top at that length was cut from the jacket, so an overcoat (and the demo's black overcoat) drew as a blazer with a breast welt that read as a pocket square. Both the drawing and the demo item were at fault. Told the kind is an overcoat, the drawing is now knee length and cut from the coat's own measurements (its wider collar and lapels, lower pockets, no darts), and the coat drops the breast welt. The demo's overcoat also says it is one (`garment`: `overcoat`, which it had left `unknown`) and its own drawing is made with the same parameters, so the closet, the item and the outfit as worn agree. A robe, drawn on the same coat, loses its breast welt too; a peacoat and a trench coat are unchanged. `DRAWING_VERSION` is 10. |
| Garments that open all the way down can be drawn open | The owner expects jackets, cardigans and overshirts to be shown both buttoned and open over a shirt. Composing an outfit is the outfit view (stop C), but the drawing has to support it, so the open state and a proof that layers line up on the shared anchors are built in track 1. In the closet grid an item is shown closed by default. |
| A garment worn open is drawn as the layers it is made of | The first version cut the closed drawing down the middle and slid the halves apart. The owner rejected it: the under front was missing the strip hidden beneath the overlap, only one front had fastenings, a wide coat could not open, and a shirt underneath was cut off at the hem. Now the garment is a back, two fronts and what is sewn to them. Both fronts are the same size: each reaches past the centre by the overlap, and the part of the under front that the closed drawing hides is the mirror image of the top front's. The front that lies on top (the wearer's left, as on men's clothes) carries the buttonholes, vertical on a shirt or cardigan and horizontal on a jacket or coat; the one underneath carries the buttons. Each front swings open about its side seam like a door, so it is drawn narrower, as a turned panel looks, while sleeves and sides stay put: the garment keeps its width and its shoulder points, and a double-breasted coat opens as far as a shirt. The inside of the back shows between the fronts and inside the neck. Worn over another garment the order is: the outer garment's inside back, the whole garment underneath, the outer fronts and sleeves; a long tee hangs below a short jacket and a shirt collar stands above a jacket's. Each garment only states where its top front's edge runs; the renderer does the rest, so every garment that opens gets this. Open or closed is a choice made when wearing, not a tag on the item. |
| Reference drawings may be downloaded from Wikimedia Commons, Openclipart and SVG Repo | Added to the `daily-clother` environment at the owner's prompting. They are looked at and measured, never copied into the repo. Commons rate-limits hard: one request, then wait. |
| A shoe's upper material is a surface laid over the whole shoe, not a different shape (#184) | Suede, nubuck, roughout, pebble grain and felt are fine marks in the item's own colour; canvas, knit mesh, woven leather and velvet are small repeating tiles; calf, cordovan and pull-up are a soft gloss or paler patches; rubber, waxed canvas and velvet add a sheen. On the figure's front-view shoes every one of these follows the shoe as a solid: a sheen is a highlight along the toe's crown and down the lit side of a boot's shaft (not a flat band across the drawing), and a moulded slide's sole is a shade off its strap so its depth reads; nothing is drawn outside the shoe's outline, so a hem still falls over it. The shape still comes from the kind of shoe, so an upper never changes how a shoe is cut. Synthetic leather stays approximate: drawn it looks like smooth calf, and nothing in a drawing tells them apart. |
| A knee-high boot stays approximate | Every shoe on the card is drawn at one scale, so that a boot and a sneaker look their true sizes side by side. A real knee boot at that scale does not fit the card: it is drawn at the tallest shaft that does, about a shoe length high. |
| A colourway is drawn as the colour measured from the photo | The colourway (oxblood, sand suede, white, cream with a gum sole) is the shoe's colour, and the drawing already paints the measured colour; a suede colourway gets the suede surface when the upper is tagged suede. A "multi" colourway stays approximate: the drawing shows the main colour and one trim colour, not every colour. |
| A shoe's construction shows round the sole from the side (#184) | A Goodyear welt is a stitch row; a storm welt adds a darker bead above it; Blake and cemented shoes show none; a stitchdown shows the upper's flange, stitched, on top of the sole. From the front only the toe, laces, straps and sole thickness change. |

### The outfit as worn

`backend/closet/drawing/figure.py` (`figure`, `plan`), served by `POST /api/outfits/figure`. It draws the existing item drawings, fitted to a drawn person (`drawing/fitting.py`).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| One front-view figure worn by a person drawn in the garments' own flat style (same line weight, flat fills): head, neck, arms, hands, legs and feet, an adult man about 7.5 heads tall standing front-on, the arms a little away from the body. The body is drawn first, behind every garment, from the same anchors (neck, shoulders, waist, hem, ground), so skin shows only where nothing covers it: face and neck, hands below the cuffs, forearms below short sleeves, legs below shorts, ankles in low shoes, feet in sandals. Legs are drawn only with a bottom, slim enough to stay inside every trouser leg; an arm follows the outermost sleeve (straight to a long cuff, bent under a short one) | The owner corrected #175: a mannequin looks like plastic; they want a person, with the parts today's figure lacked. Drawn behind the clothes, nothing of the body can show over a garment, and the templates did not change. With an open shirt or jacket the chest shows in the opening. |
| The skin is one mid tone (`SKIN = #c68e6a` in `drawing/figure.py`), with its outline and the toe lines shaded from it; every skin part reads it from there | So a skin tone choice can be added to Settings later without touching the drawing; it is not built yet. |
| The head has a simple face: dot eyes, brows, a hint of nose and mouth. There is no other head | The owner was shown a face and a plain head side by side and chose the face. |
