| The body sets the proportions: an adult man about 7.5 heads tall (a 24 cm head, 180 cm to the ground), an average, healthy build rather than a slim fashion figure: the shoulder points 43 cm apart (2.7 head widths of his 16 cm head; 47 cm across the arms' round tops), the hips 31.6 cm (2 head widths), so he is clearly broader at the shoulders than the hips (1.36 times), with a chest 30 cm across between the arms; the neck 9.6 cm wide (six tenths of the head) and flaring into shoulders that slope about 20° down to the shoulder points; the hands as long as his face from chin to hairline at the temples. The arms hang as a standing man's do: from the shoulder joint the upper arm goes 5° out from the body to the elbow at the waist (11° until 18 November: see "His arms, and sleeves in front of a jacket worn open"), the forearm straight down from there (so the hands clear the hips), the arm tapers from 10 cm across at the shoulder to 5.6 cm at the wrist, the wrists are level with the crotch (2 cm below it) and the fingertips reach mid-thigh. Every garment is fitted to him in the figure only: the shape its template builds is moved point by point (`fitting.warp`) and drawn with `illustrate._draw(..., shape=)`. A top's shoulder seam lands on his shoulder point; a long sleeve ends round the wrist (a jacket's or coat's 1.5 cm past it), a shorter sleeve as far down the arm as on its flat drawing; every sleeve goes round the arm and bends at the elbow as the arm does; its width is the arm's and some ease either side, by the top's fit and not by the flat drawing's sleeve (at the upper arm 0.7 cm for a slim top, 1.2 cm for a regular one, 1.8 cm for a jacket, chore coat or heavy overshirt, 2.3 cm for a coat or a jacket worn as the outer layer, 2.8 cm for an oversized one), narrowing to the cuff (0.6, 0.65, 1.0, 1.3 and 1.0 cm) (its straight edges are cut into short pieces first, so they can bend), and starts at an underarm 55 cm below the crown, higher than a flat drawing's deep armhole; where a sleeve hangs within 2.4 cm of the body's side its inner edge is drawn on the body's edge, so the two share one line; a regular top ends at the hip and a knee coat at the knee (a cropped top still over the waistband); trousers have their waist on his waist and their hips drawn over his (scaled across from the waist down to the hips, so the flat drawing's wider hips do not make him look wider there than at the shoulders), the crotch at his crotch and a full-length hem on the shoe. Fit and length tags apply relative to him: a fuller fit is drawn fuller again by as much as it is fuller on its flat drawing, and its wider shoulder falls over the top of the arm; a slimmer one hugs, but no top is narrower than he is (never closer than 1 cm to his sides or 1.2 cm over the hips, 1.8 cm for a regular top; a regular or slimmer top narrows from the chest to that, a fuller one or a knee coat hangs straight; an oversized top with a ribbed hem and cuffs has a dropped shoulder, roomy sleeves that its cuffs gather in at the wrist and a body that falls straight to the hem band, which draws it in; a hood worn down lies on the shoulders behind the neck, and shows over a layer worn on top), wide legs stay wide, shorts end by their length | The garment drawings are fashion flats, drawn longer in the sleeve and wider in the body than they look worn; placed unchanged on the figure (as they were at first), the sleeves decided where the arms ended and the head looked small. The owner asked for the body to be the reference. The flat drawings (closet tiles, the item page, the coverage sheets) stay byte-for-byte the same, which a test checks, so `DRAWING_VERSION` is unchanged. How much fuller an oversized top is drawn on him (`FIT_GAIN`) and how much ease a regular one has are choices made by eye from the pictures. The owner then found him too narrow (shoulders barely wider than the hips, a regular shirt hanging loose) and the hands too big, and asked for an average build. That one (shoulders 2.3 head widths) still read as thin, with a neck as wide as the head and arms that hung like sticks from a flat shoulder line, so the owner asked for the build and the arms above; the numbers are ordinary measurements of a 180 cm man, then judged by eye from the pictures. The owner then asked for a neck six tenths of the head's width and, between two pictures, for the more natural arms: 14.5° out with a strip of background between sleeve and body made the elbows stand out, so the arms hang 11° out and a sleeve against the body shares its line (two outlines side by side made one heavy line and a dark wedge at the armpit). A bare arm cannot share a line with a tee's side, so beside a tee there is still a narrow gap. The forearm is straight, not turned in, because the body is drawn behind the clothes and a hand further in would be hidden by wide shorts. A bare foot (in a sandal, or with no shoes) is drawn from the front as one shape with the leg: the ankle narrowest, the foot widening to the ball, the toes a soft row of five with only faint lines between them; a sandal is drawn sole first, then the foot, then the straps cut off at the foot's edge, on a footbed that leans to the big toe as a foot does. The owner then found the sleeves of regular shirts nearly as full as the oversized hoodie's: their width came from the flat drawing, and a sleeve sharing the body's line also filled the space between the arm and a body drawn in at the waist. So a sleeve's width is now the arm's and an ease chosen by fit, and a top's body is no longer drawn in at the waist (its underarm is a little wider instead), which leaves less between arm and body for the sleeve to fill. The layers under a top are drawn once for each place they may show (inside its outline, below its hem, above its collar), because a browser may leave out where two shapes of one clip path overlap. On Today the figure's panel is the drawing's own background (`CARD`) edge to edge in both themes, so in dark theme it is not a lighter box inside the card. |
| Each piece is drawn from exactly what its own item drawing is drawn from (`illustrations.describe`): the tagger's answer with the owner's corrections, else the category and fields, in the item's colour | The figure must show the same garment as the closet. Patterns and photo tiles are not used (solid colour), so the figure is the same whether or not the photo is there. |
| **Changed 23 November** (next row): A mid or outer layer is worn open when its front opens all the way down (buttons, French placket, hidden placket, double-breasted, zip) and the template can open it; otherwise closed. The base top is always closed | That is how they are worn: a jacket, overshirt or cardigan open over what is under it; a pullover, hoodie or polo over a tee cannot be. Open uses the existing `layer` order (the outer garment's inside back, the whole garment underneath, the fronts and sleeves). Three layers nest the same way, from the inside out. |
| A mid or outer layer is worn open when its front opens all the way down (buttons, French placket, hidden placket, double-breasted, zip) and the template can open it; otherwise closed. The base top is always closed. But under an outer layer worn open, a mid layer that fastens is worn fastened (a cardigan buttoned, a zip hoodie zipped, a waistcoat or knit vest buttoned, a chore jacket or a blazer buttoned under an open overcoat), unless it is a shirt (`OPEN_UNDER`): an overshirt or a flannel shirt over a tee stays open, its fronts hanging nearer the middle (`MID_APART`, 0.14 of half its width from the middle, against 0.21 worn on its own) and the outer layer's further out (`OUTER_APART`, 0.42). The code is `figure._worn_closed` and `Shape.apart` | Changed by the orchestrating session on 23 November, at the owner's word ("it's your codebase to edit"), after the backlog's fault: with both worn open, the outer layer's fronts lay exactly over the mid layer's, so a tee, an open cardigan and an open jacket read as a tee and a jacket with a strip of cardigan at the hem, and a blazer under an overcoat did not show at all. That is not how they are worn: under an open coat a cardigan or a blazer is buttoned, and then its front fills the V between the coat's fronts with its own neckline, placket and buttons over the shirt or tee, and a hood lies out over the coat's collar behind the neck. A shirt worn as an overshirt is the exception people really wear open under a jacket; drawn closed it would read as a second shirt over the first. Open inside an open coat, the coat rests on its fronts with more ease, so it opens further and the shirt shows as a band inside each of its fronts, with the tee between. Drawn as a picture, the mid layer now shows at least 150 cm² on him inside the outer layer (about a hand; the most it showed before was about 107 cm², under a jacket nothing), which `test_figure_layers.py` holds. On Today a cardigan, chore jacket or blazer you wear under an open coat is now drawn buttoned; on its own over a tee it is still drawn open. Dressed a layer at a time, it is drawn open with the base top and then buttoned when the coat comes on, because each layer is the whole outfit up to it |
| A closed layer shows only what would really show of what is under it: inside its own outline nothing; above its neckline a band as wide as a collar; below its hem as wide as its body; and through its own neck opening (a shirt collar in a V-neck) | A shirt collar above a crew neck and a shirt hem below a short sweater show; a tee's sleeves do not poke through a sweater's. When a layer underneath has longer sleeves (a long-sleeved shirt under a short-sleeved overshirt or a vest) nothing of it is hidden, so its sleeves show. |
| The body, the shoes (a 29.5 cm shoe) and the accessories are drawn at 3.3 px per cm, the tops' own scale | Tops and trousers are fitted to the body, so their own scale no longer sets the figure's size. |
| Every pair of shoes stands on the wearer's ground, whatever the bottom. Seen a little from above, the toes are nearer than the legs, so the toe tips are drawn 7 cm lower. Shorts and cropped trousers end where they end, and the shoes stay on the ground under the wearer's legs | So shorts look short. |
| The trouser hem is placed on the shoe in 3D (#223, `figure._hem`, `_on_shoe`), from the leg opening against the shoe round its opening (`FrontShoe.width`: a low shoe's collar, a boot's shaft). A leg whose flat opening is less than 2.35 times that width (`OVER_SHOE`) cannot go over it and rests on top: its front edge just in front of a low shoe's opening, or on top of a boot's shaft, dipping 1.2 cm over it (`REST_DIP_CM`); on a shaft it stacks one step more and bunches out to the shaft's width plus 0.5 cm (`BUNCH_EASE_CM`), so the shaft's opening never shows. A wider leg drops over the shoe, but its front is held up at the instep or the top of the shaft and only its sides fall lower (up to `ARCH_CM`, 3 cm). The break sets how far the front edge is past the opening (`HEM_FRONT_CM`: no break 0.3 cm, slight 0.8, full 1.3); cropped and ankle lengths stop at least 3 cm above the back of the opening (`CROP_GAP_CM`), the ankle or sock showing. A hem is never lower than its length takes it; with no shoe it rests 5 cm above the ground, dipping 2 cm. Only a leg that drops over the shoe hides the shoe beside it. The flat-lay drawings do not change. `DRAWING_VERSION` is 15 | The owner found trousers swallowing most of the shoe, and boots whole. The front of a real hem rests on the top of the foot by the shoe's throat; a slim or straight leg sits on a boot's thick shaft and bunches above it; only wide and bootcut legs drop over it, and even then the toe box and the front of the sole show. 2.35 is between a relaxed leg (21.5 cm flat, 2.29 times a boot's 9.4 cm shaft) and a bootcut one (22.5 cm, 2.39 times), so on boots only bootcut, wide and flared legs drop over. It is close to a slim leg over a dress shoe (2.41), which drops over it, as a slim trouser does over a narrow oxford. |
| The shoes are drawn from the front (`drawing/frontshoe.py`), not from the item's side view: a small solid (a footprint-shaped sole and an upper rising from the toe to the collar, with a shaft for a boot or a high top) built from the same style words as the side view, turned 11° toe out and seen from 24° above. Each ankle is centred under the middle of its trouser leg's opening, or under the wearer's legs with shorts, and the toes turn out from it; the other foot is its mirror image. A trainer (sneaker, runner) has a two-tone sole edge, a stitched overlay round the toe box and laces down to the toe box, so it reads as a trainer when the hem covers its top. The wearer's ankle rises out of each shoe and the leg ends in it; in a sandal or flip-flop a bare foot is drawn on the footbed, and with no shoes the feet are bare. Under full-length trousers nothing of a shoe is drawn beside the leg above the hem, so a boot shows only its lower part | The owner found the side views splayed outward and not under the legs. A front view shows the toe box and the front of the sole, and sits under the hem as a worn shoe does. Sneakers, runners, slip-ons, high tops, loafers, derbies, oxfords, boots, chelsea boots, sandals, slides and flip-flops each have their own shape and details. The item's own drawing is unchanged. |
| Back to front: the body, shoes, trousers, then the tops from the inside out, then the neck and head | The body is under everything; the head goes over the collars; trouser hems fall over the shoes; tops hang over the waistband. |
| The depth model (#193): every garment is a shell round a 3D body, seen from the front and flattened, and the figure is painted in depth layers, back to front: (1) the back of garments where it shows (an open layer's inside back, a hood, the back of a scarf's loop); (2) the body, shoes and trousers; (3) the tops from the inside out, each whole; (4) the arms and hands again (`figure._arms_over`), from below the lowest sleeve opening on each side and masked out of every sleeve, so they are over what of a loose body stands out past his sides and under every sleeve; each top's sleeves are drawn again over its own body (`_sleeves_over`), as tubes round the arm, a wide opening showing a little dark inside at its lower edge (`TUBE_INSIDE_CM`); (5) the neck and head; (6) a bag's straps, then a wrapped scarf's ends and band, then the hat. A sleeve's inner edge is no longer moved out onto a loose body's side: it stays a tube round the arm, inside the body's outline | The owner: "think of these items as if they were 3D models flattened". An oversized tee was a wide box over the arms, hiding them; a real one's loose sides are at his side and back, behind the arms, and its sleeves are tubes the arms come out of. Repainting the arms (rather than splitting each garment's outline at the arm line) keeps every template, anchor point and flat drawing as it was: the arm is the only thing that moves forward, and the sleeve mask keeps the sleeves over it. Fitted tops look as before, as their sides barely pass his. Above the sleeve opening the top's shoulders stay over the arm. |
| A low shoe on the figure (low sneaker, runner, slip-on, loafer, derby, oxford) is painted back to front too: the shoe whole, with the inside of its collar, then the ankle or sock over it wherever it is in the opening or above the back half of the collar (`frontshoe._opening`, up to `BEHIND_CM` above it), then the shoe's details. Only the collar's sides, curving round the ankle, and the front of the shoe (vamp, laces, toe) are in front of the ankle. Boots and sandals are unchanged | The owner found a grey pointed tab (the top of the heel counter) drawn in the middle of the ankle on every low shoe. Seen from the front and a little above, the back of the collar is behind the ankle. Checked on `docs/screenshots/shoes-depth-{before,after}.png` (`tools/make_shoe_sheet.py`, removed 20 November; in the git history): every low style with a bare ankle, a no-show sock, a crew sock, and trousers short of the shoe or resting on it. |
| A low sneaker, runner or slip-on cut no-show (`footwear_height` no_show, `cut` low) has, seen from the front, its top line behind the throat 2 cm lower (`NO_SHOW_CM`, easing back to the style's line at the tongue) and its opening dipping 0.6 cm more at its sides (`NO_SHOW_DIP_CM`); the rounded collar and the depth order are the low shoe's (above), and a trouser hem meets the lower opening (its `opening` and `opening_back` anchors move down with it) | A no-show cut's collar sits well under the ankle bones, so more of the ankle (or the hidden no-show sock's place) shows above it; before, the front view ignored the cut and only the side view showed it. Checked on `docs/screenshots/no-show-cut-{before,after}-{phone,2x}.png`. |
| Shorts tagged with a rule pocket (`trouser_pockets` rule_pocket) draw it on the wearer's right leg as on trousers (as wide, the same height when the leg allows, following the flared outer seam), kept 3 cm clear of the hem and started higher over the thigh on a short inseam | Before, only full-length trousers drew it, so carpenter shorts lost their one distinctive pocket. Checked on `docs/screenshots/shorts-rule-pocket-{before,after}-{phone,2x}.png`. `DRAWING_VERSION` 16 (#226 took 15). |
| Between an open layer's fronts is what is in front of its back: the wearer is drawn again inside the layer's outline (his body, shoes and trousers), then the layers under it, then its fronts; its lining shows only at the edges, past him | The owner found a flat grey strip running down to an open overcoat's hem below the sweater: the coat's inside back, shown where in 3D the trousers and the sweater's hem are. |
| A long or longline coat reaches just above the knee (`HEM_AT["above_knee"]`, 6 cm above it; the item page says "just above the knee"), an overcoat (knee length) the knee; a long jacket mid-thigh | The owner found the "longline coat" only reaching the hip. |
| A wrapped scarf (knit, woven, cashmere, silk) is drawn on the figure in four layers (`templates.SCARF_LAYERS`, from `templates._worn_scarf`): the part going round the back of the neck, a little higher than the front and darker inside, drawn before the neck and head; the under end; the band across the throat, resting on the base of the neck and the top of the collar, sagging a little at the front; the over end, which comes over the band at the crossing with a fold. The ends hang from where the scarf crosses, a little off the middle, overlapping and narrowing evenly down to about the belt, one a little shorter (a fringe on a woven one, ribs and a ribbed end on a knit one); a silk one is narrower, tied in a soft knot, its pointed ends short and close. A backpack's chest strap sits a hand below the collarbones and is drawn under the ends. The scarf's own drawing is unchanged; a bandana and a neck gaiter stay as they were | The owner found the scarf a flat ring with a hole (the chin and collars showing through), too high and thick, with two stiff parallel ends from the ring's sides, and a rucksack's chest strap across it; then that the ends were short and stubby, reading like a turtleneck collar at phone size. The sizes were chosen by eye from renders of the Cold outfit with the coat open, closed and absent. |
| A rain shell's hood is drawn down (`hood: "down"`, set by the rain shell kind in `taxonomy._KIND_TOP`): a low shell sewn to the collar, lying behind the neck on the shoulders, its inside a crescent just above the collar seam; on the figure it rises less above the shoulders than a hoodie's hood. A hoodie's and a parka's hoods are unchanged. `DRAWING_VERSION` is 13 | The owner found the rain shell's hood floating above the jacket in its flat drawing: it was the hoodie's standing hood. A rain shell's hood is mostly worn packed down. Only the rain shell's drawing changes (a test checks the others and the scarves are byte-for-byte the same). |
| The figure has its own canvas: everything drawn, plus 18 px on every side | A knee-length coat, shorts or boots each change the outline, so no fixed card fits all. The Today screen scales it to fit. |
| It never fails: a missing place is not drawn; an item in the wrong place (shoes as the bottom) is skipped; a piece that cannot be drawn from its description is drawn as the plainest of its category; if nothing can be drawn, an empty card | An outfit with one odd item should still be shown. |
| A sheet of figures from the demo closet is written by `tools/make_figure_sheet.py`, now into the git-ignored `tools/out/` (the copy kept earlier is `docs/screenshots/outfit-figures.png` and `.svg`); five outfits on a phone-sized Today card, light and dark, were written by `tools/make_figure_shots.py NAME` as `docs/screenshots/figure-<outfit>-<theme>-NAME.png` (the tool was removed on 20 November; it is in the git history) | So a change to the figure can be judged by looking, before and after, as the issues asked. |
| The kind of shoe (a footwear id such as `tassel_loafer` or `cap_toe_oxford`, or a footwear word such as `wingtip`, `open_derby` or "5 eyelets") comes from the words the tagger writes in `other_features`, read by `taxonomy.shoe_words`; no schema field was added. A footwear id sets the shoe style (a brogue keeps a tagged derby lacing, otherwise oxford); its details refine it. Defaults: a loafer has a penny strap, a sandal two straps, a dress shoe five eyelets. Words the drawing shows leave the item's "seen but not drawn" list | The issue asked for no schema change, and a schema change makes every stored answer stale. The taxonomy's 47 footwear ids are listed in `COVERAGE` as drawn, approximate (drawn as the closest shape, the note says which) or not drawn. |
| A bottom's kind (`chinos`, `dress_trousers`, `jeans`, `corduroys`, `fatigues`, `cargo_pants`, `work_pants`, `shorts`) gives it that kind's usual look (`taxonomy.BOTTOM_KINDS`: a crease, rivets, cargo pockets, a cloth) as the weakest layer; anything the tagger saw in the details overrides it. Outerwear kinds do the same for tops (a trench's self belt and gabardine, a parka's hood and length, a varsity jacket's rib trims and contrast sleeves, a waxed jacket's cord collar, a robe's sash) | A kind says more than plain details about how the garment usually looks, but the photo outranks the usual. A value moves to "drawn" only when it reads at item size on the render sheet (`tools/make_coverage_sheet.py`, removed 20 November; in the git history); one that can only be suggested stays "approximate" with a note (fatigues' back-sateen cloth, drawn as a twill: a sateen sheen made them look glossy). Reverse and forward pleats are drawn as the fold with a shade on the side it opens to (a `shade` role: a darker tone of the cloth with no edge), which reads at item size; at grid size it is only a darker speck. An apron toe is "drawn" as the moc seam it is from the side. |
| What is on the back of trousers and shorts (back pockets, a jeans yoke, a leather label, darts, a V belt loop, honeycombs) is drawn in a small second drawing from behind, shown under the drawing on the item page only (`pants(view="back")`, `GET /api/items/{id}/back.svg`). It keeps the front drawing's outline and anchors and leaves out what is on the front; it is made only when something on the back is known (told, or a kind's usual back pockets: welt on chinos and corduroys, jetted on dress trousers, flap on fatigues and cargo pants, patch on work pants, a yoke and patch pockets on jeans) | The closet grid, the outfit figure and the drawing itself are front-on, and turning them would hide the fly, pockets and pleats that say more. Leaving the back unmarked made half the bottoms vocabulary "not drawn"; an inset over the drawing would cover a wide leg. The back view is not stored: it is drawn from the same answer when the item is opened, which is quick. |
| Accessories are placed by the point their template is drawn about: a belt round the trousers' waistband, the first top then tucked in under it (and under any layer worn over it); a scarf at the neck point, over every top; a hat on his head, in its worn form (a cap's peak pointing at us, a brim's back behind the crown), its band at a height per style (`worn.HAT_BAND`); a bag carried as such a bag is (a briefcase, duffel or dopp kit in his right hand, the hand drawn again round the handle; a tote's handles over the shoulder; a messenger or sling strap across the chest; a belt bag round the waist; a backpack's straps over the shoulders and behind the arms), placed by `worn.place`, which `figure._place_accessory` asks first (with no wearer, the old places: a hat above the neck point, a bag beside the figure). Every accessory on the figure is checked as a 3D object flattened: would it sit like this on a real person. One of each is drawn, the first given; the small accessories are worn by `worn.py` (see Accessories); gloves, jewellery and the rest are not drawn on the figure | A belt over an untucked top would hide it; a belt is worn to be seen. With no head drawn, a hat floats where the head would be. The sizes and heights were chosen by eye from the sheet `docs/screenshots/accessories.png` (written by `tools/make_accessory_sheet.py`, removed 20 November; in the git history), and someone could reasonably choose them differently. |
| How a garment sits on him is set by its fit word (#179), the fit and length tags read in this order: the owner's details, the tagger's details, its silhouette; anything not told is drawn regular, at standard length. Ease: a slim or tailored top is shaped at the waist (its sides 2.4 cm off his at the underarm, 0.4 cm at the waist, 1.2 cm at the hips) with narrow sleeves (0.45 cm off the arm); a regular one falls straight from the chest with its sides 4.5 cm off his chest all the way down (`FIT_ROOM_CM`, 6.5 cm for relaxed), so slim and regular read apart at phone size (#193); a relaxed or boxy one falls straight from the shoulder with fuller sleeves (boxy wider again, and 4 cm shorter); an oversized one has a dropped shoulder, wide short sleeves to just above the elbow (`ROOMY_SHORT_CM`, the forearm showing below) and a body 6 cm longer. Trouser legs are scaled about their own middle from none at the crotch to a factor at the knee and at the hem by the leg shape (`LEG_EASE`), taken from the trousers template's own knee and opening widths for that shape (`templates.LEG_WIDTHS`, #188's leg shapes) against a straight leg's, to the power 0.35 at the knee and 0.55 at the hem: skinny, slim and tapered narrow to the hem, straight falls straight, relaxed and wide widen, bootcut and flare open out below the knee, barrel bows out and comes in; no leg is narrowed to less than 4.6 cm either side of its middle, room for his ankle | The owner wanted each garment's fit visible: longer, baggier, slimmer, tighter. The numbers were chosen by eye from the pictures at 390×844 (`tools/make_fit_shots.py`, removed 20 November; in the git history), so that the widths grow in order slim < regular < relaxed < oversized (a test checks). Shaping a slim top shows less under long sleeves, which cover its sides, than on a tee. |
| A top's hem lands at a body landmark by its length (`HEM_AT`, `reaches`): cropped just below the waist (still over the waistband, so no skin shows), regular at the hip, longline below the hip over the seat; a jacket or coat ends cropped at the waist, regular at the hip, long at mid-thigh (a coat just above the knee), knee length at the knee. A sleeve ends by `sleeve_length` (`SLEEVE_ENDS`): short halfway down the upper arm, elbow at the elbow, three-quarter halfway down the forearm, long round the wrist; sleeveless has none. Full-length trousers end on the shoe with the break; cropped (ankle) ones 10 cm higher, so the ankle shows; shorts end by their length | Before, a hem landed as far down as on its flat drawing, scaled, so a "longline" tee and a regular one ended almost alike. Landmarks make the length words mean the same on every garment. |
| The sleeve's outer edge rounds from the shoulder's slope onto the arm over 7 cm (a curve, not a corner); a sleeve sharing the body's line leaves it gradually, so no long dark sliver opens between them at a slim top's waist; under a jacket a hood shows as one smooth mound at least as wide as the neck | The owner found the sleeve tops slightly square, a dark sliver at a slim shirt's waist and a lumpy hood under a jacket. |
| No fold, drape or crease lines yet | The owner wanted to see fit and length first: extra lines may not suit the flat style. If wanted later they would be a layer of their own drawn over each fitted garment in `figure._draw`, chosen by the fit word and the ease already worked out there (and the taxonomy's drape), so the outlines stay as they are. |
| The item page says how an item sits in one short line under its name, such as "Regular fit · hip length · short sleeves" (`as_worn` in the item's JSON, worded by `labels.wornWords`); a word whose tag is unknown is left out. Each word opens "Fix the details" at the detail that changes it: the fit and the top's length at the cut (`fit_detail`, which has cropped and longline), the sleeves at the sleeve length, a trouser leg at the leg shape, a trousers' length at the break (shorts at the inseam). A correction redraws the figure, which is drawn from the same corrected tags | The owner can see what the drawing believes and correct it in one tap. The top's length links to the cut rather than to the silhouette's length, which the owner cannot pick; someone could reasonably link it elsewhere. |

#### His arms, and sleeves in front of a jacket worn open (18 November)

The owner found that he still looked "hunched", "arms in front of the body", and that a jacket showed "a bunch of
overlapping lines in the arms". A look at the figure (on Fable) found three causes: the upper arm went 11° out, so he
was widest at the elbows; the arm started inside the chest; and a sleeve's inner edge, with its full ease from the
underarm down, ran diagonally across the front.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The shoulder joint is 2 cm in from the shoulder point (was 3.3) and the upper arm hangs 5° out (was 11°), the forearm straight down: the arm falls nearly straight beside the chest. A sleeve's inner edge has no ease at the underarm (the cloth is held between the arm and his side) and comes to its full ease 14 cm down (`UNDERARM_EASE_CM`) | Seen before and after on a tee, shirts, a sweater, a hoodie, a coat, an open jacket and outfits with a bag: the arms read as hanging at his sides. The owner accepted that the hands now touch wide trousers and shorts (1 to 2 cm over them) rather than being held out clear |
| The arms are in front of a jacket or coat worn open: its sleeves are drawn again over its fronts, and on a sleeve only what lies on it (`_sleeve_only`: a cuff, the buttons at the wrist), never the body behind it (a pocket's corner, the hem, a front's edge) | The owner's choice: "the arms go in front". Before, the fronts were drawn over the sleeves, so a pocket and the body's side ran across the arm beside the sleeve's own edge |
| On the figure a sleeve's armhole is a thin seam (`SEAM_W`) only where the flat drawing has one (`templates.seam_shows`: a dropped, raglan or saddle shoulder, or a sleeve in another colour), and none on an ordinary set-in sleeve. Its cloth is drawn a little past the armhole (`_past_armhole`), so an edge under it (an open front's) does not show | The owner's rule of 16 November ("An armhole seam is drawn only where it marks something"). A seam on every top was tried and joined the sleeve's inner edge to the shoulder, but it went against that rule, so the inner edge still starts at the underarm on a plain set-in sleeve; drawing it there too would be the owner's call |

On 19 November the owner found the arms still covered the front pockets of an oversized jacket ("in reality arms would
not be covering front jacket pockets"). A look at the figure (on Fable) found the sleeves' inner edge 5 cm over the
lower pockets, a roomy body drawn wider than his shoulders and hidden behind the sleeves, the open fronts swung about
that hidden side (which pushed the pockets under the sleeves), and a dropped shoulder that read as padded: a corner at
his shoulder point, then the sleeve puffing out past the seam. A first fix held the arms out over every roomy top; a
second look (on Fable, which the owner asked to judge the design) found that over-corrected: soft cloth does not push
the arms out, and an oversized tee or hoodie read as a wider man holding his arms away from his body.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Only a stiff top (a jacket, a coat, a heavy overshirt) roomier than a regular one holds the elbows, and the hands with them, off it: by as much as its body reaches past a regular top's, at most 2 cm (`ARM_OUT`, `ARM_OUT_MAX_CM`; 1.65 cm for the oversized chore jacket). Soft cloth (a tee, a knit, a hoodie) and regular tops leave the arms hanging as they do with nothing on: a soft roomy body goes round behind them | Stiff cloth keeps its shape against the arm; soft cloth takes up its fullness at the sides and in folds. The owner's rules stand: the arms hang naturally at his sides, and the hands may overlap the trousers. Every top in an outfit is fitted round the same arms, and a bag in the hand or a watch follows the wrist |
| On such a stiff roomy top, what is on the front lies a tenth nearer the middle (`FRONT_IN`), and the last eighth of each side is drawn out to the side behind the sleeve (`FRONT_KEEP`) | The front panel curves round toward his sides, so what is near its edge faces forward from further in than the flat drawing's straight scale puts it. With the 2 cm hold this keeps every pocket of the chore jacket in front of the arms, about 1.3 cm clear of the sleeve, worn open or closed |
| A fuller top's body comes in from its shoulders to fall straight high on the chest (`FULL_IN`): as far off his chest as a regular top's, and further by 0.27 cm (soft cloth) or 0.13 cm (stiff) for each hundredth fuller its fit is on the flat drawing (`FULLER_ROOM_PER_FIT`, `STIFF_ROOM_PER_FIT`) | Coming in only under the arm bent a chest pocket out of shape. A soft oversized tee stays at least 4 cm a side roomier than a regular one, as a test asks; a stiff jacket is narrower, since the arms rest on it |
| Where a top's body lies against the arm, the sleeve's cloth between them is pressed flat: its inner edge stands 0.3 cm off the arm (`PRESSED_EASE_CM`) instead of its full ease | The sleeve's inner ease was what reached over the front. It also takes a regular blazer's sleeve off its hip pockets; the blazer's chest welt still touches its sleeve, left as it was |
| A dropped shoulder rounds from the shoulder's slope over his shoulder point (`DROP_ROUND_CM`), falls the same way all along its shoulder line (it lessens only below its own seam), and its sleeve hangs from the seam: the outer edge stands a quarter of its ease off the arm there, and all of it 12 cm down (`DROP_EASE_SHARE`, `DROP_EASE_CM`). The round of a set-in sleeve's head on its flat drawing is not kept where the seam lands out on his arm (an oversized top) | The owner asked for the shoulder bulge to be fixed. Seen before and after on the chore jacket, an oversized tee and an oversized hoodie: one smooth curve from the neck into a sleeve falling close to straight, and the roomy sleeve still stands well off the upper arm. The owner then saw a small dip at the seam of an oversized jacket and hoodie described with set-in sleeves: the flat sleeve head's round, kept on top of the shoulder's own rounding, made a short ledge there |
| What is drawn again over a top's sleeves (`_sleeve_only`) is judged along its length (`_spaced`), not by how many points it is written with | An oversized hoodie's ribbed hem band, whose short ends lie on the sleeves, was drawn over them: most of its written points are at those ends |

### Accessories

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The tagger names an accessory by the taxonomy's accessory garment id (`accessory`: cap, beanie, brimmed_hat, belt, scarf, bag, tie, pocket_square, gloves, eyewear, watch, socks) and its kind from the taxonomy's `accessories` section (`accessory_kind`); this raised the tagging schema to version 3. Answers kept from earlier versions still load, with both fields "na" | The owner asked for templates chosen from the taxonomy's ids, not free words. The issue said "no schema change", but without these fields the drawing had nothing reliable to choose from. |
| Bags, belts, caps, beanies, brimmed hats and scarves each have a template, and their kind picks its style (a fedora, a trucker cap, a rucksack, a web belt...). The other accessories are drawn as a plain tag in their colour, never as a top | The issue's four, with the hat split as the taxonomy splits it. A tag says "an accessory" without pretending to a shape. Every id and kind is listed in `COVERAGE`. |
| A bag, belt, hat or scarf whose kind is not said is drawn as the plainest of its kind (a tote, a plain leather belt, a cap or, when knitted, a beanie, a woven scarf), with a note saying so | Better than no drawing; the note tells the owner why it may not look like theirs. |
| Ties, pocket squares, gloves, glasses, watches and socks have templates of their own too (`closet/drawing/accessories.py`, #185), replacing the plain tag. Jewellery, braces and umbrellas are still listed as not drawn | The owner asked for every accessory in the taxonomy to have a drawing; jewellery is too small to read at card size and was left for later. |
| The details the taxonomy has no field for come from the item's pattern name and the tagger's own words: a tie's pattern from its pattern name (stripes as a repp stripe, a dot, a paisley, a foulard; a novelty print as a club motif) or words like "repp" or "skinny"; a watch's dial shape, strap and case from words like "square", "bracelet" or "gold", its dial colour from a named colour there or the second colour; glasses' lenses from words with "lens" in them; socks' pattern from the pattern name. A watch's main colour is its strap's | No schema change. The words are only trusted for what they plainly say; otherwise each kind's usual look is drawn (a dive watch on a steel bracelet, a dress watch on leather). |
| The small accessories are worn on the figure by their own module, `closet/drawing/worn.py`, which `figure._svg` calls once; what it returns is put in at the points of the drawing order where each belongs (socks over the legs and under the trousers, a tie between the innermost top and the next, the rest over everything but a scarf, hat or bag) | Keeps `figure.py` and `fitting.py`, which other work is changing, almost untouched: one call and its uses. |
| On the figure: a tie only on a closed innermost top with a collar, its knot under the collar points (the collar is drawn again over it), its blade ending 2.2 cm above the belt's middle; a pocket square only in a tailored outermost top's breast pocket, cut off at the welt and turned with the front when worn open; a watch on the viewer's right wrist, left out when a sleeve covers its middle; glasses on the eyes; socks only above low shoes that are not sandals, when the trousers stop short of the shoes, as high as their kind (a crew sock 17 cm, over-the-calf 44 cm) and in the shoe's opening where the ankle would show | Each is where a person would see it; one that would be hidden is not drawn. Heights and sizes were set by eye from `docs/screenshots/coverage-accessories-worn.png` and could reasonably be chosen differently. |

### Coverage of tops and knits (#182)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Every plain weave, shirting, smooth cloth and jersey has a faint tone-on-tone surface of its own (poplin's broken cords, oxford's paired pale flecks, end-on-end's upright streak, jersey's stitches...), kept faint enough that the cloth still reads as plain | They used to look identical. Each surface was sized to survive on a closet tile; the faintest (plain, challis, melton) are barely there at tile size by design: those cloths are plain. |
| A colourwork knit is drawn where the garment carries it: Fair Isle and Nordic as a yoke round the neck (and a border above the hem), argyle as diamonds all over the body, intarsia as one motif on the chest, jacquard knit and Cowichan as bands, guernsey tone-on-tone | Placement is what tells these knits apart at item size. The motifs are knitted in the other colours seen in the photo; with none, in cream on a dark cloth or a deep shade of a light one. A colourwork pattern the tagger names (`pattern_name`) counts as well as the fabric. |
| A top's fibre is drawn when no fabric says otherwise: leather's creases and shine, suede's nap, worsted's fine twill, lambswool's soft edge, merino's finer knitting | Applied on tops only (`top_drawing`), so trousers and accessories keep their own look. Acrylic stays approximate: drawn like wool, which it looks like at item size. |
| A kind of top (`_KIND_TOP`) has a look of its own, some of it always (a hockey jersey's stripes, a singlet's missing sleeves) and the rest only where the details were silent; a kind's usual cloth and fibre, collar and sleeve likewise fill in only what was not seen | The same rule as the trouser kinds: what the tagger saw wins. A hockey jersey's stripes are in the trim colour, else the other colour seen; jerseys are bound in a contrast trim only when one was seen. Crests, numbers, lacing and thumbholes are too small to draw. |
| A pyjama or lounge set is drawn as its top; a set's trousers or shorts, tagged as their own item, are drawn as the bottom they are cut like (`taxonomy.SET_BOTTOMS`: lounge pants or sleep shorts for pyjamas, joggers for a knit set, sweatpants or training shorts for a sweatsuit); the set counts as drawn | The trousers of a set are their own item, drawn with the trouser kinds. |
| A heathered or marled top is drawn with pale and dark flecks over plain knitting | The commonest irregular cloth on tops (grey marl). It replaces only a plain surface (jersey, flat knit, French terry, fleece or none); a cable or rib keeps its own. |

### Tracing shoes from the photo (#192, stage 1)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Tracing a shoe photo is tried on the bench first (`closet_bench.trace_shoes`), on the owner's photos, before anything changes in the app | The owner doubted tracing would hold up on real photos (shadows, glare, laces, angles); Phase 0 had found templates beat tracing for garments. The bench shows the traces next to today's template and scores them, so the owner can judge. |
| Six variants from three approaches: k-means colours (4 and 6), mean-shift then colours (gentle and strong), a bilateral filter then colours (light and strong smoothing) | The issue asked for two or three approaches with a couple of settings each; all use OpenCV, already installed. No new dependency. |
| A colour and the same colour in shade are one region, drawn in its lit shade | Phase 0 found shading split into extra regions. Two colours count as one when their hue and chroma match and one is up to 25 L darker. |
| The sole is found and quantised on its own (two colours at most) | It is usually a different material and colour from the upper, and the drawings keep it as a separate region. |
| Each variant gets a combined score: 0.35 outline overlap with the cut-out, 0.25 colour closeness (ΔE), 0.2 cleanliness (fewer paths), 0.2 marks kept | Overlap and colour are what make the drawing read as the owner's shoe; cleanliness and marks tell the variants apart once those are good. The weights are a first guess, to be checked against what the owner prefers on the sheets. |
| The quality gate turns a photo away with a plain reason and the measured value: no shoe found, too small, blurred, low contrast with the background, cut off at the frame, two shoes, outline broken up, not side-on (no flat sole, no toe end), or a trace that does not match the cut-out | So the app can later fall back to the template by itself. The limits are set on synthetic images only; the owner's run is what checks them. |

### Tracing shoes from the photo (#192, stage 2a): the gate and the traced shoe as data

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The tracing lives in the app's code, `closet/drawing/trace.py` (cut-out, turning, tracing, gate) and `closet/drawing/traced.py` (the traced shoe as data); the bench command imports them and keeps its other five approaches to itself | The same rule as the templates, the renderer and the fills: one copy, shared. OpenCV is imported only inside the functions that read a photo. No app screen uses it yet (2b does). |
| The app traces one way, `bilateral-5` (a bilateral filter, then five colours); this is provisional | The six approaches scored within a few points of each other on the owner's 30 photos. The choice is to be made on the owner's photos with this stage's gate, not on synthetic ones; changing it is one line (`trace.APPROACH`). |
| "Runs off the edge" counts only the frame pixels the shoe comes near that are not plain background | A shoe in a white-padded square was turned down: the cut-out met the frame where the frame was only white padding. |
| "Side-on" means the sole rests on its heel (the back 30%) and its ball (55–85% from the heel): the ground line is the hull edge with the most outline points on it, and neither zone may stay more than 3.5% of the length above it | Toe spring and curved outsoles are normal; the old test wanted a flat run and turned down trainers, a brogue, a leather sneaker and hiking boots. |
| The outline's pair checks only advise. `second_sole` (going in from the toe or the heel, the bottom line runs level above the ground, then steps down steeply) and `second_top` (an edge between alike colours running below the outline's top along most of the shoe) are measured, kept in `Gate.notes` and shown on the bench sheet, but never turn a photo down (`trace.ADVICE`). A pair is turned down only on unambiguous evidence: two pieces apart (`second_piece`), or the tagger saying so (below) | On the owner's 30 photos neither separates pairs from single shoes: `second_top` read 0.22–0.82 on single shoes and 0.01–0.76 on pairs; `second_sole` was 0 on most pairs and up to 6.9 cm on single shoes (lug soles, heel tabs, shadows), above the one pair it found (4.5). Every limit turned down clean single shoes, so the outline's shape is not used to decide. The earlier notch check (`end_notch`) failed the same way. |
| The tagger says, for shoes, how many shoes the first image shows (`shoes_in_photo`: one, pair or more) and where it sees the shoe from (`shoe_view`: side, front, back, top or angled). Schema version 5; older answers read both as `na`, which says nothing. The gate takes them when given (`trace.told_from(answer)`): a pair, more, or any view but the side turns the photo down in plain words ("two shoes: Claude sees both shoes of a pair in the photo", "not side-on: Claude sees the shoe at an angle"), and no other cut-out is tried. The bench reads them from the answer saved as `<photo>.json` beside the photo. They describe the photo, not the shoe (`schema.PHOTO_FIELDS`), so they are not offered to the owner as details, not shown on the item page and not drawn | Claude already looks at every item photo, and seeing two shoes or a three-quarter view is easy for it and hard for an outline. One question per photo adds two short fields to an answer the app already asks for. The geometric side-on checks stay, so a photo with no answer is still checked; the pair checks only advise (above). The demo answers were rewritten for version 5 (both fields `na`, so nothing about the demo closet changes). |
| White on white: the cut-out's edge is refined (GrabCut in a narrow band round it, kept only when it agrees with the first cut-out), contrast is also read right at the edge, the contrast limit is ΔE 6 (was 8), and when a cut-out fails the gate the other one (model, then GrabCut) is tried before the photo is turned down | A white shoe on a white ground leaks out of one cut-out and not the other, and differs from its ground mostly at its edge, where it turns away from the light. The real white shoe turned down measured 6.8. A shoe that truly does not stand out (about 2) is still turned down. |
| The traced shoe is a versioned JSON record (`TracedShoe`, version 1): a side profile (the upper's top line, the sole's underside, the sole's thickness, at 41 steps from heel to toe), parts, marks, laces, eyelets, stitching, the nearest `frontshoe.STYLES` entry, a confidence and the gate | Stage 2b stores it with the item and 2c draws the front view from it. Its coordinates are `frontshoe`'s: share of the length from the heel, cm above the ground, scaled to the 29.5 cm reference shoe, so the two drawings meet without conversion. A newer record than the program knows is refused rather than misread. |
| The style hint first decides shaft or no shaft from the collar: its median height along the back 30% of the length, at least 12 cm above the ground and 9 cm above the sole for a shaft; then picks the nearest style of that kind by the whole top line standing on the measured sole | The first hint (the nearest table by the upper's rise, with the collar's highest point) read two low trainers on the owner's photos as boots: a heel tab or pull loop raised the collar, and a sole measured thinner than it is made the upper look taller. A median ignores a tab; a thick-soled runner's collar is high above the ground but not above its sole. |
| Parts come from the traced colour pieces, never cut with a straight line. Each piece is labelled with the part it mostly lies in (toe cap past 82% of the length, heel counter behind 20%, collar and tongue along the top, vamp and quarter between; a piece of the sole as midsole or outsole, the lowest colour the outsole; a piece in the laces', eyelets' or stitching's colour along that row as that detail), and `TracedShoe.regions` keeps every piece with its label (record version 2). A part's colour is that of the piece covering most of where it sits: when that piece is labelled with the part, the part takes its outline; otherwise the part is `within` that piece ("upper": the shoe's base colour). A part not seen is marked inferred and takes the colour of the part it joins | On the owner's photos, splitting at fixed lines blended a red mudguard and black overlays into one colour per part. Labelling whole pieces keeps each panel's real shape and colour, and the labelled region map lets the front view (2c) place the real panels on the 3D shoe instead of one colour per part. Version 1 records still read: the new fields are empty. |
| A midsole in another colour than the outsole is found as a band of one colour along most of the sole, just above it; marks that touch it (stripes running down to the sole) are cut off at its usual height | The trace finds the sole as the bottom colour only, so a white midsole over a grey outsole was taken for a long mark on the upper. |
| Laces, eyelets and stitching are found in a second look at up to 1200 px along the shoe: small things that differ from the shoe just round them by ΔE 14, then rows of them (round ones in a straight line for eyelets, short bars by the throat for laces, four or more small dashes in a line for stitching), each row with its count, spacing and colour | At five colours the trace loses them. |
| Laces are counted along the lacing: in the throat (40 to 88% of the length, up to 3 cm below the top), for each of the four commonest colours that are not the upper's, the line the laces run along is found (their middle depth below the top, smoothed) and the peaks along it, at least 0.8 cm apart, are counted; of the colours whose peaks repeat regularly, the one covering most of the line is the laces', unless its peaks fall on the eyelets. A peak much closer to its neighbour than the rest at either end is dropped. Counting pieces is kept as the fallback | Laces that cross or touch join into one piece, so counting pieces found 2 of about 7 and 3 of about 5 on the owner's photos. Each lace, or each crossing, meets the lacing's middle line once. The dropped end peak is a mark (stripes) reaching into the lacing. |
| Marks are the trace's other colours on the upper that are not a part's colour block or a lace: straight bands of one colour at one angle form a stripe set; three or more small pieces along a line form a text block; the rest are a curve (long and thin), a panel (large) or other | The issue's kinds. Each mark keeps its outline, middle, size, angle and colour, so 2b and 2c can redraw it. |
| Stripe sets are found before the laces: in one traced colour, two to four long pieces (at least twice as long as wide, at least 1.2 cm) side by side at one angle (within 15°) and evenly spaced, each at most 6% of the upper, with its middle on the quarter or midfoot (15 to 75% of the length from the heel) and at least 1.5 cm below the top edge. They are one `stripes` mark, never a part (even where they meet the sole or the outline), and the lace and eyelet search does not look at their pixels (a little wider, for their soft edges). With eyelets, laces are looked for only down to 1 cm below them. With no lace colour of their own, a shoe has no laces row and the front view draws its laces a shade off the upper's colour | On the owner's white court shoe with three red stripes, the trace kept only a heel tab as a mark and took the stripes' red for the laces: thin, tapered stripes with soft edges fill too little of their box for the per-piece test, and their ends reach into the lacing. Laces in the same colour as the stripes (a navy runner with white stripes and laces) are still found, because the stripes are left out by place, not by colour. The depth below the top keeps a row of laces from being taken for a stripe set. |
| Confidence is the trace's combined score, halved when the gate turned the photo down | Simple and in one place; 2b decides what to do below a level. |

### Tracing shoes from the photo (#206, stage 2b): the traced drawing in the app

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A shoe (sneakers, boots, dress shoes, sandals) with a photo is traced by the cut-out queue after its cutouts, one at a time, never in a request. The `TracedShoe` record is kept on the item (`items.trace`, migration 0024) with the tracing code's version (`tracejobs.TRACING_VERSION`); a shoe traced by an older version, or whose photo is replaced, waits to be traced again. Claude's answer re-traces a shoe only when its word on the photo (`shoes_in_photo`, `shoe_view`) differs from the one the trace used | A trace takes two to three seconds with GrabCut. The same queue already owns the photo and its cut-out, and a request must answer at once. |
| A traced shoe is drawn from its side, on the same 400×460 card, outline weight and flat colours as the templates (`drawing/sideshoe.py`). An untraced shoe, one whose photo was turned down, or one set to the plain drawing keeps the template drawing exactly (a digest test). *Since 20 November, a shoe with a side drawn by Claude shows that instead of its trace: see "A shoe's side drawn by Claude".* | The closet tile and the item page must read as the same family of drawings; nothing changes for shoes that are not traced well. |
| When the photo is turned down, the item page says why in one plain sentence taken from the gate's first reason ("The photo shows the shoe from the front, so it's drawn from its details instead. A side-on photo gives a closer drawing.") and offers Retake, which replaces the front photo; the new photo is cut out, described and traced again | The owner should know why the drawing is the plain one and what to do about it, without the gate's measurements. |
| The owner's fixes (a part's colour, a mark removed or moved, up to 4 cm each way) are kept apart from the trace (`items.trace_fixes`). Part colours are keyed by part name; a mark's fix goes with the new trace's mark of the same kind whose middle is nearest, within 2.5 cm, and is dropped otherwise. "Use the plain drawing" is a flag of its own (`items.trace_plain`) | A re-trace, a new photo or newer tracing code replaces the trace; fixes that still apply should stay, and a fix must never land on a different mark. |
| An item that stops being a shoe keeps its trace and fixes, unused; said to be a shoe again, it is drawn from them | The owner may correct the category by mistake; nothing is lost. |
| ~~Add has a third mode, "A shoe": an outline of a shoe side-on with the toe to the left, shown over the live camera where the browser allows it (a secure address) and over the chosen photo otherwise; the photo is checked at once (`POST /api/shoes/check`: the cut-out and the gate, with the model when it can run, else GrabCut, never Claude) and the owner can retake or add it anyway~~ Replaced by "A shoe in one step on Add" (16 November, below) | |
| The optional second photo (from the front or from above) is kept as a detail photo with its view (`photos.view`), not cut out and not traced | Stage 2c read it for the "From your photo" front view (below; removed 19 November, so it is no longer read for a front view); keeping it saves asking the owner twice. |
| The demo closet's grey runners and brown boots carry traces made by `tools/make_demo.py --traces-only` from made-up side-on photos drawn by the tests' generator (`tests/shoe_photos.py`); an older demo closet is given them once. They have no photo, so they are never traced again | The feature should be visible in the demo, and no real photo of a shoe may be in the repository. |

### Tracing shoes from the photo (#192, stage 2c): the traced shoe seen from the front

Until 19 November a traced shoe also had a front view on the As worn figure, in two versions the owner chose
between in Fix the details: A, "From the shoe's shape" (the template's drawing painted in the trace's colour panels),
and B, "From your photo" (the template's shape painted by sampling the side trace, its toe-box and vamp colours, laces
and marks read from an optional front photo by `frontlook.py`). B kept the template's outline because every outline
traced from the photo drew square toes, points or horns; small colour pieces, marks, sole bands and laces each needed
rules of their own to read at phone size. Both views were removed on 19 November (see "The traced front view is
removed", below), and the front is now Claude's drawing or the template's; the rows that described them are in the
git history. One row still holds:

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Four rules hold for every front shoe, A and B, traced or not, each with a test: the collar's back and the lining are painted before the leg, and nothing of the shoe covers the leg more than 1.5 cm above the opening's front (the padded front of a collar); the toe is rounded (B's outline is A's); every detail (laces, marks, stitching) lies on the shoe; the outline, sampled every pixel, turns less than 65° anywhere (no points or horns) (Since the traced front was removed, 19 November, they hold for the template front view and are tested in `tests/test_frontshoe.py`.) | The owner's hard rules (Drawing in 3D). They are checked on every synthetic traced shoe, with and without a front photo, and on every template.  Under cropped trousers a bare ankle shows where a low shoe's collar meets the leg, and the owner found a spike of a dress shoe's collar standing up beside it (#223 review): the dress shoes and slip-ons now get the rounded collar the trainers had, and on every low shoe each shoulder rises no more than 1.3 times as high as it is wide (`LIP_RISE`), the narrow inner one topping out 0.4 of the way in (`LIP_SHOULDER`), the outer one level past the ankle before it rises. A fifth test: where the collar meets the leg, at least a quarter of a 4 px circle there is shoe (it was 28° of 360 on the dress shoes, 82° on the low sneaker). |

### The traced front view is removed (19 November 2026)

Decided by the owner.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The As worn figure no longer draws a shoe's front view from its trace (views A, "From the shoe's shape", and B, "From your photo"). A shoe with a drawing by Claude is drawn from it, exactly as before; a shoe without one, traced or not, wears the template's front view for its style, the same drawing as an untraced shoe or "Use the plain drawing". Fix the details no longer offers the two views or "Add a front photo", the two `trace/front` routes and the item's `trace.front` are gone, and a photo from the front or from above is no longer read (`frontlook.py`) or makes the shoe traced again | The traced front was only ever shown before Claude's drawing of the shoe exists (a new pair for a few minutes, an older shoe until "Draw again", the demo's grey runners, or with no route to Claude). There the template lines up better with the shoe's side drawing: in the line-up the owner matched 8 of 10 shoes from the template against 3 of 10 from the photo laid on. It also cost a second cut-out at every trace and extra re-traces when photos were sorted. What is lost is a multi-coloured shoe's colours in those cases; "Draw again" gives the shoe its own drawing. |
| The columns `items.shoe_front` and `items.front_look` (migration 0025) stay in the closet, no longer read or written; no migration drops them | Dropping them would be a destructive migration for no gain; an old closet keeps working and nothing reads them. |
| The traced side view (the closet picture), the trace record, Fix the details' colours and marks, Re-trace and the plain drawing are unchanged; so is everything in `frontshoe.py` the side view, the turned Claude drawing and the template share. The template's rules keep their tests (`tests/test_frontshoe.py`, moved from the traced front's tests, digest unchanged) | Only what drew the traced front was taken out. |

### A traced shoe's front on the figure: what is aimed for, and the test that decides it

Decided with the owner before anything was built (the plan is in `BACKLOG.md`, Track 1, "A traced shoe's front,
from a front photo"). The aim was a front view as recognisable as the real shoe is from the front, and never one that
contradicts it, since much of what tells one model from another is on the side and the heel. A line-up was to decide
whether the front-view work went on: ten real pairs (photos from shops' pages, kept out of the public repository),
each front photo traced and placed on the figure by hand, to be matched by the owner at phone size; it would pass at
8 of 10 from the placed traces and 3 more than from today's drawing. It failed: 8 of 10 from the template, 3 from the
photo laid on, 2 with the toes turned 20°. The front is now drawn by Claude from the photos (next section). The rows
on what a pass would have built, the turn of the toes and "No more shoe templates and no new shoe words in the
taxonomy for this" are in the git history. One row still holds:

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| In the app the photos are the owner's: taken by them, or copied by them from a web page when it is good enough. The app does not search the web for a shoe's photos | Only the owner knows which model and colourway they have, and a wrong colourway looks right. A search by name breaks whenever a shop changes its pages, and many shops refuse automatic requests. |

### A shoe on the figure's feet: drawn from understanding, not traced

Worked out with the owner on 15 November 2026, on five photos each of five of the owner's own pairs. The plan and
the numbers are in `BACKLOG.md` (Track 1, "Shoes on the figure drawn from understanding"); the experiments were in
`bench/experiments/shoe_drawn/` and `bench/experiments/shoe_solid/` (now in the git history; the drawings the owner liked are
kept in `backend/tests/data/shoe_drawn/`). Each row says whether it is **decided** with the
owner or **to be confirmed** by running the method unchanged on pairs it has not seen. Nothing here was in the app yet; steps B to E below built it.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **Decided.** A shoe's front on the As worn figure is drawn by a vision model from the shoe's photos, as clip art: first a feature sheet (what kind of shoe, its silhouette, proportions, 2 to 6 true colours, its parts, what makes it recognisable from the front), then a drawing made from that sheet and from what the model knows of that kind of shoe, then the model renders its drawing, looks at it beside the photos and fixes it, at most three times | After the line-up failed, the owner said the feet were off in shape on all ten shop pairs, missing detail on eight and badly traced on four. A hand redraw from those notes was "more detailed, not better". The owner then observed that a model asked for clip art of a German army trainer from the front would do better than any tracing; drawn that way from the owner's photos in about ten minutes, in four flat colours, it read as the shoe at phone size. Drawn this way, the model named 5 of 5 and then 15 of 15 shoes correctly, and the owner called round 1 "almost exactly what I want". |
| **Decided.** This replaces, for shoes on the figure, two rows above: "No more shoe templates and no new shoe words in the taxonomy for this" (in "A traced shoe's front on the figure"), where a drawing made from words was said not to be the owner's shoe, and the rows of stage 2c that draw the front on the template's shape painted by the trace ("B is drawn on the template's shape", "A traced shoe has two front views"). Those rows were left as the record; since 20 November they are summed up in their sections and kept in full in the git history. What still stands: nothing is traced as an outline from a photo; a shoe with no photos, or whose drawing fails, is drawn from today's template | The drawing is made from the owner's own photos, not only from words, and it is checked against them, so it is the owner's shoe and not just one of that description. Every traced outline so far drew square toes, points or horns, and the drawn shoes did not. |
| **Decided.** The measured solid is dropped: a 3D shape built from the side profile and the plan from above, rounded to fit the front and back photos, painted with colours sampled from the photos and drawn from the figure's view | Each solid had its shoe's proportions, but the feet read as blobs; every shade and dirt patch became a colour, so the colours looked like camouflage; the front and back photos barely mattered. Painting it in the colours a model named fixed the colours but not the blobs. Its cut-outs (clean on 25 of 25) and the measured height and width are kept for later use. |
| **Decided.** Colours are a few named true colours (2 to 6) written in the feature sheet; one material is one colour; flat fills, no shading, textures or lettering | Grouping a photo's pixels by colour always turns shade into panels; the palette has to come from outside the image. With named colours the tan dress shoe became one brown. |
| **Decided for the experiment; to be confirmed.** Every drawing is made in one fixed frame (about 17 units per cm, ground at the bottom, a low shoe about as tall as it is wide, a boot's shaft top from its measured height), with one rule for the view (the toe box is the nearest and biggest shape and everything behind it sits higher; a boot's shaft rises behind the toe box, narrower than it) and one build order (the sole's front edge, the whole upper, colour panels, defining seams, tongue and laces last, the outline again). The leg goes into the shoe: the part behind the leg and the part in front are drawn apart. The sole is drawn no thicker or wider than the photos show | Round 2's frame put the opening too high, so low shoes came out too tall (10 of 15 runs said so themselves), and every boot was a tube with no toe box. With round 3's frame, view rule and order, all three boots got a toe box in front with the shaft behind, and the runners came out low with a less bulky sole. |
| **Decided for the experiment; to be confirmed.** The prompt carries worked examples: drawings the owner liked (the session's trainer, round 1's dress shoe and boat shoe), as examples of the style and the build, not shapes to copy | The examples fixed the simplicity and proportions that round 2's runs varied on. Whether they also pull other kinds of shoe towards these three shapes is what the check on unseen pairs must show. |
| **Decided for the experiment; to be confirmed.** The model names the most specific kind of shoe first (for example "max-cushion running shoe with a thick foam midsole"), writes how that kind looks from the front, draws that silhouette, then fits it to the photos | No runner was good before; drawn this way the foam sole read as a thick tub with the upper sitting in it, and the owner said the first of the two runs "looks more like a shoe". The toe rubber came out too big and needs its own correction. |
| **Decided.** The left and right shoes are drawn separately when a mark is on one side only, each with the mark on its outer side and reading the right way round; otherwise the right shoe is the left one mirrored | A mirrored runner showed its N backwards on one foot; drawn separately, the N read the right way on both. |
| **Decided.** The owner corrects a drawing with a one-line note; the model starts from the existing drawing and changes only what the note asks and what must change with it | Asked to make the boot's elastic panels broad, the model did it in one round, in about 35 seconds, with nothing else changed. Redrawing from scratch would lose what the owner already liked. |
| **Decided; how it looks in the app is still open.** The app sorts the photos into views itself; the owner is asked only when the outer and inner side cannot be told apart and it matters (a mark on one side only) | A cheaper model sorted the 25 photos shuffled: 5 of 5 shoes grouped right, above, front and back named right 15 of 15, outer and inner side swapped on 3 of 5 shoes, and it flagged all six of those photos as unsure itself. The owner should not have to say which photo is which view. |
| **To be confirmed.** The method is judged by running it unchanged on three to five pairs it has not seen, of other kinds, before it is built into the app; then drawn at the figure's own view (24° down, toes out 11°) and placed on the figure | It was tuned and judged on the same five pairs, so how often a run comes out poor elsewhere is not known. The drawings so far are straight from the front, about 15° above, tilted 7° by the preview. |

### A shoe's photos sorted into views by the app (step B)

Built on 15 November 2026, the first step into the app of "drawn from understanding" above: the owner's words were
"I should not have to say which photo is which view. The app sorts them and asks me only when it cannot tell the
outer side from the inner side." Nothing draws from the sorted photos yet; a later step has Claude draw the shoe
from them. The code is `backend/closet/shoeviews.py` and `backend/closet/tagger/views.py`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| ~~A shoe has its main photo (the guided side photo) and up to 5 more, added in any order on Add ("Add more photos of this shoe") or on the item page; a 6th is refused with a plain sentence. Nothing is deleted when a photo is added~~ Replaced by "A shoe in one step on Add" (16 November, below) | |
| One call sorts every photo of the shoe, the main one first, on the mid-priced model (Sonnet; the new `sorting` role in `tagger/models.json`), through the same routes and monthly limit as describing, recorded as `sort_views` | It is the model the experiment sorted with: above, front and back right on 15 of 15 photos, and every photo whose outer and inner side it swapped flagged as unsure by itself. One call with all the photos lets it compare them, which telling the sides apart needs. At most about 5¢ a shoe with the API key, at the prices written down. |
| Sorting waits until the newest photo is 10 seconds old (`SORT_QUIET`) | The owner adds several photos in a row; this makes one call for all of them instead of one per photo. A photo added while a sort is on its way sets that answer aside and the shoe is sorted again with it. |
| The owner's word always wins: a view the owner gave (a photo named "front" or "top" when added, the old second photo, or an answer to the question) is kept and never changed by sorting again; the main photo is sorted like the others | Claude can be wrong about the sides; once the owner has said, asking again or overwriting it would undo their answer. The main photo is usually a side, and which side matters as much as for the others. |
| The owner is asked only when the sides look different (a logo, stripe or panel on one side only: Claude's `sides_differ`) and a side photo Claude sorted is unsure, or two photos claim the same side (not both the owner's). With several such photos: which shows the outside; with one: the outside or the inside. Naming one side gives the other side to the other side photo, when there is exactly one | When both sides look alike, which is which changes nothing in a drawing, so the owner is not bothered. Asking on "unsure" would have caught all three swapped shoes of the experiment, which were all flagged. With two side photos one answer settles both. *Since 22 November the owner can also say which photo shows the outside at any time once the photos are sorted ("Change which photo shows the outside"), since a swap Claude was sure of could not be put right; naming a side then gives the other side to the side photos Claude gave the same one: see "The item page in the owner's order".* |
| No backfill: a shoe kept before this is sorted only once a photo is added or removed (or, once sorted, its main photo is retaken). A new shoe from "A shoe" on Add is sorted once Claude (or the owner) has said it is a shoe, even with its main photo alone | Nothing uses the views yet, so sorting every old shoe would spend calls for nothing; the owner adding photos to an old shoe is the sign it is wanted. |
| Photos can be added to an item Claude has not described yet | A new item is described only after its cutout and a call, while the owner adds the other photos straight away on Add. |
| ~~The photo read for the "From your photo" front view is the newest photo from the front, else the newest from above, whoever named it, never the main photo; when sorting, an answer or a removal changes which photo that is, the old reading goes and the shoe is traced again. A photo added without a view is not read until it is sorted~~ Removed 19 November: see "The traced front view is removed" (below) | Before, there was at most one second photo and it was always read; with several, the sorted view picks the right one. Not tracing again on every added photo saves the tracing when nothing it reads changed. |
| The demo closet's photos are never sorted; its item says so | As for describing: the demo closet is never sent to Claude. |

### A shoe drawn by Claude in the app (step C)

Built on 15 November 2026, the second step into the app of "drawn from understanding" above. The owner's words: "The
drawing step in the app: after a shoe's photos are added, write the feature sheet, draw, check against the photos (at
most three rounds), and store the drawing and the sheet with the item. Use the same route to Claude that tagging uses.
Tell me the time and cost per shoe. Never redraw unless I ask." The drawing is made, kept, checked and shown on the
shoe's page; the As worn figure uses it since step D (below). The code is `backend/closet/shoedraw.py` (the worker),
`backend/closet/tagger/shoedrawing.py` (the prompts and calls) and `backend/closet/drawing/drawn.py` (made safe, shown,
checked; no call to Claude).

The owner's verdicts on the saved examples laid on the figure (`bench/experiments/shoe_drawn/on_figure.py`, in the git history), which
step C builds on:

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **Decided.** The shoe is drawn from the front and a little above, as the saved examples are, not at the figure's steeper view (24° down). This replaces, for the view, the last row of "drawn from understanding" above | The owner, on the examples laid on the figure: the perspective is fine. |
| **Decided.** The size relative to the leg is the frame's (the leg x 62 to 138 in a shoe up to about 180 wide) | The owner: "much better than the old shoes, which were tiny". |
| **Decided.** Claude draws ONE shoe, its toe pointing straight at the viewer; the app turns the toes out on the figure itself, below a hinge (a low shoe's opening, a boot's instep), so the collar or shaft stays upright round the leg | The owner: toes turned out is fine. A tilt drawn in would lean a boot's shaft away from the leg. |
| **Decided.** Claude draws its lines at stroke-width 4 (outline) and 1.6 (inner lines); the app thickens the ink lines (#26221f, at most 4.5 wide) to the figure's weight, 2.2 / (4 × 0.297), about 1.85 times. The item page's picture and the image Claude checks already show them that thick; thicker ink strokes (laces, a bow) are left as they are | On the figure the examples' outlines were half as thick as today's shoes'. Thickening one number in the app is surer than asking for lines that only look right once shrunk. |
| **Decided.** Low-profile shoes (boat shoe, loafer, ballet flat, slipper, driving moc) sit lower and narrower: the vamp or collar front at about y 75 to 90, about 145 wide; the boat shoe example is the lowered one (its vamp top about y 79, about 144 wide) | The owner found the saved boat shoe too large: full-length trousers that just touch an ordinary sneaker should leave the sock showing above a low-profile shoe. |
| **Still put off by the owner.** The check on pairs the method has not seen | The owner, 15 November: build it into the app first. The method has only been judged on the five pairs it was tuned on. |

What step C decided:

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Each drawing is one call to draw and at most three calls to check, on the model of the new `drawing` role in `tagger/models.json` (Opus), at effort `medium` (`shoedrawing.EFFORT`, a constant beside the prompts, not the model's effort in `models.json`, which other roles share). A change from the owner's note uses the same effort | The experiment's drawings were made on Opus at a high effort, and the app started there. On 16 November 2026 the same two shoes were drawn three ways through the app's own code: Opus at high (about $0.43 a shoe at API prices, 2.5 rounds, 139 s), Opus at medium ($0.23, 1 round, 65 s) and Sonnet at medium ($0.12, 1.5 rounds, 49 s). The owner found Opus at medium looked better than at high; Sonnet left out small details (perforations, a mark on one side only). The owner's own five shoes (a running shoe, a GAT, an oxford, a boat shoe, a Chelsea boot) were then drawn at both: on average $0.39 at high and $0.38 at medium, because the cost goes with the number of check rounds, which varied from run to run at either effort (a round costs about 15% less at medium). Each was better on some shoes (medium on the GAT's panels, high on the boot's elastic sides). The owner kept medium |
| The calls go through the same routes as describing (`correcting.ask`: the plan first, the API key only when switched on), each API request checked against the monthly limit before it is made and recorded with purpose `draw_shoe`. Its estimate (`spend.estimate_draw`) allows for the longest prompt (55,000 tokens), every image sent and the longest answer (32,000 tokens, thinking included): about $0.90 a request at the prices written down | The owner asked for "the same route to Claude that tagging uses". The estimate must hold for the longest request the app can write, two drawings at their size cap included. |
