| On the API route, an answer of up to 32,000 tokens is streamed (`messages.stream`), with the answer's schema in `output_config.format` and the whole text read against it once it has come. A call through the plan may take 10 minutes | The SDK refuses a request without streaming above about 21,300 tokens. Passing the schema as `output_format` makes the SDK read the text as soon as it ends and raise before the usage arrives when it is cut short; reading it afterwards keeps the cost of a cut-short answer in the record. |
| Each image is named to Claude: the photos `photo-1.jpg`, ... (the main photo first), each with the view it was sorted into, a side Claude was not sure of named as unsure ("the outer side, or possibly the inner side (not sure which)"); the examples' renders `example-trainer.png`, `example-dress-shoe.png`, `example-boat-shoe.png`; the image to check `drawing.png`. On the API route a short text naming each image comes just before it; renders are sent as PNG | The prompt has to say which photo shows what, and an unsure side must not be passed on as sure: a logo drawn on the wrong side is what sorting was asked to prevent. |
| The renderer is resvg (`resvg-py==0.5.0`) | Claude has to look at its own drawing, as it did in the experiment (where Chromium rendered it). Pillow cannot draw SVG, Chrome is not on every owner's computer, and resvg-py is one pure wheel with Windows and macOS builds and no dependencies (MIT). Nothing else in the app uses it. |
| The worked examples are the three drawings the owner liked (the trainer's shoe group, the dress shoe, the lowered boat shoe), kept as package data in `closet/drawing/shoe_examples/` and sent as SVG text and as renders on legs, large and at phone size, made at runtime. A test keeps them equal to the liked drawings kept in `backend/tests/data/shoe_drawn/` | As in rounds 3 and 4: the examples fixed the simplicity and proportions. Rendering them at runtime keeps the PNGs out of the repository and in step with the preview. The trainer keeps its group's round joins and caps, which its laces need. |
| The frame and view given to Claude are the experiment's (x 0 to 200 centred on 100, ground y 240, about 17 units a centimetre; a low shoe between about y 40 and 240, its opening front at y 40 to 60; a low-profile shoe's at y 75 to 90, about 145 wide; an ankle boot's toe box and instep at y 110 to 240 with its shaft behind, its top at y −30 to −50, a chukka's at about y 0, a taller boot's below y −90), with the build order, the strict style and kind of shoe first. The preview's stand-in leg goes down to y 200, behind the shoe | One self-contained prompt that ports `BRIEF3.md` and `ARCH.md` with the owner's decisions since. render3.js's leg stopped at y 60, which left a gap above the lowered boat shoe. |
| A drawing is checked automatically, from renders of it alone and over a flat-coloured leg band: it stands on the ground (lowest painted point at y 234 to 246); it stays inside x 2 to 198; a low shoe's top is no higher than y 0, a boot's no higher than y −90; from 8 units below its opening front down to y 200, the middle of the leg (x 72 to 128) is hidden; nothing of a low shoe covers the middle of the leg (x 75 to 125) more than 25 units above its opening front; and the opening front the sheet gives is within 15 units of where the drawing first covers the leg. What they find is put in plain words into the next check, and a drawing is taken as good only when Claude says so and they find nothing | They stand in for the four front-shoe rules, which are written for the templates' geometry (the opening's anchors, the template's parts) and cannot be applied to a drawing Claude made. These are the same rules measured on the picture: the shoe on the ground, the leg in the shoe and not hidden above it. The opening's height is what step D will place the hem and the turn of the toe by, so it has to be true. |
| The checks stop as soon as Claude says good and the automatic checks find nothing, and after three in any case. A check call that fails (Claude out of reach, no usable answer) keeps the drawing made so far; a fixed drawing that cannot be used is set aside and the drawing before it is checked again | The owner asked for at most three rounds. A drawing already paid for is still a drawing of the shoe; throwing it away to start again would cost more. |
| Every drawing is made safe before it is rendered, checked or kept (`drawn.sanitise`): only g, path, circle, ellipse, rect, line, polyline and polygon, only their geometry, plain colours (#rgb, #rrggbb, none), stroke and fill settings and number-only transforms; ids, text, images, links, styles, scripts and url() go; anything declared (`<!DOCTYPE`, entities, CDATA, processing instructions) is refused; at most 40,000 characters, 150 shapes and 8 groups deep | The drawing is text written by a model, shown in the owner's browser and kept in their closet: nothing in it may run, load, link or hide. |
| When a mark is on one side only (`one_side_mark`), Claude also draws the right shoe on its own, its outer side nearer x 200 and the mark reading the right way round; a sheet that says so without a right drawing is an automatic problem for the next check. Otherwise the app mirrors the left shoe | The owner's decision above, with the left/right sides spelt out in the frame's terms. |
| A shoe is drawn once by itself: when its photos first settle into views (sorted, or the owner asked which shows the outside, when it waits for the answer) and it has never been drawn. After that it is drawn only when the owner asks (**Draw again**, which asks first; **Draw it** for a shoe never drawn; **Try again** after a failure). Adding or removing photos never draws it again: the page says the photos changed since the drawing. The drawing it has stays shown until a new one is done, also when that fails | The owner: "Never redraw unless I ask." Waiting for the answer about the outside avoids drawing a logo on the wrong side. |
| No backfill: a shoe kept from before is drawn when the owner taps **Draw it** (its photos are sorted first if they never were, or if their sorting failed), or by itself the next time its photos are sorted, after a photo is added or removed | As for sorting: no calls for shoes the owner is not working on. |
| The time and cost are shown per shoe: "Drawn in 1 min 52 s, checked 2 times against the photos", and what the API key would charge for its calls at the prices in `models.json`, whichever route drew it ("about $0.61 if drawn on the API key" on the plan, "$0.61 on the API key" on the key). On the plan route, the context Claude Code reads again from its cache on each step is left out of that price | The owner asked to be told the time and cost per shoe. On the plan nothing is billed, so the API-key price is the useful number; the API route sends everything once, while Claude Code reads its earlier context again at each step, which the API route would not pay for. |
| The demo closet's shoes are never drawn this way; its page says so | As for describing and sorting: the demo closet is never sent to Claude. |
| The stand-in leg under a drawn shoe (in the app's preview and the check picture Claude looks at) wears a light grey crew sock (`drawn.PREVIEW_SOCK` #d3d0ca, its ribs #a9a59d) on the cream ground, not the experiment's green | The owner asked for light grey as the stock sock (16 November 2026): green pulled the eye and clashed with a shoe's own colours, such as an orange accent. A white shoe still stands apart from it by its outline and the sock's ribs. The preview is drawn each time it is asked for, so shoes already drawn show the grey sock at once; the drawings themselves and the As worn figure's socks do not change |

### A shoe drawn by Claude on the As worn figure (step D)

Built on 15 November 2026, the third step into the app of "drawn from understanding" above. The owner's words: "The
figure draws the stored drawing when there is one, with the leg going into the shoe, and keeps today's drawing when
there is none or the step failed. Left and right are drawn separately when a mark is on one side." It ports the bench
prototype the owner approved by its sheets (`bench/experiments/shoe_drawn/on_figure.py`, in the git history, and `docs/screenshots/shoe-drawn-
on-figure-*.png`: "toes turned out is fine", "the perspective of the new shoe is fine", "the proportions seem much
better", "hem looks right"). The code is `backend/closet/drawing/figure.py` (`_drawn_place`, `_on_drawing`,
`_drawn_pair`, `_turned`), `drawn.py` (`Side`, `stored`) and `worn._socks`; the sheets to look at are made by
`tools/make_drawn_figure_sheet.py`, now into the git-ignored `tools/out/` (the first ones are
`docs/screenshots/drawn-figure-closeup.png` and `drawn-figure-phone.png`).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A shoe with a drawing kept is drawn from it on the figure whatever its drawing state says now (a shoe being drawn again, or whose redraw failed, keeps the drawing it has). A shoe with none, or whose drawing cannot be read (not made safe, or its sheet does not say where its opening is), is drawn from today's front view exactly as before, byte for byte | The owner: keep today's drawing when there is none or the step failed. A redraw keeps the old drawing on the item page until the new one is done, so the figure does the same. |
| The drawing is placed by the front view it replaces: the frame's leg (x 62 to 138) as wide as the front view's ankle (its `ankle` and `ankle_r` anchors), the frame's middle (x 100) on each ankle, the frame's ground (y 240) on the front view's lowest point | The bench placement the owner approved: the leg fills the opening. The frame's leg is narrow for its shoe, so the drawings come out about a quarter wider than the front views, which the owner called much better ("the old shoes were tiny"). |
| The ink lines (#26221f, at most 4.5 wide) are thickened as the item page's picture already shows them (`drawn.thicken`, 2.2 / (4 × 0.297), about 1.85 times); thicker ink strokes (laces, a bow) are left as they are | One factor for every shoe: the frame lands at the same scale on every front view (their ankles are the same width), 0.297 px a unit. |
| The toes turn out by sliding the part of the drawing below a hinge sideways (a shear), as far as the front view's toe is turned (its slope from the ankle to the toe tip); the hinge is the sheet's `opening_front_y` for a low shoe and its `instep_y` for a boot, and above it nothing moves. The viewer's right shoe is the left one mirrored, or the right drawing as drawn (not mirrored, with the opposite slope) | The owner: toes turned out is fine. Tilting the whole drawing, the bench's first try, leant a boot's shaft away from the leg. |
| Each shoe is drawn by Claude in two parts: `behind` (what is behind the leg seen from the front: the back of a collar or a shaft, a pull tab at the back, the lining seen inside the opening) and `front` (everything else). The figure draws `behind` before the wearer, so the leg covers it, and `front` where the front view was drawn (after the legs and the socks, before the trousers), both in the clip that keeps a shoe within the legs under trousers that drop over it. The item page's picture and the image Claude checks draw behind, then the leg, then front | The owner's 3D rule: on step C's boot the pull tab at the back of the boot was drawn over the sock, but seen from the front it is behind the leg. |
| The automatic check that nothing hides the leg more than 25 units above the opening's front now measures only the part in front of the leg, and holds for every kind of shoe (for a boot, above the front of its shaft's top); a failure goes back to Claude in the next check as before. A step C test that said a boot was free of it now says a boot is held to it, unless what covers the leg is behind it | It is what makes Claude put a back pull tab behind the leg. Before the parts, a boot could not be held to it: its tab had nowhere else to go. |
| The sheet's `top_y` now names the back of the opening (the top of a low shoe's collar at the back, the top of a boot's shaft at the back, not a pull tab above it), no longer the drawing's highest point | It is where cropped trousers stop above the shoe (below). Counting a hidden pull tab left ankle-length trousers well above the boot the owner approved. |
| The hem rests on the drawing: for a drawn shoe, the front view's `opening` is the sheet's `opening_front_y`, its `instep` the sheet's `instep_y` (kept above a low shoe's opening front and below a boot's, by the sheet's kind, which is how the figure tells a shaft the hem rests on) and its `opening_back` the sheet's `top_y`, put into the front view's own coordinates; its ankle, its toe and how wide it is round its opening stay the front view's | The bench sheets did this, and the owner said the hem looks right: with the hem placed for the front view, the trousers covered the dress shoe's laces and most of the boat shoe. |
| Over a drawn shoe, full-length trousers keep their own length: never longer than it takes for their hem's front to just meet an ordinary sneaker's collar (the frame's y 50), so over a lower shoe (a boat shoe, a loafer) the sock shows; and the sock is drawn under full-length trousers there. Template and traced shoes keep today's hem and sock exactly | The owner, on the bench sheets: trousers that just touch an ordinary sneaker should leave the sock showing above a low-profile shoe. The figure's own length (5 cm above the ground) would bury a low shoe drawn at this size. Front views are unchanged, so their tests and drawings are too. |
| A drawing is kept as version 2, each shoe `{behind, front}`. A drawing kept as version 1 (one drawing per shoe) is all in front and stays shown as it is, on the item page and on the figure; nothing is drawn again by itself | The owner: never redraw unless I ask. **Draw again** gives a version 2 drawing, with a boot's tab behind the leg. |
| The item list carries each shoe's drawing state and a short hash of its drawing; the outfit figure on Today and a trip's days is asked for again when that hash changes, and the list is asked for again every 10 seconds while a shoe waits to be drawn or is being drawn | So an open Today or Trips view shows the new drawing after a redraw without being reopened. |
| The figure's canvas makes room for a drawn shoe: the frame's width, the toe turned out, and up to 30 units above the back of its opening (a pull tab, the collar's sides) | A figure of the shoes alone was fitted to the smaller front view and cut a boot's tab at the edge. With the wearer drawn the canvas is the same as before. |
| A drawn sandal has no bare foot drawn with the leg; the leg goes into the drawing as into a shoe | The front view's bare foot is not the drawing's, and would show beside it. The drawing brief is written for shoes with an opening; sandals have not been tried. |
| The demo closet's brown boots carry a drawing made in advance from the boot the owner liked (`backend/tests/data/shoe_drawn/boot.svg`, all in front of the leg, its pull tab the one at the front, which the owner wants shown (16 November; it was first split with the tab behind the leg, taken for a back tab): `closet/drawing/shoe_examples/boot.*.svg`; a test keeps it equal to that file), with a feature sheet written for it, route `demo`. It is not shown to Claude as a worked example | The owner can see a drawn shoe on the figure in the demo without sending anything to Claude. The worked examples stay the three the owner liked for that (above). The demo's boots are tagged as a laced service boot, so their side drawing and their drawing for the figure (a Chelsea boot) are not the same kind of boot. |

### The owner's correction of a shoe's drawing (step E)

Built on 15 November 2026, the fourth step into the app of "drawn from understanding" above. The owner's words: "A
place on the item page to type a one-line correction; the redraw changes only what the note asks and is checked
against the photos, not only the note." From the plan the owner approved: the old drawing is kept so the owner can go
back to it in one tap, and if the note goes against the photos the app says so in one line. It builds on round 4 of
the experiment (`bench/experiments/shoe_drawn/CORRECT.md`, in the git history): the boot's one-line correction worked in one round with
nothing else changed, but the runner's toe correction made the drawing less true, because the note was wrong about the
shoe. The code is `backend/closet/shoedraw.py` (`correct`, `back`, the worker), `backend/closet/tagger/shoedrawing.py`
(`correct`, its prompts) and `frontend/src/components/ShoeDrawn.tsx`; `tools/correct_shoe_drawing.py` runs one
correction outside the app.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A correction is one call on the model of the `drawing` role, at the drawing's effort, through the same routes (the plan first, the API key only when switched on). Claude is shown the photos named by their views, the drawing as it is (its render, `drawing.png`, its sheet and its SVG, each shoe behind and in front of the leg), the owner's earlier notes on it and the new note, and changes only what the note asks and what must change with it, keeping every other shape with the same coordinates and colours; it copies the sheet, changing only what the note changes, and says in one plain sentence what it changed | Round 4's boot: asked for broad elastic panels, the model starting from its drawing changed only those, in one round. Starting again from the photos would lose what the owner already liked. |
| The app, not Claude, writes the owner's notes into the sheet (`owner_notes`), and keeps each note with the drawing it made: `notes`, a list of `{note, at, against_photos, changed}` in the drawing as kept | The sheet's shape is the drawing's, shared with every call; the notes are the owner's words and must stay exactly as typed. |
| The change is then checked against the PHOTOS, at most two times (`MAX_CORRECT_ROUNDS`), with the photos, the drawing before the note (`before.png`) and after it (`drawing.png`), the note and what the automatic checks of step C find. The check fixes anything else the change broke (compared with the drawing before), never undoes or weakens the owner's change, and stops as soon as Claude says good and the automatic checks find nothing | The owner's lesson from the runner: "a correction note can be wrong about the shoe; the redraw should be checked against the photos, not only against the note." Two rounds, not three: a correction changes one thing in a drawing already checked, and four of step C's five drawings were said good after one check. |
| When the note asks for something the photos do not show, the shoe is drawn as the owner asked, and the page says so in one plain line (`against_photos`, for example "The photos show the toe rubber lower than your note asks; drawn as you asked."). The last check's word on it is kept (it looked at the change beside the photos); with no check, the correction's own | The owner's word wins over the photos, as it does for the views and the details; but the runner showed a note can be wrong, so the owner is told, and can go back in one tap. Refusing the note, or drawing what the photos show instead, would take the decision away from the owner. |
| Before drawing a correction, Claude checks in every photo each thing the note says or takes for granted about the shoe (where a part is, at the front or the back, on which side; how many there are; its colour, size and height), and the check does the same; the example in the instructions is a neutral one (stripes on one side), not a case tried | The first real correction, "The pull tab is at the back of the boot, so from the front it should be hidden behind the leg", was wrong about the shoe: the side photo shows a tab at each end. The first check did not say so. With these instructions the same note was drawn as asked and the page said "The photos show two pull tabs, one at the back and one at the front of the opening; the back one is hidden behind the leg as you asked." The runner's toe correction had taught the same: a note can be wrong about the shoe. |
| **Decided with the owner (16 November).** A pull tab or loop at the FRONT of a shoe's collar or shaft shows, drawn in front of the leg, centred on it: the one exception to the rule that nothing of a shoe hides the leg more than 1.5 cm above its opening (the four front-shoe rules; for drawings, step C's automatic check, 25 frame units). The sheet says so (`front_pull_tab`); the check then leaves a strip 28 units wide in the middle of the leg (x 86 to 114) to the tab, still allows nothing else there, and refuses a tab rising more than 60 units above the opening; it finds the opening's front edge beside the tab (x 76 to 84). A tab at the back stays behind the leg | The owner's boots have a pull tab at each end (seen in the side photo); the one at the front is in front of the shin and shows on the real boot. Asked whether it should show or be hidden, the owner said: "It should show the pull tab". The demo boot's single tab is that front tab, so it is drawn in front again. |
| The drawing before the last change is kept (`items.drawn_prev`), one level only, and **Go back to the drawing before** swaps the two; the same button then reads **Go to the newer drawing**. A new drawing (**Draw again**) keeps the one before it the same way | The plan: the old drawing kept, one tap back. One level, swapped, lets the owner compare the two as often as they like without a history to manage. A new drawing from scratch replaces a drawing the owner may have liked as much as a correction does, so it gets the same way back; it could have been kept for corrections only. |
| The owner's notes stay with the drawing they made: going back takes the drawing's notes with it, and **Draw again** gives the current drawing's notes (the latest 20, `NOTES_SENT`) to the draw call and its checks, as the owner's word, and the new drawing carries them on | The owner's notes are not lost when the shoe is drawn from scratch. A note the owner went back from, as from the runner's toe, was wrong, and is not given again. |
| The note is one line, at most 200 characters (spaces and line breaks made one space); an empty or longer note is refused with a plain sentence. It waits in its own column, `items.drawn_note`, with the shoe `waiting`, and is made as `correcting` by the shoe drawing worker, with the drawing's rules: Claude out of reach gives the attempt back and the page says so, three tries in all, `failures.report` once it gives up | A column keeps the note apart from the drawing, which does not change until the new one is done, and from `drawn_error`, which says what holds the shoe up. One worker, so a shoe is never drawn and changed at once. |
| A correction that fails three times leaves the drawing as it was, `done`, with a plain message to send the note again or say it another way; not `failed` | `failed` offers **Try again**, which draws the shoe from scratch: a costly answer to a note that could not be drawn. |
| A correction does not wait for the photos to be sorted; it uses them as they are, a photo not sorted yet named as a view not known | The drawing it changes already exists; waiting on a re-sort after a photo was added would hold up a one-line change for nothing. |
| One change at a time: a note is refused (409) while the shoe waits to be drawn, is being drawn or a correction waits or is being made; **Draw again** while a correction waits changes nothing; going back is refused while either is on its way | A change is made from the drawing as it was when the note was sent; swapping or redrawing under it would change a drawing the owner is not looking at. |
| Recorded with its own purpose, `correct_drawing`, and its own estimate (`spend.estimate_correct_drawing`: a 55,000-token prompt, every image sent, the longest answer, 32,000 tokens; about $0.90 a request at the prices written down) | The owner can tell corrections from drawings in the record of calls; the estimate must hold for the longest request the app can write (a drawing at its size cap, 20 earlier notes). |
| The page shows the change's own time and cost ("Changed in 41 s on your Claude plan, checked once against the photos · about $0.12 if changed on the API key") and what changed ("Changed as you asked: “…”. …"); what **Draw again** says it costs stays the drawing's (`drawn_cost_usd`) | The owner asked to be told the time and cost; a change costs far less than a drawing, and the question before drawing again must not understate it. |
| A drawing kept as version 1 (all in front of the leg) is given as such and comes back as version 2, behind and in front of the leg | Step D: the owner's 3D rule. A boot drawn in step C, whose pull tab sits over the sock, was the first case tried (the owner's boots turned out to have a tab at the front and another at the back; see the row on checking a note's facts). |
| Never in the demo closet (409, with a plain sentence) | As for drawing: the demo closet is never sent to Claude. |

### A shoe drawn turned out, in two steps (17 November 2026)

The owner asked to revisit the drawing's angle once the shoes were drawn from scratch rather than traced, and found
the first turned drawings (12° and 20°, drawn turned directly from the photos) not good enough: the GAT's suede panels
wrong, a cap toe and a boat shoe's vamp stitching turning the wrong way, a running shoe's curve lost. The owner's
conditions: "compromising on design or forcing it to be manually corrected by me is not the solution", and check the
perspective, which had been misjudged before. After the overnight runs the owner found the new drawings satisfactory
and chose the fully automatic way (option A, about $2 a shoe at API prices): "The new drawings are satisfactory. I
would continue with your recommendation." The code is `backend/closet/drawing/turned.py` (the 3D turn),
`backend/closet/drawing/photocheck.py` (the photos cut to the shoe, the light material measured),
`backend/closet/tagger/shoedrawing.py` (`draw`: the steps below) and `backend/closet/drawing/drawn.py` (`TURNED`, the
turned view's frame, checks and preview).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **Decided with the owner.** The drawing is turned out 12°, as a man stands, seen from in front and 16° above; the figure draws it as drawn (no shear). A straight-on drawing (a sandal, a drawing kept before, or one whose turn failed) is turned by the figure's shear as before | The owner, comparing 12° and 20°: 12° looks natural. A turn drawn in place of a shear shows the inner side, which comes into view as the foot turns, as the photos show it. |
| Two steps: Claude draws the shoe straight on, with the brief and the three worked examples tuned in the earlier rounds; the app then turns that drawing with its own 3D shoe (`turned.py`), and Claude draws the shoe turned with that computed turn (`computed.png`) and the 3D shoe's outline at 12° (`guide.png`, with its lines as coordinates) as its reference, keeping every panel and mark of the straight-on drawing | Drawing the turn directly from the photos made Claude invent the perspective, and it turned details the wrong way. The straight-on drawing holds everything the earlier rounds tuned; the turn is then computed, not guessed. Measured on the owner's five shoes: the details move with the surface under them (no drift), lines across the shoe tilt 1.7° to 2.5° with the turn where the 3D shoe tilts them 2.3°, and a cap toe's seam turns outwards with the toe. Claude redraws the outline itself, because an outline is not painted on the surface and warping it gave kinked silhouettes (the owner: "less smooth"). |
| The 3D shoe is the front view's solid (`frontshoe.STYLES`, a runner with its own rocker sole), seen by a camera 70 cm away; the turn of a drawing is an exact mapping of points on the solid's surface (its top, the sole's walls, a boot's shaft up to 30 cm), smoothed into one cubic fit (`turned.Turn`) that leaves the collar or shaft upright above a hinge, easing in over 30 units. Its style is the item's own (from its description, with the owner's corrections), else read from the drawing's sheet; a sandal stays straight on | A smooth fit carries thin lines and small shapes without kinks. The hinge is the one the figure's shear already uses (a boot's instep, a low shoe's opening), so a shaft stays round the leg. |
| Two straight-on drawings are made side by side (three at first; see the row on what it costs), each checked against the photos at most three times; Claude, as a judge, compares them with the photos and keeps the more faithful. Two turned drawings are made the same way and judged against the photos and the straight-on drawing. The faults the judge names in the turned drawing it keeps are given back as a note (`correct`, at most twice checked); the fix is kept only when the judge prefers it to the turn before. All on the `drawing` role's model at the drawing's effort | Run to run the same prompt gave drawings of very different faithfulness, and a check of one drawing on its own said good to drawings with plain faults (the GAT's panels). Asked to compare drawings side by side with the photos, Claude found the faults that a check of one drawing let through. The owner asked for a reliable first result, not one to correct by hand. |
| A drawing the automatic checks find a fault in is not chosen while another has none: the judge sees every candidate and ranks them, and the choice is its best-ranked one with no measured fault (all of them when every one has some). A fix of the turned drawing that has a measured fault the turn before it had not is set aside without being judged | A measured fault (off the ground, the leg hidden, the light material's share against the front photo, a boot's shaft leaning) is certain where the judge's eye is not. |
| Carrying a drawing with the turn moves every piece of every line and curve with the surface under it: a segment whose moved control points would not follow the turn to within half a unit is split in halves until it does (at most 64 pieces); one the turn carries whole is written as before | Built first, only each segment's own points were moved. The first run through the app's code on the owner's shoes showed what that does to a Chelsea boot: its sides, single lines from the shaft's top down into the foot, leaned from their unmoved top to their moved bottom, and the turned drawing copied the lean, widening the elastic side on the outer side, which a turn out narrows. The turned drawing given to Claude grows by about 1,000 to 3,000 characters. |
| The guide's boot (a boot, a Chelsea boot, a high top) outlines the shaft, hulled with the heel it stands on, together with the foot, row by row, instead of one hull of the shaft and the whole foot | One hull ran a straight line from the top of the shaft to the toe; turned, it leaned the shaft out towards the toe, against the brief's "the shaft stands upright". The guide and the computed turn now agree with the brief: the shaft upright, the foot turning below it. |
| A turned boot's shaft is measured against the computed turn above the hinge (where the turn moves nothing): either side more than 8 units from it (in nine rows of ten) is an automatic problem for the next check, in plain words (`turned.upright_problems`). Low shoes have too little above their hinge to measure | It found the leaning boot (19 units out) and said nothing on the other four drawings of that run or on the five drawings the owner approved after the overnight runs. |
| The owner's earlier notes on the shoe go to every call of a drawing from scratch, the judge's included: a candidate that keeps to them is right there, one that goes against them has a fault | Otherwise the judge, comparing with the photos only, could prefer a drawing that undoes the owner's note. |
| The photos are cut to the shoe (with a margin of 12% of its size) by the app's background model before they are sent, and the light material's share of the upper's front (white leather, white mesh) is measured in the front photo and in each straight-on drawing on a 24 × 24 grid; more than 8% apart is an automatic problem for the next check. Only when the drawing has a large light area and a large, clearly darker one beside it; never for a shoe of one colour or of near-whites. Without the model yet, the photos go whole and the measure is left out | A front photo taken from a metre away has the shoe in a third of its width. The GAT's panels were the fault the checks missed most; on the owner's photos the measure caught both wrong GATs and said nothing on eleven right drawings (a Chelsea boot and a canvas plimsoll with a grey toe among them, where a measure without that guard found faults that were not there). |
| The GAT worked example (`shoe_examples/trainer.svg`) was corrected: white leather sides with the grey suede eyestay band and toe cap, its centre strip running up to the lacing | Claude copied the example's panels onto the owner's GAT. The owner corrected two attempts at the corrected example ("too much suede", "the shape of the white wedges is not right") and approved the third. |
| The turned frame is x −100 to 300 (the straight one 0 to 200), the same leg, ground and scale; the automatic checks keep the drawing inside x −98 to 298 and a low shoe's top below y −40; the preview and check picture are 640 wide | The turned toe reaches past x 0, and the back of the collar rises a little in the view from above. |
| The stand-in leg under a turned drawing stops just below where the shoe first wraps all the way round it (`drawn.leg_down`), measured on each drawing | The owner saw a small line outside the dress shoe's and the wingtip's silhouette, on the inner side by the sole: the leg's lower corner, drawn to a fixed depth, showed past the turned shoe's lower edge. |
| Kept as version 3, with `view` (`turned` or `front`), `style` and the straight-on drawing it was turned from (`straight`, for a later fix). A version 2 drawing is shown straight on, as before, and never drawn again by itself | The owner: never redraw unless asked. **Draw again** gives the turned drawing. A correction keeps the drawing's view. |
| The turned drawing's geometry is read with `svgelements==1.9.6` (MIT, pure Python, no dependencies), imported only when a drawing is turned | Every point of every shape has to be moved with the surface, arcs, curves and transforms included; Pillow and resvg do not give a path's points. |
| What it costs and how long it takes: on the owner's five shoes through the app's code (three photos each: outer side, front, inner side) drawn twice, $1.58 to $3.08 a shoe ($2.14 on average) and, after the turn was fixed (above), $1.44 to $3.35 ($2.19), at the API key's prices; 3.6 to 10.1 minutes a shoe. The running shoe and the GAT cost most: their drawings took more check rounds. **Draw again** says "four to ten minutes" and roughly $2.00 (`shoedraw.ABOUT_USD`, until the shoe has a drawing of its own to go by) | The owner was told about $2 a shoe at API prices before choosing it. |
| **Decided with the owner (17 November).** Two straight-on drawings instead of three. Three ways to spend less were tried on the owner's five shoes, one run each: a check that finds nothing wrong saying only "good" instead of copying the drawing out again ($1.71 a shoe), two straight-on drawings ($1.93) and three drafts judged before any check, only the kept one checked ($1.65), against $2.14 and $2.19 for the two runs before | The owner, on the drawings side by side: the lean checks were "by far the worst of the three in terms of detail", two drawings the best and judging first a close second. One run each is within the run-to-run spread, so this is the owner's eye on these five shoes, not a measured difference. |
| A drawing's calls mark the last of the shoe's photos for the prompt cache (`correct_api`'s `cache_photos` as a number: how many of the first images are the photos), not the last image of the request; and each call's shown cost prices what the cache wrote at its write price and what it read at its read price (`Call.cache_write_tokens`), as the month's spend already does. Built alongside "Prompt caching on the API key" above, and merged into it | A cache is read back only by a request that is the same up to where an earlier one was marked. Marked on the last image, a check (photos, then its own render) and the judge (photos, then the candidates) never matched what came before and only wrote; marked on the last photo, which every call of a drawing starts with, every call after the first ones reads the photos back. Worked out from the runs' calls, it saves about 3 to 6% of a drawing on the API key, and at worst (nothing read back) adds about 4 cents. The owner asked for it without a live test: it cannot change a drawing, only what is paid for it. Not measured on the API yet: the session that built it had no API key it could use without the key also reaching the plan route's requests. |
| Three photos are enough for the drawing: the outer side, the front and the inner side (the README says those matter most; the page still takes up to six) | The owner asked how many photos are needed. The five shoes were drawn from these three alone, and the owner found the drawings satisfactory. |

### A shoe in one step on Add

Built on 16 November 2026. The owner found adding a shoe clunky: Add, then "A shoe", forced one side photo first (toe
to the left, filling an outline), checked at once whether it would "trace well", then "Add this shoe", then a separate
step for up to five more photos. That flow was built for tracing, which is no longer what the figure shows: the shoe
on the figure is drawn by Claude from all of its photos (step C). The owner approved ("yeah do both") this plan: "A
shoe in one step: you take or choose all of a shoe's photos at once (1 to 6, any order). Closet sorts them as it
already does, uses the outer side as the closet picture, and asks only when it can't tell the sides apart. The
outline becomes a tip, not a gate; the 'will it trace' check goes, and Closet speaks up only if there's no side photo
or it's the pair. A clearer switch at the top: 'Clothes, one per photo / Several in one photo / A shoe'." The code is
`frontend/src/screens/ShoeAdd.tsx` (since folded into `Add.tsx`; see "One Add for clothes and shoes"), `backend/closet/shoeviews.py` (`promote`), `tracejobs.told` and migration 0029.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **Approved by the owner, 16 November.** "A shoe" on Add takes all of a shoe's photos at once, 1 to 6, in any order: **Choose photos** picks several, **Take a photo** adds one each time it is tapped (where the page can show the camera, at a secure address, the camera stays open between photos until the owner closes it). They wait in a row of thumbnails, each with a way to remove it, and nothing is sent until **Add this shoe**: the first photo makes the shoe (`POST /api/items` with `shoe`), the others are added to it one by one (`POST /api/items/{id}/photos`). A seventh photo is not added, and the page says so in a sentence. This replaces "Add has a third mode, "A shoe"" (stage 2b) and "A shoe has its main photo (the guided side photo) and up to 5 more" (step B) | The owner found the guided side photo, the instant check and the separate step for more photos clunky. Sending nothing until the end lets the owner change their mind about a photo without leaving a half-made shoe in the closet. |
| **Approved by the owner, 16 November.** No outline is shown or asked for; the page says in a few words which photos help: one shoe on its own, not the pair; its side, the other side, from above, the front and the back; a plain floor or wall behind it, and even light; any order. The instant check is gone, with its route (`POST /api/shoes/check`) and its code (`tracejobs.check`) | The owner: the outline becomes a tip, not a gate, and the "will it trace" check goes. The outline only made sense laid over a side photo to line it up, and with the photos in any order the first need not be the side. Add was the check's only caller, so removing it was contained. |
| **Approved by the owner, 16 November.** The main photo follows the sorting (`shoeviews.promote`): once a shoe's photos are sorted (after the owner's answer, when asked), a main photo that is not a side view gives way to the outer side, else the inner side; among several of the same side, one the owner named, then one Claude was sure of, then the oldest. That photo becomes the closet picture (cut out as usual; its own thumbnail shows until then) and the photo traced for the shoe's side drawing (the old trace goes, the owner's fixes stay). ~~A main photo that is already a side view~~ A main photo that is the outer side (*22 November*: an inner side main photo gives way to an outer side photo too), or a shoe with no side photo, stays as it is. The old main photo stays with the others, with its view, and can be removed like them; the main photo is still replaced only with Retake | The owner: "uses the outer side as the closet picture". With the photos in any order, the first is often not a side. Waiting for the owner's answer when the sides are asked about avoids making the inner side the picture of a shoe whose mark is on the outside. ~~Never swapping one side photo for another keeps the rule simple.~~ *Changed (the owner, 22 November):* "for the shoe i'm noticing the photo it chooses to save as default is the inside of the shoe, not the outside of the shoe? so the drawing and cutout differ quite a bit." Claude's side drawing shows the outer side, so a closet picture of the inner side was a different shoe to look at. A main photo the owner named the inner side gives way too: their word is on the view, which stays, not on which photo is the picture (they choose the main photo only by Retake, and a retaken photo is sorted with the others and follows the same rule, as a retaken photo from above already did). Shoes sorted before are put right by the cut-out queue at its next round (`shoeviews.catch_up`: a sorted shoe whose main photo is the inner side and that has an outer side photo), without asking Claude again; a shoe put right no longer matches, so each changes once, and no migration is needed. A shoe sorted before 16 November whose main photo is not a side view still stays as it is (the row below). |
| A view the owner named wins over Claude's when the main photo is chosen, as it does in sorting. A shoe kept from before is not changed until its photos are sorted again (a photo added or removed); the demo closet is never sorted, so its main photos never change | The owner's word always wins (step B). As for sorting and drawing, nothing changes for shoes the owner is not working on. |
| Claude's description is not asked for again when the main photo changes. Its words on "the first image" (how many shoes it shows and from where: `shoes_in_photo`, `shoe_view`) stay with the photo it saw first, kept as `items.tag_photo_id` (migration 0029: an item described before is given its main photo when the description is not older than that photo, the rule used until now), so a photo that becomes the main one is traced without them. The cut-out queue describes an item again after a cutout only when the photo is newer than the description (a new or retaken photo), not for a photo Claude has already seen | Describing again would cost a call for each such shoe and could change a description the owner has already checked. Without the column, Claude's word about a photo from above would turn down the trace of the side photo that took its place. *Changed (the owner, 19 November):* the photo Claude described, cut out again (the second background model redoing a see-through cutout, a plaid's made again to leave out the bedding), is described again from the new cutout, once; items already in that state are found and described again (`jobs.OLDER_CUTOUT`, `jobs.redescribe`). A shoe's other photo becoming the main one is still not described again. 0.9 had cut the owner's striped linen shirt out again cleanly but kept what Claude had said from its see-through cutout (a print, and black), so it was drawn as a patchwork of its photo called black. |
| A shoe not traced yet (none since its main photo changed) waits to be traced while its photos wait to be sorted or are being sorted, unless something holds the sorting up (Claude out of reach, the monthly limit) | Sorting may make another photo the main one: tracing the first photo would only tell the owner, for a few seconds, that it is not a side view, next to a Retake button they do not need. |
| The sorting prompt no longer says photo 1 is "usually of the side": photo 1 is the main photo for now, and the owner took the photos in any order, so any of them, photo 1 too, may show any view | Photo 1 is now whichever photo the owner picked first; saying it is usually the side would lean Claude towards calling it a side, and then the outer side would not become the picture. |
| **Approved by the owner, 16 November.** Closet speaks up about the photos only when none is a side view once they are sorted (on the shoe's page, under its photos: "None of these photos shows the shoe from the side. A photo of its side would help the drawing.") or when Claude's description saw the pair in the main photo, which the shoe's page already says from the trace ("The photo shows both shoes of a pair, so it's drawn from its details instead. A photo of one shoe on its own, side-on, gives a closer drawing.", with Retake). No new call to Claude for it | The owner: Closet speaks up only if there's no side photo or it's the pair. The description already says how many shoes its first photo shows; asking about every photo would be a new call. |
| The switch at the top of Add reads "Clothes, one per photo", "Several in one photo" and "A shoe"; its name for screen readers is "What you're adding". On a phone the two longer choices take two lines each, split evenly | The owner asked for a clearer switch. Three choices this long cannot fit one line at 390 px without making the text smaller than elsewhere in the app. |

### A shoe's side drawn by Claude (20 November 2026)

Decided by the owner, who called the traced side drawing "really bad": the shoe's side drawing (its own drawing: the
closet tile when there is no cut-out, the Wardrobe's Drawings view, the item page) is drawn by Claude from the shoe's
photos, the way its front is drawn for the outfit figure. The owner's wider aim, which this serves and which every
later drawing step is held to: the drawing is the standard picture across the app, in one clean style; an item's
name tells near-identical items apart, so the drawing need not; but every drawing step should capture what is
particular to that item (its panels, marks, sole and proportions), and the owner will keep pushing on that. Before it
was built, one call with a first brief (no check) drew the open-licence held sneaker in the app's style with its
perforated toe box, panel seams, ribbed sole and painted mark, but about two thirds of its true height for its length,
with too pointed a toe; so the app version writes down proportions first, measures them where it can, and checks.
The code is `backend/closet/drawing/drawnside.py` (the frame, made safe, the checks, the card), `tagger/shoedrawing.py`
(`draw_side`, `_side`, the prompts) and `backend/closet/shoedraw.py` (kept with the drawing, drawn by itself).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **Decided by the owner.** The side is drawn by Claude, in the templates' frame and style: the 400 × 460 card, exactly side-on, toe to the left at x 40, the back of the heel at x 360, the ground at y 330, the bold outline 3.8 and inner lines 1.5 in #26231f, flat fills, no lettering (a letter-shaped logo as a shape) | It is the picture that stands for the shoe beside every other drawing in the closet, so it must sit in the grid as the templates and the traced drawing do. |
| Tracing is not removed. The trace stays the picture until Claude's side exists and whenever it cannot be made or read, and it is now also a measurer: a shoe with a finished trace gives the side its heights as shares of its length (`drawnside.measures`: the sole under the heel and the toe, the top at the toe box, the throat, the collar's lowest point and the heel), sent as numbers "measured from your photo" and checked | A standing wish keeps shoe tracing. Its outline is the one part of a trace that was always good, and proportions were the first try's main fault. |
| The side is one draw call and at most two checks (`MAX_SIDE_ROUNDS`), through the same routes, model, effort, purpose (`draw_shoe`) and monthly limit as the front; the checks stop as soon as Claude says good and the automatic checks find nothing; a failed check keeps the drawing made so far | Two checks, as for a correction: one shoe, one view, and no judge between candidates. The same purpose keeps a shoe's drawing in one line of the record of calls. |
| The prompt starts from the first brief, in the house manner of the front's: the photos named by their sorted views (the side photos matter most, the outer side above all), the front drawing's sheet (kind, palette, parts, signature) so both drawings agree, the owner's notes, the app's two plain template drawings (a sneaker and a boot) as renders and as STYLE examples only, and a first step that writes down the proportions read from the side photo before drawing. Its answer also lists 2 to 6 things that make the shoe itself from the side, kept and shown under it | The proportions written first are what the drawing is then held to. The templates show the frame and the line weight but are plain because they know nothing of the shoe; their SVG text is not sent, since it uses clip paths an answer may not. The list puts the owner's aim in words the page can show. |
| Automatic checks, measured on a render (`drawnside.checks`): on the ground line (y 324 to 336), on the card, the toe tip at x 28 to 54 and the heel at x 346 to 372, the toe on the left (the left end not taller than the right), nothing above y 60; with measures, the height at the heel and at the toe box (15% in from the toe) for its length within 15% of the trace's (at least 6 units at the toe box) | 15% allows for a photo taken up to about 30° off side-on (which foreshortens the length, so a trace's heights come out high) and still catches a drawing a third too low, as the first try was. The heel and the toe box, not the highest point: a bow of the laces or a pull tab may stand above the collar in one and not the other. |
| In `draw`, the side is made once the straight-on drawing is chosen, in its own thread beside the turns, through its own record of calls; its calls are added to the drawing's (its tokens and cost are in the drawing's totals), its rounds are its own (the page's "checked N times" is the front's). A side that fails, for any reason, is left out: the front is kept as it is | The owner already finds four to ten minutes long, so the side is not made after the turns, which would add its two to three minutes to every drawing. |
| A drawing never has more than two calls under way (`shoedrawing.CALLS_AT_ONCE`): the turns, the side, the judge and the fix wait for one of two places (`_limited`). So two shoes drawn at once (`shoedraw.AT_ONCE`) are still at most four Claude Code processes on the plan, as "Two shoes drawn at once" decided; a side drawn by itself makes one call at a time | Beside the two turns, an unlimited side made three calls per shoe, six with two shoes: half as many again as the four that decision allowed, and a rate limit met inside a check quietly costs that drawing a check. Sharing two places, the side's three calls mostly fill the time when only one call is under way (the judge, the fix and its checks), so it adds a call's time or so to a drawing rather than none; "four to ten minutes" stays, as no shoe has been timed since. |
| A side check that says good, with no automatic problem and no fix set aside, stops being read there, as a front check does: in its schema `good`, `problems` and `features` come before `svg`, so the answer is made of them and the drawing already held (`shoedrawing._side_good_as_is`, on the API route only, counted as "A check that says good stops being read there" counts it) | The same saving and the same rule as for the front's checks; `_check` does not serve the side, whose drawing is one SVG text, so it gets its own small hook. |
| The side is kept in the drawing's `side` entry in `items.drawn` (its SVG made safe, its features, proportions, rounds, good, problems, calls, cost and seconds). **Draw again** makes both; a correction from the owner's note about the front changes the front and keeps the side as it is (a note about the side: see "Which drawing a note is about: correcting the side"); going back to the drawing before takes its own side with it. No migration: the side lives in the drawing it was made with | One drawing, one side: going back to a drawing should bring back the side made with it, and a correction of the front should not cost a new side. |
| A kept drawing with no side (drawn before sides were, or whose side failed) is drawn its side by itself (`run_side`) in one of the worker's `AT_ONCE` places that no waiting shoe needs, at most one side at a time (`SIDES_AT_ONCE`), at most three tries, counted in the drawing itself (`side_tries`, so a new drawing starts again from none); Claude out of reach or the monthly limit gives the try back and stops, as for a drawing, without a message on the page; never in the demo closet, nor a drawing made for it. Tests standing in for Claude draw no sides by themselves unless they give a stand-in. The shoe is taken inside the `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction that counts its try and marked busy in the worker, like a shoe being drawn: no other place takes it again (its tries are counted, but it has no side until it is done), and `due` skips it, so a drawing or a correction asked for meanwhile waits until the side is kept; `recover` never touches it (it only sets back shoes `drawing` or `correcting`) | The owner's shoes already drawn should get a side without being drawn again at four to ten minutes and about $2 each. One side at a time keeps a place free, so a shoe that becomes due starts at once; a side is one call at a time, so it never adds more to the plan than one drawing's share. A correction made while the side was drawn would otherwise copy the drawing as it was before the side and lose it. |
| Which drawing is the item's (`illustrations.make`): the owner's "Use the plain drawing" still wins; else Claude's side when it exists and can be read; else the trace; else the template. The stored illustration is made again whenever the drawing changes (a new drawing, a correction, going back, a side drawn by itself), so the closet tile follows | The owner's choice of the plain drawing has always won over what the app drew. |
| The shoe's page shows the side beside the drawing for the figure under **Drawn by Claude from its photos**, with one line of what makes it itself ("From the side: …"). In Fix the details, while the picture is Claude's side, the trace's part colours, marks, Re-trace and Undo my fixes are not offered (one line says why: "The picture is Claude's drawing of the shoe, so the traced colours and marks are not used."); **Use the plain drawing** stays, and once chosen it is undone with **Use Claude's drawing**. The owner's fixes to the trace are kept, for when the trace is the picture again. The line under the picture that a photo was turned down for tracing ("so it's drawn from its details instead") is not shown while the picture is Claude's side | The smallest honest handling: controls that change only the trace must not be offered as if they changed the picture. Correcting the side itself with a note is left for later. |

### Which drawing a note is about: correcting the side (21 November 2026)

The owner can correct the side drawing with a one-line note, as the front (step E above). Built on the side drawing
and the front's correction; the design is the orchestrating session's, approved for building. The code is
`backend/closet/shoedraw.py` (`correct` with `about`, `run_one` with the side corrector), `backend/closet/tagger/
shoedrawing.py` (`correct_side`, its prompts) and `frontend/src/components/ShoeDrawn.tsx`; `tools/correct_shoe_drawing.py
--side` runs one outside the app.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **The owner says which drawing a note is about.** Once the shoe has a side, the note box offers **The front** and **The side** (the front unless the owner chooses); with no side, no choice shows and a note is about the front, as before. The endpoint takes `about`, `"front"` (the default) or `"side"`; anything else is refused (422), and `"side"` on a drawing with no side is refused (409, "This drawing has no side drawing to change yet.") | Predictable: nothing changes that the owner did not point at. One note for both drawings would spend a call and risk a change on a drawing that was right; letting Claude guess which drawing a note means would sometimes guess wrong, and a wrong guess changes the drawing the owner liked. Asking costs one tap and keeps each correction one call and its checks. |
| A side correction is one call and at most two checks (`MAX_CORRECT_ROUNDS`), on the model of the front's: Claude is shown the photos, the side as it is (its render at both sizes and its SVG), what makes the shoe itself from the side, the measured proportions when the shoe has a trace, the owner's earlier notes on the side and the new note; it changes only what the note asks, in the same frame and style, checks the note against every photo, draws it as asked all the same, and says so in one plain sentence (`against_photos`). The checks compare the change with the photos and with the side before it (`before.png`) and never undo the owner's change | The same lessons as the front's correction: start from the drawing the owner already likes, and a note can be wrong about the shoe. |
| The automatic checks of a side correction are a fresh side's frame checks (`drawnside.checks` without the measures: on the ground line, on the card, the toe and the heel in place, the toe on the left, not too tall). The measures are shown to Claude, as words, not checked | A note such as "the shaft is taller" changes a height the trace measured; a measured check would then tell the check to redraw the shoe to the trace, undoing the owner's change. The owner's word wins over the measures, as it wins over the photos. |
| Recorded with the front's correction purpose, `correct_drawing`, and the same estimate (`spend.estimate_correct_drawing`; a test checks the longest side correction fits it) | A change from the owner's note, whichever drawing it is on, is one kind of call in the record; the side's prompt and its SVG are shorter than the front's two shoes. |
| Kept: the corrected side replaces the drawing's `side` (with `corrected`, its own calls, rounds, seconds and cost); the front is untouched, its SVGs, sheet, notes, `made_at` and cost the same. The drawing before goes to `drawn_prev`, as for any change, so **Go back to the drawing before** undoes a side change too. The kept drawing says the last change was to the side (`last_change`) and when (`changed_at`), which decides which of the two kept drawings is the newer | One level of going back for any change, as decided for the front. `made_at` stays the front's, so the front's picture is not versioned again and a side drawn by itself still finds the drawing it was made for. |
| The owner's notes on the side are kept in the drawing's `side_notes` (each `{note, at, against_photos, changed}`, as the front's `notes`), apart from the front's, outside the side entry. A correction of the front and a new drawing carry them on; every later side drawing (with the drawing, or by itself) and every later side correction is given them as the owner's word. Going back takes a drawing's own side notes with it | Kept apart, a note about the heel's height is never given to the front, which cannot show it. Outside the side entry, so a new drawing whose side fails still has them for the side drawn later by itself. |
| Which drawing a waiting note is about is kept in a new column, `items.drawn_note_about` ('side', or null for the front), by migration 0038 | The note itself is what the page shows back and what Claude is given; a prefix or a wrapper in it would leak into both. The kept drawing does not change until the change is done, so it is no place for a request that may still fail. |
| State and messages are the front's correction's (waiting, correcting, three attempts, Claude out of reach or the limit gives the attempt back, a failure leaves the drawing as it was), said of "the side drawing" where the note is about the side ("Changing the side drawing: “…”", "Waiting for Claude to change the side drawing: …") | One way of waiting and failing for every change; the owner should still see which drawing is changing. |
| After a side change, the line on what changed ("Changed as you asked: “…”. …"), the note against the photos and the change's time and cost sit under the side; the front's line on its time and cost is left out meanwhile (one line on how the last change went). The front's fields in the item keep their meaning (its last correction) and new ones say the side's (`correcting_side`, `side_corrected`, `side_note`, `side_changed`, `side_against_photos`) | The owner reads what changed beside the drawing that changed. Adding fields rather than changing the front's keeps every older reader of them right. |
| In Fix the details, while the picture is Claude's side, the line that says the traced colours and marks are not used now also says how to change the side: write what is wrong under **Drawn by Claude from its photos** and choose the side. No button: the note box is on the item page, outside the sheet | The natural lead from where the owner looks to change the picture, without a second place to write a note. |

## Patterns drawn from the photo

The owner's original spec asked for patterns to come from the photo; it fell out when the drawing moved into
the app (issue #37 said the app must not need OpenCV) and was never decided against. Rebuilt and tested on the
owner's own and other real photos (bench/experiments/patterns, with notes there; now in the git history).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| OpenCV is part of the app (`opencv-python-headless`), used by `closet/patterns` (the cutout's see-through check among it) and by shoe tracing. The drawing code imports it only inside the functions that read a photo (`trace.py` and `traced.py`, the fills measured from a cutout, the bench's traced style), never to draw an item from its tags | The owner chose quality over the program's size. A numpy/Pillow port was built and matched OpenCV, but the methods that mattered (template matching, k-means, image quilting) are better kept on the library the bench uses. |
| A plaid is flattened, not sampled: its repeat is found over the whole garment, every area matched against it to a tenth of a pixel, the crumpled cloth mapped back into one straight repeat (each front on its own when they agree), and that repeat drawn | Every square cut from a photo carried its folds, a pocket or the button strip, and mirrored squares made kaleidoscopes. Redrawing the plaid as flat bands lost the thin white lines. |
| A plaid with no regular repeat (madras) is measured whole and kept as one garment-sized tile | Quilting broke a plaid's lines. |
| A plaid is measured three ways, each tried when the one before finds nothing: seen straight on (as above); made regular from its own lines first (`patterns/straighten.py`); whole, as a madras. Whatever a way rebuilds is laid back over the photo and kept only when it explains the photo (a correlation of 0.5 for a repeat, 0.35 for the whole-garment measure); a straightened repeat must also cover 15% of the garment in 12 areas and not have been slid along by more than three repeats. A plaid none of them explains is not drawn: the item keeps its colour and says "Closet could not measure the checks from this photo… A photo of it laid flat on a plain surface, taken from straight above, usually works." | The owner's navy flannel jacket (17 November), photographed from the foot of a bed on a duvet, was drawn as a pale smear and called done: no repeat was found, so the whole-garment measure meant for madras ran over a cutout that was half bedding, and nothing checked its answer. On that photo the three good results agree 0.82 to 0.88 with the photo and the smear 0.05 to 0.17; the demo's madras agrees 0.53. The check also caught the straight-on way "finding" a repeat by chance in made-up cloth that has none (agreement 0.00). |
| Straightening uses only the plaid: its lines run two ways, and where each family of lines would meet in the photo gives the cloth's tilt, which is undone and the lines turned along the rows and columns (each area of the garment votes for the two directions, so a sleeve at another angle or bedding is outvoted); then the repeat is measured area by area and one smooth map fitted that makes it the same size everywhere | A photo from the foot of a bed has checks half as big again at the hem as at the collar (55 to 81 px on the jacket), and a garment over a hanger or a duvet is not flat, so undoing a tilt alone left the repeat 73 px at the hem and 88 at the chest. The repeat finder and the flattening both expect one size all over and lines along the rows and columns: a jacket turned 3° was already enough to lose it. |
| The repeat's length is checked for an overcheck: each small area is averaged along the lines and the two halves of a repeat twice as long compared; a line that is in one half and not the other, the same way in two repeats running, doubles the repeat | The jacket's checks repeat every 35 px and its white line every 70. Going by the checks alone the white line lands on half the repeats and averages away, which is exactly what the drawing was missing. |
| When the plaid made regular from its lines does not explain the photo, the repeats that the garment's whole band sequences suggest are tried in turn on the photo with only its tilt undone, and the one whose rebuilt repeat agrees best with the photo is kept, a shorter one unless a longer one agrees 0.05 better (`straighten.by_bands`, `measure._straightened`). Spacings shorter than a twelfth of the garment are left to the small areas | The owner's five more plaid shirts (18 November): on hanging or creased cloth with a repeat a sixth to a third of the shirt wide, the small areas gave a wrong repeat (44 px for 144) or none; the sequences, lined up strip by strip over the whole garment, gave it. Two of the five (a creased purple check, a rust plaid on a hanger) went from "could not measure" to a repeat agreeing 0.86 and 0.85 with the photo. Trying each reading and checking it against the photo is what makes a wrong spacing harmless: 148 for 156 px agreed 0.80, 162 px not at all. |
| A rebuilt repeat is kept only when the part it was measured from reaches over more than 1.1 repeats each way | With matching loosened for soft cloth (next row), a made-up madras, whose bands never repeat, was "rebuilt" at 0.94 agreement from one column of areas 0.9 repeats wide: a repeat cut from anywhere matches the place it was cut from. The real shirts span 1.2 to 4.5 repeats. |
| When matching soft cloth, an area is left out as a near-tie only when another peak rivals its best match, not when the match is still good a tenth of a repeat away (`local_offsets_match(peaks=True)`); the straight-on path is unchanged | Bold bands one way and faint ones the other match nearly as well slid along the bold bands; counted as a tie, not one of 450 areas of the rust shirt could be measured. |
| The slow change of light is measured on a small copy of the picture when it is taken over more than 300 px (`common.soft`) | For a 340 px repeat the blur was 1,000 px wide and one shirt took eight minutes. |
| Flannel's brushed surface is a large tile (72 units) of scattered marks: fourteen broad clouds, each three faint ellipses laid over each other so it fades out at its edge, and ninety short fibres lying every way; no mark is stronger than 7% (`illustrate._brushed`). The fuzzy edge is as it was | The owner, 17 November, on the navy plaid jacket: "way too much brush". The surface had been four ovals on an 18 by 16 tile at 7 to 9%, both kinds white on dark cloth: a grid of pale ovals that read as a quilted print on plain flannel and blotched a measured plaid. The heavy one-sided fringe in the pictures shown that day was not the app: those were drawn with resvg, which pushes the fuzzy edge to one side; a browser, which is what the app uses for garments, draws it evenly round the outline. |
| Soft, nearly colourless cloth is matched on the three Lab channels added as they are, smoothed over 2 px, and its repeat is the average of the pixels on each spot, not the middle one (`common.soft`, `flatten.rectify(soft=True)`); only on the straightened path, so plaids measured before come out as they did | The usual signal scales each channel to its own spread: on a navy, black and grey flannel the two colour channels are noise (spread 1 to 2 against 19 for lightness) and counted for two thirds of the match; with the weave's grain, 14 areas in 2,700 matched, against 660 afterwards. A woven grey is light and dark yarns side by side: the middle pixel is a dark yarn, and the repeat came out near black. |
| When the first background model's mask is see-through, runs out of the photo's frame (on more than 8% of its edge) or holds two kinds of surface (overall colours 40 apart), the second model is asked, and what only one model kept is left out when it is a different surface from the garment that runs out of the frame; otherwise the masks are combined as before (`patterns/masks.py`: `doubtful`, `settle`). Replaces "asked as well when a cutout is more than 20% see-through" below, which combined the two whatever they showed | On the jacket the first model kept the duvet and the second cut the jacket out cleanly; combining them kept the duvet. A sleeve only one model found is the garment's own cloth and stays, and so does a part in another colour inside the photo (a varsity jacket's sleeves): bedding, a floor and a wall run out of the frame, parts of a garment do not. The demo's seven photos come out exactly as before. |
| A plaid's repeat is made lighter or darker when the photo was too dark or too light for the colour the item is called: the largest colour of the repeat is brought to the nearest edge of that word's range of lightness (its three shades), and every colour moves with it by the same gain in linear light, so their contrast stays. Only when that colour then is the word's colour (within ΔE 18), by at most two stops up and one down, never so far that the lightest part passes white, and not at all for a correction under a quarter (`palette.exposure`, kept as `"lit"`). The word is the owner's own colour when they set it, else the colour Claude called the item, not the item's current colour | A phone sets its exposure for the whole scene: the navy jacket on the white duvet measured at lightness 4, where the darkest navy the app draws is 14, and was drawn near black; the first measurement had also turned the item's colour to black, so the current colour cannot be the guide. White is not used to set the gain as it is for stripes: a plaid's thin white line is blurred in the repeat and reads grey, so chasing it would wash the cloth out; it only limits the gain. A cream ground is never darkened into navy: it is not navy at any exposure. |
| When a cutout still holds two kinds of surface, a pattern is measured on each on its own, the busier first (`masks.surfaces`) | Both models can keep the bedding. Seen from far enough that a pattern blurs into one colour, a plaid is one colour all over and a jacket on a duvet is two (60 apart on the owner's photo; 23 at most on the demo's patterned items). |
| Plaids the older code found no repeat in (kept as the whole-garment tile, or failed) have their cutout made again and are measured again, once, the first time the newer program runs; a new cutout also clears the item's landmarks and, for an item already described, measures its pattern again (`jobs.remeasure`, `patjobs.stale`) | So the jacket is put right without a new photo. Landmarks are points in the old cutout's own pixels. What is kept now says how it was measured (`"how"`: repeat, straightened or whole), which is also what stops a second round. No migration: the kept pattern is JSON and reads as before. |
| Stripes are measured as a sequence across the whole garment: regular stripes folded onto one sharp repeat (each area matched to the whole repeat), placed bands (a polo's chest band) drawn over a crisp ground; the collar is cut from the ground, and the sleeves too unless the photo shows the bands on them | A polo's band landed on its collar and sleeves; rugby shirts carry their bands onto the sleeves, so it is measured, not assumed. |
| Colours of stripes are corrected from Claude's colour words: when it says the item has white or cream, the lightest neutral yarn is made that colour and every yarn moves with it (brightness freely, balance by at most 8%) | Most photos are taken in tinted light; a white shirt measured grey. |
| Florals, camouflage and other prints are pieced (image quilting) from big squares from all over the garment, after turning it upright and evening out the light | One repeated square showed rows of the same flower; a slanted shirt gave slanted streaks. |
| Placing a plaid: Claude gives the buttons, neck, hem, shoulders and underarms (`tagger/landmarks.py`, the larger model); each front is placed by matching the photo beside the button line against the repeat, held out against cloth lower down | Finding buttons in the image alone took pocket edges and round shapes in a plaid for buttons; the tagging model put underarms on the chest. Placing through the flattening map alone was right on one front only. |
| Measuring runs in the background after an item is described (`patjobs.py`), and again when the owner changes its pattern; a failure leaves the item drawn in its colour with a note | It takes from a second to two minutes; no item waits for it to be usable. |
| Clothes can have their main photo retaken too (`POST /api/items/{id}/photo`, `items.replace_photo`), not only shoes: the same endpoint, a shoe going the shoe's way (traced again) and anything else cut out, described again and its pattern measured again. The steps both share (the old photo's rows and files go, a queued cutout of it is dropped, refused while its cutout runs or for a photo already in the closet as another item) are one function (`items.replace_front`); a shoe's retake is unchanged | A plaid whose checks could not be measured says a flat photo from above usually works, and the cutout's failure says to retake against a plainer background, but the only way to give a new photo was to delete the item and add it again, losing its wears, rating and fixes. One endpoint keeps the page's call the same for both. |
| A clothing item's retake refuses a photo waiting on Add as a photo of several pieces, as adding does (`items.WAITING_SOURCE`); a shoe's retake still does not check it | Otherwise the same file would give a piece twice once that photo's pieces are added. The shoe path is left exactly as it was; the gap there is small and is noted for later. |
| What was measured from the old photo goes at the retake, not when the new one is measured: the kept pattern (`pattern_fill`), its state and note (`pattern_state`, `pattern_error`: a "could not measure" note included) and Claude's landmarks. The pattern is not marked to be measured then: the new description marks it (`patjobs.mark`), as for a new item | The old note and the old checks are about a photo that is gone; leaving them until the new measurement would show them as if they were about the new one, and keep them for good if the item is never described again (no route to Claude, the demo closet). Measuring before the new description would be thrown away when the description marks it again, and would ask Claude for landmarks twice. Meanwhile the item is drawn in its colour, as any item waiting to be measured. A measurement of the old photo still running when the photo is retaken is not kept (`patjobs.run_one` keeps only what it was asked for). |
| In the demo closet a retaken photo is cut out again and nothing else: it keeps its description (never sent to Claude), and its pattern is not measured (the demo is never measured), so it is drawn in its colour | As for shoes, the demo never sends photos to Claude; what was measured from the demo's own photo is not about the new one. |
| The item page offers a piece of clothing "Retake the photo" in two places only: in the notice that its background couldn't be removed, beside "Try the cutout again", and beside the note that its pattern couldn't be measured; when both show, only in the first. The note was shown twice on the page (above the name and above Details); it is now shown once, above the name | The owner should meet the button where the page says a new photo would help, and see one button, not two. A general "Replace the photo" elsewhere was not asked for. |
| The second background model (u2net) is downloaded after the first and asked as well when a cutout is more than 20% see-through inside its outline | The first model takes a plaid's dark checks for background (22% of the owner's shirt kept); the two combined kept 66%. Six plain or striped test shirts were left exactly as they were. |
| The second model's download address is a setting of its own (`second_model_url`, in the settings' defaults), and a model download that breaks for any reason is marked failed with a sentence, never left "downloading" | The setting was missing from the defaults, so reading it failed inside the download thread, which only caught network and disk errors. From 0.5.0 to 0.8.0 the second model never downloaded, stayed "downloading" (which also refused every new try) and nothing said so: every cutout was made by the first model alone. The owner's striped linen shirt (19 November) came out 82% see-through, its navy taken for background between the white stripes. The tests stood in for the cutter, which leaves the second model out; a test now builds the closet as the program does. |
| Each photo's cutout records whether the second model was there to be asked (`photos.cutout_second`, migration 0035: 1, 0, or NULL before it was recorded). Once the second model is ready, a front photo's cutout that is not 1 (of an item not archived, its cutout ready) is looked at once, from the stored cutout (`masks.doubtful_cutout`: see-through, on the photo's edge, or two kinds of surface, as the cutter asks of the first model's mask), and made again when the second model would change it; the others are marked 1. Twenty at a time, between rounds of the queue (`jobs.recheck`) | The stored cutout's transparency is the first model's mask, cropped with a margin, so the check needs neither model and takes a twentieth of a second; running the first model again on every photo would take seconds each. On the owner's shirt and the demo's seven photos it gives the same answer as the cutter's check on the full mask: the shirt is made again (3% see-through after), the demo's are not.
