Making every cutout again would ask Claude for landmarks and measure every pattern again; this makes again only those the two models would change. New photos never wait behind more than twenty looks. |
| The closet's notice says when the second model's download failed, with Try again, and **Advanced** gives its address and folder (Settings → Background removal also showed it until that card was removed on 20 November). On 22 November the owner asked "in the settings there's a background remover section, but i thought we had two libraries for it?", so Advanced → Background removal now starts with a line for each model: what it is for, how it stands (ready, downloading and how far, not downloaded yet, didn't download and why), and Download or Try again on one that is missing or failed. Only there: the main Settings page stays without the card, and the lines come from `GET /api/model` as it was, with no new endpoint. The one download button starts whichever model is not ready (`POST /api/model/download`); `GET /api/model` carries the second as `second` | A second model that cannot download should not be invisible again. One button keeps the page as it was for the first model; the second's own address cannot be changed from the page (it never has been), only put in by hand. |
| The kept pattern is JSON in `items.pattern_fill` (migration 0013), a tile as a small WebP inside it | One row holds the item; backups and restores carry it with no new folder. |
| A pattern tile's picture reaches past its cell to hide seams by 0.4 units or 1% of the tile, whichever is less (`illustrate.TILE_OVERLAP`); drawing version 19 | The fixed 0.4 units stretched a pinstripe's 3-unit repeat by 13% and cut off its last part, where the white line was: at phone sizes the shirt showed no stripes at all (0% of the chest white, 12% after). Tiles of 40 units and more, every measured pattern so far, are drawn exactly as before. |
| A graphic on a top (a logo, a chest print) is lifted from the cutout (`fills.extract_graphic`, the bench's own) and kept in `items.pattern_fill` as an optional `graphic` part: a WebP with its transparency and its box in fractions of the garment, drawn over the cloth in its colour; no new migration, and the kept format stays version 1 because data without the part reads as before | The issue asked for no schema change if `pattern_fill` could hold it. Fractions of the garment (not of the padded cutout) put the print in the same place on the drawing's outline. |
| A print is lifted when the item is a top and its pattern is `graphic`, or `other` with the pattern name `novelty`; Claude is not asked for landmarks for it | The tagging schema does not yet ask for the taxonomy's `graphic_technique` and `graphic_placement` (adding them would change the schema version and every stored answer's shape), and the coarse pattern already says graphic. A print is placed by its own box, so landmarks would only cost a call. Shoes, trousers and accessories are left plain (sneaker logos are parked). |
| A measured pattern's colours come from its stripe band widths, or from its tile's pixel area reduced to 16 colours; a colour lying between two or three others (within ΔE 10) is a crossing of those yarns and its area goes back to them (`palette.py`, numpy and Pillow only) | Without that step a navy and green plaid also reported the teal where they cross. Colours under 4% are dropped and at most four kept (the main colour and three others). |
| Each measured colour gets the nearest colour word (the drawn shade plus two more shades per word); Claude's word is kept when it is at most ΔE 12 further away | Claude's words stay where the photo agrees with them; the limits are starting values to tune on the owner's photos. |
| The item's main colour becomes the largest measured colour unless Claude's main colour covers at least ¾ as much, and its shade becomes the measured one; both stay the tagger's (confidence 0.9) and a colour or shade the owner set is never changed | The outfit engine and What's missing read `color` as before, so matching improves with no change there. The measured colour is part of Claude's side like the drawing, not an entry in the history; it is laid over again after describing again or undoing. |
| The measured colours are kept inside `items.pattern_fill` with the pattern they were measured for, and used only while the item still has that pattern; the item page shows them as swatches beside the pattern word | No schema change. Items measured before this are not measured again for it: they get colours the next time they are described or their pattern changes. |

### A plaid drawn from Claude's description (the experiment; the app now uses it, see "The cloth drawn from its description")

Measuring a plaid from its photo fails on creased, hanging or dark cloth (three of the owner's six plaid shirts in
November). This tries the other way round: Claude says what the plaid is made of, in a few extra fields of the
description it already gives, and Closet draws a clean plaid from that (`patterns/described.py`). Nothing in the
app uses it yet: the description Closet asks for is unchanged.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A plaid is described as one list of bands for one repeat, the ground first, each a colour (#rrggbb) and a whole-number width, taken to be the same going down as across, and a size word for how many repeats fit across the chest (tiny, small, medium, large, huge). The middle of the ground is placed on the button line. Round one asked for bands across and down, a yes/no for "the same both ways" and a number of repeats | A woven plaid is two band sequences crossing, nearly always the same both ways, so one list is all a drawing needs. No extra call and no extra photo; for any other item the answer is just "null". The first form made each plaid description about 700 to 1,000 output tokens longer on the owner's six shirts, because Claude thought much longer about it, though the bands themselves are only 60 to 130; asked to fill it in "from a quick look", the second form adds about 200 to 350 (typical and average, two runs of each shirt, with and without). Claude writing the drawing itself (as for shoes) was ruled out as far too costly for hundreds of garments |
| Where two bands cross, the colour is the two yarns' colours averaged (in the colour space the measuring uses), tipped a little towards one yarn and then the other along a faint twill diagonal; band edges are softened over a fraction of a percent of the repeat | It matches how a measured madras is rebuilt, and how real cloth looks: the crossings are the two yarns seen together, and a hard-edged grid reads as a printed graphic rather than as cloth. The twill is kept faint so the fabric's own texture (flannel, oxford) still decides the surface |
| Odd answers are made safe, not trusted: bands with no readable colour or no width are dropped, neighbours of one colour merged, more than 40 bands cut, the repeats kept between 0.5 and 16; a description with nothing left, or one colour only, draws nothing (the item keeps its colour) | Whatever Claude writes, the drawing must not break or invent a plaid |
| The size of the repeat comes from the photo where it can: the spacings at which the garment's whole band sequences repeat across and down (`straighten._spacings`), against the chest's width between the two underarm points of the landmarks Closet already asks Claude for on a patterned top. A spacing across and one down that agree within a quarter settle it; otherwise the shortest spacing down is used, unless it is wider than the chest. Only without landmarks, or when the photo shows no repeat, does Claude's size word stand | Claude could not tell the size: asked for a number of repeats it said 4 for all six shirts; asked to count a line's crossings or the ground squares, or for the repeat in centimetres or as a size word, its answers stayed bunched and were off by up to five times. Counted by hand the six shirts have about 6, 1.8, 4.5, 8.5, 2.8 and 2.7 repeats across the chest; read from the photo they come out 5.7, 1.7, 3.0, 8.2, 3.1 and 2.6. Bands running across a garment stay straight however it hangs; those running down are broken by an open front, the placket and folds. A madras has no repeat, so a "repeat" wider than the chest is not trusted |
| A described plaid's colours are drawn as Claude gives them; moving them toward the photo's colours (`described.toned`, by the photo's average and spread, or only its hue and strength, or after first setting the photo's lightness from the item's colour word) is kept as a tried option, off | On the six shirts each correction helped one or two (the tan and purple check, the rust check) and harmed others (the green flannel went grey, the navy jacket lost its blue, a dim photo made the drawing too dark). None helped on the whole |
| The description is made from the cutout, as Closet sends it today | Sending the original photo instead costs the same (one image) and helped on some shirts (the small check hanging by a window, whose cutout is full of holes) and not on others; sending both costs one more image per item and did not help. Not worth changing what the description is made from for this |
| A described plaid's band widths can be taken from the photo (`described.photo_widths`, an option of `tile`, off): each garment pixel is matched to the nearest colour a crossing of two described yarns makes, and each broad colour's width becomes its share of those pixels (half of every crossing with another colour). Claude's band order and colours stay and are drawn unchanged; the photo's colours are only stretched (each channel's 5th to 95th percentiles matched to the described cloth's, at most doubled or halved) so they can be compared. Lines (colours under a tenth of the repeat) keep their width, the broad colours move from Claude's shares toward the photo's only as far as none goes past 1.6 times its described share, and nothing moves when the photo's colours lie far from every crossing colour | On the owner's six shirts Claude's colours were about right but the ground often too wide. Taken from the photo the widths gave the tan and purple check its tan (purple 0.62 to 0.51, tan 0.19 to 0.30 on one answer) and the rust check less slate, changed the green flannel and the dark shirt little, and harmed none badly; a cutout that lost its white squares cannot take the white away, because of the cap. It helped two of six and the two answers for each shirt came only partly closer, so it is not turned on. Reading the widths from the garment's band sequences instead was tried and dropped: unmixing them pushed every colour to an extreme |
| Round four, the recommended set-up (the defaults of `described.tile`, still not used by the app): band widths from the photo on; colour strength softened to 0.8; hues and photo toning off. How far the widths may move now depends on how much better the photo's shares explain its pixels than Claude's do: not at all when no better, up to 2.5 times when clearly better (replacing the fixed 1.6). A cutout with holes inside its outline (over 1%) is not counted at all. After the widths, the repeat starts at the widest band of the colour covering most of it, and colours nearly alike are drawn as one | The fixed cap held back the answer that made the tan and purple check purple-led and still let the dark shirt get redder on an answer the photo explains worse. With the cap tied to the gain, three answers for the same photo came out closer (the colour make-up of the tan and purple check 19 to 17 apart, the rust check 10 to 8, the demo madras 20 to 8; the others about the same, the navy jacket a little further apart); the arrangement of the bands did not get steadier, because which thin lines Claude lists and in what order differs between answers and the photo is not asked about that. The shirt by the window has 2.7% holes, the others 0.5% or less. Where a described colour crosses itself, the photo's chroma was a median 0.57 of the described one over 40 colours on eight shirts; 0.8 goes part of the way, since a photo also dulls colour. It makes the vivid demo flannel a little duller |
| Not kept: moving the hues toward the photo's, and a white balance keyed on a neutral band | Moving each broad colour toward the photo's colour where it crosses itself made the three answers closer on some shirts and further apart on others (no better overall). A neutral band was clearly seen on only one shirt; a thin white or cream line covers too little of the photo where it crosses itself to read its cast, and two shirts have no neutral at all. On the rust check the photo's off-white is only slightly pinker than Claude's, so the "salmon" look there is mostly the warm light on the whole photo, which Claude's colours had already left out |

### The cloth drawn from its description (adopted 18 November)

The garment loop test (`bench/experiments/garment_loop` and its README, now in the git history) compared drawing a garment's cloth from a
description, with and without a render-and-correct loop, against today's measuring, on 60 garments (30 used for
tuning, 30 held out and run once). One description from Sonnet with the second surface form (arm F) was best: on the
held-out photos 88% of patterned garments recognisable (the app before: 42%), the main colour right on 92% (54%),
the size of the pattern within a quarter on 71% (35%), never confidently wrong (8%). The owner adopted it.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Claude describes the cloth on the description call, in one field `surface` (`tagger/surfaceform.py`): plain, stripes, plaid (any check), blocks (parts in another colour, or bands across the body), or other (a print). Only that kind's fields are filled. Schema version 6; the field is required in the form asked for (`tagging.ASKED`) and optional in the stored form, so answers from before read as before | One general form instead of code per pattern; measuring per pattern was brittle on ordinary photos and fixing it photo by photo did not converge. The test's first form, the same without bands across the body, a finer size word and pieced prints, drew colour blocks and prints far worse (5.8 and 6.5 of 15 against 10.5 and 12.0 on the tuning half) |
| The description call is made on Sonnet: a new role `describing` in `tagger/models.json`. Corrections, shopping searches and their estimates stay on the `tagging` model (Haiku) | Sonnet's description with the second form beat the describing model's (Haiku) by 1.6 of 15 points a garment on the held-out photos and was steadier from run to run. It costs about 2 US cents and 12 seconds a photo at the API key's prices, about 11 times Haiku; the owner put accuracy first (the plan: cost is secondary) |
| Stripes and plaid are woven from the described bands by `patterns/described.py` with its defaults (widths from the photo's colour shares, colours softened to 0.8 of their strength) and sized from the photo: the band spacing against the chest between the landmarks' underarms, or a bottom's waistband read from the cutout; a pattern Claude calls micro or tiny is read first from a square of cloth at the photo's own detail; up to 150 repeats across (`patterns/surface.py`). Kept as a tile, as a measured plaid is | Claude cannot judge the size of a pattern (DECISIONS above, and the test: correcting it beside a drawing did not help either); the photo can. The old cap of 16 repeats left every fine stripe and tiny check unmatched (6 of 16 in the tuning half) *Changed (the owner, 19 November):* the fine reading is tried whatever the size word, and for a word bigger than tiny is kept only when the colour swings at that repeat like a band (`surface.FINE_STRONG`, 8 in Lab on the 0 to 255 scale): the owner's linen pinstripe called small (48 across the chest) was read from the whole-garment picture as 2.6 repeats and drawn as two broad bands. It swings 21; the demo's plain graphic tee, whose knit the fine reading takes for a 48-repeat pattern, swings 3; the demo's two plaids give no fine reading and are sized as before. |
| Bands across the body are kept as one set of horizontal stripes over the garment's height, not repeated (the stored `stripes` kind) | The drawing already draws that kind over the whole outline, sleeves included; no new drawing code and no measured proportions |
| Parts in another colour (yoke, sleeves, collar, pocket) are drawn from the answer through the template's colour blocking; plain cloth, blocks and prints take the described main colour as the item's drawn colour (`tagjobs.color_hex`), softened as described colours are; a patterned item's colour still comes from its measured pattern (`palette.settle`) | Drawn at once, with no photo work. On the held-out solids the described colour scored better than the colour word's fixed shade |
| A print ("other") is drawn with the app's own print piecing from the photo, in the pattern job | The judges scored the pieced print about 12 to 14 of 15 against about 5 for a print drawn as its main colour |
| The pattern job asks for a top's landmarks only for stripes and plaid (to size them), not for a print or bands. When they cannot be had (Claude out of reach), the chest is taken as 0.61 of the cutout's width (`surface.outline_ruler`) | They are what the scale is read against; for the others they would cost a call for nothing. Without them the size word would decide the scale, and the word was wrong too often to stand alone (Sonnet says "small" for 2 to 38 repeats); on the test's 49 tops with landmarks the outline gives the chest within 15% for 84% and within 25% for 94% |
| A print on plain cloth (a graphic tee) is still lifted from the photo. A top Claude calls a graphic whose cloth it describes as a print is pieced like any print; a logo on stripes or plaid is not drawn (the cloth is) | Lifting a logo off patterned cloth is not solved (backlog). An all-over print called a graphic, lifted, kept plain sleeves and collar (seen on the test's shirt prints) |
| A print is pieced from the photo even when Claude listed no colours for it | Piecing needs only the photo; one of the five held-out answers that changed from run to run was a camouflage tee drawn plain this way |
| When the owner changes an item's pattern to another word than the answer's, the described surface is put aside: their word is measured from the photo as before, and the parts Claude described are no longer drawn. Putting the word back brings the surface back | The owner's word wins over Claude's everywhere else; without this a plaid the owner called solid kept its plaid |
| On the Claude plan route the surface's descriptions go in the prompt (`surfaceform.NOTES`), word for word, and the form sent on the command line carries none; the API route sends them in the tool, as the test did | With them in the form the command that starts Claude Code was 33,931 characters, over the 32,767 Windows allows, so no item could have been described on Windows through the plan. Without them it is 31,307. Twelve garments asked again through the app's plan route came back with the same kind, parts and bands across the body as the test's three runs; the size word moved on 3 of the 9 with a pattern (it only counts when the photo gives no reading) |
| The careful bound on a description's cost is 20,000 tokens of prompt and form (was 18,000), so on Sonnet the spending limit holds about 9.4 US cents for each waiting photo; the test measured about 2 cents a photo | The surface adds about 4,000 characters to the form |
| An answer without a surface (every item described before this change, and the demo closet's) is measured from the photo as before, until it is described again | No migration, and nothing is sent to Claude again by itself; Retake or a new description brings the new drawing |
| Checked through the app's own code on the test's 60 garments (`bench/experiments/garment_loop/app_path.py`, in the git history: the stored Sonnet answers, the pattern job's keeping and colour, the closet drawing and the As worn figure, no new calls) | The judges scored drawings made by the bench, from Haiku's shape. The app's drawings matched them, apart from the all-over print called a graphic (fixed above); the shape now comes from Sonnet's answer, which drew one shirt-jacket with lapels |

### The cloth and its lettering, corrected in the owner's words (18 November)

The owner asked to put the drawn cloth right in their own words ("the colorblock arms are red in one arm, blue in the
other") and to have logos drawn, "only for those that are" recognisable letters.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The description asks for `lettering` (schema 7): the letters or words on the front Claude can read for certain, each with its text (at most 30 characters), place (`left_chest_as_seen`, `right_chest_as_seen`, `center_chest`, `middle`), size (small, medium, large), style (sans, serif, script) and colour. Empty for a picture or a symbol, a monogram it cannot read, or letters it cannot all read; never guessed. Optional in the stored form, required (null allowed) in the form sent | The owner's rule: only lettering that is recognisable letters. Asked of 12 garments through the app's plan route, Claude read the two with clear letters ("G.A.P.C.", "PAPER PLANES") and left out a crocodile, a crest, a round badge, a half-hidden chest print and a script print |
| Lettering is drawn as text (`illustrate._lettering_markup`) in the torso's box (`Shape.body_box`), squeezed to fit its place (`textLength`), in a common sans, serif or script font, on tops only, and not over a print lifted from the photo (which already shows its letters) | Text stays sharp at any size and needs no font file shipped; the torso's box moves with the figure, so the letters sit on the As worn figure too. Escaped: the text is Claude's, never markup |
| Blocked parts each have their own colour (`Spec.block_colors`), and each sleeve can be its own part (`left_sleeve_as_seen`, `right_sleeve_as_seen`; two in one colour are "sleeves") | One colour for every part drew a tee with a red and a blue sleeve as two red ones |
| Sides are named as seen in the photo, not the wearer's | Asked for the wearer's left, Claude gave the wearer's side for a chest print and the photo's side for the sleeves of another tee; with names "as seen" both came back right. The drawing faces the viewer as the photo does |
| A sentence can rewrite the cloth or the lettering whole (`CorrectionAnswer.cloth`, `.lettering`, on the `tagging` model as other corrections). The owner sees them as one change each, in words ("plain green with red sleeve on the left, blue sleeve on the right"), and confirms them like any other | The same form as the description, so whatever a description can say a sentence can put right, with nothing new to draw. When the sentence cannot be understood without the item ("red in one arm, blue in the other"), the photo is sent as before: tried with the real plan route, Claude asked for it and then put each colour on the right side; a crocodile logo was turned down with a sentence saying a picture cannot be drawn |
| What the owner confirms is kept in `items.own_cloth` (migration 0036), laid over Claude's answer when the item is drawn and measured, kept when the item is described again, recorded in the history as one field and undone like one. A pattern word the owner sets later puts their cloth aside (the latest word wins); their lettering stays | As the owner's details are kept (`own_details`). A pattern measured from the owner's cloth is measured again when it changes, and an undo puts the earlier measured pattern back without measuring |
| On the plan route the form goes without the titles pydantic writes on every field | The lettering took the command that starts Claude Code to 32,433 characters, inside Windows' 32,767 but past the app's margin of 1,000. A title is the field's name again ("Category Confidence"); without them it is 29,671 |
| The careful bound on a description is 21,000 tokens of prompt and form (was 20,000), so the spending limit holds about 9.7 US cents for each waiting photo | The lettering and the parts named as seen add about 1,500 characters to the form sent on the API route |
| The careful bound on a correction is 19,000 tokens of prompt and form (was 9,000) | The longest description now carries a cloth and three lines of lettering, and the form their shapes: about 18,000 characters, counted at two to a token and doubled. On Haiku that is still about a third of a cent |

## Several items from one photo

The owner photographs several pieces laid out together and gets one item per piece (#151). Video sweeps are out
(the owner's decision).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Claude finds the pieces (`tagger/garments.py`): one box per separate piece, in pixels of the photo shrunk to 1024, turned into fractions; the larger model, as for landmarks; same routes, same monthly limit, purpose `find_garments`, an estimate checked before each API request | The issue preferred this to a new detection model, and no dependency is added. Placing edges on a photo is where the tagging model was weakest. At the prices written down one photo costs at most about 8¢ with the API key. |
| The boxes are tidied before the owner sees them: cut to the photo, under 3% of a side dropped, two overlapping 80% or more (shared area over joint area) joined, at most 20 | Claude sometimes boxes the same piece twice; a suit's jacket over its trousers overlaps far less than that, so stays two pieces. The thresholds are starting values to tune on the owner's photos. |
| The owner checks every box before anything is added: remove, join two, move or resize by the corners (pointer events, so touch works), add one; nothing is guessed at "Add" | Claude's boxes on a cluttered bed are not known to be right; checking costs seconds and a wrong item costs more. |
| A slip can be taken back (22 November, the owner: "if i accidentally remove one box or change the shape, there's no way for me to undo and go back to the one that claude identified"). Claude's boxes are kept as it found them in `source_photos.found_boxes` (migration 0040) beside the boxes the owner changes. After each change a line says what it was, with **Undo**, which goes back through this visit's changes one at a time (up to 30); a box of Claude's that was moved, reshaped or joined offers **As Claude found it**, a removed one is listed with **Put back** (in its turn among the others), and **Back to what Claude found** puts the whole photo back, dropping boxes the owner drew. All of them send the whole list of boxes (`PUT /api/sources/{id}/boxes`), with each box's id, so its cutout is not made again | Undo rather than asking first (DESIGN, principle 9). Undo is kept on the page, not on the server: it is for the slip just made, and is gone when the page is left, where Claude's boxes stay until the pieces are added, so a slip noticed later can still be put right. One endpoint for every way back keeps the server's rules (cut to the photo, at most 20) in one place. Claude is never asked again to get its boxes back: it would cost money and could answer differently. Once the pieces are added the page no longer changes boxes, so the kept boxes are not offered there; they stay in the database with the photo. Photos whose boxes were found before 0040 have nothing kept, so only Undo is offered for them. |
| The photo is kept once, in `source_photos` and `images/sources/<id>/` (migration 0022); each item is an ordinary item whose photo is its box cut out of it with 2% of the photo around it, and remembers the photo and box (`items.source_photo_id`, `items.source_box`) | Each item is then cut out, described, drawn and pattern-measured by the existing queue with no special case. The margin keeps a piece's edges when Claude's box is tight. |
| Each item's photo carries the source file's hash; a photo already kept, or already in the closet as an item, is not kept again, as in bulk import | The same file sent twice, or once as one piece and once as several, never adds pieces twice. |
| Any change by the owner ends the wait for Claude: a later answer is dropped (`UPDATE … WHERE state = 'finding'`) | An answer must never overwrite boxes the owner has started on. |
| The cutouts beside the boxes are made by the same models, on demand, and kept until the items are added; while the model is not ready the page shows the box's part of the photo | The owner sees what each item will be; nothing is made for a box that moves again. |
| The photo goes with the last of its items deleted; a photo not added can be forgotten | Nothing is kept for nothing; an archived item keeps it. |
| An item cut from a photo of several pieces whose photo is retaken leaves that photo (`source_photo_id` and `source_box` cleared): its new photo is its own, and the item page no longer shows the photo of several and its box. The photo of several goes once no item is cut from it, as when the last is deleted; the other pieces keep it | The box is a place in a photo that is no longer the item's. Keeping the link would show the owner a box around a piece that may no longer look like the new photo. |
| Bulk import marks a whole choice (files or a folder) as "several items per photo" with the same switch on Add | One switch for one photo and for a folder; a mixed folder is imported in two goes. |
| The demo closet never asks Claude: its photos wait for the owner to draw the boxes | As for describing: the demo never sends photos to Claude. |

## Phase 0 findings

See `docs/PHASE0.md` for the results and what is still open.

## Trip packing

- **Usual weather from five years.** Trip days inside Open-Meteo's 16-day forecast use the forecast; later days use the same calendar days in each of the last five years from Open-Meteo's archive, averaged, and a day counts as rainy when it rained in at least half of those years (`backend/closet/tripweather.py`). Each day says which it is ("Forecast" / "Usually, on these dates"). If the archive cannot be reached, those days say the weather is not known and the forecast days still answer.
- **Re-wear is the owner's, by kind** (#155). "Before it needs washing" in Settings → Your closet sets how many times each kind is worn on a trip before it needs a wash; it lives in the program's settings (`rewear`), so the real and demo closets share it. The kinds are read from the taxonomy's categories (`GARMENT_CATEGORIES` in `backend/closet/tagger/taxonomy.py`), so no count names a piece such as jeans: **tops** (shirts, tees and polos) 1 wear, **knits** (knitwear and sweatshirts) 3, **bottoms** (trousers and shorts) 3, **outer** (jackets and coats) the whole trip, **basics** (underwear and socks) 1. These defaults (`REWEAR_DEFAULTS` in `backend/closet/trips.py`) are the owner's word: a shirt once before washing. The setting takes 1 to 30 wears or "the whole trip"; Settings offers 1 to 10. At most two pairs of shoes are packed unless the days cannot be covered otherwise; shoes have no re-wear count.
- **Hot or humid days.** A day is hot or humid when its high, or its feels-like high, is at least `SWEATY_C` (24°C). The feels-like high is Open-Meteo's `apparent_temperature_max`, which counts the humidity; it is asked for in the forecast and the archive (averaged like the high), and a day without it is judged on its high. On such a day what is worn next to the skin (tops and basics) counts as its one wear, worn fresh and not again before a wash, whatever the owner allows. Bottoms are not counted as next to the skin (underwear is), so trousers keep the owner's count on hot days; this is a choice the owner may want changed. Mid layers (knits) and trousers use the owner's full count on cool, dry days. Rain does not change re-wear; the rain rules (rain-friendly outer layer or an umbrella) stay as they were.
- **Laundry on the trip** replaces "I can wash there". A trip has no laundry, laundry on one day ("on day 4": day 2 to the day before the last) or every so many days ("every 3 days": washes on days 4, 7, …). A wash on a day cleans what was not worn that day, to be worn again from the next day, so the count only has to cover the longest stretch between washes: a week of shirts worn once is 7 shirts, 4 with laundry on day 4. Underwear and socks follow the same rule, plus one spare. The plan marks the laundry days and says what is worn again after each ("Wash on day 4: the shirts from days 1–3 are worn again from day 5"). Stored in the trip's `laundry` column (migration `0023`); trips saved with "I can wash there" became "every 3 days", the nearest to what it did.
- **Explained counts.** The plan says each kind's count in plain words ("5 shirts: one a day, laundry on day 4", "2 sweaters: 4 cool days, worn up to 3 times each", "1 jacket: one for the whole trip"), and underwear and socks say why theirs. A day the packed pieces cannot dress because everything has been worn as often as allowed says to choose laundry, allow more wears in Settings, or take more pieces.
- **Basics** are counts, not closet items: underwear and socks (above), sleepwear (one set for every seven nights, one with laundry), and an umbrella when a rainy day has no rain-friendly coat. *(Changed 23 November, from the logic review: sleepwear is one set for every seven nights of the longest stretch between washes, at least one. A month with laundry on day 2 took one set for the four weeks after it, while underwear and socks beside it were counted wash by wash.)*
- **The planner** dresses the hardest days first, preferring pieces already packed, then fewest new pieces, then the engine's score. It calls the outfit engine's own helpers; `outfits.py` itself is unchanged.
- **Where Trips sits.** *(Changed by "Four places and Add": Trips is reached from Today, a row when a trip is near and a link at the foot, on both the phone and the computer.)* In the sidebar on the computer. The phone's bottom bar is full, so there is no tab: a "Trips" link sits on Today's header and in Settings under "Where you are".
- **Migration number.** `0012_trips.sql`: migrations `0010` and `0011` are assigned to branches merging before trips; a migration must be above the closet's current version to run.
- **A replan keeps the saved weather for days a refresh cannot answer.** It says the weather could not be refreshed and does not lose a usable plan just because the weather service is down.
- **Usual-weather answers are keyed by the exact days requested, and use only past calendar matches.** If the target day is more than a year ahead, the nearest matching date can still be in the future; the archive only has dates already passed. February 29 uses February 28 in years without a leap day.
- **What is in the laundry today** is packed for a trip that starts three days from now or later (`trips.CLEAN_BY_DAYS`): it will have been washed by then. A trip starting sooner goes without it, as before. Planning a trip three weeks off used to leave out whatever was in the wash that day. *(Changed 23 November, from the logic review: see "What is in the bag" below. A piece the owner ticked, and on a trip under way everything the saved plan packed, is packed whatever the laundry says.)*
- A saved trip keeps the weather its plan was made for; opening it checks the forecast again and only offers "Plan again" when it changed. *(Changed 23 November: see "A trip under way" below. The days already gone are not looked up or compared.)*
- **What is in the bag** (23 November, from the logic review). When a saved trip is planned again, a piece whose line the owner ticked is in the bag: it can be worn whatever its laundry state at home, and it stays on the list even when no day wears it any more (`trips.plan`'s `carried`). Why: a tick was lost whenever a plan made again dropped its line, and a piece already in the suitcase is not in the wash at home. "Not this one" (left out) still takes a piece and its tick off the list, as the owner asked.
- **A trip under way** (23 November, from the logic review). From its first day: the weather is looked up again only from today on, and the days before today keep the weather the trip has for them (`savedtrips._look_up`), so "the forecast has changed" speaks only of the days still to come; planning again keeps each day before today as the saved plan had it, its pieces counted as worn (`kept`); the days still to come are dressed only from what the saved plan packed and the pins (`under_way`): the rest of the closet is at home, and what was packed stays on the list. Why: on day three every look said the forecast had changed, because the days gone had left the forecast and came back as "usually, on these dates", and "Plan again" then dressed them again for weather they never had and repacked a suitcase already travelling; on day two a tee worn on day one, in the laundry by itself, was unpacked and replaced by one at home. Today, the first day, is planned again like the days to come. Swim trunks turned on mid-trip and not packed say "None of your swim trunks can be packed: 1 at home."
- **Loungewear and sport on a trip** (23 November). Loungewear the owner pins ("I want to take this") is packed beside the days, worn on no day, as shoes for a sport are: it was the trousers of two days of a demo trip. A trip's **Playing on the trip** choice (Tennis, Golf; the trip's `sport` column, migration `0049`) packs one pair of the matching shoes beside the days without a pin: a pinned pair first, then the best rated, never one that cannot be packed; when none can go, the plan's notes say why ("You have no golf shoes in your closet."). Studded boots have no choice: "sport" does not say which. On the screen, two chips under "Swimming or the beach", not a section of their own. Why: the owner wanted the trip to pack them by itself (BACKLOG, "A trip for tennis or golf"); one pair, because the shoes are for one game.
- **Day by day as worn** (#181). One **Pieces / As worn** switch beside "Day by day" covers the whole trip, not one per day: on a phone a switch on every card would be a row of controls between the outfits, and the owner wants to read the days one way or the other. It is Today's switch (the same remembered choice) and draws each day with Today's figure (`POST /api/outfits/figure`), at most 310 px tall and 13rem wide so the next day starts on the same phone screen. A day whose pieces are gone from the closet says it could not be drawn; Pieces still lists it.

## Navigation

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| *(Superseded by "Four places and Add", below, on 20 November: the bar is now Today, Closet, Add, Wardrobe, Settings, and Review and Trips are no longer in the sidebar.)* The phone's bar has five places: Today, Closet, **Add** (a round filled button in the middle), Missing, Settings. Review is not in it: a slim note at the top of the closet ("5 pieces need a quick check · Review") and a dot on Closet say when there is something to check. On a computer the menu starts with an **Add clothes** button, then Today, Closet (with the number to review beside it, and Review under it while there is anything to review), Trips and What's missing, with Settings at the foot. Archived is reached only from the closet's In use / Archived switch. The demo closet's strip is one 32px line ("Demo closet · Leave") in a quiet surface colour | From the design review (#128). Six equal tabs gave Add, the main way clothes get in, the same weight as Review, a chore queue; Archived was reachable twice; the demo strip took 48px on every phone screen in solid green and repeated what Settings and the empty closet already offer. Trips stays out of the phone's bar and Insights stays on the closet, as decided before. Open for the owner: Review can go back into the bar if the note is too easy to miss. |

## The design system

- **Type scale, ratio 1.25 from 16px:** 12 / 14 / 16 / 20 / 25 / 31 px at the default text size (in rem since 23 November, so the browser's text size scales it) (`--text-xs` to `--text-2xl`), and 39 px (`--text-3xl`) for page titles on a wider screen (The look, below), weights 400 body, 560 labels, 650 headings. Headings of 25px and up are tightened by -0.015em and balanced over their lines. The app had about 30 sizes; `frontend/src/design.test.ts` fails on any `font-size` off the scale, a radius that is not a token, or a colour outside the token blocks (garment swatches live in `labels.ts`).
- **Spacing on a 4px base:** `--space-1` to `--space-8` = 4, 8, 12, 16, 20, 24, 32, 48. Defined now; existing paddings move onto them in the later design issues that change layout.
- **Radii:** 8 (inputs, small tiles), 14 (cards), 20 (dialogs and sheets), pill (chips, buttons).
- **Colours:** four text levels (`--ink`, `--ink-2`, `--ink-3`, `--ink-4`), four surfaces (`--bg`, `--surface`, `--surface-raised`, `--inset`) and borders as see-through ink (`--line-soft`, `--line`, `--line-strong`), so they suit any surface. Dark mode keeps each hue and only changes lightness. Item tiles stay light in dark mode.
- **Depth:** cards have a soft ring plus a small shadow in light mode; in dark mode only a 1px see-through ring, because shadows do not show on a dark canvas.
- **Primary button in dark mode** is light green `#8fb5a5` with dark text (about 7.5:1); the old `#4d6a5f` with light text was too weak.
- **Disabled** is a flat inset fill, a dashed edge and `--ink-3` text, not lowered opacity, so it reads as off rather than faded. It was the faintest text (`--ink-4`) until #162: that is under 4.5:1 on every surface, so `--ink-4` is now for decoration only (the chevrons), and `design.test.ts` fails if a rule gives it to words. A press is shown by tone, never size: on a touch screen the control dims to `--press-dim` (0.5) after `--press-delay` (70ms), so a touch that becomes a scroll does not flash; nothing shrinks (buttons shrank to 97% until 20 November, which read as the page moving).
- **Light or dark:** `<html data-theme="light|dark">` wins over the device; with no attribute the device's setting is followed. The choice is kept in this browser's `localStorage` (`closet.theme`), not on the server, since it is about the device; "Same as this device" removes it. It is applied as the app's script starts, so a forced choice can show the device's colours for a moment while the page loads, and the browser bar colour still follows the device.
- **Shared pieces** (`frontend/src/components/ui/`) style themselves with `:where()`, which has no specificity, so a screen's own class can still set size and padding without fighting them.
- **Phone or wider** is decided by one media query, `PHONE` in `components/ui/Sheet.tsx` (699px wide and below; a tablet gets the popover even though it still has the tab bar). `Sheet` is the one way to show choices that do not fit in place: on a phone a sheet from the bottom with a dim backdrop, on a computer a popover under its button or the choices opened in place. Escape and "Done" close it.
- *(Changed 23 November: see the bullet below.)* **Closet filters live behind one Filter button**, In use / Archived included, so the phone shows clothes in the first screen; the filters that are on stay visible as chips that remove themselves when tapped. Search and the Photos / Drawings switch stay in the toolbar because they are used most.
- **The closet's toolbar is the search, then Filter with the filters that are on (23 November, from the UI review).** The Photos / Drawings switch was two small pictures whose meaning had to be guessed on a phone (its words were hidden there for room), and with the search and Filter it squeezed the search to "S". It is now a choice of two in words, **Photos** and **Drawings**, a whole tap high, at the top of the Filter sheet under **Show**, beside **Larger** and **Smaller**: the sheet is where everything about what the grid shows lives, and it adds nothing to read on the first screen. Settings' **Show drawings instead of photos** is still where the grid starts. The filters that are on sit beside Filter in one line (scrolling sideways on a phone), so turning one on puts no row in above the clothes. *(Changed 23 November: see the next bullet.)*
- **The top of the closet (23 November, the closet at a glance).** At 320px with Smaller pictures the title, two status lines and the toolbar of two rows took about 270px, and the first screen showed a row and a half. Now: the closet's line is one line of text that wraps as text does ("5 to check · 4 in the laundry · 2 away"); each part keeps the dot after it so no line starts with one, and each is a 44px target by padding the line does not make room for. The away part says only how many: which are past their day is said in the list it opens, on each piece (it was "2 away, 1 expected back", which did not fit beside the others and so had a line of its own on a phone). The search and Filter share a row: the search takes what Filter leaves and is never narrower than 9rem, which holds its whole word in either font. The filters that are on take a row of their own under them only when there are any, wrapping rather than scrolling sideways. The Filter sheet is titled "Show and filter", since it holds both; the button keeps its one word. A browser test holds the first screen of the smallest phone (375x629) at two whole rows of Smaller pictures and a row and the top of the next of Larger ones, in both fonts.
- **Larger or Smaller pictures (23 November, the closet at a glance).** Beside Photos and Drawings in the Filter sheet: **Larger** is two across on a phone, **Smaller** three across, and on a wider screen the matching step (tiles of at least 160 or 120px, 180 or 130px on a computer). Kept on the device (`localStorage`, `closet.tileSize`), as the Today view is, not in Settings and not on the computer: how many fit is a matter of the screen in your hand, and the phone and the computer can each keep their own. A small tile shows the picture and the name only, on two short lines broken between words: a name too long for them is cut after the last whole word that fits, with "…" (`closet.shortName`, 22 letters, with the browser's two-line cut behind it), so it never reads as half a word; the caption (category and colour) is left out, since the picture shows both, and the laundry mark says "Laundry". The whole name is the picture's description and shows on hover.
- **The closet keeps its search, filters and place (23 November, from the UI review, P12).** The search and the filters are in the closet's address (`/?q=navy&group=trousers&laundry=laundry`, `closet.filtersToSearch`), changed in place as they change (`router.replaceSearch`, which adds nothing to history), and a piece opened from the grid carries them in its own address, so a reload, Back, "‹ Closet" and a piece's page reloaded all come back to them. The place in the list is the scroll position the app already keeps for each page of history, which now lands on the same list. Opened from its plain address (the tab bar, a link), the closet takes what this tab last had (`sessionStorage`, per closet and per In use or Archived), as it did while the app was open; a new tab or the home-screen app opened afresh starts clear. Not kept on the device for the day as Today's changes are (#318): those are part of dressing today, a filter is a way of looking that should not greet you tomorrow. Before, the filters lasted only while the page was open, so a reload lost them.
- **What a piece is is asked once in the closet's Filter (23 November, from the UI review, A13).** One list, "What it is", of the groups the closet's pieces are in (the item page's "What is it?" groups); the group chosen opens, under it, to its categories when it has more than one (Jeans, Trousers, Shorts) and to the kinds its pieces are said to be, when choosing one narrows what is shown. Choosing another group starts again, and choosing what is chosen goes back up a level, so two choices can never leave nothing to show. One chip says the narrowest level chosen and removes the whole choice, and the Filter button counts it once. Before, Category (19 chips), Group (9) and Kind (24) were three lists for one question that stacked into "Nothing matches". Every category and kind the three lists offered can still be reached, from its piece's group; the one thing lost is a category across groups ("Sweaters" no longer takes in a fleece pullover, which is Activewear), which the search still finds (`closet.whatChoices`).
- **What the closet's search looks in, and how (23 November, from the UI review, P9).** The page searches the list it already holds, as it did, so it works offline; the list now carries the brand, the material, the owner's shade and the notes (`own_notes`) for it, rather than the search asking the server, whose `q` stays for anything else. A piece is found by its name, category, kind, group, colour, shade, pattern, brand, material and notes. Every word typed must be found, each in any of those, anywhere in a word ("shirt" finds an overshirt, as the old search did), with accents and capitals set aside (`closet.matchesSearch`). The search box is never narrower than its whole word (it shrank to "S" at 320px beside the buttons): first on a row of its own, then (see "The top of the closet") on Filter's row with a width it cannot go below.
- **The item page reads first and edits on demand.** Every detail is a label / value row; tapping it opens that detail's chips. On a phone the sheet closes after a choice (one tap to open, one to choose); on a computer the chips stay open in place, so several changes can be tried in a row. "How you feel about it" stays open after a choice on both, because it has a "Why?" box to fill.
- **On a computer the item panel scrolls with the page** instead of in a box of its own, 420px wide, and only exists while an item is chosen. It opens level with the top of the screen even when the grid has been scrolled, so a tile far down does not open a panel out of sight. *(23 November, from the UI review, A5.)* The grid keeps as many tiles to a row as fit the closet's whole width while the panel is open (`closet.columnsFor`), so the tiles get narrower instead of moving to other rows, and the page scrolls by however much the chosen tile moved, so it stays where it was on the screen. The panel has Close; Escape (unless a sheet or a field takes it first) and Back close it too, going Back when the page before is the closet so history does not grow. Focus goes into the panel as it opens and back to the tile when it closes. The other way, a panel floating over the grid, would have hidden the tile chosen whenever it was in the right-hand columns.
- **The model banner shows only while it needs something done** (not set up, or the download failed), not while downloading and not offline. Hiding it lasts until the browser tab is closed, and only for the state it was hidden in, so a new failure shows again.
- **Accessibility (#162)** is checked by `frontend/e2e/17-accessibility.spec.ts`: axe-core (WCAG 2.2 A and AA, contrast included) over the login screen and the main screens on a 390×844 phone and a 1440×900 computer, in light and dark, plus nothing past a phone's right edge and 44px touch targets there. Shopping finds and several items per photo run the same axe check in their own tests. A control that looks smaller than 44px on a phone (small buttons, the segmented choices, the demo strip's Leave) keeps its look and gets an invisible 44px hit area from a `::after`, so the calm sizes of the design review stay.
- **Focus stays inside what is open:** a sheet or popover, the ⋯ menu and an inline "are you sure?" keep Tab inside them, Escape closes them, and focus goes back to the button that opened them (`trapTab` and `confirmKeys` in `components/ui/focus.ts`). What's behind a popover on a computer cannot be tabbed to while it is open, as with a sheet.
- **Reduced motion:** when the device asks for it, every animation and transition is cut to nothing, except the busy spinner, which turns slowly so the page still shows it is working.
- **Motion (16 November, the owner's choice):** one curve and two lengths, after a look at moodbit.app's motion with the owner. `--ease-out` is `cubic-bezier(0.22, 1, 0.36, 1)`, a fast start and a long soft landing; `--dur-quick` (150ms) is for a press, a colour change or a small turn, `--dur-move` (300ms) for something arriving (a sheet, a toast, a layer of the figure). Every transition and one-off animation uses them, and `design.test.ts` fails on one that does not; loops (the spinner, the skeletons) keep their own pace. Before, the app mixed 120ms, 150ms, 180ms and 200ms on two curves. Sheets now take 300ms instead of 200ms: with this curve most of the movement is over in the first third.
- **The look, from moodbit.app (16 November, the owner's choice):** three changes made together.
  - **Page titles are large and light:** 31 px on a phone and 39 px from 700 px wide, in the body weight (400) rather than the heading weight, tightened by -0.02em. Today's day follows its title in a second, lighter colour (`--ink-tone`, a mid green; the light green in dark), as large on a computer, and 20 px under the title on a phone so it stays on one line beside Worn and Trips. A larger title can push a header's button onto a line of its own on a phone (What's missing).
  - **Labels are set like notes on a sketch:** every small capital label (an item's details on its page, the heads over a group of chips, Why this works, the Insights cards' heads, What's missing's and a trip's small heads) is in the device's own typewriter-style font (`--font-label`: SF Mono on Apple devices, Cascadia Mono or Consolas on Windows), in a terracotta (`--label-ink`) and spaced out. No font is downloaded, so the app still works offline. `design.test.ts` fails on a capital label that is not set this way.
  - **A few sections sit on soft tints** (`--tint-green`, `--tint-sand`, `--tint-coral`): Today's question about yesterday's outfit (coral) and its Why this works (green), the Insights cards in turn, and How you live (sand). Item pictures keep their own light card, so a garment's colours are never seen against a tint. Every text colour used on a tint passes 4.5:1 in light and dark (the lowest is the faint grey on coral, 4.7:1).
- **On Today the outfit as worn is dressed a layer at a time (16 November, the owner's choice):** the trousers, the shoes and the innermost top together, then each top over it from the inside out, then the accessories, each layer fading in over the last, `--dur-move` apart (about a second for four layers). It is the depth order the figure is painted in, shown. Each layer is the whole outfit up to it, drawn by the server through the existing `POST /api/outfits/figure` (so a shirt under a coat is drawn as it is, not cut to where the coat shows it), and placed by its `viewBox` in the outfit's frame, which a bag or a hat makes larger. Today keeps its outfits side by side, so each one waits undressed until half of it is in sight and is dressed when swiped to. Less motion: only the outfit is asked for and shown at once. A trip's days are not dressed: they show many outfits at once, and each layer is a request. The cost is one request per layer (four for a coat, a jumper and a shirt with accessories), each a few milliseconds of drawing.
- **The item's name is a text box that wraps** (a one-line `textarea`; Enter saves instead of starting a new line), so a long name shows whole on a phone instead of running out of sight. Names on the closet's tiles still end in "…", to keep the grid even.
- **The brand's serif, for the name only (20 November, with the rename to Nines):** `--font-brand` is Charter, then Bitstream Charter, Iowan Old Style, Georgia, Times New Roman, serif. It sets the word Nines and nothing else: in the sidebar (beside the mark, at `--text-xl`) and in the headings of the first-run, login and "has been closed" / "isn't answering" screens, through `NinesName` in `components/NinesMark.tsx`. Everything else keeps the app's own type. No font is downloaded: Charter is built into iPhones and Macs and Georgia into Windows, so the app still works offline and the name looks a little different on each, as the labels' typewriter font does. The mark beside it is an inline drawing, not a font or an image, and its one token is `--brand-edge`, the jacket's outer edge: the cloth's own black in light, and in dark the pale grey of the jacket's own edge lines (`#868d95`), so the black jacket keeps an outline on the dark page.
- **Page widths:** a page that is one column (Add, Settings, Review, Trips, Worn) is at most 720px wide (`--content-w`) and starts beside the sidebar, not centred; a page with two panes (Closet, What's missing) uses the full width. A trip's packing list and its days now stack in that column, and the Review card's picture is narrower (17rem) beside its fields.

## Settings

Settings is one column of groups, in this order: **Your closet** (demo switch, start with the computer, backups, storage), **Where you are**, **How you live**, **Look**, **Describing clothes**, **Phone access**, then **Advanced**, closed until opened (the model's download address and where to put it by hand, the folder the closet is stored in, the version), and **Log out** / **Quit Closet** as a row at the end, not a card.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Where you are and How you live sit just after Your closet | They are everyday choices that the design brief's list of groups did not place; they belong with the everyday ones, before the look and the technical ones. |
| Background removal has no card in Settings (it had one in Your closet until 20 November); only its address and the folder to put it in by hand are under Advanced. The closet's notice says when a model is missing or its download failed, with Try again, and a failed download is tried again by itself after 1 minute, 10 minutes, then every hour (`ModelStore.keep_trying`) | The card only offered a download that startup already makes. The owner still sees at once if photos are not being cut out, from the notice; a download that failed while nobody was looking heals without a button. |
| Describing clothes lists the plan and the API key once each, in the order they are tried, each with one status line. The key's line is the one that says where the key is kept ("No API key yet."), not the route check's "No API key is set" as well | The key was described twice in two wordings. |
| **Which to use** (the route order) sits in Describing clothes just above **Allow the API key**; when only the plan is chosen the API key is listed below the routes as not used, with its box | The choice is about the two routes listed above it; the key can still be replaced or removed when it is not in use. |
| The monthly limit, this month's spending and the history are under **Monthly limit and spending**, closed until opened (and opened by itself when the switch needs a limit typed); "limit reached" and what the waiting items would cost stay outside it | What has to be acted on is seen without opening anything; the figures are there when wanted. |

*Superseded by "Settings regrouped" below (step 6 of the assessment's order of work): the groups and their order
here are from before it; the decisions about the API key, the route order and the folded limit still hold, on the
Advanced screen.*

## Demo closet, second wave (#165)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| 13 items are drawn from their tags by the app's own drawing code (`drawing_from_answer`), the plaid and floral from made-up pattern tiles kept as a measured pattern; they are marked `"wave": 2` in items.json | Shows the new kinds, accessories and shoe styles without any real photo or network. An older demo closet gets the wave-2 items, the missing outfits, the wishes and the trip once, only when none of them is there, so what the owner removed is not brought back. |
| The finds' "Would this work?" verdicts are worked out by `tools/make_demo.py` (with a fixed today), not when the demo is opened | Judging four finds takes about 20 seconds; the demo must open in about a second. The saved trip is planned when the demo is first opened (about 1 s) from weather given in items.json, starting 12 days later. |
| Tests whose exact numbers were written for the first 30 items (`test_wouldwork.py`, two in `test_trips.py`) read the demo without its wave-2 items | Their rules are unchanged; the parka and the oxfords filled the gaps they were built on. |

## Upload limits (#217)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The login and the size limits are checked by one ASGI middleware (`closet/gate.py`) before a request's body is read, not by the routes' login dependency | FastAPI reads and parses a body (writing an uploaded photo to a temporary file) before it runs a route's dependencies, so the dependency stops nobody from sending a large upload. Every call under `/api/` except `health`, `session`, `setup`, `login`, `logout`, `instance/quit` and `instance/show` is answered 401 there without reading its body; the dependency stays on each route as well. |
| Bodies are counted as they stream in and answered 413 the moment they pass the limit, with or without `Content-Length`; a `Content-Length` over the limit is refused before anything is read. The 413 asks the client to close the connection | A chunked upload has no length to check, so only counting stops it. Nothing is written to the closet until the whole photo has arrived, and Starlette closes the temporary file of a form cut short, so a refused upload leaves nothing behind. |
| A request carrying a photo (`POST /api/items`, `POST /api/sources`, `POST /api/would-this-work`, and the shoe's `POST /api/items/{id}/photos` and `POST /api/items/{id}/photo`, added 16 November after the owner's phone photo was refused there (with the shoe check's `POST /api/shoes/check`, gone the same day with the one-step shoe); a test now checks every route that takes a file) may send 61 MB: a photo of up to 60 MB plus 1 MB for the rest of the form. Every other request may send 1 MB. All are in `closet/limits.py` | Each of those calls carries one photo: an Add of several photos and a bulk import send them one request each, and a photo of several pieces is one photo. The page shrinks photos to 2400 px JPEG (a few hundred KB to 2 MB) before sending, but sends the file as it is when the browser cannot read it (HEIC on a Windows browser); phone photos are a few MB, a 200-megapixel JPEG can reach about 50 MB, so 60 MB is the limit photos already had (`images.MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES`), now enforced while reading. The form's other fields are a few hundred bytes. The largest JSON body (settings, a list of item ids) is a few kilobytes, so 1 MB leaves plenty of room. Backups are restored from a folder on the computer, not uploaded, so they need no limit here. |

## Closet's own window (approved by the owner, 16 November)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Opening Closet shows it in its own window, titled Closet (pywebview: Edge WebView2 on Windows, WebKit on the Mac), instead of a browser tab. Closing the window leaves Closet running in the tray; "Open Closet", opening the program again and (on the Mac) clicking it in the Dock bring it back; "Quit Closet" quits. A copy started at login opens no window. If the window cannot be used, the browser opens as before | The owner asked why Closet opens in a browser when the owner's other app runs in its own window and is still reachable from the phone through Tailscale, and approved: "To match [the owner's other app]: opening Closet would show its own window. Closing the window would leave Closet in the tray, so your phone and the nightly backup keep working; Quit stays in the tray. The login stays, and the window would remember it like a browser does. It adds one dependency, pywebview. If the window can't open, it falls back to the browser." The server, the page, the login and the phone are unchanged: the window shows the same page from 127.0.0.1. |
| The window keeps its web storage in `window/` in the data folder, not in private mode; on the Mac, WebKit keeps it where macOS keeps every app's (under ~/Library), since WebKit has no public way to choose a folder | So the login cookie (90 days, as in a browser) and the page's own settings (theme, Photos or Drawings) survive closing and reopening, as the owner asked. It is never backed up or restored with the closet: it holds nothing that is not on the server, and a restore leaves it alone. |
| The browser is used instead when the window cannot open: no window library (Linux from source without GTK or Qt), Windows without the Edge WebView2 Runtime (pywebview would otherwise fall back to Internet Explorer's engine, which cannot run the page), an error while starting it, or a page that has not loaded 30 seconds after the window showed it. The reason goes into closet.log. `--browser` asks for the browser on purpose | A blank or broken window with no way out is the worst outcome for an owner who does not use a command line; the browser always worked. WebView2 comes with Windows 11 and current Windows 10, so the fallback should be rare. |
| No tray icon unless asked for (`--tray`): the owner does not want Closet adding an icon near the clock or in the menu bar (17 November). Closing the window hides it and Closet keeps running in the background, so the phone and the nightly backup keep working; opening the program again brings the window back, and Quit is at the foot of Settings. `--no-tray` (the tests, the smoke test) still means no window to ask questions in. | Until then closing the window only hid it while there was a tray icon to come back from, because a hidden window with no icon leaves Closet running out of sight. The owner prefers no icon; a second launch shows the window and Settings quits it, so Closet can always be seen and stopped. |
| On Windows only the owner closing it hides it: when Windows shuts down or signs out (or Task Manager ends it) the window closes and Closet quits. On the Mac, Quit (Cmd+Q, the Dock, logging out) quits Closet completely | Holding the window open when Windows is shutting down would make Windows wait on Closet ("this app is preventing shutdown"), so the close is checked on the WinForms window, which says why it is closing, rather than with pywebview's own `closing` event, which does not. On the Mac the window's Quit is the app's Quit, which macOS also sends at logout. |
| On the Mac, Closet is still a menu-bar program (LSUIElement): it has a Dock icon and a menu bar only while its window shows | pywebview gives the program a Dock icon as it loads; with the window hidden that icon would do nothing useful, and while the window shows the menu bar is what makes Copy and Paste work from the keyboard. |
| Opening the program while a copy runs asks that copy to show its window with `POST /api/instance/show` and the token from the lock file, like `POST /api/instance/quit`; when it answers that it has no window (it uses the browser, or it runs with neither tray nor window), or it is a copy from before the window, the browser opens as before | Only something that can read the closet's own folder can ask, so this adds no way in from a browser page. On Windows the new launch lets the running copy bring its window to the front (`AllowSetForegroundWindow`), which Windows otherwise only allows the program the owner just used. The "newer copy replaces an older one" flow is unchanged. |
| The tray icon and the window share the main thread: the launcher calls pystray's `run_detached()` and then runs pywebview's loop on the main thread. On Windows (and plain X11) the tray runs its own loop in a thread; on the Mac (and GTK) it lives on the window's loop | pywebview's `start()` must run on the main thread, and on the Mac AppKit allows only one loop, on the main thread, for both. `run_detached()` is pystray's way of joining another library's loop. When the window cannot be used, the tray runs its own loop on the main thread exactly as before. The details, with the library code they rest on, are in `closet/window.py`. |
| An update starts the new program with its window showing only when the old one's window was showing at the time | The page in a browser tab reconnects by itself after an update; a window closes with the old program, so the new one shows its own instead, and stays hidden when the owner was not looking at it. |
| pywebview 6.2.1 is pinned with its own dependencies (bottle, proxy-tools), pythonnet, clr-loader and cffi on Windows, and the six PyObjC packages it and pystray use on the Mac (12.2.2, which also pins what pystray installed unpinned before) | The rule for every dependency. 6.2.1 is pywebview's current release; PyObjC 13.0 was only two weeks old. proxy-tools is published as source only; pip builds it without a compiler. |
| cffi's tools for compiling C code are left out of the program (`cffi._shimmed_dist_utils`, `ffiplatform`, `recompiler`, `verifier`, `setuptools_ext`, `vengine_cpy`, `vengine_gen`, `pkgconfig`, in `closet.spec`'s excludes) | The Windows build of 0.6.0 stopped with `Target module "distutils" already imported as ExcludedModule`: cffi came in with the window's .NET loader (clr_loader, for pythonnet), its `_shimmed_dist_utils` imports distutils, and the spec excludes distutils. The loader only calls `cffi.FFI()`, `cdef` and `dlopen`, which never reach those modules, so leaving them out keeps distutils excluded; including distutils (and setuptools with it) would have added build tools the program never runs. Reproduced and checked on Linux with the same pins: the error, then a program that does what the loader does built and ran. The Linux build never met it: cffi is among the Windows imports only. |

## What each photo became, on Add (approved by the owner, 16 November)

The owner said adding clothes feels clunky. One part of that, approved as "Show what was added": the import list on
Add showed each file's name and Waiting, Resizing, Uploading or Done, then "See them in your closet", so what each
photo became had to be hunted for in the closet. The code is `frontend/src/added.ts`,
`frontend/src/components/AddedItem.tsx` and the rows in `frontend/src/screens/Add.tsx`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **Approved by the owner, 16 November.** Once a photo is in, its row shows the item it became: its cut-out (its photo until the background is removed) and, once Claude has described it, what Claude called it (its name, else its category and colour); the whole row opens the item, where it can be fixed. Until then the row says how far it has got, in the item page's words: "Removing background…" (or "Waiting for the model"), "Waiting to be described by Claude", "Claude is describing it…", and when it failed "Claude couldn't describe this item" or "The background couldn't be removed". A photo already in the closet opens the item it is, archived or not. A photo of several pieces keeps its "Check the pieces" | You never saw what each photo became without going to look for it in the closet. |
| The file's name stays in the row, small and muted, after "Added" (it said "Done") or "Already in your closet" | The name is how the owner tells which photo a row was, but once there is an item it is the item that matters. "Done" beside "Claude is describing it…" read as if nothing more would happen. |
| The rows read the closet's own list of items, which the app already asks for every 2 seconds while a background is being removed or an item described, every 10 seconds while items wait for Claude, and not at all once nothing is left; Add asks for nothing more, apart from the archived items, once, when a photo already in the closet is an archived item's. No new endpoint | A call for just the rows' items would come on top of that same list, asked for anyway while the new items are worked on. Offline, the list keeps what it last showed, and so do the rows, also when the page is opened again (the service worker's saved copy). The list is still asked for while the page is hidden, as before this change. |
| `ItemSummary` carries `tag_error` | Why items wait (the API key's monthly limit, Claude Code's sign-in) is kept on each waiting item in plain words; the list has to carry it for Add to say it. |
| Why the items wait is said once, above the rows, in the item page's sentence with "Open Settings", and only when Closet knows why; each waiting row says only "Waiting to be described by Claude" | Those reasons are two or three sentences long, and every waiting item waits for the same one: on every row they would bury the list. While items just wait their turn (Claude describes one at a time) there is no reason to give, and the item page's "It starts by itself once Claude can be reached" would wrongly suggest that Claude can't be reached. |
| Until it has a name, the row's first line is smaller (14 px) than a name | "Waiting to be described by Claude" then fits one line at 390 px, so every row stays two lines; a name, once there, stands out from the rows still being worked on. |
| After "Add this shoe", the done screen shows the shoe in the same kind of row, under the sentence saying it was added | It costs one row: the shoe goes into the closet's list as soon as it is added, so it is followed like any other new item. |

## Quick wins from the assessment (step 2 of its order of work, 20 November)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Every push and replace writes `{ idx, key, from }` into `history.state`. A back link (`BackLink`, `goBack` in `router.tsx`) calls `history.back()` only when the entry before is the page the link names; otherwise it replaces the current entry with that page | "‹ Closet" pushed a new entry, so the closet reopened at the top, history piled up and Back went forward into the item. Going Back only to the named page means a link never lands somewhere its label does not say (an item opened from Insights, or straight from its address). |
| Scroll is kept per history entry in `sessionStorage` (`closet-scroll`, the last 60 entries) with `history.scrollRestoration = 'manual'`: a push starts at the top, Back and Forward restore the position, retrying for up to 2.5 seconds while the page grows and stopping if the owner scrolls. No animated transitions | The browser restored scroll before the page was tall enough. Safari's own swipe back already animates; a second animation would fight it. |
| One `ErrorBoundary` around the screen inside the shell, cleared when the path changes, and one around the whole app | A response of an unexpected shape blanked the whole app with no way out. Inside the shell the tab bar stays, so another screen is one tap away. |
| The touch rules (16px fields, no sideways overflow, `overscroll-behavior-y: contain`, no long-press menu on controls, the delayed dim) apply on `pointer: coarse` only and are Homestead's, value for value | One feel across the owner's two apps. A mouse press shows nothing beyond hover. `contain` also turns off the browser's pull to refresh in a Safari tab; the Home Screen app never had it. |
| Fix the details is a `Sheet` that opens on the sentence box; the details one by one are a second view of the same sheet, not a route of their own yet. That Claude could not be reached is remembered only while the app stays open, from the last answer, rather than asked of the server each time the sheet opens | The box was the last of about 24 rows in a panel that pushed the page down. Asking the server runs a sign-in check on every open; the list still offers the way back to the box, so a wrong guess costs a tap. |
| Release notes: the release workflow puts CHANGELOG.md's section for the version (else Unreleased) under `## What's new`, read by `tools/whats_new.py`; the server sends only that section as text (`updates._notes`), nothing for a release without one; the page draws a safe subset (`markdown.ts`, `components/ui/Markdown.tsx`) as elements, never as HTML, with no library. The workflow fetches the two files at the released commit rather than checking the repository out, and leaves the section out if anything fails | The notes were install steps shown as raw Markdown under "What's new". `main` takes no direct pushes, so the workflow cannot move Unreleased under a version itself: the next pull request that touches the changelog does. A changelog problem must never stop a release. |

## Repo clean-up and the slow-test split (step 3 of the assessment's order of work, 20 November)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| No test and no tool writes into `docs/screenshots/`. A browser test's pictures go to its own output (`test.info().outputPath`, in the git-ignored `frontend/test-results/`); the two figure sheets go to the git-ignored `tools/out/`. The 98 pictures no tracked file named were deleted; the history is not rewritten | Every local run of the browser tests changed tracked pictures, and agents committed them: 88 MiB of the repository's history is old versions of these files. Pictures that DECISIONS or BACKLOG still name, by name or by a pattern such as `figure-*.png`, stay (about 150 files, 25 MB). |
| Four browser tests that only took a picture were deleted (Today and What's missing, on a phone and on a computer) | Each logged in, waited for the first piece or gap and took a screenshot; the tests beside them already check that the piece or gap is there. The phone check takes the pictures now. |
| `bench/experiments/` and `packaging/spike/` (with its workflow) are deleted; the six shoe drawings the owner liked are test data in `backend/tests/data/shoe_drawn/` | Nothing ran or imported them. The drawings are what `test_drawn.py` pins the app's examples against, so they stay as files of their own rather than being compared with `closet/drawing/shoe_examples/`, which would compare two of them with themselves. |
| The tests of the tagger and the drawing moved from `bench/tests` to `backend/tests` (`test_taxonomy.py`, `test_templates_and_tagger.py`), same names and assertions; the stand-in modules they went through are gone, and the bench installs nothing of its own | They tested backend code from another folder through stand-ins. The bench is now only the shoe tracing command, its fork of the cut-out and the open-licence photos in `bench/hardcases/` (which `tools/make_demo.py` and the browser tests read). |
| The demo closet's seven photographed items are cut out with `closet.cutout` and drawn as the app draws a described item: from the answer `tools/make_demo.py` writes, with the pattern the app's own measuring keeps (two plaids and one print). Three answers gained what the tagger would have given (the cloth's colours for the turtleneck and the jeans, the kind of shoe for the leather sneakers). The colour words stay as written in the script | The demo was drawn by phase-0 code no real item goes through, which kept about a thousand lines alive, and a demo item changed its look the first time a detail was picked. A test now checks that redrawing these items in the app gives exactly the drawings in `items.json`. Settling the colour word from the photo, as the app does, would have renamed two shirts and contradicted the demo's review questions. |
| With that, the phase-0 fills (dominant colour, swatch, measured stripes), the flat, soft and traced drawing styles, the tagging bench with its answer key (`bench.py`, `hardcases.py`) and their tests are deleted. `_draw` keeps its `style` argument and refuses anything but `line` | Only the demo generator and the bench used them. The tagging bench is in the git history if the owner's own photos with an answer key are ever wanted again. Keeping the argument avoided touching every caller. |
