| Seven look-at scripts in `tools/` are deleted (the review gallery, the shoe, hem, accessory and coverage sheets, the fit and figure shots), as are `POST /api/outfits/suggest` and seven functions nothing called | No test, workflow or other script used them; the app asks `/api/outfits/today`. Three of the endpoint's four tests repeated what `test_weather.py` checks for `/today`; the fourth now asks `/today`. |
| Backend tests that are pure image work and take seconds carry `@pytest.mark.slow` (93: all of `test_patterns.py` and `test_shoedrawing_turned.py`, most of `test_trace.py`, a few in `test_turned.py` and `test_surface.py`). A plain `pytest` still runs everything | They are about a third of the suite's time and do not depend on the computer. Leaving them out by default would let anyone skip tests without saying so, so the choice is made on the command line. Two tracing tests (one photo accepted, one turned down) stay unmarked so every pull request still traces a shoe. |
| On a pull request the Linux and Windows jobs run `-m "not slow"`. A fourth job, part of the required check, runs the slow tests when the change touches the code they cover (decided inside the job from `git diff` against the base, no outside action); `nightly.yml` runs everything on Linux and Windows on request and on any night when `main` changed in the 25 hours before (asked for by the owner: a night with no merge has nothing new to find) | The split is where the minutes are. The job always runs and passes with nothing to do, so the required check can count on it. A change that breaks an image test from code outside the listed paths is found the next morning, by GitHub's usual email to the owner. On a push to `main` only the last commit is compared; the nightly run covers the rest. |
| The two window tests that waited out a 10 second timeout now send Quit and check that the server is gone and the lock file removed | They passed even if Quit did nothing, because the launcher runs inside the test and its process never ends. |

## Settings in one quick request (step 4 of the assessment's order of work, backend, 20 November)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Phone access is looked up in the background (`phone.Watch`): once when the program starts, and again when `GET` or `PUT /api/settings` finds the last lookup 10 seconds old or more. The request gets the last lookup at once, or `phone: null` before the first is in. One lookup runs at a time; its two `tailscale` commands run at the same time, each still given up after 4 seconds. There is no timer of its own | A hung Tailscale held most of Settings for up to 8 seconds. Ten seconds is short enough that a share made in the Terminal shows the next time the page asks (it should ask again every few seconds while Phone access shows no address), and long enough that a page asking several times runs the commands once. Asking only when Settings asks means nothing runs `tailscale` while nobody looks. Who is asking (the computer or the phone) is still decided by the page, from its own address. |
| `GET /api/settings` (and what `PUT` answers) also carries `model`, `updates`, `style_profile` and `tagging_spend`, each exactly as its own endpoint answers it; those endpoints stay | Settings drew each card when its own request landed, so the page grew by about 2,500 px while it loaded. These four are read from memory, a file or the closet in milliseconds, so the page can be drawn from one answer. The store and other screens still use the endpoints, and nothing was taken out of the payload. |
| Whether Claude Code is signed in (`GET /api/tagging`), the space used (`GET /api/storage`) and the weather (`GET /api/weather`) stay separate requests | Each can take seconds (a command, a walk of every image folder, an outside service). In the payload they would make the whole page wait again. |
| The API key's line (`apikey.info()`, which reads the keychain) and start at login are read once per program run and kept until they are changed through Closet: the key by saving or removing it, start at login by `PUT /api/settings` (also when that fails part way) | On a Mac reading the keychain starts the `security` command, and every switch on the page answers with the whole payload. A key put in the keychain by hand, or `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` set, while Closet runs shows after a restart; `GET /api/tagging` reads the key's presence live. |
| `GET /api/tagging` gives the same answer again for 10 seconds (`TAGGING_FRESH_SECONDS`), unless the route order or whether a key is set changed meanwhile | The page asks for it more than once per visit, and each ask ran `claude auth status`. Signing in to Claude Code happens outside Closet, so nothing can tell the cache it changed; 10 seconds is the longest the owner waits to see it. |
| Routes that are `async def` (they await the body) hand their blocking work to `run_in_threadpool`: changing an item (`PATCH /api/items/{id}`, which redraws it), a shoe's drawing fixes, plain drawing and photo views, correcting a shoe's drawing, keeping a thing Claude offered to remember, saving Your style, decoding the photos sorted on Add, and keeping a photo of several pieces | On the event loop each held up every other request, the closet's polling among them, while it drew or decoded. The connection from `conn_dep` is opened with `check_same_thread=False` and used by one thread at a time, as the uploads already did. |

## Data loading: a query cache with one key file (step 4 of the assessment's order of work, first part, 20 November)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The page reads and saves through TanStack Query (`@tanstack/react-query` 5.107.1, pinned; the only new dependency) | The hand-written store replaced the whole list of items every 2 seconds while work ran, with a new array each time, so every screen that read it, and the whole frame, drew again even when nothing had changed. A cache with structural sharing keeps the same objects when a poll brings the same data, keeps what a screen read so it opens at once next time, and calls off a read that a save has overtaken. Homestead, the owner's other app, uses the same library the same way. |
| Every key is made in `src/queryKeys.ts` and nowhere else, each starting with its own name; `TOUCHES` there says which keys each kind of edit makes stale, and a save names its kind (`useEdit`, `useItemEdit`, `itemSaved` in `src/queries.ts`). Keys for every read the app makes are there already, including screens still on their own `useEffect` | One place to see what a save refreshes, instead of each screen guessing. With every key written down first, the screens can be moved over in parallel without editing the same lines. A test checks that `TOUCHES` names only keys that exist. |
| `today` is in no kind of edit | Asking for today's outfits can ask Claude, which costs money; a screen decides when to ask again. |
| Defaults (`src/queryClient.ts`): fresh for a minute (`staleTime` 60 s), kept half an hour once nothing shows it (`gcTime`), no automatic retry, asked even when the browser says it is offline (`networkMode: 'always'`), and asked again on coming back to the app only after 10 seconds away. Structural sharing is left on | A minute is long enough that moving between screens does not ask again, short enough that a second device's change shows up soon. Retrying would hide an error the owner should see, and polls ask again by themselves. The service worker can answer offline from its saved copy, so the browser's idea of offline is not a reason to wait. A glance at another app should not reload the screen. These are Homestead's numbers, except `gcTime` (Homestead keeps a day because it saves the cache on the device; Closet does not, yet). |
| Each open closet gets its own cache (made in `ClosetProvider`, which App mounts again when the closet is switched) | The owner's closet and the demo never share what was read, without putting the closet's name in every key. |
| The closet's list is asked for again each time the Closet screen opens (`refetchOnMount: 'always'`), shown at once from the cache meanwhile; an item is asked for again each time its page opens (`staleTime: 0`) | Both change behind the owner's back while work runs, and the closet is the main screen. Opening them is instant either way, since what was kept shows first. |
| `useCloset()`, kept as a thin facade while the screens moved one by one, is deleted now that none reads it; screens use `useApp()` for what does not change with data and the query hooks for the rest | Its value changed with every piece of data, which was the fault being fixed. |
| (Replaced by "One progress model", step 5, below.) One poll for the list, in `ClosetProvider`: 2 s while a cutout is made (unless the model is missing or failed) or an item is described, 5 s while an item known in full has a pattern measured or a shoe traced, 10 s while items wait for the model or a way to reach Claude, or a shoe is drawn for the figure; the model every 1.5 s while it downloads. The choice is a pure function (`closetPoll` in `src/work.ts`). A tick does not call off a read still on its way, and no tick runs while the page is hidden | Equivalent to before, plus measuring and tracing, which the list does not carry (`GET /api/items` has no `pattern_state` or `trace_state`; the next step of the order of work gives progress its own endpoint). So when an item's cutout or description finishes, its full item is read once, and while any item known in full is measured or traced the poll goes on and reads those items too. Calling off a slow read at each tick would mean a slow server never answers at all. |
| The review count is asked again after an edit whose kind touches `review`, and when an item's description changes state; no longer on every move between screens. The Review screen keeps the queue as it was when it opened while it is worked through | Each confirmed piece makes the shared query read again behind it (the badge follows), which must not move the card under the owner. |
| Fix the details' list, a detail's choices and the history are cached by item and asked again only after a save that touches them, or when the item comes back described or drawn anew; no longer each time the item changes | The item changes at every poll while work runs, and each change asked for the list again, in a loop while the sheet was open. |
| The browser test "slow polling makes progress…" now checks that a tick does not pile a second request on one still on its way, and that the slow answer lands | It counted two requests at once, which was how the old store did it, not what the owner sees; the thing it guards (a poll slower than the interval still shows what it brought) is unchanged. |
| Settings is drawn from the one settings read, held behind one line ("Opening your settings…") until it is in; the read also fills the `model`, `updates`, `styleProfile` and `spend` keys. A save writes the answer into the cache and asks again only for what the answer does not carry (the weather after a new place, Claude's status after a new route order or key) | Cards no longer arrive one by one and push each other down, and a switch costs one request. |
| `GET /api/tagging` is asked once per visit to Settings and the last status stays on screen meanwhile; storage and the weather are their own reads too, each in a row that keeps its height | They are the slow ones; the page must not wait for them or flash back to "Checking…". |
| On the computer, Settings (and the last step of setup) ask for the settings again every 3 s while the phone has no address, never on the phone | The server only looks Tailscale up again when it is asked; this is how a share set up in the Terminal appears by itself. |
| Today's outfits are kept for the day, the choices and the closet's places, availability and ratings (`staleTime: 'static'`), and are asked again only when Today itself asks; only the current question's answer is kept, and a request on its way is not called off when you leave | Asking can ask Claude, which costs money; coming back to Today must not ask by itself. |
| A figure's key holds its drawing version and whether it is layered, and never goes stale; the last figure stays while the next is drawn | A drawing with the same pieces and version is the same drawing. |
| Worn's later pages are asked with the cursor of the page before as it stands now; trip ticks are drawn from local state and then taken from the saved trip | An edit to the first page must leave no outfit missing in between; a checkbox driven by the cache alone snapped back for a moment. |
| A screen that keeps a copy on this device (What's missing, Insights, Worn, Trips) shows that copy when a read cannot reach Closet, and otherwise keeps what it read, saying in a toast when a refresh fails (`src/savedCopy.ts`) | The offline behaviour is unchanged; saving the whole cache on the device is left for later. |
| A shop search, a "Would this work?" check and finding the pieces in a photo are followed with the query's own `refetchInterval`, worked out from the data by a pure function; starting one is always the owner's own POST, never a query | No refetch may start something that costs money. |

## Settings regrouped, with an Advanced screen (step 6 of the assessment's order of work, 20 November)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The main page has eight groups in a fixed order (`mainGroups` in `src/settingsLines.ts`): Your style, Everyday, Claude, Updates, Phone, This device, Your data, then a link to Advanced settings and Log out / Quit as a row | The assessment's outline. Your style is first because it is the one setting the owner is expected to come back to; what keeps itself running (Claude, updates, backups) is one line each further down. |
| Claude, Updates and backups each say how they stand in one sentence worked out by a pure function (`claudeLine`, `updatesLine`, `backupLine`), green when all is well, red with at most one action when not. The Claude line counts the API key only once it is allowed and while the month's limit is not reached; when nothing works it names the one cause (the key turned off, the limit reached, only the key chosen, the key not allowed, else Claude Code to install) | One place decides the wording, and each case has a unit test. The old "Describing clothes" showed every route, the key box and the limit even when the plan worked. |
| Advanced is its own route, `/settings/advanced`, loaded with `React.lazy`, not a `<details>` on the main page. It reads the same settings query, so coming from Settings it opens at once; it asks storage and how Claude is reached itself | A route has its own Back and reload, and its code and its slow reads cost nothing until it is opened. |
| Quit Closet stays at the foot of the main page, beside Log out, on the computer only; it is not repeated on Advanced | The outline listed it in both places; one home is the rule (DESIGN, principle 2), and the foot of the page is where it was. |
| A phone is any page not opened on a loopback address (`deviceOf` in `src/here.ts`, the same test step 2 used for the QR code). On a phone Settings leaves out Quit, Start at login, the typed backup folder, the model's files to put in place by hand and the data folder (`computerOnly`) | Each is about the computer; Quit from the phone could not be undone from there. Back up now and the model's download address stay on both: they act on the server, not on the device. |
| Your style shows its text clamped to three lines; **Edit the text** opens the existing editor in a `Sheet` (a bottom sheet on a phone, in place on a computer). Nothing is shown yet where "Talk to Claude about your style" will go | The style conversation is step 8; a button that does nothing would be worse than none. |
| The place is one line, "Weather for …" with Change, which opens `WhereYouAre` in a `Sheet`; the weather is looked up only there | The privacy paragraph and the search were 75 words on the page every visit, and the weather lookup an outside request merely to fill a line. |
| The Phone group, once a share reaches Closet, is one line with the QR code in a `<details>` under it, rather than a sheet | The code is read by a camera held at the screen; folded in place it keeps its size and the address under it, and the browser test still reads it. |
| Updates: the main line carries Install and what is new; the mode, Check now, the last check and the last install are on Advanced. The sentence that a copy run from source cannot replace itself is gone; such a copy says "Download it from the release page" when there is a release | A choice made once does not need to be on the page every day. |
| Removed: the Plan a trip link, "No items are waiting to be described" (the count shows on Advanced only when some wait), the token Details table and its second list of months, the long hints (the price note and the laundry hint are one sentence each), and "My style in a few words" in How you live with its echo on What's missing. The server still accepts and keeps `life.style_words`, so saved answers stay valid | All were named dead weight by the assessment. Nothing read the style words ("not interpreted or used"); Your style is where Claude reads how you dress. |
| On the computer, Settings asks again for the phone's address every 3 s, or every 30 s while Tailscale is not installed (`PHONE_SLOW_MS`) | Installing Tailscale takes minutes; asking every 3 s for as long as Settings is open was wasted. Not stopping keeps a fresh install showing without a reload. |
| Not done: turning start at login on at first run, and proposing a backup folder by itself (the assessment's "Automatic") | Both change what the program does on the owner's computer without being asked; that is the owner's call. |
| The demo closet switch is the last group on Advanced ("The demo closet"), also while the demo closet shows; Start Closet when I log in is a group of its own, first, on the computer only (later, 20 November) | The owner found it odd to give the sample closet pride of place once their own clothes are in. While the demo closet shows, the strip at the top of every screen has Leave, so the way back does not depend on finding the switch; keeping it first while the demo shows would move it each time it is used. |
## One progress model (step 5 of the assessment's order of work, 20 November)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| One function, `progress.of` in `backend/closet/progress.py`, works out an item's progress from the columns it already has (`cutout_state`, `tag_state` and `tag_error`, `pattern_state`, and a shoe's `trace_state`, `views_state`, `drawn_state` and their notes). No migration, no new state stored | Every state was already kept where its worker writes it; a second copy could disagree with the first. |
| Three steps, always "of 3": 1 cutting out, 2 describing and drawing, 3 finishing the drawing (a pattern measured, or a shoe's photos sorted, traced and drawn by Claude). A plain piece is done after step 2; the third dot is never filled for it | What a step is does not change with the piece, and before Claude has described it nobody knows whether there will be a third. The drawing is made in the same transaction as the description (`tagjobs.apply_answer`), so "describing" and "drawing" are one step, and the page says the drawing appears as soon as Claude has described it. |
| Four states: `working`, `queued` (next in line, nothing in the way), `waiting` (held up by something named in `waiting_for`) and `failed`. A description in the queue with no reason to wait is `queued`, not waiting | The old page treated every waiting description as stuck and asked every 10 seconds, which is how a one-second description was never seen (the assessment's cause f). Only a named reason slows the poll down. |
| What the whole program waits on is passed in (`progress.Waits`), not stored: whether the cutout worker can run (the model, unless a cutter stands in for it) and whether the describing queue found no way to reach Claude when it last tried (`tagjobs.Worker.blocked`, kept in memory). Only `GET /api/progress` passes it; an item's own summary assumes nothing is in the way, and the page prefers the endpoint's entry for an item that has one | Threading the model's state into every place that returns an item (two dozen) would be noise for a fact about the computer, not the item. The flag is in memory because it is about this run of the program; a restart tries Claude again anyway. Asking `claude auth status` on each poll would take seconds. |
| A failure counts as progress only for the two steps every piece goes through: the cutout and the description, each with Try again. A pattern that could not be measured, a shoe that could not be traced or drawn, and photos that could not be sorted are not: they keep their own notices and controls | Those leave the piece usable (drawn from its description), and each already says what to do in its own place. |
| The words live in `progress.py` (step names, what is happening, where it runs and how long it takes, why it waits) and travel with the progress; `src/progress.ts` only picks which to show. It replaces `addedLine`, `tagNotice`'s waiting and working lines, `drawingPlaceholder`'s and Closet's `busyLabel`; the trace notice no longer speaks while a trace is made, and a shoe's photos and its drawing use the progress's words while their part is the one going on | One phrase per stage, in one file; the assessment found three phrasings of the same step in four files. "Model" stays out of the page ("background removal"). |
| `GET /api/progress` returns only the items under way, with their progress, name and small picture, how many go and how many wait, and one sentence for why they wait when it is something to see to in Settings. The closet's provider polls it with the query's own `refetchInterval` (`progressPoll` in `src/work.ts`): 2 s while anything is working or queued, measuring and shoe drawing included, 10 s while items only wait, and not at all when it is empty | The list of every item (36 KB for 49) was read every 2 seconds, and full items were read to learn about patterns and traces. A poll that stops when nothing is under way costs nothing; an edit that can start work names `progress` in `TOUCHES` (`item`, `upload`, `source`, `settings`, `apiKey`, `model`), a finished upload asks for it, and the Closet screen asks for it each time it opens, so work started elsewhere is seen. |
| When an item's stage or state changes, or it leaves the list or joins it (`movedOn`), the closet's lists and that item's entry are made stale and read again once. The item page no longer polls at all | The poll itself never reads the whole list; what changes on screen when a step finishes (a name, a drawing) arrives with that one read. |
| The strip lies over the bottom of the picture, the tile's mark sits on the tile's picture, and "Working on N items" floats above the grid and the tab bar (the toasts rise above it while it shows, and the grid's end leaves room for it) | The page holds still (DESIGN, principle 3): none of them pushes anything when it comes or goes. A line in the flow above the grid would have moved the grid twice for every import; the toolbar has no room for it on a phone. |
| On Add, a row shows the upload's state until Closet knows the item, then the item's progress (with the step's mark) and, once described, its name; the file name stays small below. "Already in your closet" stays, since it is not progress | The assessment's cause a: "Added" beside "Waiting for the model" read as two answers to one question. |
| Browser tests that waited for a row to say "Added" now wait for the row to open its item; the item page's notices about describing and the limit are now the strip's words ("Waiting for this month's limit to allow it", with Open Settings); "slow polling makes progress" now holds back `GET /api/progress` instead of the list | The wording and the request the page polls changed; what each test guards (the photo went in, the wait is explained with a way to act on it, a slow poll does not pile up) is unchanged. |
| A browser test follows one item through every step (`e2e/30-progress.spec.ts`), with stand-ins that take 4 seconds per step (`CLOSET_E2E_STAGES`) and answer plaid so a third step happens | The tests audit found nothing watched progress stage by stage. Four seconds is longer than two polls, so no step can be missed. |


## The item page consolidated (step 7 of the assessment's order of work, 20 November)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **The owner's choice.** The item page reads a garment's look from one store: the description the drawing was made from, with the owner's corrections folded over it. That store already existed: `illustrations.drawn_answer` (Claude's answer, or a plain one made from the item's fields; the owner's fields, coarse shape, details and cloth laid over it). `closet/look.py` reads it for everyone | Three stores described the look and disagreed on screen: the `silhouette` column (the Details rows), Claude's raw details plus `own_details` (Fix the details and What Claude saw), and `as_worn` (the line under the name, already drawn from the description). Only the drawing's own input folds every correction in, so it is the one that cannot disagree with the drawing. |
| No migration and no data changed. The other stores are not derived and copied; they are no longer read for the look. The item's JSON `silhouette` is now the shape as drawn (`look.shape`), its `details` the details as drawn; the review queue, corrections by sentence and `today.cloth` (the outfit engine, the stylist, gaps and trips) read `look` too | The smallest change that makes disagreement impossible: whatever the columns hold, every reader sees the folded result. A copy kept in step would be a fourth store to keep in step. The `silhouette` column stays as the owner's coarse shape (the review queue still sets it) and, for a described item, a copy of Claude's that only the history reads; old backups restore as they are. |
| The coarse shape is read the way the drawing reads the answer (`tagger.schema.drawing_for`): the details decide, and the coarse shape fills in what they leave unsaid ("na", "none", and "regular" for the cut, which is the details' way of saying nothing). A detail is put in the coarse words by a fixed table in `look.py` (a relaxed cut counts as regular, boxy as oversized, a cropped or longline cut as a length); a part neither says is left out | Someone could map relaxed or boxy differently; the coarse vocabulary has three fits. Leaving an unsaid part out keeps the engine's own guess from the category, as before. |
| Readers that changed behaviour because of it: the outfit engine and the stylist now see a sleeve length or a cloth weight the owner set in Fix the details (before, they read Claude's raw answer and the column), and the review queue no longer asks about a part of the shape the owner's own details decide | Those were the disagreements the owner could not see on the item page but would have felt in Today's outfits. |
| (Replaced on 22 November by "The item page in the owner's order": the essentials come under the name, the line and Fix the details move under How it looks, and a shoe's photos go near the end.) The page: picture and view switch, with "Not in the drawing" as a mark on the drawing; the strip; the name, one line ("Polo · Navy (raw indigo) · Regular fit · Short sleeves": the kind, the colour with its shade, the pattern when there is one, and `as_worn`), and one button, "Something wrong? Tell Claude" ("Change the details" in the demo closet), that opens Fix the details; Wearing and washing; About it (the rating and one notes box); then folds: Details (what it is, colour, pattern, where it lives, formality, season), Shopping facts, History, and the last fold with Describe it again, Archive and Delete. The failure notices sit together under the picture | The report's proposed layout. The words of how it sits still open their detail, as decided before (the fit line row in "Illustration"); the button is the one general way in. The shape rows left the Details: they are the same facts as the line and the details one by one. |
| What Claude saw is removed. "Described with your Claude plan on …" is one line on the details one by one; what was seen but not drawn is in the mark on the drawing | The fold repeated the details one by one almost row for row, in other words, read only. |
| One notes box, "Notes for you and Claude", over the four columns (`wear_notes`, `fit_notes`, `notes`, `rating_reason`), all kept. It shows every note, each once; when the owner changes it, the whole text goes into `wear_notes` and the other three are emptied in the same save. Until then nothing moves. Search looks in all four | Nothing is lost and nothing is kept twice. The stylist reads `wear_notes`, `notes` and `fit_notes`, so it still reads every word; once moved they are under "how I wear it", and the reason for a rating, which no reader had, is now read too. Someone could have migrated the text at once; doing it on save leaves an untouched closet exactly as it was. |
| Browser tests: the specs open the fold they need (`openFold` in `e2e/fixtures.ts`), the notes tests save through the one box and read `wear_notes`, and the assertions on What Claude saw, the Details hint ("Suggested by Claude…") and the shape rows were replaced by what took their place (the line under the name, the mark on the drawing, the details one by one) | Those parts were removed on purpose; what each test guarded (the description arrived, a change persists, nothing is lost through a reload) is still asserted. |
## The item page in the owner's order, and every new item checked once (22 November)

The owner: "the ordering of things in the closet item is not very user friendly. for shoes all the alternative views
come first, then the claude drawing and description, then the name, then the laundry status, etc. seems really out of
order. for every item I need to make sure it's classified correctly, then add details like the real item name (not the
one claude gives), the brand, and price info if I have it. there needs to be an easier way for me to do that."

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The item page, top to bottom: the picture (with the view switch and the failure notices under it) and the name; **the essentials** (What is it?, brand, price); Wearing and washing; About it; **How it looks** (set apart by a thin line rather than a heading, which would add words: the line about its look, "Something wrong? Tell Claude", a shoe's trace notice and its drawings by Claude), then the Details fold (colour, pattern, where it lives, formality, season) and **Size, material and more** (size, material, date acquired, condition); a shoe's photos; History; the last fold. The same order on a phone and in the computer's panel | The order the owner works in: say what it is, its real name, brand and price, then the rest. Before, a shoe showed its photos, the trace notice and Claude's drawings above its name, and brand and price were behind "Shopping facts". The look line keeps the kind as its first word, because it is the drawing's reading and its other words open their details; the essentials say the kind once more, editable. Someone could have dropped the kind from the line; that would make the line and the drawing disagree in a test of their own for no gain to the owner. |
| The page has 65 words at 393 px for the phone check's garment, 13 more than before (52): the essentials' words (What is it?, the kind and who said it, Brand, Price) were behind folds and are now in view, which is what the owner asked for. Nothing else was added; the price box lost its "0.00" placeholder | The reading-load budget is 350; the page stays far under it. |
| The essentials save as you leave each box, as the rest of the page does, with "Saved" or the reason it could not save; a price that is not a price says "Enter a price like 45 or 45.50." and goes back to what was saved. No Save button | One Save for one block would be the only one on a page that saves as you go (DESIGN, principle 9). Leaving a box (Tab, Enter or a tap elsewhere) is the save, so the keyboard and the phone work the same. Offline they are disabled like every other field. |
| The review offers every new item once, besides the items with a doubtful field. A new item is one the owner has not checked: `items.checked_at`, migration 0039. It is set by "That's right" in the review and by changing the name, the kind, the brand or the price on the item page. New items come first, then the doubtful ones, each most doubtful first; an item whose cutout is still being made is not offered yet | The owner's routine is the same for every item, and the review is already the place for "one at a time" (the assessment made it a way of looking at the closet). A second, parallel flow would split one job in two (principle 2). The item page counts as a check so the owner never does it twice. A column, not a guess from who set the name or the kind: the owner may keep Claude's name and still have checked it. |
| Items kept before 22 November count as checked (the migration sets `checked_at` from `updated_at`), and the demo closet's pieces are made checked | Without it, an existing closet would wake up to "100 to check" every day. The owner can still put any old piece right on its page, where the essentials are now first. *Which way to go is the owner's to change: leaving the closet's existing pieces unchecked would offer them all once.* |
| A review card: the picture (smaller on a phone, so the rest is in the first screen), the name to type over (selected when you go into it), What is it? with who said it and how sure Claude was (a sheet of kinds, kept until "That's right"), brand and price, then the doubtful fields as before. "That's right", or Enter in a box, sends everything in one call (`POST /api/items/{id}/check`), all or nothing, one entry in the history. What it is becomes the owner's word even when unchanged, so "Describe it again" keeps it; with no kind, Claude's category is confirmed | The owner's routine in one place and one tap. One call, so a price refused by the server does not leave a name saved and the item still in the queue. Confirming the kind is what "make sure it's classified correctly" asks for. |
| Add offers "Check them one at a time" (to the review) once the uploads are done and a piece just added is in the review; "See them in your closet" stays beside it | "Newly added items can be gone through straight after Add" without a new screen: the review already exists and reads the queue. It appears only once a piece is described, since a piece waiting for Claude has nothing to check yet. |
| A shoe's photos offer "Change which photo shows the outside" once they are sorted, opening the same choice the page asks when Claude is unsure (one photo: the outside or the inside). Naming a side gives the other side to the other side photo when there is one, and now also to the side photos Claude gave the same side when there are more. Not while the photos are sorted, nor for a shoe never sorted (the demo closet's, or one kept from before), whose main photo would not follow the answer | Found on 21 November: the page asked only when Claude was unsure, so a swap Claude was sure of could not be corrected. The server already took the owner's word at any time (`shoeviews.answer`); only the way in was missing. |
| `WhatItIs` (the "What is it?" row and its sheet) is one component, used by the item page (saving at once) and the review (keeping the choice); `KindPicker` takes only what it reads of the item | One look and one set of words for the same question on two screens. |
| Tests: `backend/tests/test_review.py`'s described items count as checked unless a test asks for a new one, so the tests about doubtful fields keep testing those alone; the browser tests that open the Details fold before "What is it?" still pass, the row being outside it now. The phone check pictures a shoe's page too | What each test guards is unchanged; new tests cover new items, checking, and the item page's essentials. |

## Four places and Add (step 8 of the assessment's order of work, second part, 20 November)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| *(Changed by "The wishlist's own page": the wishlist, `/wardrobe/wishlist`, is a page one level down in the Wardrobe too.)* The tab bar and the sidebar hold four places and Add: Today, Closet, Add, Wardrobe, Settings. Worn (`/today/worn`) and Trips (`/trips…`) are pages one level down in Today; the review (`/review`) and Archived in the closet; Would this work? (`/wardrobe/check…`), a wish (`/wardrobe/wish/<id>`) and an item opened from there (`/wardrobe/item/<id>`) in the Wardrobe. The place a page belongs to stays lit on it (`placeOf` in `frontend/src/places.ts`) | The assessment (ux-pages, section 5): eleven destinations, with Trips lighting Today and Review lighting Closet, so the lit tab and the page disagreed. Every feature is still two taps or fewer from the bar. Trips is not a fifth item on the computer either: the same four everywhere, so the two layouts read alike. |
| *(Changed by "The wishlist's own page": "Thinking of buying something?" holds Would this work? and one row that opens the wishlist's own page.)* What's missing and Insights are one page, **Wardrobe** (`/wardrobe`): the three things most worth buying and "See all N", "Thinking of buying something?" (Would this work? and the wishlist), Not worn, Worn most, and the other figures (how wears are spread, what the closet is made of, cost per wear) folded under **More numbers**. "Also worth knowing" is gone | "Missing" read as lost items, and the same facts (not worn, worn most) were on both pages. Nine suggestion cards are not a priority list: three lead, the rest are one tap away. The page waits for the gaps, the wishlist and the insights and then shows everything at once. The Insights cards no longer cycle through the tints, which are kept for sections that mean something. |
| A wish is a row in the list that opens its own page, which holds its note, its price range, the shop search and price checks, "I found one: would it work?", "I bought it" and Remove | The shop search and price checks read as experiments the owner never asked for (assessment); they are kept, one level down. The note saves when you leave the box and says so beside it (`SaveStatus`). |
| *(Changed by "The wishlist's own page": `/wishlist` leads to the wishlist's own page, `/wardrobe/wishlist`.)* The old addresses keep working: `/missing`, `/insights`, `/missing/check`, `/missing/check/wish/<id>` and `/missing/item/<id>` lead to their Wardrobe address, and `/worn`, `/would-work` and `/wishlist` (which never existed) to the obvious place. The screen of the new address shows at once and the address is replaced, not pushed (`movedTo`, used by `parseRoute` and `Shell`) | A bookmark, the home-screen app, a reload or Back into an entry made before the change must land on the place that holds it now, without an extra history entry for Back to bounce on. |
| Today takes in Worn: under the outfits, **Recent outfits** shows the last outfits worn (three on a phone, seven on a computer), each rated in place, with yesterday's question first among them and "See all" for the history. The ratings say "Saving… / Saved / Didn't save" beside them | One place to record and rate. The question was already meant to sit below the outfits (Worn outfits, above); it no longer stands above them as one of about 17 controls before the first garment. |
| Today takes in Trips: a "Packing for a trip?" row when a trip is under way or starts within two weeks (`tripComingUp`, `TRIP_AHEAD_DAYS` in `trips.ts`), and a plain "Trips" link at the foot of the page. The trips list has "‹ Today" | Trips was hard to find on a phone; a trip near is what makes it worth a row. Two weeks covers planning and packing without a row for a trip months off. |
| Removed or folded on Today: the Worn and Trips pills in the header; the arrows and the computer's thumbnail strip under the outfits (a swipe and the dots stay); the teaching line in every card, said once behind "Changing an outfit" (`HowItWorks`); "Claude on today", now said in "Why this works" under "Chosen by Claude" (`readFor` in `today.ts`); the note about the day, folded under where you are in Change | Three ways to page through the outfits, the same line in every card, and who chose the outfit said three times (assessment, Today, 5 to 7). The day note is rarely used; folded, Change opens on the place and the temperatures. |
| The closet has one status line, "5 to check · 4 in the laundry". Each part is a filter shown as a removable chip; **Needs a check** then offers "Check them one at a time" (the review) and the laundry part "All clean". The review has "‹ Closet". The filter is kept while the app is open, like the others | Review becomes a way of looking at the closet (assessment). Three stacked notes put the first garment halfway down a phone screen. The review is still a page of its own, since one piece at a time with large choices does not fit in a grid. |
| The Insights link left the closet's header; the background-removal banner is said once, on Add, not on the closet | Insights is in the Wardrobe; a new owner's first screen was a technical warning, and the banner matters where photos come in. |
| The Ready screen says in one line that Settings, Phone, says how to use the app from an iPhone; the Tailscale command is only in Settings | A command with a port on the first screen after the passphrase is a developer instruction, and on the phone itself it is beside the point. |
| The review's keyboard keys are said only where there is a mouse or a trackpad (`(hover: hover) and (pointer: fine)`), not drawn on a phone at all | It was hidden by a stylesheet rule only; now it is not in the page on a touch screen. |
| Four shared pieces were added to `components/ui/`: `Fold`, `ShowMore`, `HowItWorks`, `SaveStatus` (with `useSaveStatus`), listed with the tokens in `components/ui/README.md`. Screens touched here use `PageHeader` for their back link (Worn, Trips, Review) and `Fold` for their folded sections | Homestead's list (homestead-borrow, section 6): each screen had invented its own `<details>` and hint paragraphs. Plain CSS with tokens and `design.test.ts` stay; no Tailwind. |
| The phone check counts each screen's words at 393px (the page, and the first screen above the tab bar) and lists the screens over the budget in DESIGN as warnings, without failing | The budget is new; the check reports first so the screens can be brought under it before it is enforced. |
| Browser tests that went through a link that no longer exists now go the new way in: the sidebar's Review link → the closet's "5 to check" and "Check them one at a time"; the Worn pill → "See all" under Recent outfits; the Missing tab → Wardrobe; the Insights link → `/insights` landing on the Wardrobe with More numbers opened; the wishlist switch → the wish's row and page; the outfit arrows → the dots. The closet's model banner test checks Add instead. `otherFindings` (the "Also worth knowing" list) and its unit test were deleted with the section | What each test guarded is unchanged; only the way to it moved. `e2e/32-places.spec.ts` checks that every old address lands, after a reload, and that Back and each back link return. |

## Two shoes drawn at once (20 November)

The owner found a shoe's drawing "really long" (four to ten minutes, 6 to 14 calls to Claude one after another) and
asked for it faster without changing what is drawn. This part changes when shoes are drawn, not how: `shoedraw.Worker`
works on up to two shoes at the same time, each through the same `run_one` as before.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Two shoes at once (`shoedraw.AT_ONCE`), a correction from the owner's note counting as one of the two | Each drawing already makes two calls side by side (the two straight-on drawings, then the two turns), so two shoes are four calls at once. More would meet the plan's or the API's rate limits sooner, and a rate limit met inside a check is not an error the owner sees: the check fails, the drawing made so far is kept, and that drawing quietly gets one check fewer. Two halves the wait for a batch of new shoes without making that likely. |
| One loop thread drains; each shoe gets a thread and a database connection of its own, and the same shoe cannot be taken twice because `run_one` takes it inside one `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction | The claim was already atomic across connections; every other write in `run_one` is short and holds the write lock for milliseconds, well inside SQLite's 15-second wait. |
| A drain still returns only when no shoe is due and none it took is still being drawn, and returns how many were worked on. A shoe that becomes due during a drain is started at once when a place is free (a wake reaches the drain); a drain stops taking shoes when Claude cannot be reached, the worker stops or another closet is opened, and lets the shoes it took finish | Tests and the loop keep the same contract as before. A drawing that has started is paid for, so it is never abandoned half way because another shoe was held up. |
| Setting interrupted drawings back to waiting (`recover`) leaves alone the shoes this worker is drawing right now. The worker keeps a set of the shoes it has taken; a shoe enters it inside the transaction that takes it, and `recover` reads it inside a transaction of its own, so the database's write lock orders the two | `recover` ran at every drain, which was harmless with one thread. With several, or a second drain started while a drawing runs, it would have set a shoe being drawn back to waiting and let it be drawn twice. Only one worker draws shoes in the program, so any other shoe left `drawing` or `correcting` was cut short by a quit (or by an error in this worker) and is started again, as before. |
| When Claude cannot be reached for one shoe, the drain stops taking others, without trying again and again; the other shoe being drawn finishes. Its success clears the "waiting for Claude" note on the shoes still waiting, as one shoe's success always did, and they are tried at the next wake or within a minute | One shoe's success shows Claude can be reached; a note saying otherwise would be stale. |
| Opening another closet stops the drain taking more shoes from the one it was drawing; a drawing already under way finishes into the closet it came from. The demo closet is never drawn | The next drain draws the closet that is open. Before, a drain went on drawing the closet it started in until none was left. |

## A check that says good stops being read there (20 November)

The second part of making a shoe's drawing faster without changing what is drawn. In `shoedrawing._check`, a check
that says good, when the automatic checks found nothing and no fix was set aside, ends the checks and its copy of the
drawing is thrown away; writing that copy out takes most of the call's time.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| On the API route, such a check stops being read once its answer says good: `correcting.CutShort` is a small, general hook (a top-level key, and a function given the fields before it that returns the answer to use, or None to read on), and `shoedrawing._good_as_is` holds what is shoe-specific | The owner turned down the "lean checks" on 17 November (DECISIONS, "A shoe drawn turned out, in two steps"): there the check was *asked* to say only "good", which changed the prompt and the answer, and the drawings were worse. Here the request is the same byte for byte (prompt, schema, model, effort, `max_tokens`); only the reading stops, after Claude has decided. Claude writes its verdict before it writes the drawing either way, so nothing it decides can change. |
| It is offered only when a good answer's drawing would be thrown away: no automatic problem and no "could not be used" note pending. It cuts only when `good` is true, whatever Claude's own `problems` say, because `_check` treats a good answer that way today | Behaviour stays identical: the same drawing is kept, with the same rounds and `good`. When `good` is false, or anything is unexpected, the whole answer is read as before. |
| The answer is read from the stream only once the text has reached the `"sheet"` key: the fields before it are read with the JSON decoder, each counted only once the comma after it has come, never by matching a half-written string. They must be exactly `good` and `problems` (and `against_photos` in a check of a correction, which `correct` reads from the last check); otherwise the answer is read whole | Structured output keeps the schema's order, and `good`, `problems` and `against_photos` come before `sheet` in every check's schema. If the order were ever different, nothing is cut. |
| **The output of a check cut short is an estimate, and errs high.** It is recorded once, through the same meter and hold as any request, as the most the model could have written in the seconds from the request being sent to the stream being closed: `ceil(seconds × CUT_TOKENS_PER_SECOND)`, with `CUT_TOKENS_PER_SECOND = 200`, and never more than the request's `max_tokens` (32,000) (`correcting.cut_output`). The prompt's tokens (plain, written to the cache, read from it) are exact, from `message_start`. The meter still holds the request at its `max_tokens` before it is sent, as any other. `Call.cut_short` and `CorrectionResult.cut_short` mark the estimate | The true output count comes only in the last `message_delta`, after the whole answer (Anthropic's streaming docs: the usage in `message_delta` is cumulative; `message_start` carries the input usage and one or two output tokens). Part of the output is thinking, which this model streams as empty blocks unless asked otherwise, so it cannot be counted from the text received. A model cannot write more tokens than its writing speed allows in the time it had; 200 a second is well above what the drawing model writes at, so the estimate errs high as long as that holds. It is still an estimate, not a count: if the model ever wrote faster than 200 tokens a second, a check would be counted low. Built first as `max_tokens` alone, which is safe but at about $0.64 a check overstated a drawing's cost by more than the drawing itself costs. The docs do not say whether a request closed early is billed for what was generated up to the close, so no saving in money is claimed. |
| The plan route (`claude -p`) ignores the hook and reads the whole answer | Claude Code returns its answer whole, at the end. |

## The computer's own door (the first of three steps to the access model of the owner's other app, 20 November)

The owner decided that Closet moves, over three pull requests, from one passphrase to the access model of their other
app: a request is let in only from the app's own window on this computer (proved by a key), through Tailscale Serve,
or through Cloudflare Access with a verified signed token. This first step is the computer's own door. The passphrase
stays exactly as it is and is still asked everywhere; who someone is through Tailscale or Cloudflare is the next step,
and the passphrase goes in the third.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Every request (pages, images and calls) is refused 403 unless its `Host` header, without the port, is `127.0.0.1`, `localhost`, `[::1]` or ends in `.ts.net` | Listening on 127.0.0.1 keeps other computers out but not other websites: a site can point a name of its own at 127.0.0.1 and have the owner's browser talk to Closet as that name, where the browser's own rules no longer keep it apart from Closet. Its requests carry its own name, so checking the name stops it. Tailscale Serve passes the phone's requests on with the phone's `.ts.net` name, so that name is let in and goes on to the passphrase as before. The check is in `gate.py`, before a body is read, like the login check. |
| `POST`, `PUT`, `PATCH` and `DELETE` under `/api/` need the header `X-Closet-Client: 1`, which the page sends on every call (`api.ts`, the one place the page calls the server from; the service worker passes changes straight through); without it, 403 with "This page is from an older Closet. Reload the page, then try again." | A form on another website can make the owner's browser send a change, but it cannot add a header of its own to a request to another site without that site agreeing first, which Closet never does. The same rule as the owner's other app. The only page of Closet's own that leaves the header out is one loaded before this update (a phone, or a tab left open), so the refusal tells the owner to reload, and the save they tried shows that sentence as its error instead of failing silently. A reload gets the new page: the service worker asks the server first for every page. |
| The program's own window opens Closet with a key: `secrets.token_urlsafe(32)`, made once and kept in `settings.json` as `window_key`. Opening `/?key=…` once swaps it for an HttpOnly, SameSite=Strict cookie named for the port (`closet_key_8765`), kept 400 days, and leads on to the same address without the key. With a key in force, every request by a local name must carry the cookie, or it is refused 401 | This is what keeps a program or page that only knows the address out, which the address check alone cannot. The key is a local secret of the same standing as the session hashes beside it in `settings.json`: never in a backup (the file is not backed up), a log (the launcher logs and prints the plain address, and the gate swaps the key before anything else sees the request) or an answer. It is kept across restarts, unlike the owner's other app's key, which is new at every start, because Closet's fallback is the owner's own browser: a tab opened once from the icon must keep working after Closet restarts or updates, and a key made anew would turn it into the "open from its icon" page at every restart. A cookie is kept per host, not per port, so its name carries the port. 400 days is the longest a browser keeps one. Only a page (`GET`, not under `/api/`) swaps the key, so the key never sits in a call's address. |
| Every place the program opens Closet for the owner uses the keyed address: the window, the browser it falls back to (also when the window stops), the tray's Open Closet, and a second launch that opens the browser because the running copy has no window (it reads the key from `settings.json`, as the running copy keeps it) | Anything else would open the "open from its icon" page. |
| Only the launcher sets a key, and it always does: `cli._serve`, which both the packaged program and `python -m closet` from source run, makes the key and gives it to `create_app`. `create_app(sv)` without it (the unit tests' `client`) has none; the browser tests' backend (`tests/e2e_backend.py`) makes the app without it | As in the owner's other app, "only the window and the hub set a key". There is no setting or environment variable to turn it off, so the program the owner runs cannot be told to skip it. Tests stay simple: the address check and the header are the real ones everywhere; the enforced key is tested once at the lowest layer that shows it (`test_gate.py`, through the app with a key) and once through the real launcher (`test_window.py`: what the window opens, the plain address refused, the keyed one let in). The packaged program's smoke test opens Closet with the key from `settings.json`, as the window does. |
| A `.ts.net` name is never asked for the key, and `?key=` there sets no cookie | The phone has no key and needs none: Tailscale already decides which devices reach the computer, and the passphrase is still asked. A key offered on a Tailscale name is ignored, so the cookie can only ever be set by this computer's own address. |
| Exempt: `GET /api/health` from the key (another copy of the program, the tests and the smoke test ask it before anything else); `POST /api/instance/quit` and `/api/instance/show` from the key and from the header | Those two are called by another copy of the program, never by the page, and already prove themselves with the token from `closet.lock`, which nothing outside the data folder can read; a header would add nothing. Exempting them (rather than sending the header from `instance.py`) also keeps an older copy of the program, opened while this one runs, able to ask it to show its window or make way. |
| A refusal by the address or the key is a sentence under `/api/`, as every other error is, and a small page of its own anywhere else, in the page's colours and type, with a title and plain sentences | An old bookmark to `http://127.0.0.1:8765` in the owner's browser is the case the owner will meet: raw JSON there would look broken. The page says where the icon is and that nothing in the closet has changed. |
| Every test client speaks as the page on this computer does: `tests/conftest.py` makes `fastapi.testclient.TestClient` address `http://127.0.0.1` and send `X-Closet-Client: 1`, once for every test; the browser tests' own calls (`page.request`, `request`) get the header from `e2e/fixtures.ts`, and the page itself gets none from the tests | Changing the shared client once rather than thirty test files. The page is never handed the header by the tests, so a call the page makes without it fails a browser test. The made-up phone name in the phone tests is a `.ts.net` name now, as the phone's really is. |

## Claude Code installed through npm on Windows (20 November)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| When the only Claude Code on Windows is an npm `claude.cmd` and the command is longer than `cmd.exe` takes (`claude_cli.CMD_COMMAND_LIMIT`, 8,191 characters), the plan route is unavailable with a sentence saying why (`claude_cli.NPM_TOO_LONG`), so the API key takes over | Windows starts a `.cmd` through `cmd.exe`, which stops at 8,191 characters, and every plan call is far longer (the schema alone is a command-line argument), so describing an item through an npm install could never have worked; before, it failed as "no usable answer" with `cmd.exe`'s "The command line is too long", was retried, and the API key was not tried. Starting the `node` program behind the `.cmd` instead would rest on how the npm package is laid out, which can change. Reasoned from the code and Microsoft's documented limit; not yet tried on a real Windows PC with an npm install. |

## The name: Nines (20 November, the owner's choice)

The app is called **Nines**, as in "dressed to the nines". The owner chose it on 20 November 2026 over names such as
Valet, Kitman and Squire. Until then it was Closet, which is also the word for the collection of clothes and one of
the four places, so the app's name and the place's were the same word.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The name is written out, in a serif (`--font-brand`, "The design system"); the logo carries no numeral 9 | A 9 beside a jacket reads as a size or a price. The word alone says it, and a serif suits evening dress. |
| The mark and the icon are an empty dinner jacket in the air, leaning back a little, over its shadow on the ground, in a softened flat style on cream (`#f6f2ea`, the app's own background). The source is `frontend/scripts/nines-icon.svg`; `npm run icons` renders the web app's icons and `backend/closet/icon.png` (the tray's and the program's) from it with the browser tests' Chromium, so no new dependency. In the app the jacket is drawn inline by `components/NinesMark.tsx`, without the cream square | Dressed with nobody inside: the clothes are the point, and the jacket is the "nines". One drawing everywhere, from one file. The phone icons are a full cream square (the phone rounds the corners); the maskable one has the jacket smaller, inside the middle 80%; the tab's 32px icon frames the jacket closer, as the old one drew its hanger larger. |
| Renamed: everything the owner reads as the app's name: the page title, the home-screen name, every sentence in the app and in the server's messages, the tray's menu and tooltip, the window title and the program's own dialogs (`APP_NAME`), and the living docs | The name the owner sees is one name. |
| Not renamed: the place called Closet and its hanger icon, "your closet", "the demo closet", and anything that is not read as the app's name | Closet is still what the place holds. |
| Not renamed: the Python package `closet`, identifiers, the database, environment variables, the instance header, cookie and storage names, the service worker's cache. (The data folder was on this list until 21 November; it is now `Nines`, see "The program file: Nines". The keychain entry, the backup subfolder, the log file and the user agents were on it until 22 November; they now say Nines, see "The word sweep: Closet to Nines".) | They are stored on the owner's computer and phone, or are names the code and the updater rely on. Renaming them would cost an installed copy its data, its login, its saved key or its start at login, for a name nobody sees. |
| Renamed later, on 21 November: the program file and the release assets (`Nines.exe`, `Nines.app`, `Nines-mac-arm64.zip`, `ASSETS` in `updates.py`, the names in `packaging/closet.spec` including `CFBundleName`, the smoke test, the release workflow), the start-at-login entry, the updater's user agent and the data folder. See "The program file: Nines" | They needed a hand-over for the copy already installed, which the owner settled as a clean break. The spec keeps its file name, and the Mac bundle keeps its identifier `app.closet`. |
| Comments, docstrings and log lines still say Closet, as do older docs (past CHANGELOG entries, earlier entries here, the assessment, BACKLOG's existing lines). (Until 22 November: comments, docstrings, log lines and the living docs now say Nines; history keeps the old name. See "The word sweep: Closet to Nines".) | To keep the change reviewable, and history as it was written. |

## Which checks run where (20 November, the owner's choice)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Before a pull request opens, an agent runs the linters, the type check, the frontend's unit tests and build, and the tests the change touches (the backend test files for the modules changed, the browser spec files for the screens changed). CI runs every check and is the full run. A wide change (shared test setup, the data model, `api.ts`, the design system, text many tests quote) still gets the full sets locally | Every check used to run twice: once before the pull request and once in CI. The full backend and browser sets took 15 to 25 minutes of each piece of work, and the pull request cannot merge until CI is green anyway. A failure now shows a few minutes later, in CI, instead of before the pull request opens; the agent watches CI and fixes it. |
| The phone check stays a local step for any visible change | CI does not run it, and its point is that someone looks at the pictures. |
| The slow tests, the nightly run and the rule to read the last nightly run before changing code are unchanged | They catch what pull requests leave out. |
| Each new cloud session's container installs the Python and npm packages from a setup script kept on the owner's cloud environment (not in the repository), and sets `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_PATH` to the Chromium already on the machine | A fresh container had neither, so every conversation spent its first minutes installing them. |

## The doors from outside (the second of three steps to the access model of the owner's other app, 20 November)

The second step adds the two ways in from another device: Tailscale, which says who is asking, and Cloudflare Access,
which signs who signed in. The passphrase stays exactly as it is and is still asked on every device; the third step
removes it. This replaces the earlier rule (Security, the first table) that "Tailscale serve is the only way in from
outside": once the owner sets it up, Cloudflare Access on a hostname of its own is a second one. The server still
listens on 127.0.0.1 only; Cloudflare's connector, like Tailscale, runs on the computer and passes requests on to it.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Who Tailscale says is asking (`Tailscale-User-Login`, `Tailscale-User-Name`, RFC 2047 decoded) is **recorded, not yet required**: a `.ts.net` request that names no one goes on to the passphrase as before, and the gate records `viewer` and `came_in` in the request's state, which `GET /api/settings` gives as `came_in` and Settings → This device says in one sentence. The place where the third step refuses is one marked line in `gate.py` | The owner must see, on the real iPhone and the real hub, that Tailscale names them before that is made the lock: if it turned out not to (an older Tailscale, Serve set up another way, the Windows check saying no), requiring it now would lock the owner out of the phone, with the passphrase about to go. Recording it first costs nothing and shows the answer on the phone itself. |
| On Windows, Tailscale's headers are believed only when the other end of the connection is the Tailscale service (or the `tailscaled.exe` it started), asked of Windows (`tailnet.py`); elsewhere they are believed. A request whose headers are not believed is treated as naming no one (logged once) | Any program on the computer can connect to 127.0.0.1 and send the same headers. On a Mac there is no such question to ask without special rights. Not believing, rather than refusing, keeps this step from locking anyone out; the third step makes it a refusal. |
| uvicorn runs with `proxy_headers=False` (`cli.server_config`) | uvicorn otherwise believes `X-Forwarded-For` from any connection from 127.0.0.1, which every connection is (Tailscale Serve, Cloudflare's connector), so whoever sent it would choose the connection the Windows check asks about. A test runs the app as the launcher serves it. |
| Tailscale's headers are read only on a `.ts.net` name; on a local name or the Cloudflare hostname they are ignored | Only Tailscale Serve, which removes what the browser sent, can be trusted to have set them. |
| A `.ts.net` request carrying `Tailscale-Funnel-Request` is refused 403, health check included, with a page of its own ("Nines is not on the open internet") | Funnel is Tailscale's way of publishing an address to the whole internet. The README already said not to use it; now Nines refuses it even if someone does. |
| Cloudflare Access is **required** on its hostname: every request there (pages, images, calls, the health check) needs a token in `Cf-Access-Jwt-Assertion` or the `CF_Authorization` cookie that PyJWT verifies: RS256 only, against the team's published keys (`https://<team>/cdn-cgi/access/certs`, one client per team, kept an hour), for the configured audience, issued by `https://<team>`, with `exp`, `iat` and `email` required. Otherwise 401 with "Sign in through Cloudflare to use Nines at this address" (a page outside `/api/`). The email comes from the token only. Every failure is logged with its reason, never with the token | A public hostname is reachable by anyone who finds it; the token is the only proof Cloudflare's sign-in happened. Fixing the algorithm stops a token that names `none` or HS256 (with the public key as the secret). The plain email header Cloudflare also adds could be sent by anything that reaches the port. Unlike Tailscale's word, this is required at once: nothing changes for the owner's existing devices, and an unsigned public door would be worse than none. Then the passphrase is asked as on any device. |
| The keys are fetched with `net.ssl_context()` (truststore, then certifi) and the check runs in a worker thread (`run_in_threadpool`) | A packaged Python on a Mac has no certificates of its own, as for the model download and updates. Fetching keys can take seconds; the event loop must keep answering. |
| Three program-wide settings in `settings.json` (`cloudflare_team`, `cloudflare_aud`, `cloudflare_host`), no migration; the door is off until all three are set. The team is kept as its domain and must be under `cloudflareaccess.com`, with no other address for a test server (the tests hand in keys through `create_app(access_keys=…)`); the hostname is kept bare and must not be an address in numbers, `localhost`, a `.ts.net` name or the team's own | They belong to the program, not to a closet: the demo closet must not have a door of its own, and a backup must not carry one to another computer. Accepting only Cloudflare's domain means the keys can only ever come from Cloudflare, whatever is typed. |
| The settings are part of the one settings payload: `PUT /api/settings` takes `cloudflare_team`, `cloudflare_aud` and `cloudflare_host` (an empty value clears one), and `GET` gives `cloudflare{team, aud, host, on}` and `came_in`. No endpoint of their own | The repository's rule is one settings payload that Settings is drawn from; a parallel `/api/access` would be a second home for settings and a second read. |
| A request that came in through Cloudflare and sends any of the three is refused 403 ("Change this on the computer Nines runs on, or from your phone through Tailscale."), before anything in it is saved | A stolen Cloudflare sign-in must not be able to move the door it came through, or open it for someone else. The computer and Tailscale can still change it. |
| The address check accepts the configured hostname only once all three are set. A request carrying Cloudflare's forwarding headers (`Cf-Ray`, `Cf-Connecting-Ip`) under any other name is refused 403 | A tunnel set to rewrite the Host to `127.0.0.1` (or to a `.ts.net` name) must not arrive as "this computer" or as the phone. Cloudflare adds those headers to everything it forwards, whatever the browser sent. |
| The Cloudflare hostname is never asked for the window's key, and `?key=` there sets no cookie | As for Tailscale: the key is for Nines' own window on this computer, and a cookie set by a public name would be a way around it. |
| Left out on purpose: a list of who is allowed in, a log of who came in, and a switch for keeping an offline copy on a browser that came in through Cloudflare | One owner: the Cloudflare policy names the only email, and Tailscale's tailnet is the owner's own. The offline copy is a question for the third step, when the owner has used the door. |
| In Settings the door is a fold under Phone, on the computer only: what it means in two sentences and a link to the README, the three boxes and Save. Its title says where Nines answers once it is on. On a device that came in from outside, This device says how in one sentence | Its connector is installed on the computer, so that is where it is set up, beside the other way in (principle 8). A fold keeps the main page inside its word budget. This device is shown on every device, including a phone opened from the Home Screen where Phone is not, so the sentence the owner checks is always there. |
| When Cloudflare's sign-in runs out on an open page, the page (`src/signin.ts`), on the Cloudflare address only, asks the health check again with `redirect: 'manual'` each time it goes offline; a redirect (or Nines' own 401) means the sign-in ran out, and the page is loaded again in full at its own address with `?signin=`, which the service worker always fetches from the network (the saved page only if the network fails); `?signin=` is then taken out of the address. At most once every two minutes, noted in this browser's storage, and never without storage | A `fetch` cannot follow Cloudflare's redirect to its sign-in on another origin, so the page would think the computer is off and the service worker would keep serving the saved copy for ever. Only a full load lets Cloudflare ask for the code. Waiting for the network on that one load stops the saved page from answering after four seconds. The two-minute note keeps a Cloudflare that keeps redirecting from making the page reload in a loop. On the computer and through Tailscale offline means offline, so none of it runs there. What Cloudflare answers is assumed from how it is documented to behave; it could not be tried here. |
| PyJWT 2.15.1 with cryptography 50.0.2 (and cffi and pycparser, now on every system), pinned in `requirements.txt` | Verifying an RS256 signature needs RSA, which Python's standard library does not have. MIT, Apache-2.0 or BSD, MIT-0 and BSD. `closet selfcheck` signs and checks a token with a key it makes, so the packaged program is proved to have them. |

## The browser tests run in parallel (21 November)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Each spec file gets a backend of its own: a worker-scoped fixture in `e2e/fixtures.ts` starts a fresh one (free port, empty data folder) at the first test of each file and stops the last when the worker ends; `baseURL` points at it. The single backend that `global-setup.ts` started for the whole suite is gone (it still builds the page) | The suite ran in one worker because one backend was shared and its state carried over from file to file. One backend per worker would still have carried a file's leftovers into the next file that worker happened to run, so a file's result would depend on which files ran before it. A backend starts in about a second, which costs a few seconds over the run, and every file starts from the same first run whatever runs it and in whatever order. |
| Files run side by side; the tests inside a file still run in order on the same backend (`fullyParallel: false`) | Many files build up a closet over several tests (a conversation, a trip, a wishlist). Splitting them test by test would mean setting each test up again, for little gain once the files run in parallel. |
| Workers: 4 in CI, half the cores (at most 4) on a computer, `E2E_WORKERS` or `--workers` to choose | GitHub's runner for a public repository has 4 cores; each worker runs a browser and a backend. On a 4-core computer the whole suite took 11.5 minutes in one worker before this change; after it, 13.6 minutes with one worker (each file now starts its own backend and, where it uses the demo closet, prepares it), 8.2 with two, 6.1 with three and 5.2 with four. Four passed five full runs in a row there, one of them with the files in reverse order. A computer with fewer cores gets fewer, so it is not swamped; any computer with 8 cores or more gets 4. |
| Logging in is one helper, `signIn` in `e2e/fixtures.ts`, through the API (the passphrase on a first run, the login after); `login(page)` uses it rather than typing the passphrase | Each file now starts from a first run, so each needs the passphrase set; the screens themselves are tested in `01-auth.spec.ts`. One place to change when the passphrase goes. The specs that start their own backend with stand-ins still go through the first-run screens themselves, as before. |
| Tests that go offline wait with `serviceWorkerInCharge(page)`, which reloads until a page has loaded with the service worker in charge from the start | With several tests at once, a page loaded while the worker was starting sometimes stayed outside it (still without a controller 15 seconds later, even after a reload) or had read its data before the worker took charge, so nothing was saved for offline; the tests waited for a controller and failed, 1 to 5 times in every 150 or so runs of those tests with 2 to 4 workers. With the reloads: none in 212, and none in the full runs since. |
| `25-conversation.spec.ts` is two parts on two closets, and the test of the long message and the checks on a phone and a computer moved up to follow the "not answered" one | All its tests on one closet asked Claude 17 to 19 times, against at most 20 a day (`stylejobs.MAX_PER_DAY`); when the computer was busy Today was asked once or twice more and the last message was refused. The checks still look at the conversation with saved lines and a failed message in it. |
| `12-would-work.spec.ts` empties the demo closet's wishlist before each test; `12-missing-life.spec.ts` waits for "Apply to similar suggestions" to reach its stand-in before checking it | The demo closet has two example wishes; the test expected one wish after adding one only because an earlier file had emptied the list on the shared backend. The other test checked the stand-in right after the click, before the request had arrived, and failed once on a busy computer. |
| A wait in a browser test lasts up to 20 seconds, and a test up to 90 (they were 10 and 60) | With four files at once on GitHub's runner, each on a backend that has just started and has drawn and worked out nothing yet, Today's first outfit took over 10 seconds twice in the first run there. The answer was right and late. A wait only lasts that long when the test is about to fail, so passing runs are no slower. |

## A background copy, and the window as its own program (the first of two steps, 21 November)

The phone often could not reach Nines because Nines was not running on the computer: it ran only in the signed-in
session, in the same process as its window, and nothing started it again. The owner's other app does not have this
problem: a copy with no window runs in the background and its window is a separate program that only shows it. The
owner asked for Nines to work the same way. This first step is the part that works on every system; the second adds
the Windows scheduled task that starts the background copy when the computer starts and again when it stops, the
switch for it in Settings, updates through that task, and a home for the API key that a copy started before sign-in
can read. The code is in `closet/cli.py` (`--background`, `_window_only`) and `closet/instance.py`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| `closet --background` runs the server alone: no window, no tray icon, no browser, no questions and no message boxes. What it would say goes into closet.log. Its lock file says `"background": true` | A copy started by the system before anyone signs in has nobody to show anything to, and a dialog nobody can answer would hold it up. Other launches read the lock to know that this copy has no window to show. |
| A background copy never takes another port. If its port is busy it waits up to 60 seconds (logged once), then logs which port and that phone access depends on it, and stops with exit code 3. Beside a copy already running for the same closet it starts nothing: exit code 0 when that copy has its port, 5 when it is on another one | Phone access (Tailscale Serve, Cloudflare's connector) is set up for one port; a copy on the next port would look fine on the computer and leave the phone with nothing. A non-zero exit lets whatever started it try again. The ordinary launch still moves to the next free port, as before. |
| Opened while a background copy runs (of any version), the program becomes a window onto it: Nines' own window in a process of its own, at the background copy's keyed address, with the same storage folder. Closing that window ends its process; the background copy keeps running. When the window cannot be used, the browser opens at the keyed address, as before | The background copy has no window, and under a task started before sign-in it could not show one. A window in the owner's own session can. The window process logs to `window.log`, so two processes never rotate one file. |
| Opening the program again while such a window is open brings that window forward. The window process listens on a loopback port of its own and keeps `closet-window.lock` (its process id, that port and a random token); the next launch sends the token and the window process shows its window.
