A lock whose process has gone, whose port does not answer or whose answer is not "shown" is stale, and the next window writes its own over it | The same idea as the token in the main lock file: only something that can read the closet's folder can ask, and there is no web endpoint to add. Launches stay serialized by the startup lock, which the window process lets go of only once its lock is written, so two quick double-clicks open one window. Considered: asking the background copy to relay the request (it would need to know about the window processes, and could not reach the owner's session from a task anyway), and a named mutex or pipe (different on each system). |
| A launch newer than the running background copy does not replace it here; it opens the window onto it and logs that a newer program was opened. Replacing an older ordinary copy is unchanged. An update of a background copy starts the new program with `--background` (`_launch_args`) and `--replace`, which replaces the old one as before | The background copy is to be restarted by what started it (the scheduled task, in the second step), so that it keeps running as the task's program rather than as the owner's. |
| Quit in Settings stops a background copy as it stops any copy; a window open onto it then shows the page's closed screen (where Quit was pressed) or its offline screen | Nothing in Quit assumes a window. Building more for a window whose background copy has gone was not worth it: the page already handles being offline. |

## The program file: Nines (21 November, the owner's choice)

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A clean break: from the release after 0.11.0 the release carries only `Nines.exe` (Windows) and `Nines-mac-arm64.zip` holding `Nines.app` (Mac), and the updater looks only for those. There is no code that hands over from `Closet.exe`: the installed `Closet.exe` 0.11.0 looks for an asset called `Closet.exe`, finds none, and says the release has no download for this computer. The owner downloads `Nines.exe` once from the release page, puts it where `Closet.exe` was, starts it and deletes `Closet.exe`. The release notes and the README say so | One owner on one PC. One download by hand is safer than code that renames a running program under a new name, which would be written once, run once, and could not be tried on the owner's computer beforehand. Moving the data folder is a rename of a folder no program is using, which is safe to do in code (below). |
| Windows only: no hand-over for `Closet.app` | There is no installed Mac copy. |
| If `Closet.exe` is still running when `Nines.exe` starts, nothing new handles it: the folder is not moved (see the row on the data folder below), the data folder's lock finds the running copy, `Nines.exe` is newer, so it asks whether to close the old one and use the new version, as any newer copy does, and closes it with the token from the lock file | Both read the same lock in the same data folder, so there are never two servers on one closet. Keeping the old copy just opens it, as before. |
| Start at login: the entry is now named Nines (the Windows registry Run value `Nines`, the Mac launch agent `app.nines.plist`, the Linux `nines.desktop`). At every start the packaged program carries an old entry named Closet over (`autostart.carry_over`, called in `cli._serve` once this copy is the one that serves): if it exists, it is removed and the new one is written pointing at the running program. Settings shows start at login as on only for the new entry | An old entry would open the deleted `Closet.exe`, or, while it is still there, start both programs at login. A copy that only opens the one already running changes nothing. Checking costs one registry read or two file checks at start. |
| The data folder moves from `Closet` to `Nines` in the user folder (`datafolder.py`, the owner's choice the same day). At start, before anything opens it (the instance lock, the database, the log): when no data folder is chosen with `CLOSET_DATA_DIR`, `Closet` exists and `Nines` does not, it is renamed with one `os.rename`. When that fails for any reason (a file the old program holds open on Windows, no permission), nothing changes, this run uses `Closet`, the log says why, and the next start tries again. When both exist, `Nines` is used and `Closet` is left alone; nothing is merged or deleted. When the lock in `Closet` shows a copy still running there, it is not moved: the start goes on as for any copy already running in that folder (a newer one offers to close it, then serves from `Closet` this time), and the next start moves it. A start that is an update's hand-over (`--replace`) never moves it, since the old program's helper is still watching that folder. After the move, the settings that hold a path into the folder (`backup_folder`, `last_backup.folder`, `update_ready.path`) are pointed into `Nines`; a settings file that cannot be read is moved as it is | So that nothing keeps the old name and nobody later has to remember why it does. A rename on the same disk is all or nothing, where copying and deleting could leave half a closet in each folder. Falling back to `Closet` for a run costs nothing, where forcing the move could cost the data. A folder chosen with `CLOSET_DATA_DIR` belongs to whoever chose it, so it is never moved. |
| The Mac bundle keeps its identifier `app.closet` | It is the bundle's identity, not a name anyone reads; macOS ties the app's permissions to it. With no Mac copy installed either choice would have worked; keeping it is the smaller change. |

## Kept running by Windows (the second of two steps, 21 November)

The owner asked for Nines to start with the computer and stay running, as the owner's other app does, so the phone
can reach it whenever the computer is awake. On Windows, in the downloaded program, **Settings → Phone → Keep Nines
running** sets up a scheduled task that runs a background copy (`--background`, from the first step). The code is
`closet/background.py`, with the launch and the hand-over in `closet/cli.py`, updates through the task in
`closet/updates.py` and the sealed key in `closet/apikey.py`. The Mac and Linux keep start at login and are offered
nothing new.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Turning it on copies the running program to `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Nines\Nines.exe` and the task runs that copy with `--background --port <the port this copy answers on now> --home <the user folder>` (and `--data-dir` only when the data folder was chosen with `CLOSET_DATA_DIR`). `--home` sets `USERPROFILE`, `HOME`, and `LOCALAPPDATA`/`APPDATA` when missing, before the data folder is settled | The task must not depend on where the owner keeps the downloaded file (it was to be renamed, and it is moved). The port is the one in use, never the default: on the owner's computer another app holds 8765 and Nines has been on the next free port. A task started before sign-in may not have the owner's environment, so the home is passed; the data folder is not, so that the folder's move from Closet to Nines (`datafolder.py`) still happens in the task's copy and the task follows the closet (`task_data_dir` works out the folder from the home each time). |
| One administrator prompt. The elevated script is written to `setup.ps1` in Nines' folder, and what Windows' prompt starts is a short command (under 2,000 characters, a test checks) that reads the file's bytes once into memory, checks their SHA-256 against the hash given on that command line and runs exactly those bytes only if it matches. The script asks Windows whether it will run a task as the owner without a sign-in, by registering and running a throwaway task (`whoami`, S4U, no stored password) and reading its result. If it ran, the task starts when the computer turns on (S4U); otherwise when the owner signs in (interactive). A declined prompt (exit code 1223, ERROR_CANCELLED) is reported in plain words; the copied program is removed and nothing else was changed | The script itself is about 10,000 characters encoded, and `Start-Process -Verb RunAs` goes through ShellExecuteEx, which has long taken only about 2,048 characters of parameters. Reading the file once and running what was checked leaves no moment in which another program could swap it. The other app tries the boot task, waits for its copy to answer on its port, and asks again for a sign-in task if it did not. Here the ordinary copy the owner is using still has the port at that moment, so the background copy could not answer on it yet; asking Windows directly with a throwaway task decides the same question in the same prompt, without a second one. |
| Task Scheduler's own restart ("If the task fails, restart every…", `RestartCount`/`RestartInterval`) is kept, but what keeps Nines running is a second trigger that repeats every 5 minutes, with "do not start a new instance" (`IgnoreNew`) and no time limit | Microsoft's documentation describes the restart as applying when "the task fails to run (the last run result of the task is not a success)" ([Task Settings](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/previous-versions/windows/it-pro/windows-server-2008-r2-and-2008/cc722178(v=ws.11)); the schema says "if the task fails for any reason", [RestartOnFailure](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/taskschd/taskschedulerschema-restartonfailure-settingstype-element)). Neither says that a program that ran and ended counts, and reports from people who tried it (a program exiting with 1 is recorded as completed and not restarted) say it does not: Task Scheduler does not look at a program's exit code. "Do not start a new instance" means "the Task Scheduler service will not run the new instance of the task and will not stop the instance that is already running" (same Task Settings page), so the repeating trigger starts a stopped copy within five minutes and does nothing while one runs. The repetition lasts 9,999 days, written out, because leaving the duration out has meant different things on different Windows versions. |
| The task's security descriptor gives SYSTEM and administrators full control and the owner's account read and run (`GRGX`). The account is named by its SID, taken from `whoami /user /fo csv /nh`, in the task's principal, its sign-in trigger and the descriptor. Windows' commands are run so that their words, in the computer's own language and code page, are decoded with replacement or not at all: starting and ending the task never raise. The task runs at normal priority (`-Priority 4`; Task Scheduler's default, 7, is below normal, with low I/O and memory priority) | A launch, an update and "Start it again" run the task as the owner without another prompt. Full control, as the other app grants, would also let any program running as the owner change what the task runs or its run level, which a later boot would then carry out. A SID is ASCII on any Windows and needs no translation from a localized account name; a decoding error must never stop a launch or the update helper. Normal priority keeps the server quick for the phone on a busy computer. Stopping the copy never needs the task: it is asked to quit with the token in its lock file (and ended by its process id, which is the owner's own process, if it does not). Whether `schtasks /End` works with read and run alone is not known; it is only tried as a last resort after a failed update. |
| Once it is on, the program never serves by itself. A launch that finds the task set up for its closet and no copy answering starts the task, waits up to 90 seconds outside the startup lock (the background copy takes that lock too), then takes the lock again and becomes a window onto the background copy. When Windows refuses to start the task (it was removed, or is not allowed), that is asked at once, in those words; when the background copy does not answer, after the wait. The owner may then open Nines on its own this time; it serves as before the switch and Settings says the background copy is not running, with **Start it again**. If the copy Windows runs has updated itself past the program opened, that newer copy is started on its own instead (`--on-its-own`), never the older program. A launch never shows a message box or asks a question while it holds the startup lock: what it says then is shown once it lets go, and moving the background copy (below) is asked and done outside the lock. A background copy waits for its port before it takes the lock | Opened just after boot, before the task's start, the launch and the task meet without a race: Task Scheduler starts nothing beside a copy already starting (`IgnoreNew`), and the launch only waits for whichever answers. A copy of its own on the port would keep the background copy out. The question is a way out when the task is broken, rather than a launch that does nothing. Nothing is shown during the wait (usually a few seconds); the outcome is logged. |
| Turning it on, the ordinary copy the owner is using registers the task (it does not start it), then starts this program afresh with `--after <its process id>` and quits. The fresh launch waits for it to be gone (30 seconds; if it is still answering after that, it is treated as going rather than opened), then starts the task, waits, and opens a window onto the background copy | The port must be let go of before the background copy can take it, and the owner should end up looking at Nines without doing anything. A fresh launch is the same path as any later double-click, so the hand-over has no code of its own beyond starting it. Turning it on also turns off start at login (an entry there would only open a window onto the background copy at every sign-in) and remembers whether it was on. |
| While it is on, updates go to the copy in Nines' folder, which can become newer than the program the owner opens. The marker records the copy's version (at set-up and each time it starts) and the owner's program and its version (at set-up and each time a window opens onto the copy). Turning it off never throws the newer program away: before the prompt its bytes are kept aside; once the task is gone they take the place of the owner's program, the older one moved aside as `<name>.old.exe` as an update does (and tidied away as after an update), and the owner is told in a sentence that their file is now that version. If the owner's file cannot be replaced (a folder Nines may not write to), the newer copy stays in Nines' folder, is what carries on, and the sentence says to open Nines from there. Start at login, if it is turned back on, opens whichever program carries on | Running an older program against a closet a newer one has upgraded ends in "this closet was made by a newer Nines" and no Nines at all. Putting the newer program where the owner's shortcut points keeps everything the owner knows working, and is the same move an update makes; keeping the copy in Nines' folder instead would have left the owner's shortcut opening the older file. |
| Turning it off is done in the owner's session: the background copy asks the window open onto it (through the window's door, with its token: the command `off`), which shows the prompt, removes the task, the copy and the sealed key (putting the key back in Credential Manager), turns start at login back on if it was on, starts an ordinary copy of itself and closes. Without such a window (a browser), the page is told to open Nines from its icon first. A declined prompt is written to a file in the data folder that Settings reads for ten minutes. An ordinary copy (while the background copy is not running) does it there and then | A background copy started before sign-in runs where Windows' prompt cannot be shown. The window is the owner's own program in the owner's own session. |
| Endpoints that change it (`on`, `off`, `restart`) are refused from a phone or through Cloudflare (`gate.came_in`); reading it is allowed from anywhere. The phone shows one sentence in Settings → Phone; a Mac or a copy run from source shows nothing | Windows asks on the computer's screen. A sentence about something that cannot exist on the Mac would be reading for nothing. |
| A background copy run by the task updates itself in its own folder as any copy does (backup, old program kept beside it, put back if the new one does not answer within 3 minutes), but the helper is started outside the task's job where Windows allows it (`CREATE_BREAKAWAY_FROM_JOB`, falling back without it, which is logged), asks the old copy to quit, and starts the new version through the task. Going back puts the old program in place first, then ends the task (in case it still counts the failed copy as running) and starts it again. A helper that finds itself still in the job (`IsProcessInJob`) never ends or starts the task: it starts the new version, and if need be the old one, itself, with the task's own arguments, and the repeating trigger takes the copy back under the task the next time it stops. A newer `Nines.exe` opened by hand beside an older background copy asks, in the same words as the older-copy question, whether to move the background copy to this version, and does it through the same path, with no administrator prompt (the folder is the owner's, and the owner may run the task) | So the background copy stays the task's program, restarted by Windows if it stops later. Task Scheduler would start nothing while the old copy still runs, so it is asked to quit first. A helper left in the job would be stopped by ending the task, and while it lives in the job Task Scheduler may count the task as running, so starting it would do nothing. The other app's helper also leaves the job. The helper also looks in `Nines` for a copy that was in `Closet`, since the task's new copy may move the folder as it starts. |
| While it is on, the API key is also kept in `api-key.sealed` in the data folder, sealed with Windows' data protection (DPAPI) under the machine's key (`CRYPTPROTECT_LOCAL_MACHINE`), with fixed extra entropy. It is written when the switch is turned on (copied from Credential Manager) and whenever the key is saved or removed while it is on (a removal writes "no key", so a key Credential Manager could not forget stays forgotten); it is read only when Credential Manager gives no key; it goes back into Credential Manager and is removed when the switch is turned off. Never in a backup (backups copy the closet's database and photos only, and a test checks), never logged, never sent to the page. The owner was told this changes the letter of the hard rule in AGENTS.md and agreed; the rule now names it | A task started before sign-in (S4U) has no password-derived key, so Credential Manager, which is sealed with the user's own key, most likely cannot be read or written there. The trade-off, honestly: the machine's key means anything running on this computer that can read the file can unseal it (the entropy is in this public repository); the file is in the owner's profile folder, which other accounts cannot normally read, and it is useless on another computer. User-scope DPAPI would be safer and would not work before sign-in, for the same reason as Credential Manager. |
| The Claude plan's route looks for Claude Code in the owner's own folders as well as on PATH (`~/.local/bin`, WinGet's links, and now npm's `%APPDATA%\npm\claude.cmd`), with the home and `APPDATA` from `--home` | A task's PATH may lack the owner's own entries. Whether Claude Code's own sign-in (kept under the user folder) works for a copy started before sign-in has not been tried. |

What could not be verified without a real Windows computer: the administrator prompt itself and its declined exit
code; whether the throwaway S4U task decides correctly on the owner's account (a Microsoft account may differ from a
local one); `New-ScheduledTaskTrigger -Once -RepetitionInterval -RepetitionDuration` attached to the boot or sign-in
trigger; whether read-and-run (`GRGX`) lets the owner start the task with `schtasks /Run`; the environment of an S4U
task (profile loaded or not); DPAPI, Credential Manager and Claude Code before sign-in; leaving the task's job; and
WebView2 holding files in `Nines\window` when the folder is to be moved; a SID in `-UserId` and in the sign-in trigger's `-User`; whether `-Priority 4` is taken together with the other settings; and the hash-checked launcher behind the prompt (the CI test runs the same launcher without the prompt). Also open: `SetSecurityDescriptor(..., 0)` lets Task Scheduler add an entry of its own for the task's principal, which may give the owner's account more than read and run (flag 0x10 stops that, but whether the task then still runs for its principal is not known); the elevated script writes its result into a folder the owner's programs can write; and whether Task Scheduler counts a task as running while a helper that could not leave its job lives on. One test registers a real task on the CI's
Windows runner, without the prompt (its account is an administrator), and sees it start a background copy, killed and
started again; see `tests/test_background.py`.

## The doors decide who comes in (the third of three steps to the access model of the owner's other app, 22 November, the owner's choice)

The owner decided, before the three steps began, that Nines needs no passphrase of its own: "this app doesn't need a
passcode for what it is… i'm not concerned about someone on my tailnet seeing my closet". The first two steps built
the doors ("The computer's own door", "The doors from outside"); the owner used all three on real devices, and on
their phone through Tailscale Settings → This device named their login. This step removes the passphrase and makes
Tailscale's word the lock at its door. Each door now has exactly one check, in `gate.py`: on this computer the
window's key; through Tailscale, Tailscale saying who is asking; through Cloudflare, the verified Access sign-in. This
replaces "one passphrase, one owner" (Security) and the passphrase rows of the two earlier steps.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A `.ts.net` request (pages, images, calls; not `GET /api/health`) that Tailscale's word does not name is refused 401: a sentence under `/api/` ("Nines opens through Tailscale only when Tailscale says who you are. Open it on a device signed in to your Tailscale, or open Nines on the computer to check that Tailscale is set up there."), and a page titled "Nines could not tell who you are" elsewhere. 401, as for Cloudflare's missing sign-in, so the page treats both the same way | With the passphrase gone, Tailscale's word is the only thing standing between another program on the computer, or a tagged device on the tailnet, and the closet. The sentence covers both reasons a refusal happens: a device Tailscale does not name, and Nines not believing that the connection came from Tailscale on this computer. The health check stays open, as before, so another copy of the program and the tests can ask it. |
| On Windows the check (`tailnet.WindowsCheck`) has three answers. **Believed**: Windows shows the Tailscale service (or the `tailscaled.exe` it started) at the other end. **Refused**: Windows positively shows otherwise: another program owns the connection; the connection is not in Windows' table at all; or the Tailscale service is not installed or not running. **Believed, logged once**: Windows could not be asked (a call failed, or refused, such as opening the service for its status, which is now told apart from "not installed" by its error code) | The owner asked not to be shut out by a check that is merely unsure, and to be strict where Nines positively knows the request is not Tailscale's. A connection missing from the table is counted as a "no", not an unknown: Tailscale Serve keeps its connection open until it has the answer, so only a program that sent its request and dropped the connection (to slip past the lookup) is ever missing. Tailscale not running is a "no" too: then Tailscale cannot have passed the request on. What stays believed is a lookup that fails outright, which a program on the computer cannot cause; the case expected in practice is a copy started by Windows before sign-in that may not open the service (only reasoned, not tried). Without Tailscale's headers there is no one to believe, and the request is refused whatever the check says. Elsewhere (a Mac) there is no check, as before. |
| The passphrase, its screens, the session cookie and its store, wrong-guess slowing, `closet reset-passphrase` and `auth.py` are gone. Every route lost its `dependencies=protected`: no route checks anything of its own; the gate checks the door before any route, the same for all of them. One test (`test_gate.py`) calls every route of the API through Tailscale without a name and expects the refusal, in place of the thirty-odd "needs the login" tests per module | One check in one place. The per-module tests tested the passphrase; the door's check is shared, so it is tested once over every route, which also catches a route added later. |
| An existing closet's `settings.json` loses `passphrase`, `sessions` and `failed_logins` the first time it is opened (`state.GONE`); nothing reads them. No database migration | The old hash and session hashes should not be left lying in the file once nothing uses them. |
| `GET /api/session` stays, answering `{authenticated: true, version, profile}`; a leftover `closet_session` cookie is ignored and taken away there. `POST /api/setup`, `/api/login` and `/api/logout` answer 410 with "This page is from an older Nines. Reload the page, then try again." | The page from 0.12.0 opens with `/api/session` and shows its passphrase screen unless `authenticated` is true, so it now opens the closet as it is. If it is already on its passphrase screen (or Log out is pressed in it), what it sends gets the sentence it shows, which says to reload; a reload brings the new page. |
| A 401 on any call (the window's key missing, Cloudflare's sign-in gone, Tailscale's word missing) shows a screen of its own, "Nines didn't let this device in", with the server's sentence and **Reload**, instead of the login screen | A page the service worker kept can be opened at a door that turns it away; every call would fail with nothing said. |
| **Log out** is gone. What it also did, forgetting the copies the device keeps for offline (Today's and the other screens' saved answers in storage, the service worker's saved data and pictures), is **Forget saved copies**, in a closed fold under Settings → This device, with one sentence; not while offline. It does not touch photos waiting to be sent, the theme or the app's own files | There is nothing to log out of. Forgetting the saved copies is about this device, so it sits with This device; it is occasional, so it is folded (principle 5). Photos waiting to be sent are the owner's own work, which logging out never removed either. |
| Given up | A second factor: if Tailscale or Cloudflare is set up more widely than meant (a share, an Access policy) or an account there is taken over, nothing more is asked. Anyone on the owner's tailnet, and anyone the computer is shared with in Tailscale, can open the closet. The owner's choice for what Nines holds; the README says it plainly. |

## "Phone" in Settings becomes "Other devices" (22 November, the owner's choice)

The owner: "the 'phone' section isn't really anything to do with phone, since instructions apply if im accessing
through another computer as well."

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The Settings group is titled **Other devices**, and its sentences say "your phone or another computer" where both apply ("Your phone or another computer can open Nines at …", "To reach Nines from your phone or another computer, run this once on this computer"). "Phone" stays where the thing is about a phone: the QR code to scan with the iPhone's camera, and adding Nines to the Home Screen | A Tailscale share reaches every device signed in to the tailnet; a computer opens the same address in its browser. The sentences grew by a few words, not by a sentence, so the screen's reading load stays where it was. |
| Not renamed: `phone.py`, `PhoneAccess`, the `phone` group key, `s-phone`, test ids, `/api/settings`' `phone` field, and older entries here and in past CHANGELOG versions | Words the owner reads change; names the code relies on do not. |

## The word sweep: Closet to Nines (22 November, the owner's request)

The owner: "can you check to make sure 'closet' the term for the app is removed everywhere." The screens already said
Nines; what was left were the program's own words, three names on the computer, and the docs. Every occurrence was
read and put in one of three groups.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **Changed: every word a person reads that means the app.** Log lines ("Nines opens in its own window", "Nines %s did not start"), the command-line help (`prog` is `Nines`) and the entry point's usage lines, the user agents sent out (`Nines/1 (model download)`, `Nines (sign-in check)`, the weather's `Nines/<version>`; generic, nothing about the owner), the temporary folders a call to Claude Code runs in (`nines-tag-…`, `nines-correct-…`), comments and docstrings in the code and the tests, the README, the living docs and `packaging/README.md`. A test (`tests/test_app_name.py`) reads the code for the old name in log calls, user agents, the tray's labels, the command-line help and the messages the owner is shown, with a short list of the names kept on purpose to carry the old one over | The name the owner reads is one name. The test is narrow so that "the closet", the place, never trips it. |
| **Changed, with the old name carried over: the names the owner can meet on the computer.** The backup subfolder is `Nines backup` (`backup.SUBFOLDER`). The first backup after the update renames `Closet backup` in the chosen folder to it with one rename, under the backup lock, so none of Nines' own backups is using it; when the rename fails (a sync app or another program has a file in it open, no permission) that backup goes into `Closet backup`, the log says why, and the next one tries again; when both exist, `Nines backup` is used and `Closet backup` is left as it is, nothing merged or deleted. A restore looks in `Nines backup`, then `Closet backup`, then the folder named itself | Renaming once rather than reading both: the snapshots share one store of images, so a second folder beside the first would copy every photo again and prune the two separately, for a folder name. One rename on one disk is all or nothing, the same move as the data folder's (`datafolder.py`). A synced folder sees one folder renamed, which OneDrive and iCloud Drive carry over as a rename; the changelog says so for anything else that reads the folder. |
| The API key's keychain entry is named Nines (`apikey.SERVICE`; the Windows credential `Nines/anthropic-api-key`, the Mac's generic password with service `Nines`). At every start that serves, `apikey.carry_over` looks under Closet: a key there is saved under Nines, read back, and only then is the old entry removed. When saving or reading back fails, the old entry is kept and read as before (`keychain_key` reads Nines, then Closet), and the next start tries again. When both hold a key, the one under Nines is the owner's latest and the old one is removed. Removing the key removes it from both, so an old entry cannot bring it back. The key is never logged; the log says only that it moved | The owner does nothing and loses nothing: at no moment is the key in neither entry. On Windows a background copy started before sign-in most likely cannot read Credential Manager: carrying over then fails quietly and it goes on using the sealed file, whose own names (`api-key.sealed`, the entropy `closet/api-key/v1`) are unchanged, since changing the entropy would make a sealed key unreadable. Turning Keep Nines running on seals the key from either entry. |
| The log in the data folder is `logs/nines.log`. At start, before the log is opened, `closet.log` and its older parts are renamed to it when `nines.log` is not there yet; a part another copy still holds open is left as it is | Cheap and safe: it is only a log, and a file that cannot be renamed costs nothing. |
| **Left alone, on purpose: technical names nobody reads as the app's.** The Python package `closet` and its imports, logger names, the environment variables `CLOSET_*`, the cookie `closet_key_<port>`, the headers `X-Closet-Client` and `X-Closet-Instance-Token`, the lock files (`closet.lock`, `closet-window.lock`), the database `closet.db`, the snapshots' file names in a backup (`closet-<date>.sqlite`, the closet's database), localStorage keys, test ids, CSS classes, thread names, the Mac bundle identifier `app.closet`, URL paths, `packaging/closet.spec` and the repository's name | Renaming them would sign devices out, lose a setting or break what the code relies on, for a name nobody sees. |
| **Kept: "closet" the place, and history.** The tab called Closet, "your closet", "the demo closet", the closet picture, the Closet screen's component and its docs heading are about the owner's clothes. Past CHANGELOG versions, the entries here before this one and the assessment reports record what was true then ("the program was still Closet.exe") and keep the old name. The tagging prompt's "a private closet app", which Claude reads and the owner does not, is left so that tagging does not change | Closet is still what the place holds; history is kept as it was written. |

## Ribbed cuffs on trousers (22 November)

The owner: "i don't see a way to add ribbed cuffs to my joggers". The choice was there (the hem, in Fix the
details), but three things hid it: it sat under a heading that said only "Hem"; on joggers, sweatpants and the other
sportswear and lounge bottoms whose kind sets the hem (`BOTTOM_DRAWINGS`), no hem choice changed the drawing, so all
of them were offered as words that did nothing; and the ribbed cuff that joggers were always drawn with was a flat band
that looked like a turn-up.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A hem that was told (by Claude or picked by the owner) wins over the kind's usual hem; "plain" and "na" leave the kind's (joggers' and sweatpants' ribbed cuff, running shorts' split, track pants' plain hem). One rule, `taxonomy.kind_hem_finish`, used by the drawing (`schema.drawing_for`) and by the details the item is drawn from (`illustrations.drawn_answer`), so the Hem row says what the drawing shows | The rest of the kind's look (a drawstring, side pockets, a tapered leg) still wins over the details, as before (row on `BOTTOM_DRAWINGS` above). The hem is the one part people change and the photo shows: track pants with ribbed cuffs exist, and a hem seen on the photo outranks the usual one, as with the everyday trouser kinds. "Plain" is what the tagger says when it saw nothing special, so it cannot be told apart from an open hem chosen on purpose |
| Picking "plain" on joggers keeps their ribbed cuffs (it is offered under "These look the same in the drawing") | In the owner's taxonomy joggers are defined by their ribbed ankle cuffs; trousers like joggers with an open hem are the kind "Open hem sweatpants" (`open_hem_sweatpants`). Could have been: the owner's own "plain" wins too, which needs the drawing to know who said the hem |
| The trouser hem group on Fix the details is **Hem and cuffs** (`details.GROUPS["hem_cuffs"]`); a top's stays **Hem** | A trouser's cuffs (turn-up, roll, rib) are hem finishes; the owner looked for "cuffs". Tops keep "Sleeves and cuffs" |
| A ribbed trouser cuff is drawn as a knitted tube gripping the ankle: ribs down its depth (one about every 2 cm), and three short folds fanning up from its top where the leg gathers into it; a shorts' ribbed hem gets the ribs only. The outline is unchanged, so the figure as worn (which reshapes the same drawing) shows the same cuff | The flat band read as a turn-up at every size. Drawing version 20; the demo closet's heather grey joggers are drawn again, and no other demo drawing changed |

## A top's ribbing, a collar in the trim colour, and plain weave on a knit (22 November)

The owner, with a drawing and a photo of each: "it wont let me change the collar color of this overshirt. the collar is
the same color as the rest of the coat. also for this crewneck sweatshirt there's a weird texture plus the cuffs are a
different color, even when i tell claude to change it so that it all matches."

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A top's rib bands (cuffs, hem band, a turtle or knitted collar, a cardigan's front band, a vest's armholes) are drawn in the body's colour, or in the trim colour when there is one, with fine ribs a tone of that colour (`illustrate.RIB_GAP`, `RIB_W`, `RIB_OP`): along the sleeve on a cuff, upright elsewhere. They are no longer tinted | The tint was white at 12% on a dark cloth and black at 12% on a light one: on a black sweatshirt the cuffs and hem read as grey, a colour the garment does not have, and no detail held it, so neither Fix the details nor a sentence to Claude could change it. Real ribbing in the same yarn is the same colour; what sets it apart is its ribs and its edge. A rib running on a curve (a vest's armhole) is still drawn upright, which reads as ribbing at item size |
| Only the top template's bands changed. A band on trousers, shoes or accessories (a waistband, the turned inside of a rolled hem, a boot's elastic gore, a belt's keeper, a sock's or beanie's cuff) keeps its tint | There "band" means several things, and for most of them the tint is what shows the piece; a woven waistband has no ribs. Joggers' rib cuffs and a sock's cuff are left as they were: in "Left over" in BACKLOG |
| The detail `trim_color` is called **collar and cuff colour** on the item page, in the Collar and neck group; the correction prompt says that trim_color is the colour the collar, cuffs and ribbing (and a binding strip) are drawn in, that "the collar is the same colour as the rest" sets it to same, and that it is not the cloth | Claude had read the overshirt's black edging as a trim colour, and the drawing paints the collar in it. The detail that fixes it was there, but its name did not say collar, and a sentence about the collar's colour went to the cloth, which has no collar in it unless the collar is a part in another colour |
| Kept: one trim colour for collar, cuffs, ribbing and binding | A second field would need a schema change and a new question to Claude for every item. The drawing cannot tell a contrast collar from contrast binding, so an overshirt with black-bound edges and a plaid collar still comes out with a black collar until the owner sets it to same: in "Left over" in BACKLOG |
| The fabric `plain` said of a top that is always knitted (a T-shirt, polo, rugby, henley, sweatshirt, hoodie, sweater, cardigan or vest, the knitted sport and lounge tops: `taxonomy.KNIT_KINDS`; or, with no kind, a tee, polo, sweater or hoodie) is drawn as if no fabric was said: no surface, or the kind's own cloth where it has one. Claude's instructions say that plain is a plain weave and a smooth knit is jersey or french_terry; Fix the details words it "plain weave" | "plain" is the first value of the woven structures and reads as "nothing special": the owner's black sweatshirt was described as plain and drawn with the plain weave's woven check. The note is in the describing instructions, not the schema's field description, so the schema (and its version) is unchanged |
| Drawing version 21 (20 was the ribbed trouser cuff, which keeps its tint: it is not a top); the demo closet drawn again (seven tops changed) | Every item is drawn again the first time it is asked for, so the owner's existing pieces get the fix without being described again. |


## Claude adds to a garment's drawing (22 November, the owner's request)

The owner: "it might be useful to have a backup drawing option for garments so i can add custom things to the existing
templates", and, asked how: "I thought claude would use the existing template as a guide and just add the details i
tell it to." The code is `backend/closet/additions.py` (the owner's side), `backend/closet/tagger/adding.py` (asking
Claude), `backend/closet/drawing/added.py` (the parts read safely, the grid and the shape in words) and
`frontend/src/components/AddToDrawing.tsx`. This is not the open question of 21 November in the backlog (whether
garments should be drawn by Claude at all): Claude draws nothing by itself here, only what the owner asks for, on the
piece they choose.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Claude adds parts over the template's drawing, in the template's own coordinates; it never redraws the drawing. Each part is one of four kinds the renderer already paints (`inlay`: a piece in its own colour with an edge; `mark`: one with no edge; `stitch`: dashed topstitching; `line`: a line with no fill), laid over the template's parts (`Spec.added`, `illustrate.built`) | The owner's words: the template is the guide. The template drawing stays as it is under the parts, so the details, the owner's corrections and newer drawing versions keep working. Because the parts belong to the template's shape, the outfit figure moves them onto the wearer with the rest of the garment (`fitting.warp`): a patch on a sleeve bends with the sleeve round the arm, and what lies by the side seam goes behind the arm, as the owner's 3D rule asks. |
| Nothing Claude writes reaches a drawing as written: each path is read and written again from its numbers (absolute M, L, Q, C and Z only, every point an "x,y" pair, on or near the card), each colour must be `#rrggbb`, and a part that cannot be read is left out and named to the check. At most 30 parts of at most 120 points | The drawing is shown in the page, so markup from outside must not get into it. The figure's warp only moves "x,y" pairs. The limits keep the drawing light and the request's size known (`spend.ADD_TEXT_TOKENS`). |
| Claude cannot change, move or remove the template's own shapes (the outline, collar, sleeves, pockets, seams); a line asking for that is answered in words, pointing to the details, and the drawing stays as it was (`state: "said"`) | Those are the details, which the owner can already set and which every screen reads (`look.py`). A reshaped part would be a second drawing that no detail or version could follow. |
| Claude is shown the photo (the cutout laid on white), the drawing as it is with a grid in its own coordinates (a faint line every 10, a stronger one every 50, numbered along the top and left with strokes, so no font is needed), the shape in words (the outline, the body's box, each sleeve's corners, the landmarks, where the template's own parts lie), what was drawn on before (to keep exactly), and the owner's earlier lines (the latest 20) | Placing a part means writing its numbers, so Claude needs the frame they are in; the grid is drawn in the same coordinates as the parts, shifted with the drawing on its card. The rules ask Claude to read "left" and "right" as the wearer's unless the photo shows otherwise, to size things by the garment's landmarks rather than the photo, and to check each thing the line says against the photo, drawing as asked and saying so when the photo disagrees (as for a shoe's correction). |
| Then one check (`CHECKS`): the photo, the drawing before and after, the line, the parts and what the automatic checks found (a part that could not be read, one reaching past the garment's edge without being cut by it); Claude says good, or gives every part again, fixed, never undoing the owner's change | A part placed by numbers can land beside the place meant; looking at the result once catches that. One check, not two: the parts are small and the drawing under them is already right, and the first two real tries were said good after it. |
| On the model of the `drawing` role at medium effort, through the same routes as describing (the plan first), recorded with its own purpose, `add_to_drawing`, and its own estimate (`spend.estimate_add_to_drawing`: a 36,000-token prompt, the photo and two renders, the longest answer of 16,000 tokens; a test checks the longest prompt fits) | As a shoe's drawing: placing shapes is drawing. Its own purpose lets the owner tell these calls apart in the record. |
| One line at a time, at most 200 characters, drawn on a thread of its own (`additions.Drawer`) with the line kept on the item (`additions_state`, `additions_job`), so the page can be left; the item's progress shows it (`stage: "added"`), and the item page reads the item again every 2 s while it is drawn (`work.itemPoll`) | A drawing takes a minute or two, longer than a request should wait (Cloudflare's door gives up after 100 s). The closet's progress poll only reads an item again when it saw it under way, and a line can be done or refused before that. |
| When Claude cannot be reached, or the monthly limit holds it, nothing waits: the page says so and offers to send the line again. A line being drawn when Nines closes is not drawn again by itself (the page says so) | The owner is right there when they send a line, and a drawing costs something, so it is not started again unasked. A queue that waits, as a shoe's drawing does, can come later if it is missed. |
| The additions before the last change are kept, one level (`additions_prev`): **Go back to the drawing before** swaps the two, then reads **Go to the newer drawing**; **Take Claude's additions off** keeps them the same way. The owner's lines stay with the additions they made | As for a shoe's correction (DECISIONS, "The owner's correction of a shoe's drawing"): one tap back, without a history to manage. |
| The template's outline under the parts is kept as a fingerprint with them (`base`); when it changes (a detail, a newer drawing version), the sheet says the additions may sit out of place | The parts are absolute coordinates. Saying so is honest and costs nothing; moving them by themselves would guess. |
| In Fix the details, under the box and "Change the details one by one", in the same sheet. When Claude cannot turn a sentence in "What's wrong with it?" into details, **Have Claude draw it onto the drawing instead** puts the sentence in the new line, not sent | The details come first (they cost nothing and every screen reads them); drawing on is the fallback the owner asked for. The sentence is not sent for them: a drawing costs something, and the owner may want to shorten it. |
| Not for shoes (their drawing by Claude has its own note under it), nor a piece with no drawing, nor the demo closet (never sent to Claude). Only the front: a pair of trousers' small back drawing gets no parts, and Claude says so of anything on the back | A shoe's drawing is not a template drawing. The back drawing is a different view, whose coordinates the parts are not in. |

Tried with the real calls through the plan, before the owner, on two of the demo's open-licence photos (nothing kept):
the marl turtleneck with "brown suede elbow patches on both sleeves" (44 s, one check said good, 28,000 tokens read and
3,800 written, $0.14 at the API key's prices): an oval brown patch with its stitching on each elbow, along the sleeve,
and on the figure on each elbow as the arm hangs; Claude said the photo shows no patches and that real ones sit at the
back of the elbow, so they are drawn on the front as an approximation. And the plaid flannel shirt with "a thin black
strip binding the top edge of each chest pocket, and a small black leather tab at the hem on the left" (37 s, one
check, $0.11): a black strip across the top of each pocket and a small tab at the hem on the wearer's left (on the
viewer's right); Claude said the photo's pockets sit low, near the hem, not on the chest, and that it saw no binding or
tab, so it drew them as asked.

## Wishlist links and their prices (22 November, the owner's request)

The owner: "the wishlist needs to be set up so i can send urls and they can be logged for price drops". The code is
`backend/closet/productpage.py` (the address checked, the page fetched, its product read), `backend/closet/shop.py` (a
link kept as a find, read on a thread, and in the daily round), migration `0042_wishlist_links.sql`, and on the page
`LinkForm` and the find card in `frontend/src/screens/Shop.tsx`, the wishlist's row in `Missing.tsx` and the page
`/wardrobe/link` in `Wardrobe.tsx`. It builds on "Shopping for a wish" above, which still holds for Claude's finds.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A link's page is read by Nines first, from the owner's computer: what shops publish for search engines and link previews (schema.org `Product` and `Offer` in JSON-LD, with `@graph`, lists, an `AggregateOffer`'s `lowPrice`, a `priceSpecification` and a `ProductGroup`'s variants; then OpenGraph's `product:price:amount` and `product:price:currency`; then microdata's `itemprop="price"` given as `content`). Claude is asked to read that one page only when this gives no price, or the page cannot be opened, as a price check (`tagger/shopping.py`'s `prices`, purpose `shop_prices`, the same routes and monthly limit) | Reading the page costs nothing and takes a second; most shops publish their price this way. Claude's price check already does what is needed for the rest and is recorded and held under the limit, so nothing new asks Claude. Someone could have asked Claude every time: it would cost something on every link and every day. |
| Among several offers the price is the lowest that can be bought; when every offer is sold out, the lowest, marked sold out. A price of 0 is taken as none (shops write it for "ask us"). A price written as text is read with its comma or point ("1,299.00", "89,50 €"); its currency is the page's, else its sign's ($, €, £), else the find's own (US dollars at first, the owner's home) | The sizes of one product are usually offers at one price, and the lowest one you can buy is what you would pay. A 0 would show as a false drop. The owner lives in New York (DECISIONS, the first table). |
| Every value from a page is checked as Claude's answers are (`shop.clean_read`: the name at most 200 characters, the shop 120, a picture only by a web address, a price from 0 to 1,000,000, a currency of three letters) and shown as text, never as markup | A page is written by anyone. |
| The address is checked before anything is kept, and every time the page is fetched: only `https://` on the usual port, at most 2,000 characters, no name or password in it; the host a name, never a number, and every address the name stands for on the internet (not this computer, the home or office network, Tailscale's shared range, link-local, multicast, reserved or unspecified, in IPv4 or IPv6, an IPv6 address carrying an IPv4 one judged by that one too). A redirect is checked the same way, at most 3 of them. Refused with a sentence (422) when the link is sent | The address comes from a phone's share sheet or a paste, and Nines runs inside the owner's home network: without these rules a link could make Nines open the router's page or another computer's. Plain http is refused because a page read over it could be changed on the way. The owner hears at once why a link was not taken, and an empty wish is never left. |
| The connection is made to the address that was checked (`productpage._Pinned`), with the shop's certificate checked against its name | A name can be made to point somewhere else between a check and a second lookup; connecting to the checked address closes that gap. |
| GET only, no cookies sent or kept, a generic browser's User-Agent (nothing about the owner or Nines), 5 seconds for any step and 10 for the whole page, at most 2 MB read; the standard library (`http.client`, `html.parser`, `ipaddress`) and `net.ssl_context` for the certificates, no new dependency | A product page needs nothing more, and shops often turn away what does not look like a browser. Nothing has to be installed, and the packaged program trusts the same certificates as for updates. |
| Nothing about a link is logged: a page that breaks the reader logs only the kind of error | As for shopping: nothing about a wish, a product or a link is logged. |
| A link is a find, in the same table and price history, with three new columns (`link`, `read_at`, `read_by`); a wish made from a link is an ordinary wish whose one find is the page | One history, one rule for a drop, one daily round and one page for both kinds of find. Claude's finds are unchanged (0 and null). |
| A wish made from a link is called by the shop's address until its page is read, then named after the product, cut at a word with "…" to 60 characters (`shop.LINK_NAME_MAX`); its find keeps the whole name. A name the owner gave is never changed | The wishlist needs a name at once, and the product's own name is the one the owner knows it by. The wish's name is its page's heading, and shops' product names run long: at 120 characters a name filled six lines of a phone's screen. |
| *(Superseded by "A link judged against the closet, and Google Shopping": a link is described by Claude from its page's words and judged.)* A link is not judged against the closet: it has no verdict and no "Claude saw" line. A page Claude found and the owner then sends keeps its verdict | A page's data does not say the closet's words (the kind, colour, formality); describing every link would cost a call to Claude each. "I found one: would it work?" on the wish does it when the owner wants. |
| Sending the same page again gives back the open wish made from it (200), and it is not read again | A share sheet tapped twice should not make two wishes. |
| Confirm before adding: the share target and the Shortcut open `/wardrobe/link?url=…` with the link in the box, and only **Add to the list** adds it (and opens the new wish's page, which says how the reading goes). The link is taken from `url`, else `text`, else `title`, words around it left out | A slip in a share sheet should not add a wish or fetch a page. A page opened by an address must change nothing by itself. Apps put the link in any of the three, often with words around it. |
| A page of its own, one level down inside the Wardrobe, rather than the box opened on the Wardrobe page | The share sheet needs an address to open. On a phone the wishlist sits below Worth buying, which waits for the analysis; landing there and scrolling to a box would move the page under the owner. |
| A Web Share Target in the manifest (GET, to `/wardrobe/link`, with `url`, `text` and `title`) for Android and Chrome on a computer; for the iPhone, which does not offer share targets to web apps, an iOS Shortcut in the share sheet that opens the same address (README) | The page address is the same for both, so there is one way in to keep. The Shortcut opens Safari rather than the home-screen icon, as an iPhone opens any link. GET keeps it a plain page: no new route, and the doors are unchanged. |
| The daily round reads a link's page whether or not Claude can be reached, at most once a day per page; Claude is asked about the rest as before. A wish whose finds Claude found but could not be asked is tried again at the next look, as before. **Check prices** on a wish with a link is never refused for Claude: its pages are read and the wish says what Claude could not be asked. In the demo closet a link's page is read, its price can be checked again, and Claude is never asked; the daily round never opens the demo closet | Reading a page costs nothing, so a price drop is seen even on a day Claude is out of reach. Once a day per page keeps Nines from fetching a shop's page every hour while it waits for Claude. The demo closet is never sent to Claude, as for "Find some". |
| A drop is shown on the wish's row in the wishlist: the biggest drop among its finds, "Down from $68.00 to $54.40", in that find's currency through the app's price words; the row also shows the newest link's price and shop ("$68.00 at Example Shop") where a hand-added wish says "Added by hand" | What the owner asked for: to be told about drops. The row is where the list is read; the wish's page already showed each find's drop. |
| While a page is read the wish says who is reading ("Nines is reading the page…", or that Claude is, when the page does not show its price); a find sent as a link says "You sent this link", and "Claude read its price from the page" when Claude did | Claude's reading costs something, so the owner can see when it was used. A price that was not read is never shown: a page with none says so. |


## A link judged against the closet, and Google Shopping (23 November, the owner's request)

Step 1 of "Finding clothes you might like" (BACKLOG). The code is `describe_prompt`, `Described` and `describe` in
`backend/closet/tagger/shopping.py`, `_describe_link`, `clean_described`, `page_words`, `not_judged` and `mark_unclear`
in `backend/closet/shop.py`, the page's words in `productpage.Product` (`description`, `brand`, `color`, `material`),
`notJudgedWords`, `shoppingWords` and `googleShoppingLink` in `frontend/src/shop.ts`, the find card in `Shop.tsx` and
the gap card in `Missing.tsx`. It changes one row of "Wishlist links and their prices" and builds on "Shopping for a
wish".

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Once a link's page has given a name, Claude is asked once to describe the product in the same listed values a search's product uses (`Described`, checked by `wouldwork.parse_piece` as a find's are), and the piece is judged with `shop.judge`, exactly as a search's finds, then again at every price check | The owner wants a page found elsewhere to answer as Would this work? does. One description per page keeps the cost to one short call; the verdict is worked out again from the closet as it is, as for every find. |
| Claude is sent only what the shop's page says, as text: its name, the shop, and the brand, colour, material and description where the page gives them (the description cut to 2,000 characters). Nothing from the wish (its name, note or price) or the closet; no picture; no web search. Through the same two routes and limit (`correcting.ask`, the plan through `claude -p` then the API key), recorded with its own purpose, `shop_describe`, and refused before asking through the API key when it could take the month past the limit | Enough to tell a shirt from a coat and its colour; the closet is what Nines judges it against, on this computer. Sending the picture would make it better at colour and pattern but cost more each time: text first, to see how good it is (BACKLOG, Left over). Someone could have had Claude read the page with web fetch: slower, and it costs a search's estimate. |
| Not asked in the demo closet, for a page that gave no name, or when Claude cannot be asked (out of reach, signed out, the limit). The link then waits, its card says so in one sentence, and it is described at the next reading of its page with Claude reachable: **Check now**, or the daily round (which reads a link's page at most once a day) | The demo closet never sends anything to Claude. A page with no name gives Claude nothing to go on. Waiting costs nothing, and the price is read whatever Claude can do. |
| An answer that cannot be used (Claude says it is not one piece of menswear, gives values outside the closet's words, or no usable answer) is remembered as a fingerprint of the words asked about, kept in the find's verdict column (`{"unclear": …}`) while it has no piece, and the same words are not asked about again; a page whose words changed is asked about once more. Out of reach is not an answer: asked again later | No migration: the verdict column has no other use for a find with no piece, and a piece and a fingerprint never coexist. Someone could have added a column for it. Not remembering would ask every day about a gift card that will never be a piece. |
| On the wish's page the link uses the same find card as a search's finds: verdict, reasons, "Claude saw", the shop's picture, and "You sent this link" kept. While Claude describes it, the wish says "Claude is describing it…", the words an item's progress uses for the same step | One card, one way to read a verdict. |
| **Search Google Shopping** on each thing worth buying and on each wish: an ordinary link opened in the owner's browser (new tab, `noopener noreferrer`, as Open in shop), to `https://www.google.com/search?tbm=shop&q=…`. The words are "men's", the first colour to look for and the kind of piece in plain words (`kindWords`, or the gap's example kind), or, for a wish with nothing to look for, its own name. No price, size, brand, note or anything else about the closet is in the address | A shop search browses better than Claude's web search can (BACKLOG, "Finding clothes you might like"); Nines adds what to look for, and nothing else leaves through the address. A price cap was left out on purpose: Google's own filters do that better, and it says something about the owner. Size and brands join the words only once step 3 keeps them, and this row changes with it. |

## You might like (23 November; BACKLOG "Finding clothes you might like", step 4)

A small card in the Wardrobe, not a feed: three things a week chosen from the owner's taste rather than from a gap. The
asking is `picks_prompt` and `picks` in `backend/closet/tagger/shopping.py`; what a look is told, how three are chosen,
the weekly look, Save, Not for me and the line offered are `backend/closet/picks.py` (migration 0048); the card is
`frontend/src/screens/YouMightLike.tsx` with its words in `frontend/src/picks.ts`. Builds on "Shopping for a wish" and
"What the search is told about you", which still hold.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A look sends Claude, with no wish: the block "About the person this is for" for the closet as a whole (`aboutyou.for_picks`, the same block a wish added by hand gets, its lead line saying to look for what suits it rather than ranking by it), what the closet already has plenty of (three or more active pieces of one kind, colour and pattern, at most eight lines, from the items' own words) and the products turned down (the newest twelve: colour, pattern, kind and the shop's name for it). Its own system line; the same answer shape as a search's, checked by the same cleaning (`shop.clean_find`). Through the same two routes and limit, with web search (5 searches), recorded as `shop_picks` | Taste is what the closet knows that a shop does not; plenty and Not for me keep it from offering a fourth navy crewneck or what was refused. Nothing the owner typed (names, notes) is sent, as for a search. |
| Claude is asked for up to eight; each is judged with `shop.judge` exactly as a find is, and the best three are kept (`choose`): never one the closet calls hard to wear or "you have it already" (any owned piece doing the same job), never an address shown before (a pick's address is kept whatever became of it), the ones that fill a gap first, then those that add something, in Claude's order, one of each kind of piece first. Fewer than three, or none, is an answer: the card says Claude found nothing new | "You have it already" is strict (one piece of the same kind, level and colour group is enough), so in a full closet many products go; better three good ones, or none, than a repeat. Someone could have kept repeats and marked them. Never made up: what is shown came back from the web search and passed the same checks as a find. |
| Not searched with too little to go on: taste with no evidence by its own thresholds (`taste.learn`) and Your style with no line of the owner's own (the starting lines do not count). The card says so in one sentence and offers nothing to tap | A look from nothing would be a generic shop search, which a shop does better, and it costs money. |
| A look runs when the owner taps Look now, on More like this, and by itself once a week on the daily price round (`Picker.tick`, called by `shop.PriceScheduler` after the prices): only the owner's own closet, never the demo, only when a route can be used and the estimate fits the monthly limit, and when no look (Look now or weekly) was made in the last 7 days. A weekly look that cannot run (no route, the limit, nothing to go on) changes nothing on the card and is tried at the next hourly look; one whose answer cannot be used counts as the week's | The same round and the same promises as the price checks, so nothing new runs in the background. Counting an unusable answer keeps a broken answer from being asked for every hour. |
| **The weekly switch** ("Look for me each week", behind the card's one fold, "How Nines looks"): off until the owner has tapped Look now once, then on by default; set by hand, it stays as set | Nothing is ever searched, and nothing spent, before the owner has asked once; after that, three a week is what was asked for. The switch is shown only once there is a first look to repeat. |
| More like this: a look seeded with the pick's described values (kind, colour, pattern, formality, material) beside the same block, its picks joining the card rather than replacing anything. The card shows three at a time, newest first; older ones behind "See earlier ones" (at most 30 kept on it) | The owner asked for something like it; the three already there may still be wanted. |
| Save makes a wish from the pick in one go: named after the product (cut to 60 characters, as a link's wish), its `look_for` the pick's described values, with the product as its first find, its verdict and price as found, so the daily round watches its price like any find's. The pick stays on the card as "On your wishlist" | A wish with what to look for can also be searched (Find more) and judged like a gap's; the price watching is what makes saving worth it. |
| Not for me hides the pick for good (Undo brings it back, in its place), and the next look is told about it | DESIGN principle 9; and a refusal is the plainest evidence of taste there is. |
| After three or more turned down in bright colours (red, pink, orange, yellow, purple), in one pattern, or of one kind, one line is offered for Your style ("Nothing bright.", "Nothing striped.", "No hoodies."), kept only on **Save to Your style**, through `stylejobs.keep` and `unkeep` (Undo), as a line offered in a conversation is (its source is "talk", "From a conversation" in Settings). Rules, not Claude; a line is offered once, never while another waits, never when Your style already says it; an Undo of Not for me that breaks the rule takes back a line not yet answered | Small and plain, through the one mechanism that keeps a line on a tap. Someone could have asked Claude to word the line, or marked it with a source of its own; neither seemed worth a second path. |
| The card: a heading, at most one line under it, up to three rows (the shop's picture in a kept 64×80 space with a hanger when there is none, the name cut to about two lines and opening the shop's page in the browser, the shop and price, one line from the verdict, Save and More like this), and Not for me as a × with its name for screen readers. Its states are each one sentence: never looked (with Look now and the API key's cost), looking (the words a search uses, "Claude is searching shops"), nothing new, too little to go on, Claude out of reach or the limit (the search's own sentences), the demo. Offline it shows what it last showed, kept on the device, with its actions off. Placed after Worth buying's first suggestions and before Thinking of buying something? | Reading load: three picks with long names (and a long shop name) add 61 words to the Wardrobe at 393px (510 to 571 in the phone check). Not for me as words would add three more on every row; the × is the usual mark for dismissing a suggestion, and Undo says what it did. After Worth buying because it is the same job (what to buy), led by what is missing. |
| *(Used by "On sale now": `kind` `sale`, `price_before` kept for a sale.)* A pick has a `kind` (`taste`) and a place for a price before a markdown (`price_before`), unused | Step 5, "On sale now", can be a second kind of pick on the same card. |
| Not measured: what a real look costs and how long it takes through the plan and the API key. The estimate shown is the search's bound (`spend.estimate_shop` with 5 searches) | Only the stand-ins were run; see BACKLOG, Left over. |
## On sale now (23 November; BACKLOG "Finding clothes you might like", step 5)

The owner: "I also would love to discover sale items across the internet to find gems that I otherwise might never
see." A second kind of pick (`kind = 'sale'`) in the machinery of "You might like", which still holds: the asking is
`sale_prompt`, `SaleFound` and `sale` in `backend/closet/tagger/shopping.py`; where it starts, the page check, the
weekly look and the daily re-read are `sale_asked`, `rated_brands`, `before_lines`, `check_sale`, `Picker.check_pages`,
`due` and `Picker.recheck` in `backend/closet/picks.py`; the price before a markdown and the sizes are read in
`backend/closet/productpage.py`; Shops you like are `backend/closet/shops.py` (migration `0050_shops.sql`) and
`frontend/src/components/ShopsYouLike.tsx`; the card's second view is in `YouMightLike.tsx` with its words in
`picks.ts`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A second question beside `picks_prompt`, sharing its words where they are the same (the block about the person, More like this, plenty, Not for me, the rules on real pages and made-up products), with its own system line, recorded under its own purpose, `shop_sale`, through the same routes, web search (5 searches) and limit | One machinery, and the spending record shows what sales cost apart from the rest. |
| Where it starts, in this order, each bounded: (1) the shops the owner names (up to 12); (2) up to 6 brands on active pieces they love, like, or wore on 3 days or more, ordered as the search block orders brands; (3) up to 8 shops they found things at before (every find of a wish and every saved pick, newest first, once by site, as "Example Shop (shop.example)", not one they named); (4) Claude's own searches in the kinds their taste favours, "including smaller makers and independent shops they are unlikely to know", with "at least a third of your answer" from outside (1) to (3) when it can find them (said only when there is a (1) to (3)) | Web search cannot read every shop's sale, so it needs somewhere to start; but the owner asked for gems they would not see, so the prompt says plainly that finding the unknown is the point and keeps room for it. Brands only rated or worn well, since a brand owned once and never worn is no lead. |
| **What is sent to Claude for a sale look**: everything a look for things you might like sends (Your style, what taste favours, loved and most-worn pieces in the closet's words, the brands on your items, your sizes, plenty and Not for me), and also the names you put in Shops you like and the shop names and sites you found things at before. Never a photo, an item's name or a note. The card's fold says it in one sentence, `LOOK_SENDS` in `frontend/src/shop.ts`, the one place it is worded ("A look sends Claude your style, … never a photo or a note. A look for things on sale also sends the shops you like and the shops you found things at before."); the Shops you like sheet says the names are told to Claude | The owner should know what leaves the computer, where they tap. A shop's name says little about a person, but it is theirs. |
| **A sale is checked before it is shown**: each product's page is read by Nines itself through `Shopper.read_page`, the very reader and checks a link the owner sends goes through (https only, no private or local address, redirects checked, 10 seconds and 2 MB a page; no second fetching path), at most 8 pages and 60 seconds a look. Kept only when the page can be read, is not sold out, gives a price (the price shown is the page's, never Claude's), and the price before is above it | Claude's web search can be days old and is not always right; a sale that ended is worse than none. Bounding the reads keeps a look from hanging on slow shops; what is not read in time is dropped, not shown unchecked. |
| The price before is the page's own when its data gives it: a `priceSpecification` whose `priceType` is `ListPrice`, `StrikethroughPrice`, `SRP` or `MSRP` on the offer that sets the price, else OpenGraph's `product:original_price:amount`, else a `product:sale_price:amount` below `product:price:amount`. Only when the page gives none is Claude's used, in the page's currency, and the row says "Claude read the earlier price from the page.", as a find whose price Claude read is marked. A "was" price nobody read is never shown | These are what shops publish for search engines' strike-through prices. A price only Claude saw may be right, but the owner should know who read it. |
| **A markdown under 15% is not a sale worth a pick** (`picks.SALE_MIN`), measured against the price before shown | A few percent off is a rounding of the list price, not a find; 15% is roughly where a markdown starts to change what you would pay. Someone could have chosen 20% or 10%. |
| "Your size is in stock." only when said: when the page lists its sizes as a product group's variants, Nines compares your size for that kind (your size in its brand first, when you saved one) and a page listing yours sold out drops the product; otherwise Claude's word on the page, a false one dropping it too. Said nowhere else | The page's own data first, as for prices; otherwise Claude reads the page anyway. |
| Judged against the closet and chosen exactly as a taste pick (`shop.judge`, `choose`), the best three; never an address shown before in either view | The same promises; a product seen in For you is not news in On sale. |
| The weekly sale look runs on the same daily round, under the same switch ("Look for me each week"), only once the owner has tapped Look now on On sale once, inside the limit, never in the demo closet; no weekly look of one kind within a day of a look of the other (the taste look first when both are due) | One switch for one card. A week's two looks on different days spread what they cost. A sale look is never made, and nothing spent on it, before the owner has asked for one: the switch was turned on by a taste look. |
| A shown sale's page is read again once a day on the round, by Nines alone (no Claude, at most 12 a round): a sale whose page no longer shows a price before it gave, that is under 15% off, sold out (or sold out in your size), or whose page could not be read on two days in a row leaves the card quietly (`ended_at`); a price that changed is kept. A saved one is left to its wish, whose price is watched like any find's | Reading a page costs nothing. Two misses rather than one, so a shop down for an hour does not take a good find away. |
| Saving a sale keeps its price before as the first point of the find's history (at the time it was found), then the price now, and marks the wish's news seen | So the wish page shows the drop in its usual words; a drop you saved on purpose is not news to you. |
| The card does not grow: one card, the app's segmented switch (as Pieces / As worn on Today), **For you** and **On sale**, remembered on the device; each view its three, "See earlier ones", its own states (never looked, looking, nothing new, too little to go on, Claude out of reach or the limit, the demo, offline with what it last showed). A sale row is a taste row with one more line for its prices, in the wishlist's words (`dropWords`: "Was $120.00, now $84.00 (30% less)"), and one for the size or who read the earlier price when there is one | The Wardrobe is over its reading budget; a second card would add a heading, a sentence and three more rows on every visit. The wishlist already has words for a price that came down. |
| Shops you like: a row in Settings → Everyday with a sheet (Add, Remove beside each, saved as you go), up to 12, each a name or site of at most 60 characters, cleaned (one line, a pasted link kept as its site without "www.", never twice in any letter case), kept in the closet's database (`shops`). The empty On sale view offers it once, as a link to `/settings?open=shops`, which opens the sheet | Where the owner's other facts about themselves live (Your sizes). One home: the card links to it rather than editing it in place. |
| Not measured: what a real sale look costs and how long it takes through the plan and the API key, and how many of Claude's products survive the page check on real shops | Only the stand-ins were run (BACKLOG, Left over). |

## See it with your clothes (23 November, the owner's request)

Step 2 of "Finding clothes you might like" (BACKLOG). The code is `wouldwork.outfits_with` and `most_shown_first`,
`Shopper.outfits` and `GET /api/wishlist/{id}/finds/{find_id}/outfits` in `backend/closet/shop.py`,
`illustrations.new_piece` and `figure_request` (the figure's route taking `piece`), `kinds.from_words`, and on the page
`components/WornWith.tsx` (`WornOutfit`, `SeeItWorn`), `SeeWorn` in `screens/Shop.tsx`, the example outfits in
`screens/WouldWork.tsx`, and `canSeeWorn`, `drawnFromWords` in `shop.ts` and `wouldwork.ts`. It builds on "A link
judged against the closet" and "The outfit as worn".

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The outfits shown are the example outfits the "Would this work?" check makes for the piece (`goes_with.examples`, at most three, one per kind of day), worked out again when the sheet opens (`wouldwork.outfits_with`: the engine built around the piece on every kind of day, without the rest of the check, about half a second on the demo closet) and not kept. Judging a find only counts them (`outfits` in the verdict, no migration: the verdict is JSON already) so the card knows whether to offer the button | The same outfits the verdict rests on, so the drawing and the reasons agree. Keeping them would go stale as the closet changes; counting them costs nothing at judging time. A find judged before the count offers the button unless the closet was too small to say, and the sheet says when none can be made now |
| In the sheet, the outfits where the find shows most come first (fewest layers worn over it), then in the check's order; Would this work? keeps the check's order | A shirt under a sweater and a parka shows only its collar; the first outfit is the one most looked at |
| **Changed 23 November** (next row, for the pattern): The find is drawn from its described values as a new item with them would be (`illustrations.new_piece`): its category, its colour word's colour, its shape fields, solid. When the product's name says one of its category's kinds in so many words (`kinds.from_words`: "chelsea boots", "trench coat", "chinos"; the longest such name, and only a kind of the same category) it is drawn as that kind, as the kind picker draws one | A shop's description gives a few words, not a photo: no measured pattern, no lifted print. The kind in the name is the one detail that changes the shape most (a trench's belt, a chelsea boot), costs nothing and is never a guess beyond the shop's own words. Someone could have asked Claude for a full description: a call for every find, for a picture that is still drawn from words |
| A find, or a piece typed into Would this work?, whose pattern word is stripe or plaid is drawn with a plain, typical pattern of that word (`drawing/wordpattern.py`, from `illustrations.new_piece`): stripes across a knit (a tee, polo, sweater or hoodie) and down anything woven (a shirt's stripe; a pinstripe on a jacket, coat or trousers) unless its name says which way ("Breton", "rugby", "hoops" across; "pinstripe", "bengal", "chalk", "vertical" down); a plaid as a wide ground, a darker band and a thin line, a quiet windowpane on a blazer, sport coat or suit jacket, a gingham when its name says gingham.
