The ground is its colour word's colour, as drawn plain; the other colour is a quiet neighbour, the ground's own hue made 0.36 lighter (HLS lightness) on a ground darker than 0.62 and 0.30 darker on a lighter one; the plaid's darker band is the ground 0.12 darker (lighter on a very dark ground). It is woven as one repeat's tile and sized as a pattern Claude described for one of the owner's pieces is (`patterns.surface`): a shirt's stripe 13 repeats across the chest, a knit's hoops 6.5, a pinstripe 8, a plaid or windowpane 4, a gingham 13, across the waist on trousers and shorts. Florals, camouflage, prints and "other" are still drawn in the piece's colour (`COVERAGE["described_patterns"]`). The sheet's line then says the drawing shows "roughly its shape, colour and pattern" | The owner: "A striped shirt in 'See it with your clothes' is striped." A plain shirt where the shop sells a striped one misleads more than a typical stripe does. The description gives a pattern word and nothing more: no direction, no second colour, no scale. Asking Claude for those would be one more value in an answer it already gives, but the direction is what a knit or a shirt usually has, and the colour and scale would still be guesses at a product nobody has measured; so nothing more is asked and nothing sent changes. The neighbour colour keeps the pattern quiet, so it reads as "striped" and not as a claim about the product's colours. Woven and sized as the owner's own described patterns are, a find beside his striped shirt is the same kind of drawing at the same scale, flat and on the figure; the weaving is done again with numpy because that path uses OpenCV and the drawing never imports it to draw from words. The owner's own pieces keep their measured or described pattern exactly (a test holds their drawings to the demo closet's, byte for byte) |
| It is drawn by the figure's own route (`POST /api/outfits/figure`), which takes the piece as `piece` and wears it wherever a place names `~candidate`, the id the check gives it; nothing else is new in the drawing | The piece takes its place in depth order among the owner's layers as any garment does (an outer layer over the tops, shoes on the feet, a bag on its strap), because it is one more piece to the same code. Every kind the check accepts (tops, bottoms, shoes, a bag, a belt, a hat, a scarf) can be worn on the figure, so nothing needs leaving out; a piece that cannot be drawn from its words is drawn as the plainest of its category, as for an item |
| The sheet says once, in one line above the figure, that the piece is drawn from the shop's description and shows roughly its shape and colour, not how it really looks; and shows the shop's own picture beside the drawing when there is one. Would this work? says the same line under its switch, naming "your description" or "Claude's description of your photo" | DESIGN principle 7: the drawing is a guess and is marked as one, once. The shop's picture is what the owner compares the drawing with |
| A `Sheet` from the bottom on a phone, a popover under the button on a computer; one outfit at a time with arrows (44px) and a swipe, "Outfit 1 of 3, for a cool, rainy casual day" above the figure; the figure's height and that line are kept while the outfits are worked out. On Would this work? the example outfits use the Pieces / As worn switch of Today and Trips (the same remembered choice), each drawn in place at a trip day's size | The page holds still (principle 3). A carousel that scrolls would have to keep every figure's height; stepping keeps one. Would this work?'s outfits are already below its first screen, so drawing them there adds nothing to it |
| Nothing is asked of Claude, so it works in the demo closet and needs only Nines to be reachable; when it is not, the sheet says so in one line, as the figure does elsewhere | It is drawing by rule on the computer |

## Today's first screen, and a piece's choices in a sheet (23 November, the review of the screens)

The review of 23 November (Today, Worn and Trips) and BACKLOG "Today's first screen". The code is `Today.tsx` and
`Today.css` (the first screen, the sheets, the outfits kept while Claude chooses), `components/ui/Sheet.tsx` (`anchor`),
`Worn.tsx` (`OutfitMenu`, `RecordByHand`), `Trips.tsx` (`PlanView`), and `temps`, `TodayChanges` in `today.ts`,
`reasonsFor`, `recordGroups` in `worn.ts`, `tripProblems` in `trips.ts`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The first screen is made to fit the phone check's smallest phone (an iPhone 13 mini with Safari's bars, 375x629) with five pieces, with and without the weather line, by four piece tiles to a row in a narrow card (76px, names on two lines in the small size), the date beside "Today" instead of under it, the weather failure as a line of text with its two links in it, and a few spacings made smaller. Nothing is hidden: every piece, the occasion and mood row and the day line stay | Each of these is a few pixels the owner does not miss; together they are the 100px the button was under the tab bar. Five tiles to a row (59px) was tried on paper: one row, but names cut to a word. Hiding the occasion row behind a tap would have saved more, but it is what the owner changes most after the outfit. A browser test (10-today) holds it at that size, in the demo closet whose ribbon takes 32px more |
| The weather failure is one short line, "No forecast.", with Try again and Type temperatures as links inside it, each with a 44px hit area that does not push the lines apart, all in body weight. The service's own explanation is no longer shown | The day line already says "a mild day"; the box said the same thing twice and took 176px. It was "The weather could not be fetched.", which took two lines at 375px; with both actions kept at their names, a full sentence does not fit one line there in CI's wider system font (DejaVu Sans), so the line says only what is missing, and the weather line above says what Nines dressed you for |
| On the weather line, the summary takes the room there is and wraps inside it, and Change stays at the end of its first line | Change on a line of its own cost the first screen a line in a wide font, and the button under the outfit went under the tab bar in CI |
| A piece's choices (Today, Worn, a packed piece on a trip) open in a `Sheet`: from the bottom on a phone, a popover beside the piece on a computer. `Sheet` takes an `anchor`, the button that opened it: the sheet is then put at the end of the page (a portal) and floats beside the anchor, below it or above it where it fits. Worn's ⋯ was a menu with arrow keys; it is now a sheet of buttons like the other two | Principle 3. Today's pieces are inside a carousel slide, which clips what it holds and (being a size container) holds anything fixed inside it, so a sheet drawn there could not float; the anchor is the smallest change to the shared piece that serves all three. One kind of overlay for the same kind of choice, rather than a menu on one screen and sheets on the others |
| Change and Type temperatures open the same sheet, titled "Where you are" as in Settings. It is in the page only when there is no day to dress for until a place is chosen (the 422 answer), because then it is the screen's content | Principle 3, and one name for one thing |
| While Claude chooses again, the outfits that were on the page stay there, dimmed and out of reach, with the waiting card over their top; on the first visit of a day, the rules' outfits stand in. "Show Nines' own picks meanwhile" shows the rules' outfits and moves the card under them. The space of Claude's line and the conversation under the outfits is kept while it is gone | The page lost about 1,050px and left the owner a screen and a half below the outfit they had just changed. The old outfits rather than the rules' new ones, because they are what was there: the rules' are often much taller (nine reasons) than Claude's (two), and showing them moved Recent outfits 460px |
| The outfit carousel is as tall as the outfit in front, not the tallest of the three | The blank band (up to 340px). Swiping to a taller or shorter outfit changes the height below it, which is a move the owner made |
| "Not this piece" and the piece built around are kept on this device for the rest of the day (`closet-today-saved:changes:<closet>`), like typed temperatures | A reload, or iOS closing the app overnight, lost them |
| Building around a piece is called that everywhere: "Build around this piece", "Stop building around it", "Built around …". Wearing the outfit ends it | The page said it four ways; "build around" was the one it used most. Once worn, the piece is often in the laundry, and nothing can be built around it |
| Temperatures are written one way on Today, Worn and a trip, by `temps` in `today.ts`: "9–16°C", "-3 to 4°C" when the low is below zero, "About 22°C" when the two round to the same degree. Claude's and the rules' own sentences ("Two layers for 14°C to 20°C") are the server's and are not changed here | The compact form fits the trip's day boxes and Today's line; a dash after a minus would read as another minus |
| After "Loved it" the reasons offered are Got compliments and Other; after "Not again" all but Got compliments; after "Fine" all of them. A reason that does not fit a new rating is dropped when the rating changes | "Too cold" after "Loved it" contradicts it; "fine" can be either |
| Record an outfit is a sheet (a popover under its button on a computer) with the day, a search, and the pieces in four groups (tops with knitwear and coats, bottoms, shoes, extras), most recently worn first, eight to a group until "See all" or a search | A second short screen was the other way; a sheet keeps Worn where it was. Recently worn first, because an outfit recorded by hand is usually from the last few days |
| A trip says what its days have to say once: the weather that could not be had is the trip's own sentence, not repeated for each day; days with no outfit and days with something to watch for share a line when their sentence is the same; the watch-outs are one line that opens, above What to pack | The trip's first screen was a 310px notice; a failed forecast was said eleven times. Typing a trip's temperatures by hand, as Today allows, was not added (BACKLOG, left over) |

## Swim trunks (22 November, the owner's request)

The owner: "you need to add swim trunks as a category. mostly used for trips". The kind is in
`docs/menswear-taxonomy.json`, `backend/closet/tagger/taxonomy.py` (`SWIM_KINDS`, its drawing in `BOTTOM_KINDS` and
`KIND_BOTTOM_FIBER`) and `backend/closet/kinds.py`; Today leaves it out in `outfits._unavailable`, What's missing in
`gaps._closet`; a trip packs it in `backend/closet/trips.py` (`SWIM_HOT_C`, `SWIM_WEEK`, `_Planner.pack_trunks`),
keeps the choice in `savedtrips.py` (the trip's `swim` column, migration `0043`), and the Trips screen shows it
(`SwimChoice`, with its words in `frontend/src/trips.ts`).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A garment kind, `swim_trunks`, in the owner's taxonomy under its category `bottoms` (so in Trousers & shorts on "What is it?") with `app_category` `shorts`; not a new coarse category. Its entry names the taxonomy's own `swim_fabric` (`performance_fabrics`), its `inseam_in` of 3 to 7 inches, which the bottoms block already gave swim trunks, and an elastic drawcord or drawstring waistband | The coarse category is what the outfit engine, What's missing and the Closet's filter read, and swim trunks are shorts as clothing; a new category would change every screen and rule that reads categories, for one kind. The owner's file already put swim under shorts ("Chino/denim/cargo/swim/linen", `shorts_inseam_in.swim_trunks`), so the kind sits there rather than under activewear, which would have made them exercise clothes in How you live. Board shorts and other swimwear were not added, as the owner asked for swim trunks; the picker's search finds swim trunks for "board shorts", "swimming shorts" and "swimsuit" |
| Claude learns the kind from the schema's `garment` field (its values, and its description of swim trunks) and the describing instructions, which file them as shorts, formality casual. `SCHEMA_VERSION` 8, its fingerprint recorded, the demo's stored answers rewritten (only their version changed) | The garment values are part of the form Claude answers in. Casual rather than athletic: they are not sportswear for exercise, and no rule puts them in an outfit anyway |
| Never in an everyday outfit: `outfits._unavailable` says "for swimming", so the rules engine, the stylist's list and the trip planner's days all leave them out, as they leave out what is worn at home. An outfit built around them is still made | One rule, in the one place every outfit reads. Building around a piece is the owner asking for it by name, as for loungewear (DECISIONS, "The owner's outfit rules"), and a beach outfit around swim trunks is a fair answer to that. Could have been: refuse even then |
| What's missing leaves them out of the closet it judges (`gaps._closet`), so the plain analysis, How you live and Would this work? never count them as shorts, compare wears with them or call them unused | Otherwise a closet whose only shorts are swim trunks would not be told it has no shorts, and the trunks would show as "in almost no outfit", since the engine never uses them |
| A trip's choice **Swimming or the beach**: `true` or `false` is the owner's word, kept through every plan made again; null, the default, follows the weather: on when a day's high reaches 27°C (`trips.SWIM_HOT_C`) | 27°C is where the owner's hottest band starts (`outfit.temperature_bands_c`: "base only; shorts or light trousers; sneakers, loafers, sandals"), so a trip's hot days are the same days the rest of the app calls hot, above the 24°C at which a day is "hot or humid" for re-wearing. One such day is enough: a pair of swim trunks is small, and forgetting them on a hot trip costs more than carrying them on a cool one. Every day whose weather is known counts, the usual weather of far-off dates as well as the forecast, so a trip planned a month ahead to somewhere hot turns it on too; a day whose weather is not known does not |
| Kept as the trip's `swim` column (migration `0043`): 1 on, 0 off, null to follow the weather | The trip's other choices are columns of the trips table, and null keeps "not chosen" apart from the owner's off. Trips saved before it follow the weather the next time they are planned; their stored plans are not changed. The number is the one the orchestrating session gave (`0042` is taken by a pull request still to merge) |
| One pair for a trip of up to seven days, two for a longer one (`SWIM_WEEK`), never more than can be packed (not archived, in the laundry, being mended, left out or disliked, as for every piece); pinned pairs first, then the best rated; without swimming only the pinned are taken. Laundry on the trip does not change it | The owner's numbers. Swim trunks dry quickly, so a second pair is for a longer stay, not for the wash. A pin is the owner's word, as for every piece |
| Packed apart from the days' outfits, under **For swimming** (`group: "swim"`, worn on no day), with a count of their own in "Why these counts" ("1 pair of swim trunks: one for a trip of up to a week"). When it is on and none can be packed, the plan says so in that place: "You have no swim trunks in your closet.", or "None of your swim trunks can be packed: 1 in the laundry." | They are extras for swimming, never a day's bottom. The sentence stands where the trunks would be, and nothing is made up in their place |
| On the screen, a switch in the new trip's form and under "Change the trip", whose line says who decided ("It is on because 3 days reach 27°C.", "You turned it off. It stays off whatever the forecast.") | On and off look the same on a switch whoever chose them; one sentence tells them apart. *(Changed 23 November: once the owner has turned it on or off, a link "Follow the forecast again" under the switch sends `swim: null`.)* The screen offers no way back to "follow the weather" once the owner has turned it (the API takes null): turning it again is the owner's word, which is what they asked to be kept. In BACKLOG, "Left over" |
| Drawn with the shorts template: a short inseam (5 inches, the middle of the taxonomy's 3 to 7, unless one was told), a gathered drawstring waist with its cords, on-seam side pockets and a plain hem, in the swim fabric drawn as nylon: light, a slight sheen, no texture (`BOTTOM_KINDS`, `KIND_BOTTOM_FIBER`). A told inseam or cloth wins, as for the everyday trouser kinds. `COVERAGE` lists the kind as drawn; the mesh liner is inside and out of view | The template's parameters cover it. On the figure the trunks sit under the top and behind the hanging arms, and the legs come out of their openings. No drawing that existed changes, so `DRAWING_VERSION` stays and the `UNCHANGED` digests are as they were |

## Check now says what it found (22 November, the owner's report)

The owner, on 0.12.0: "when i click check now it doesn't seem to detect that it needs to upgrade. even though when i
click the releases link, it directly links me to 0.15.0". The check had found it: the Settings line said "Nines 0.15.0
is out." with Install now, but Advanced, where Check now is, only changed "Last checked" and added the release link.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Under Check now, Advanced says what the last check found, in the words of the Settings line ("Nines 0.15.0 is out.", "Nines 0.15.0 is ready to install.", "You have the latest version."), with a problem fetching it and **Install now** when this computer can install it. This changes the 22 November layout above ("the main line carries Install"): Install is now also beside Check now | The answer to a button belongs beside the button. Under it rather than above it, so the answer comes in without moving the button. |
| When Check now finds a newer release this computer can install, the background loop looks again at once (`Updater.check_now`) instead of at its next half-hourly look: in "Ask me first" it downloads and checks the release and the banner offers it; in "Install updates by themselves" it installs it, as that look would have | Otherwise the banner came up to half an hour after the owner had asked. "Never check" still only reports. |
| Checking by itself stays once a day | Recorded above. With several releases a day, a release can wait up to a day to be noticed by itself; Check now is the way to ask sooner. Left as it is, for the owner to decide. |

## Choosing a shade (22 November, the owner's request)

The owner: "i feel like i don't have that many color choices". The item page's Colour row offered the 18 colour words
only, and a shade (one of the taxonomy's named shades, `colors.shades`) could be named by Claude alone. The row's
choices are `ColourChoices` in `frontend/src/screens/ItemDetail.tsx`, with what each choice saves in
`frontend/src/shades.ts`; the server keeps the shade in `backend/closet/items.py` (`_shade`, `_with_shade`,
`shade_of`, `shades`).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| The Colour row offers, under the colour words, the shades of the item's colour, one colour's shades at a time, each with its own swatch and a heading that names the colour ("Shades of green") | Over fifty chips at once would fill several phone screens. The colour word is what outfits, What's missing and Would this work? reason with, so it is chosen first and the shade narrows it; both are one fact, chosen in one row (DESIGN, "One thing, one home"). |
| Choosing a colour that has shades keeps the sheet open on its shades; choosing a shade, a colour without shades (multicolour) or no colour closes it on a phone, as a choice did before. Each choice saves at once | A shade can be chosen only once its colour is showing. A colour chosen alone saves what it always did (the word, drawn in its swatch). |
| A chosen shade is kept with the owner's details (`own_details.shade`), and the same save makes `color` the shade's colour word and `color_hex` the shade's colour, both the owner's (`source` `user`). No migration | The owner's details are already laid over Claude's answer and kept when the item is described again, and `source` already keeps the tagger and the measured palette (`palette.settle`) off a colour the owner set. The drawing already follows a `color_hex` the owner set, so it is drawn in the shade. The history records one change (the colour, its colour and the shade) and undoes it as one. |
| The shade decides: one sent with another colour word sets its own. A colour word or colour set without a shade puts aside the owner's shade unless it is still that shade's word and colour; `null` gives the shade back to Claude and leaves the colour | The latest word wins, as between a shape and the owner's details. A shade that no longer matches what is drawn must not stay named on the page. |
| The shade shown can't be tapped off; History undoes a choice | When nobody named a shade, the one shown is the nearest to the drawn colour, so tapping it off would bring it straight back. Tapping the one shown makes it the owner's word. |
| Fix the details no longer lists the shade (it left the cloth groups, `details.BY_KIND`), but every item has it as a detail (`details.EVERY_ITEM`): a sentence ("it's sage") can still name it, and a shade sent to `PUT /api/items/{id}/details` or confirmed from a sentence goes through the same rule (`corrections._split` hands it to `items.update`) | One place to choose it, beside the colour it narrows. Fix the details offered all 50 shades in words, and one of another colour than the item's changed nothing the page showed. Every item, not only clothes: a belt or a bag is cognac or tobacco too, and every item has the Colour row; a change of kind keeps it. |
| The page reads the shades from `GET /api/vocab` (`shades`, from `taxonomy.SHADES`), not from a list of its own | The page and the server agree on the list, and a shade added to the taxonomy shows on the page with no change there. A vocabulary saved before (offline) has none, and the row then offers the colour words only. |
| The item's shade on the page is the owner's when it is a shade of the item's colour, else Claude's, else the nearest to its colour, as before; the owner's is read from their details directly | An item with nothing to draw (a bag Claude has not described) has no details as drawn, and would not show the shade its owner chose. |
| A shade's plain name is its name with spaces, capitalised; Off-white, Mid-wash denim and Light-wash denim are hyphenated, on the page (`labels.ts`) and in the history (`details.py`) | "Off white" reads as a misspelling. |
| The phone check pictures an item's Colour sheet (`item-colour`) | So the sheet is looked at whenever something visible changes. |
| The review's question about a doubtful colour, and Would this work?'s colour, still offer the colour words only | The owner asked about their own pieces' colours. Left over in BACKLOG. |
| Five shades join the taxonomy (`colors.shades`, `taxonomy.SHADES` and the extended reference's table): Mint (green, `#bcdfc6`; summer shirts, polos, knits), French blue (blue, `#4277c9`; dress shirts, knits), Taupe (brown, `#8b7c6d`; suede shoes, knits, chinos), Salmon (pink, `#f0a08a`; chinos, shorts, summer shirts) and Mauve (purple, `#ad91a9`; knits, linen) | Each fills a gap a menswear wardrobe plainly has: green had no light shade but the greyish sage; blue had nothing between denim and the bright royal and cobalt for the classic shirt blue; brown had no grey-brown; pink nothing warmer than the pale shirting pink; purple nothing between lavender and plum. Kept to five: pale yellow, light pink, beige and bottle green were already there as butter, shirting pink, sand or stone, and forest green. |
| Their colours were chosen so the nearest shade named for the demo closet's pieces stays the same (light-wash jeans stay mid-wash denim, a mid purple stays plum, a plain pink stays dusty rose). Only a plain blue (the colour word's own colour, or its swatch) is now named French blue rather than royal blue | The shade shown when nobody named one is the nearest by colour; a new shade must not rename what it does not describe. French blue is the closer name for the plain mid blue. |
| Claude can name them too: the schema's `shade` lists every shade, so `SCHEMA_VERSION` is 9, its fingerprint recorded and the demo's stored answers rewritten (only their version changed) | Keeping them out of the form Claude answers in would also keep them out of the form a drawing reads its answer in (`ItemTags`), and an answer with a shade the owner chose outside it would lose its details when drawn. |

## Formality between two levels (22 November, the owner's request)

The owner: "assigning something smart casual vs business is difficult. like a penny loafer could be either". An
item keeps its `formality` and may also have `formality_also` (migration `0044`), checked in `vocab.formality_span`
and `items._formality`; the rules read it through `outfits.span`, `steps_from`, `at_level` and `share_a_level`;
Claude gives it in the tagging schema (`schema.FormalityAlso`, `SCHEMA_VERSION` 10); the item page and Would this
work? show it with `components/FormalityChips.tsx` and say it with `labels.formalityLabel`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Kept as `formality`, the level the piece sits at most, and `formality_also`, the level right above *or below* it (null for one level), not as a lower and an upper level | Every reader that needs one level (the closet's make-up in the insights, taste, What's missing's "another like it" and orphan suggestions, the review queue) keeps reading `formality` unchanged and gets the level the owner or Claude picked first. On the page the owner's first choice stays chosen when the second is added, instead of jumping to whichever is lower. The rules use the span between the two, so the order does not matter to them |
| Only levels next to each other, in the vocabulary's order; the same level is one level; anything else is refused with a sentence (and in Claude's answer read as one level) | A piece that is casual or formal is not a piece, it is a mistake. Reading a slip in Claude's answer as one level keeps the rest of the description rather than failing the whole answer |
| In the rules, a piece fits any formality inside its span with no penalty and is as far from one outside as its nearer end; two pieces are as far apart as their closest levels (the dressier piece's lowest less the more casual one's highest); the owner's exceptions (one dressier top, shoes a level down) and the sporty rule are measured the same way. A piece with one level gives exactly the numbers it gave before | The owner's point is that the loafer is either, so it should cost nothing on a smart casual day or a business day, and should go with a tee and jeans as well as with grey wool trousers. Measuring from the nearer end is the smallest change that says that |
| An outfit's reason ("Everything is business, right for a business day.") reads each piece at the level nearest the day; a pair rated too dressy or too casual is judged by the middle of each piece's span | So a pair of loafers worn with business pieces on a business day is said to be business, not "between smart casual and business". For a rating, a piece spanning two levels sits between pieces of either level |
| The stylist's list says "smart casual to business", the lower level first; Would this work? takes and describes a second level, and counts two pieces as doing the same job when they share a level, as does What's missing for "a second one like it". Home and exercise accept a piece that can be casual | The same idea everywhere formality is compared; for one level each, sharing a level is the same level, so nothing changes for single-level pieces |
| What's missing still aims each gap at one level, and the closet's make-up counts a piece under `formality` only | A gap is something to buy for the days that need it, at the level those days ask for; counting a piece twice would make the make-up's numbers add up to more than the closet |
| Claude may give it (`formality_also`, "none" for one level, a word rather than a null: the form sent is near the API's limit of nullable properties); the describing instructions name the pieces that genuinely sit between two (penny loafers, a navy blazer, dark selvedge jeans, a knit polo) and say most sit at one level | A range for everything would make formality mean nothing; examples the owner would recognise keep it to the pieces the owner meant. `SCHEMA_VERSION` 10, the number after the shades' 9 |
| `formality_also` goes with the formality's owner: when the formality is the owner's (changed on the page, or confirmed in the review), a new answer never adds, changes or clears its second level (`tagjobs.who`); a formality chosen alone is one level, so a single level the owner chose stays single | One choice, made once: the owner who picked "smart casual" has said what it is, and a second level from Claude would quietly change that. Items whose formality the owner set before this existed are covered too, without changing their data |
| On the item page, the Formality row's sheet shows the levels, then "It can also be" with the level below and the level above the one chosen, and one line: "Choose a second level when it could be either, like penny loafers." Tapping a level is one level, as it always was; the row reads "Smart casual to business" and may take a second line on a phone | Tapping a neighbouring level among the five to extend a range was the other way, but tapping another level has always meant "this one instead", and the owner and the tests rely on it; a second, named group says what it does. On a phone the sheet closes after either choice, as every row's does, so the common case (the level was right, add the second) is one tap |
## Tennis and golf shoes (22 November, the owner's request)

The owner: "we're also missing specific shoes for specific use cases. like shoes I play tennis or golf in". The kinds
are in `docs/menswear-taxonomy.json`, `backend/closet/tagger/taxonomy.py` (`FOOTWEAR`, `SPORT_SHOES`, `DRESS_LACED`)
and `backend/closet/kinds.py`; Today leaves them out in `outfits._unavailable` (`FOR_SPORT`), What's missing in
`gaps._closet`; a trip packs a pair only when pinned (`trips._Planner`, `sport_packed`); the engine learns a shoe's
kind in `today.cloth`, from `kinds.of_row`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Two footwear kinds, `tennis_shoe` and `golf_shoe`, in the owner's taxonomy after `cleats`, named "Tennis shoes" and "Golf shoes" (plural, as "Flip-flops"). A tennis shoe is a court shoe: a herringbone outsole, a toe drag guard (`rubber_toe_cap`), a cupsole. A golf shoe's sole is `studded_rubber` (spikeless nubs) or `cleats` (spikes), with an open or closed lacing for the saddle and derby looks. Both have `app_style` `low_sneaker`, so their category is `sneakers` and "What is it?" offers them under Shoes & boots | The coarse category is what the Closet's filter and every rule read, and a sports shoe is a sneaker there, as cleats and running shoes are. A golf shoe cut like a derby is still made for the course; under dress shoes its spikes would sit beside the business oxfords. No sole value was added: the footwear block's `cleats` and `studded_rubber` already say spikes and nubs, and a herringbone tread is under the shoe, where the drawing does not look |
| Shoes made for one sport, `taxonomy.SPORT_SHOES` (tennis shoes, golf shoes and cleats, which were sneakers to every rule until now), are never in an outfit Today makes: `outfits._unavailable` says "for tennis", "for golf" or "for sport", so the rules engine, the stylist's list and a trip's days leave them out. Not even on an athletic day. An outfit built around them is still made (`build_around`; Today's screen offers that only for a piece already in one of its outfits: BACKLOG, "Left over") | One rule in the one place every outfit reads, as for swim trunks. Today's athletic occasion is a workout or a run and does not say which sport; court, course and pitch shoes are made for one ground (studs and spikes are not for pavement or a gym floor). Building around the pair is how you dress to play. Could have been: offer tennis shoes on athletic days |
| Running shoes, trail runners, trainers, basketball and court sneakers stay everyday shoes | Many people wear them every day, and nothing in the code treated them as for sport only. The owner asked for shoes for specific uses |
| What's missing leaves sport shoes out of the closet it judges (`gaps._closet`), so they are never counted as sneakers, compared with or called "in almost no outfit"; How you live does not count them for Exercise either | Otherwise a closet whose only sneakers are for tennis would not be told it has no everyday sneakers, and the pair would show as unused because Today never offers it. Exercise follows Today, which does not dress an athletic day with them |
| A shoe now has a kind for the engine: `today.cloth` takes it from `kinds.of_row`, the kind its page shows. `kinds.said_kind` reads Claude's `details.footwear` after a footwear id among the words it saw (until now only the words, from before the schema had a `footwear` field, so Claude's kind of shoe never showed), and `of_row` takes the kind of shoe the owner set in Fix the details as the owner's kind when they have not chosen one under "What is it?" | Without it a tennis shoe Claude recognised would still be offered on Today, and the item page would not say what it is. Every described shoe now shows Claude's kind under "What is it?", a change the owner sees (CHANGELOG, "Changed"). The words still come first because the drawing reads them last, so the page names what is drawn. A stored answer whose fields are not text says nothing, rather than failing |
| *(Changed 23 November: a trip can be told it includes tennis or golf; Trip packing, "Loungewear and sport on a trip".)* A trip gets no new choice. A sport pair is packed only when the owner pins it ("I want to take this"), with the shoes (`group: "shoes"`, `days: 0`), worn on no day, with no "no day's outfit uses it" note; otherwise it is never packed | The owner did not ask for packing these, but "I want to take this" must still take them. Packed pieces are dressed first, so a pinned pair in the pool the days are dressed from would become the shoes of most days (golf shoes at dinner). A "Tennis" or "Golf" choice like "Swimming or the beach" is left to the owner (BACKLOG) |
| Claude learns the kinds from the schema's `footwear` field (its values and its description) and the describing instructions, which say to choose them only for shoes clearly made for the sport, as category sneakers and formality athletic; the sole's description names golf spikes and nubs. `SCHEMA_VERSION` 11 (after the shades' 9 and formality's 10), its fingerprint recorded, the demo's stored answers rewritten (only their version changed) | The footwear values are part of the form Claude answers in. Choosing a sport kind takes the shoe out of everyday outfits, so a sneaker that merely looks sporty stays a sneaker |
| Drawn with the existing low sneaker: a tennis shoe with its toe in rubber in the sole's colour (the drag guard) and a padded collar; a golf shoe on small rubber nubs (`studded`), spikes when its sole is `cleats`, and a derby or oxford the tagger saw is kept, as for a brogue (`DRESS_LACED`). `COVERAGE` lists both as drawn. No drawing code changed, so `DRAWING_VERSION` and the `UNCHANGED` digests stay | The template's parts cover both. Nubs rather than spikes when nothing is said: the low sneaker is the spikeless golf shoe, now the usual one. As for every kind, choosing it by hand draws its usual look (nubs), even over a sole Claude saw; nubs on the dark sole they default to are faint when no sole colour is known |
| Search finds tennis shoes for "court", "padel" and "pickleball", golf shoes for "spikes" and "spikeless", studded boots for "football" and "soccer", and all three for "sport" | The words the owner may type for them. No migration |


## The wish page and price news (23 November, the owner's request)

The owner, on a wish made from a link: "what is the 'from' 'to' bucket for? why would there be a lower limit? how do i
tell it to flag to me when the price drops? what does 'I found one would it work?' even do? very unintuitive screen."
The code is `news`, `came_down`, `at_price` and `mark_seen` in `backend/closet/shop.py` with migration
`0045_wish_news.sql` and the routes `GET /api/wishlist/news` and `POST /api/wishlist/news/seen`; on the page
`WishShop`, `FindCard`, `PriceLine` and `PriceSheet` in `frontend/src/screens/Shop.tsx`, `WishCard` in `Missing.tsx`,
the banner in `frontend/src/components/PriceNews.tsx` (in `TopBanners`, `Shell.tsx`) and the words in
`frontend/src/shop.ts`. It changes the last row of "Shopping for a wish" (the range) and builds on "Wishlist links and
their prices".

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| One price, **the most you'd pay**, in place of From and To. It does both jobs: Nines tells you of a drop to it or less, and Claude's searches stay under it. Saving sends `price_min: null`; the API and the prompt still take a lowest price, and a wish that has one says it until it is changed | The owner asked why there was a lower limit, and nobody wants a cheaper one hidden. Someone could have kept the range, or kept two numbers (a search range and an alert price); one number the owner understands is worth more than the flexibility. Older wishes and callers keep working. |
| News only on a change: a find's newest price is a drop (as before, below the highest seen in its currency) and it **came down** to that price (`came_down`: the price before it was higher). The moment is the first check at that price, so a price checked again each day at the same price is one piece of news; a price that went up from a lower one, a sold-out price and a price first seen already low are none. Claude's finds and sent links follow the same rule | "Flag to me when the price drops" is about a change. A find first seen under the price is already on the page in front of the owner; telling it again would be noise. |
| With the most you'd pay set, only a drop to it or less is news; set in another currency than the find's, it is not compared and any drop is news (`at_price` false). Never converted | As the rest of the app: a price in another currency is never compared. |
| "Seen" is kept on the server, one moment per wish (`wishes.news_seen_at`, migration 0045), set by an explicit POST when the wish's page opens (and when a search or price check on it ends) or the banner's Got it; reading the news changes nothing. Existing wishes start with none, so a price already down is told once after the update | The phone and the computer must agree: kept on a device, the same drop would be told on each. Per wish rather than per find, because opening the page shows all its finds at once. A GET that changed something could be set off by a prefetch or a second tab. |
| *(Changed by "The wishlist's own page": See them opens the wishlist.)* A banner on every screen, in `TopBanners` beside the update banner and built from the same `Notice`, rather than a line on Today. One wish: its sentence with See it and Got it; several: how many, with See them (the Wardrobe, not marking them seen: the list is not each wish) and Got it (all it told) | Today is for what to wear (DESIGN, the jobs in order); the news is wanted wherever the owner happens to be, and the banner is where "something happened while you were away" already goes. One banner, not one per wish, keeps the top of the page short. |
| Asked for when the page opens, every 30 minutes while it is shown and when it comes back into view (the cache's usual rule), never while hidden or offline | Prices are checked once a day, so a half-hour rhythm, the updates banner's, misses nothing and costs nothing. |
| Never in the demo closet: the route answers nothing and the page does not ask | The demo's prices are sample data, read only when asked there, and the daily round never opens it. |
| No telling the owner on their phone when Nines is not open (a push notification or an email). The owner, 23 November: "No notification needed" | It would need Nines reachable from outside to send it, or a service that holds the phone's address or an email account's password, and so would change what leaves the computer. The banner tells them the next time they open the app. |
| The page reads in the order it is used: what to look for; what is watched (the pages sent first, then Claude's finds); one sentence about watching with the price and "Last checked … Check now" (was Check prices, and two lines about checking and buying); looking for more (Find some, Add a link, and the price when nothing is watched yet); the note; "Would it go with my clothes?", "I bought it", "Remove from the list". A find's Remove is "Stop watching" | The product watched was buried mid-page, the range read like an alert setting, and there were two Removes. What a check says goes under what started it: a price check under Check now, a search under Find some. |
| "Would it go with my clothes?" (was "I found one: would it work?", with a `title` saying it checks a piece against your closet) opens Would this work? from the wish as before, its name filled in from the newest page you sent (`link.name`, cut at a word to 120 characters) with what the wish's look for fills in | The old label did not say what it does. A sent link is not described in the closet's words, so the name is all that can be filled in without asking Claude. |
| The most you'd pay opens in a `Sheet` (a popover under the sentence on a computer), with Save, Clear and Cancel beside the title (`closeLabel`, new on the shared `Sheet`); the currency starts as the wish's, else its newest find's, else USD | DESIGN, principle 3: the inline form pushed the page down. "Done" beside the title would have read as saving. |
| A find whose price changed from one day to another says how: "The price was $177.00 on 3 Nov, $160.00 on Tuesday and $150.00 yesterday.", each day's last price, the last three changes | The drop line says how much; this says when, in the app's own day words. Several checks in one day would otherwise read "today and … today". |
| On the wishlist row, a drop to the most you'd pay or less adds "That's within your price." beside the drop's pill | Said briefly, outside the pill, which wrapped at phone width with it inside. |

## The gap list worked out once at a time (23 November, the browser tests going red overnight)

The browser tests' search and Wardrobe waits (`14-shop`, `32-places`) failed on every run from the night of 22 to 23
November, with no change to the code. It was not the date: the same code passed at 02:28 on 23 November, and every run
that failed was on a slow runner (all its tests together over 1,290 seconds, against 840 to 1,230 for the runs that
passed). What made those two waits so sensitive to a slow computer was work done twice. Opening What's missing,
reloading it and opening it again asked for the list each time while the first was still being worked out, and each
asker worked it out beside the others: `32-places` started six at once, so each took six times as long. And until the
owner has said how they live, every search, price check and "Would this work?" worked the plain list out again,
a third of what judging two finds costs on the demo's closet. Both are in `app.gap_analysis` and its callers.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| One gap analysis at a time per closet (`gap_analysis`, a lock per profile): whoever asks while it is being worked out waits for that answer and takes it from the kept list. Asking for a different closet or wear log waits too, then works out its own | Six side by side under Python's one lock take as long as six one after another, and each page waits for all of them; waiting for the first gives every page its answer as soon as one is done. The page that asked first may be gone (a reload), and its work is still the answer the next one wants. |
| "Would this work?" and the shop's finds are always given the list as kept (`closet_for_check`, `Shopper.listed`): the plain list until the owner has said how they live, the weighed one once they have. A check given the plain list still compares it with the plain analysis with the piece, as before | It is the same list the check would work out, from the same items and wears (the kept list leaves laundry out of what decides it, and so does the analysis), so the answers are the same, and seconds quicker. `shop.judge` without a list still works the plain one out, for callers that have none. |
| No test's wait was raised. The waits were long enough for the work; the work was done several times over | Measured with the browser tests' four files at once on this container: the old-addresses test 30.8 s to 10.3 s, the search's finds and drop 44 s to 28 s, the whole run 1,155 to 964 test-seconds. |

## The wishlist's own page (23 November, the owner's request)

The owner: "is there a reason the wishlist is baked into the wardrobe? It seems like a completely different feature."
It was put in the Wardrobe on 20 November ("Four places and Add"), when the app was cut to four places and Add and wishes
were mostly gaps Nines suggested buying. Now the owner sends product links and watches their prices, a job of its own.
Asked where it should live, the owner chose a page of its own, one tap from the Wardrobe, the way Trips opens from
Today, with the price banner's See them going straight to it, and the tab bar as it is. The route is in `parseRoute`
(`src/router.tsx`, `WISHLIST_PATH`) and `movedTo` (`src/places.ts`); the page is a branch of `WardrobeScreen` in
`src/screens/Wardrobe.tsx`; its parts (`Thinking` with `WishlistRow`, and `Wishlist`) are in `src/screens/Missing.tsx`;
the row's words are `wishlistWords` in `src/shop.ts`. It changes rows of "Four places and Add" and one of "The wish page
and price news".

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| A page one level down in the Wardrobe (`/wardrobe/wishlist`), lit as the Wardrobe, with "‹ Wardrobe"; not a fifth tab, and not the list on the Wardrobe page. The owner's choice | Buying is still the Wardrobe's job, and a new place in the tab bar needs a reason none of the four can hold (DESIGN, Screens). One level down keeps the wishlist two taps from the bar, as Trips is from Today. |
| The Wardrobe keeps "Thinking of buying something?" with Would this work?, and in place of the list one row: "Wishlist", how many things are on it ("3 things", "Nothing on it yet") and, in the drop's pill, how many prices went down ("2 prices went down"). A drop is counted as the list shows it, a wish whose row says "Down from …" (`wish.drop`), not as the banner's news | The Wardrobe is over the word budget, and the list was a good part of it: in the phone check at 393px it went from 501 words to 434 (the budget is 350), and from 4,506px tall to 3,901. A count and the drops are what decides whether to open it; what each drop is, is said once, on the list. Counting what the list shows keeps the row and the page it opens in agreement, and works in the demo closet, which has no news. Someone could have kept the first few rows on the Wardrobe with "See all"; that says on two screens what the list says. |
| "Add a link" and "Add by hand" are on the wishlist, above the rows, opening in place as before. With nothing on the list, an `EmptyState` ("Your wishlist is empty", one sentence on what it is for) has **See what's worth buying**, which opens the Wardrobe, as its one button | They add to the list, so they sit with it. The empty state's button is the third way onto the list, "I'll look for this" on a suggestion; the other two are already on the page, and the same button twice on one screen would be noise. |
| A wish's page and the page a shared link opens say "‹ Wishlist" and go back to the wishlist; "Remove from the list" ends there; "I bought it" still opens Add. On a computer, an item opened beside the Wardrobe says "‹ Wardrobe" (was "Back to the wishlist"), as the same address does on a phone | A back link names the page it returns to, and that is the wishlist now. The side panel holds "Thinking of buying something?" with the row, no longer the list. Would this work? opened from a wish still says "‹ Wardrobe", as before. |
| The banner's **See them** opens the wishlist and still marks nothing seen | The list is where the drops are, but it is not each wish: a wish's news is seen when its page opens, or with Got it ("The wish page and price news"). |
| The old `/wishlist`, which led to the Wardrobe, lands on the wishlist's page, through `movedTo` like every old address | A bookmark of the obvious address finds the list. |
| The wishlist and a wish's page wait for the wishes only, not for the analysis of what is worth buying; offline they show the copy of the wishes kept on this device (`savedWishes`), marked as saved earlier | Neither shows a suggestion, and the analysis can take seconds (DESIGN, principle 3: a screen waits for what it needs). From the banner, either would otherwise have waited for it. Before, a wish's page waited for both. |
| Adding a link no longer asks for the analysis again before the new wish opens, and a wish's page that first finds a search, a price check or a link's reading already over asks for the list once | A wish made from a link fills no gap (only a wish made from a gap marks one, `wishlist.py`). Reached without the analysis in, the wait was seconds, and the page had been read by the time the wish opened; the wish's page refreshed the list only when it saw a reading end, so the wish kept the shop's address as its name. Found by the browser test that now starts on the wishlist. |
| The page is a branch of `WardrobeScreen`, with its parts in `Missing.tsx`, as a wish's page is, rather than a screen file of its own | It shares the Wardrobe's queries and its actions (adding a wish by hand or from a link, which then opens the wish); a separate screen would have taken them all as props. |
| "I'll look for this" still says "On your wishlist" in a toast, with no way to open the list from it | The toast takes only a message, and the row that counts the new wish is on the same page. |

## Checkout codes (23 November, the owner's request)

The owner: "there are times where discounts are cart only, it'll say like 40% off with code X, and i think we should
include that kind of discount". Asked where the code should come from, they chose: Nines picks it up when the page's
text has it, you can type one you saw, Claude reports one when it is asked anyway, a code read from the page goes once
the page stops showing it, and the price after the code counts for drops and for the most you'd pay. The reading is
`codes` in `backend/closet/productpage.py`; the checks, the price after the code and who may change a code are in
`backend/closet/shop.py` (`code_problem`, `clean_code`, `price_with`, `best_code`, `_page_code`, `_claude_code`,
`set_code`, `remove_code`, `paid`) with migration `0046_find_codes.sql` and the routes `PUT` and `DELETE
/api/wishlist/{id}/finds/{find_id}/code`; Claude's fields are in `tagger/shopping.py` (`PriceSeen`, `price_prompt`); the
page is `FindCode` and `CodeSheet` in `frontend/src/screens/Shop.tsx` (styles in `Shop.css`), with the words in
`frontend/src/shop.ts`. It builds on "Wishlist links and their prices" and "The wish page and price news".

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| At most one code per find: the code (letters, digits, `-` and `_`, 3 to 30, kept upper-case as shops show them), either a percent off (1 to 90) or an amount off in the find's currency (more than 0, less than the price), who saw it (`page`, `claude`) or typed it (`you`), and when. The price after it is worked out by Nines, rounded half up to the cent, never typed | A shop's checkout takes one code at a time, and the card has room for one line. Someone could have kept every code a page shows; the biggest is the one you would use. A typed price after the code could disagree with the code. |
| Nines reads a code only from the page's visible text: never its scripts, styles, head, a template or an attribute. A page whose banner is put in by a script has none, and says nothing about it | The text is what a shopper sees, and reading it costs nothing. A code inside a script may be for another country, a test or a past sale. Running the page's scripts would need a browser inside Nines and would run a stranger's code. Many shops load their banners by script, so most pages have no code in their text: that is expected, not a failure. |
| A discount ("40% off", "$20 off", "save 25%") and a code (after "code" or "promo code") count together only within one short stretch of text: one block of the page, or two neighbouring blocks of at most 80 characters each; at most 50 characters apart, each comma or full stop between them counting 20 more, and each the other's nearest | "40% off with code X", "Use code X for 40% off", "Code X: 40% off" and a banner split into two boxes are the shapes shops use. A code farther from a discount, or nearer another one, is likely about something else (free shipping, another product). |
| Careful about what is passed over: an offer said, in its stretch or the block before or after, to be for a first order or purchase, new customers, signing up, subscribing, a newsletter, the app, members, loyalty, students or referrals; "up to 40% off"; a word written after "code" that is plainly not one ("CODE", "OFF", "SALE", "AT", "CHECKOUT", "NEEDED" and the like, also checked of Claude's codes); a word in small letters with no digit ("use code at checkout"); a zip, postal, style or product code | A code missed costs a saving the owner can still see in the shop. A wrong one shows a price they cannot pay and sends false price news, which is worse. "Up to" is not every product. |
| When the page shows several, the biggest discount is kept: the lowest price after the code; with no price yet, the biggest percent (an amount off needs a price to be checked against). Every value is checked as `shop.clean_read` checks the rest, and an amount off in another currency than the find's is dropped | "Prefer the biggest" (the owner's request). Prices in different currencies are never compared. |
| A code read from the page goes when a later read of the page shows none. A code Claude saw goes when its next price check gives none, and when Nines reads the page's price itself and its text shows none (Claude is not asked about that page then, so nothing else would take its code away). A code Nines read is kept over Claude's, and replaces it. A code you typed is never changed or removed by any read; it stays until you remove it | The owner: a code read from the page goes once the page stops showing it. A stale code would show a price you cannot pay. A code you typed is yours, as everything the owner sets is. |
| A code you remove is remembered on the find (`code_removed`) and not taken from the page or from Claude again; another code is | Otherwise a code that did not work at checkout would come back with the next day's read. |
| The price after the code is what a drop, price news (`came_down`, `news`) and "within your price" count, when there is one; every price point keeps the price after the code it was seen with, and that code (`find_prices.with_code`, `code`), rather than working it out later from the find's code | The owner chose the price after the code. Each point keeping its own lets a code that appears and brings the price down be news, and one that goes away be a price going back up, which is not. Prices in different currencies are still never compared. |
| A code you type is not news to you: saving it keeps the price again with the code and marks the wish's news seen (not in the demo closet). Removing it keeps the price again without it, which, going up, is never news. Saving the same code again adds nothing | You already know: the code is what you just typed. |
| No new calls to Claude: Claude reports a code only in the price checks it already makes (Check now, the daily round, a link whose page gives no price), as optional fields of its answer on both routes (the plan's JSON form and the API's strict tool), checked like everything else it says and dropped alone when they do not fit; the prompt asks for a code anyone can use, in the same careful words. The owner chose this over a daily check by Claude of every page | Reading the text costs nothing; a daily Claude check would cost something for every link every day. The prompt grew by about a hundred tokens against the 300,000 the shopping estimate allows for the pages read, so `spend.py` is unchanged (a test in `test_shop.py` keeps the longest price check under a tenth of it). |
| On the find card, under its price: "$106.20 with code TEST40 at checkout (40% off)" (or "Code TEST40 takes 40% off at checkout." when there is no price to take it from), then "Nines saw this code on the page." or "Claude saw this code on the page." (nothing when you typed it), with "Add a code" or "Change the code". The drop line, the banner and the wishlist's row say the price after the code with it ("down to $106.20 with code TEST40"), through the app's one price phrase (`priceWords`) | Who saw it matters as Claude's reading is marked elsewhere, and a code you typed needs no explaining. One phrase for a price with a code everywhere. |
| The code opens in a `Sheet` (a popover under its button on a computer): the code, "% off" or "Amount off" (a segmented choice, one of the two), the number, Save, "Remove the code" when there is one, and Cancel beside the title. The page checks only that the boxes hold a code and a number; Nines checks the rest and its sentence is shown | DESIGN, principle 3, as for the most you'd pay. One set of rules, on the server, with the sentences beside them. |

## Where a piece is (23 November; BACKLOG "Where a piece is")

The owner: "at the cleaner's, at the tailor's, lent out, needs mending. Left out of outfits and trips like the laundry,
with one line on the closet and a tap to bring it back." The code: `backend/closet/away.py` (sending, coming back,
undo, the history), migration `0051_away.sql` (`items.away`, `away_since`, `away_until`, `away_note`, and `away_log`),
`outfits._unavailable` and `outfits.AWAY`, `trips._Planner`, `gaps._closet`, `insights._closet`, `wears._record`; on
the page `src/away.ts`, `components/WhereItIs.tsx`, `WhereSheet.tsx` and `AwayView.tsx`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Away is its own state on an item (`away`: `cleaner`, `tailor` or `lent`, null in the closet), not a value of `location`, not the laundry's state and not a `condition`. A piece can be away and need mending at once; needing mending stays the condition it was | Where a piece is and what state it is in are two facts, and each is stored once (DESIGN, "One thing, one home"). As a location it would lose where the piece lives when it is home (the laundry's own reason, above); as a condition it would forget the piece needs mending once it is back from the cleaner's. "Needs mending" was already a condition, so the four words the owner gave are three places and one state |
| An away piece is left out wherever the laundry is: `outfits._unavailable` says "at the cleaner's", "at the tailor's" or "lent out" before the laundry (so a piece both in the basket and at the cleaner's is said to be at the cleaner's), and the three words are among `OWNED_REASONS`. Building around it answers with where it is and the day it is expected ("at the cleaner's until Friday 27 November"); Wear this again fills its place | One rule in the one place every outfit reads (the engine, the stylist's list, Wear this again, a trip), as for the laundry and mending. The weekday is the date's own, so the rule still needs no clock |
| A trip packs an away piece only when the owner expects it back before the first day (the first day itself is too late to pack), packing it as it will be by then (back from the tailor's, mended), and the plan says it "counts on it being back before you leave". A piece in the bag already is packed whatever | The owner's rule. Without a day, Nines cannot know it will be back, so it is left out like a piece in the laundry with no laundry day before the trip |
| Coming back does what the place means: from the cleaner's the piece is clean (its count of wears starts again, as marking it clean does); from the tailor's "needs repair" is cleared, said once on the page ("and no longer marked as needing mending"); from being lent nothing else changes. Everything coming back changed is kept with the return (`away_log.undo`) so the latest return can be undone exactly, for the rest of the day, from the piece's row and from the closet's away view | The cleaner's cleans and the tailor's mends; asking each time would be a question with one answer. Undo should undo (DESIGN, "Nothing is lost by a slip"), and a return undone must put back the laundry state and the condition too, not only the place. Could have been: ask "Is it mended?" on the way back from the tailor's |
| "All clean" and the laundry day leave an away piece away; the closet counts a piece away under away, not under the laundry, even when its laundry state is `laundry` | It is not in the basket at home. Marking it clean while it is at the cleaner's would be harmless, but bringing it back would be wrong |
| A wear for today of an away piece (the computer's today or tomorrow, as wears take; any wear call, Record an outfit included) brings it back, as the owner saying "It's back" would, and removing that very wear sends it away again. A wear for a day before today leaves it away | Wearing it is the evidence it is home; refusing the wear would make the owner bring it back first. A wear filled in from memory says nothing about where the piece is now |
| Still owned: What's missing, Would this work? and the insights count an away piece as the owner's. But nothing nudges you to wear it while it is away: "Not worn yet", "Not worn for a long time" and What's missing's note of what was never worn leave it out | It is yours, so it is not missing; telling you to wear a suit that is at the cleaner's is noise |
| The note stays on this computer: it is stored only in `items.away_note` (and the return's undo), read only by `away.py`, shown on the item's own page (not in the closet's list), and never put into anything sent to Claude. A test (`tests/test_away.py`) checks the stylist's brief and prompt and a shop search's block about the owner with a note in them, that `away_note` is read by `away.py` alone, and that nothing under `tagger/` reads where a piece is | The owner's words about a loan or a repair are private ("lent to my brother"); the stylist has no use for them, as the piece is not listed |
| *(Changed 23 November: the closet's line says only "2 away"; see "The top of the closet".)* Nothing arrives by itself: no reminder, no badge, no notification. A piece past its day is said only where you look: the closet's line ("2 away, 1 expected back") and its row ("Expected back on Friday 20 Nov") | The owner said no to notifications (BACKLOG) |
| *(Changed 23 November: see "The top of the closet".)* On a phone the closet line's away part sits on a line of its own under the others; on a computer it follows them after a dot | At 375px "5 to check · 4 in the laundry · 2 away, 1 expected back" does not fit, and a wrapped line left a dot at the start of the second line |
| In the history, going and coming back are listed among the description's changes in time order, in the history's words ("You took it to the cleaner's", "It came back from the tailor's when you wore it"), with no Undo there | One history per piece; the return's Undo is on the row that says it, so there is one way to undo it |
## The Wardrobe made short, Your nines and what your clothes cost you (23 November, the owner's request)

The Wardrobe was the longest screen (about 500 words and 4,900px at 393px). The work behind it: the things worth
buying folded (`GapCard` in `frontend/src/screens/Missing.tsx`), how clothes are used said once
(`frontend/src/screens/Insights.tsx`), and two new parts of the one insights answer, `nines` and `spending`
(`backend/closet/insights.py`, numbers only; their words in `frontend/src/insights.ts`, money through `spend.amount`).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Every thing worth buying starts closed: its name (the button; a tap anywhere on the head opens it), how much it helps and one line of why, the first reason's first sentence up to its colon or semicolon (`gaps.shortWhy`), at most two lines. Open, it shows what it showed before; where you are going stays put, since it opens downwards. None starts open | Three open cards were about 3,000px. The task allowed the first one open if the first phone screen still held two more titles; with the price banner and "Tell it how you live" above, an open card fills the screen, so all start closed. Situations ("Useful for") are inside the open card; the filters above already say them. |
| "I'll look for this" leaves the same card in its place, open, and puts "On your wishlist" with **Undo** where its two buttons were; closed, a wished card says "On your wishlist" in place of its line of why. No toast says it again. Undo removes the wish made from the gap | The review (A4) found the card folding to one row and the page moving about 450px, with the next card's button landing under the finger. The wished card is drawn by the same component, so whether it is open survives the state change. |
| "Tell it how you live · 1 minute" keeps its title and buttons and loses its sentence ("More for your everyday…") | One line each, as the owner asked; the title says it. |
| **Your nines** takes the place of Worn most: the nine pieces worn most in each of the last 30 days, 90 days and all time, ties to the piece worn more recently, then by name; it opens on the first window with nine, else the one with most. A tile is the piece's picture (photos or drawings, as Settings says) with its number of wears on it, and a link to the piece; its accessible name is the piece and its wears | One thing, one home: Worn most's list and sentence said the same as the square. The square is three across at every width, held to 26rem, so it is a square on a 375px phone and not a wall on a computer. |
| Your nines counts everything worn, lounge pants, swim trunks and sport shoes included (unlike `gaps.everyday` everywhere else) | They are what you wore: lounge pants worn every evening are honestly among the most worn. Elsewhere the rule asks whether to buy, archive or wear something out, which these never answer; here the question is only what you wore. |
| With fewer than nine, the square shows what there is and one sentence says how many ("Only 5 pieces were worn in the last 30 days."); with nothing recorded, the same sentence as anywhere else ("Nothing has been recorded as worn yet…", or "…since 20 July 2026." after a break) | Say what the app doesn't know, once. |
| How your clothes are used says each fact once: the "Not worn" heading has no label under it, and the never-worn card no figure ("4 pieces") over a sentence starting "4 pieces"; "Not worn for a long time" shows only when it lists something or after a break from recording (otherwise its "show here once…" sentence is left out); nothing about how the clothes are used shows when nothing has been recorded, except Your nines saying so; "Archive what you no longer want, or keep it." is gone (the buttons say it) | The review (P3): "Not worn" over "NOT WORN YET", "Worn most" over "WORN MOST", and how long the record goes back three times. |
| "The wear log" is not said on the page: the server's sentences say "you began recording what you wear N days ago" | The owner was never shown the phrase. |
| What you spent comes from each piece's price and its date bought (`acquired_on`, on the item page under "Size, material and more"), never the day it was added to Nines | Most of a closet is added on the first days, whatever year it was bought; counting the day added would put years of buying into this one. |
| In view, one line: "You spent $1,240 on 9 pieces this year." once at least 3 pieces have both a price and a date (`SPENT_MIN_PIECES`); with nothing bought this year, the last year there was. Before that, how many pieces have no price yet (or, when all have one, how many have no date bought), with "Add one to the piece you wear most." opening that piece | A total of one or two pieces says nothing about a closet. The piece worn most is the one whose cost per wear means most, and its page is where the price and date are filled in. |
| Under More numbers, the cost per wear card is "What your clothes cost you": each year's spending, the closet's cost per wear (the server's sentence, as before), then folded, "Earned their price" (worn 3 times or more, `EARNED_MIN_WEARS`, the least per wear first, 3 at most) and "Not yet: a price, and few or no wears" (fewer than 3 wears, the dearest first, 3 at most), then what the wishlist would cost. This replaces Best value / Most per wear on the page; `cost_per_wear` in the answer is unchanged | Two lists by one rule never hold the same piece. Pieces with a price and no wear can now be in both Not worn and Not yet (the owner asked for "a price, and few or no wears"); this supersedes, for this card only, the rule in Insights that they are listed once. |
| What the wishlist would cost: each open wish at the lowest price of its finds still on sale, after a checkout code, in the currency of its newest find; summed by currency and never across; wishes with no such price counted and said ("1 thing has no price yet") | A shop in euros and one in dollars cannot be added without a rate, and Nines converts nothing. The closet's own prices have no currency (the price field is in dollars: Insights, "Money is shown with `$`"), so a closet in more than one currency can only arise on the wishlist. |
| The phone check gives five pieces of its copy of the demo closet made-up prices and dates (three this year, two last), as it gives long names; the demo data is unchanged | The demo has prices and no dates, so the line in view would otherwise only ever show its "no price yet" form there. |

## The shared parts: failed reads, banners, dates, toasts, sheets (23 November, the review of the shared parts)

The UI review of 23 November (A1, A2, A6, B4, P1, P3, P5, P7, P8, P10, P16, P17). The code is `components/ui/Failed.tsx`,
`src/steady.ts`, `components/PriceNews.tsx`, `Updates.tsx`, `RestoreNotice.tsx`, `wears.ts` (the dates), `readAmount`
in `spend.ts`, `App.tsx` (the toasts) and `components/ui/Sheet.tsx`.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **The failed state.** Every screen's failed read goes through one piece, `Failed` (`components/ui/`), given the query: with nothing read before, "Couldn't load your closet." with why (Nines on your computer can't be reached; it answered with a fault; or Nines' own sentence) and Try again in place of the content; with an earlier answer, that answer stays under one line, "This is your closet as it was earlier. …", with Try again. While the computer cannot be reached and something is on screen, it adds nothing: the offline banner says it once. `api.ts` never lets a bare status phrase ("Internal Server Error") through as a message | Seven screens printed the server's raw words and offered nothing; Would this work? shimmered for ever. One piece means a new screen gets the words and Try again by putting the query in it, and principle 7 (say which, once, and still show what it has) holds everywhere. |
| **Banners wait for the next screen.** What is said above every screen (price news, an update ready, what a restore did) takes what is known when a screen opens and keeps it while you read (`useAsOpened`); an answer that arrives later shows from the next screen. Only your own tap (Got it, OK) changes it in between. The last price news is kept on this device with its other saved copies, so it is drawn with the page when Nines opens; the wish whose page is open is left out of it | It arrived after the page and pushed every screen down 74 to 115 px (principle 3). Keeping its space only when the last answer had news still moves the page when the answer differs; floating over the page like a toast would cover what you tap for as long as it stays, and the news stays until you look. Waiting for the next screen never moves anything, and the copy kept on the device means it is usually there from the start anyway. |
| On Add the two buttons come first, the explanation is folded (`HowItWorks`, "What to photograph") and the notice about cutting clothes out comes after both | The notice arrives late (it waits for `/api/model`) and moved the buttons 120 px; the 126 words before the buttons pushed Take photos under the tab bar on a small phone. |
| **One price reader**, `readAmount` in `spend.ts` (and `readCents`), for every box a price is typed in. With both a point and a comma, the last is the decimal point; with one, one or two digits after it make it a decimal point, three after a comma a thousand; `1.234` alone is refused rather than guessed. The boxes keep `inputmode="decimal"`, whose keypad shows the phone's own decimal key | Two readers dropped every comma, so `45,50` became 4,550 and said Saved. A phone set to a region that writes 45,50 has a comma on that key, so the comma has to be read, not refused. |
| **One way to write a date** (`wears.ts`): day first ("Thursday 12 Nov", "Thu 12 Nov", "3 Nov 2026"), the 24-hour clock ("14:05"), moments in a sentence as "today at 14:05", "yesterday at 09:41", "on 3 Nov at 08:00", and at the head of a list's line as "Today, 14:05". Nothing asks the browser's locale; `wears.test.ts` fails on a `toLocaleDateString`, `toLocaleTimeString`, `Intl.DateTimeFormat` or a month list outside it | The app wrote "Mon 23 Nov" by hand but "Nov 23, 2026, 05:07 AM" and "4:49 AM" wherever the browser's locale was asked, so the phone and the computer disagreed with each other and with the rest of the page. Day first matches the dates already written by hand; the 24-hour clock is the one the old helper's comment promised and reads the same on any device. |
| History names a shade, never a colour code: the shade with that colour, or the one nearest it (`details.words`) | "#3f7a4c to #a3ad8f" means nothing to the owner. |
| *(23 November, the item page.)* A colour chosen alone (on the item page, in the review, or said in a sentence) is stored as the colour word with no colour of its own (`color_hex` null, marked the owner's); a shade is named only when the owner chose it, or while the colour is still Claude's (the shade Claude named, else the nearest to the colour it measured). The piece is drawn in the word's own colour, and History says the shade became "none chosen" | Choosing Blue sent blue's swatch, and the nearest shade to it ("French blue") lit up as if chosen (P15). Keeping a family colour as the hex would still claim the nearest shade, and the page's swatches and the drawing's differ slightly. Claude's own description is left as it was until the owner changes the colour. |
| **Toasts** sit at the top of the screen, and at most one of each kind shows (a new one takes the last one's place); an error stays 5 s | At the bottom they covered the shades a sheet had just opened and the row above the tab bar; error toasts stayed 6 s and piled up. The top is the title, which nothing taps, and a phone sheet leaves it dimmed. |
| *(23 November, the item page.)* On a piece's page a change that saves as it is made is said beside what changed with the shared `SaveStatus` words ("Saving…", "Saved" for 3 s, "Didn't save"): over the free end of a box's label line, in a row before its chevron, or under the name, never moving anything. A toast is kept for what went wrong (the reason, beside "Didn't save") and for archiving, which changes where the piece is listed. Wearing, the laundry, where it is, Fix the details and History keep their own words | Every change raised its own "Saved" toast, one after another over what the owner tapped next. `SaveStatus` is the confirmation DESIGN already names for something that saves as it is changed (the wish's note, Your sizes); a third pattern was not wanted. Placing it over space the page already has keeps principle 3; the name's line is the one place it takes room (about 12 px). |
| **Sheets** take Escape from wherever focus is (the latest one open; something inside that takes Escape for itself calls `preventDefault`), and a phone sheet holds the page behind it still (`html.sheet-open`, and the dimmed part takes no drag). How you live is a `Sheet` too | Escape only worked with focus inside; the page scrolled under a sheet; How you live was a third kind of overlay, a full-screen dialog with no Done. |
| **The type scale is in rem** (`--text-xs` 0.75rem to `--text-3xl` 2.4375rem), still 12 to 39 px at the default size. Spacing, radii and `--tap` stay in px | The tokens were the only place type sizes are set (`design.test.ts`), so the change was contained; the browser's text size setting did nothing before. Checked with the phone check at a 125% root size. |
